The space of conormal distributions plays a very important role in our work. We mainly follow
[
KN65
,
Hör71
]
,
[
Hör85
, Section 18.2]
,
[
Sim90
, Chapters 3–5]
,
[
Mel96
, Chapters 4 and 6]
,
[
MU08
, Chapters 3 and 9]
, which are oriented to the role they play in pseudodifferential operators and generalizations of those operators. The study of its natural topology was begun in
[
Mel96
, Chapters 4 and 6]
and continued in
[
ÁLKL23
]
.

For the sake of simplicity, we consider the case of the trivial line bundle first. But all definitions, properties and notation have obvious extensions for arbitrary vector bundles, like in
Sections

2.1.7
and
2.1.9
, either by using local trivializations, or by taking
C^{\infty}(M)
-tensor products with spaces of smooth sections. When needed, the case of arbitrary vector bundles will be used without further comment.

2.2.1.
Differential operators tangent to a submanifold

Let
L
be a regular submanifold of
M
of codimension
n^{\prime}
and dimension
n^{\prime\prime}
, which is a closed subset. Let
\mathfrak{X}(M,L)\subset\mathfrak{X}(M)
be the Lie subalgebra and
C^{\infty}(M)
-submodule of vector fields tangent to
L
. Using
\mathfrak{X}(M,L)
instead of
\mathfrak{X}(M)
, we can define the filtered subalgebra and
C^{\infty}(M)
-submodule
\operatorname{Diff}(M,L)\subset\operatorname{Diff}(M)
like in
Section

2.1.7
. We have

(2.2.1)

A\in\operatorname{Diff}(M,L)\Rightarrow A^{\text{\rm t}}\in\operatorname{Diff}(M,L;\Omega)\;.

By the conditions on
L
, every
\operatorname{Diff}^{m}(M,L)
(
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
) is locally finitely
C^{\infty}(M)
-generated, and therefore
\operatorname{Diff}(M,L)
is countably
C^{\infty}(M)
-generated. The surjective restriction map
\mathfrak{X}(M,L)\to\mathfrak{X}(L)
,
X\mapsto X|_{L}
, induces a surjective linear restriction map of filtered algebras and
C^{\infty}(M)
-modules,

(2.2.2)

\operatorname{Diff}(M,L)\to\operatorname{Diff}(L)\;,\quad A\mapsto A|_{L}\;.

Let
(U,x)
be a chart of
M
adapted to
L
; i.e., it is a diffeomorphism

\displaystyle x=(x^{1},\dots,x^{n})\equiv(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}):U\to U^{\prime}\times U^{\prime\prime}\;,

\displaystyle x^{\prime}=(x^{\prime 1},\dots,x^{\prime n^{\prime}})\;,\quad x^{\prime\prime}=(x^{\prime\prime 1},\dots,x^{\prime\prime n^{\prime\prime}})\;,\quad L_{0}:=L\cap U=\{x^{\prime}=0\}\;,

for some open
U^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}}
and
U^{\prime\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime\prime}}
. If
L
is of codimension one, then we will use the notation
(x,y)
instead of
(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})
. For every
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
\operatorname{Diff}^{m}(U,L_{0})
is
C^{\infty}(U)
-spanned by the operators
x^{\prime I}\partial_{x^{\prime}}^{J}\partial_{x^{\prime\prime}}^{K}
with
|J|+|K|\leq m
and
|I|=|J|
; we may use the generators
\partial_{x^{\prime}}^{J}\partial_{x^{\prime\prime}}^{K}x^{\prime I}
as well, with the same conditions on the multi-indices.

2.2.2.
Conormal distributions when
M
is compact

Suppose
M
is compact. Then the space of
conormal distributions
at
L
of
Sobolev order
at most
s\in\mathbb{R}
is the LCS and
C^{\infty}(M)
-module

(2.2.3)

I^{(s)}(M,L)=\{\,u\in C^{-\infty}(M)\mid\operatorname{Diff}(M,L)\,u\subset H^{s}(M)\,\}\;,

with the topology like in (
2.1.1
). This is a totally reflexive Fréchet space
[
ÁLKL23
, Proposition 4.1]
. We have continuous inclusions

(2.2.4)

I^{(s)}(M,L)\subset I^{(s^{\prime})}(M,L)\quad(s^{\prime}<s)\;,

and consider the LCSs and
C^{\infty}(M)
-modules

I(M,L)=\bigcup_{s}I^{(s)}(M,L)\;,\quad I^{(\infty)}(M,L)=\bigcap_{s}I^{(s)}(M,L)\;.

Thus
I(M,L)
is a Hausdorff LF-space (
Section

2.1.1
), and
I^{(\infty)}(M,L)
is a Fréchet space and submodule of
I(M,L)
. The elements of
I(M,L)
are called
conormal distributions
of
M
at
L
(or of
(M,L)
). The spaces
I^{(s)}(M,L)
form what is called the
Sobolev-order filtration
of
I(M,L)
, or the
Sobolev-order inductive spectrum
defining
I(M,L)
. From (
2.2.3
), it follows that there are canonical continuous inclusions,

(2.2.5)

C^{\infty}(M)\subset I^{(\infty)}(M,L)\;,\quad I(M,L)\subset C^{-\infty}(M)\;.

Indeed,
C^{\infty}(M)
is dense in
I(M,L)

[
Mel96
, Eq. (6.2.12)]
,
[
ÁLKL23
, Corollary 4.6]
.

I(M,L)
is barreled, ultrabornological, webbed, acyclic and a Montel space, and therefore complete, boundedly/compactly/sequently retractive and reflexive
[
ÁLKL23
, Corollaries 4.2 and 4.7]
(
Section

2.1.1
).

2.2.3.
Filtration of
I(M,L)
by the symbol order when
M
is compact

Take a chart of
M
adapted to
L
,
(U,x=(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}))
, like in
Section

2.2.1
. We use the identity
U^{\prime\prime}\times\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}}\equiv N^{*}U^{\prime\prime}
, and the symbol spaces
S^{m}(U^{\prime\prime}\times\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}})\equiv S^{m}(N^{*}U^{\prime\prime})
(
Section

2.1.8
). The following holds true for
s,\bar{m}\in\mathbb{R}

[
Hör85
, Theorem 18.2.8]
,
[
Mel96
, Proposition 6.1.1]
,
[
MU08
, Lemma 9.33]
,
[
ÁLKL23
, Remark 4.4]
:

•

If
s<-\bar{m}-n^{\prime}/2
, then the map
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm cv}}(N^{*}U^{\prime\prime})\to C^{\infty}(U)
,
a\mapsto u
, given by

u(x)=(2\pi)^{-n^{\prime}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}}}e^{i\langle x^{\prime},\xi\rangle}a(x^{\prime\prime},\xi)\,d\xi\;,

has a continuous extension
S^{\bar{m}}(N^{*}U^{\prime\prime})\to I^{(s)}(U,L_{0})
.

•

If
\bar{m}>-s-n^{\prime}/2
, then the map
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(U)\to C^{\infty}(N^{*}U^{\prime\prime})
,
u\mapsto a
, given by

a(x^{\prime\prime},\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}}}e^{-i\langle x^{\prime},\xi\rangle}u(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})\,dx^{\prime}\;,

induces a continuous linear map
I_{\text{\rm c}}^{(s)}(U,L_{0})\to S^{\bar{m}}(N^{*}U^{\prime\prime})
.

In what follows, it is convenient to use

a\,|d\xi|\in S^{\bar{m}}(N^{*}U^{\prime\prime};\Omega N^{*}U^{\prime\prime})\equiv S^{\bar{m}}(N^{*}L_{0};\Omega N^{*}L_{0})\;.

Assume
M
is compact. Take a finite cover of
L
by relatively compact charts
(U_{j},x_{j})
of
M
adapted to
L
, and write
L_{j}=L\cap U_{j}
. Let
\{h,f_{j}\}
be a
C^{\infty}
partition of unity of
M
subordinated to the open covering
\{M\setminus L,U_{j}\}
. Then
I(M,L)
consists of the distributions
u\in C^{-\infty}(M)
such that
hu\in C^{\infty}(M\setminus L)
and
f_{j}u\in I_{\text{\rm c}}(U_{j},L_{j})
for all
j
. Every
f_{j}u
is given by some
a_{j}\in S^{\infty}(N^{*}L_{j};\Omega N^{*}L_{j})
as above. For

(2.2.6)

\bar{m}=m+n/4-n^{\prime}/2\;,

the condition
a_{j}\in S^{\bar{m}}(N^{*}L_{j};\Omega N^{*}L_{j})
describes the elements
u
of a
C^{\infty}(M)
-submodule
I^{m}(M,L)\subset I(M,L)
, which is independent of the choices involved
[
MU08
, Proposition 9.33]
(see also
[
Mel96
, Definition 6.2.19]
and
[
Sim90
, Definition 4.3.9]
). Moreover, applying the versions of semi-norms (
2.1.2
) on
C^{\infty}(M\setminus L)
to
hu
and the versions of semi-norms (
2.1.25
) on
S^{\bar{m}}(N^{*}L_{j};\Omega N^{*}L_{j})
to every
a_{j}
, we get semi-norms on
I^{m}(M,L)
, which becomes a Fréchet space
[
Mel96
, Sections 6.2 and 6.10]
.

The version of (
2.1.26
) for the spaces
S^{\bar{m}}(N^{*}L_{j};\Omega N^{*}L_{j})
gives continuous inclusions

(2.2.7)

I^{m}(M,L)\subset I^{m^{\prime}}(M,L)\quad(m<m^{\prime})\;.

The element
\sigma_{m}(u)\in S^{(\bar{m})}(N^{*}L;\Omega N^{*}L)
represented by
\sum_{j}a_{j}\in S^{\bar{m}}(N^{*}L;\Omega N^{*}L)
is called the
principal symbol
of
u
. This defines the exact sequence

0\to I^{m-1}(M,L)\hookrightarrow I^{m}(M,L)\xrightarrow{\sigma_{m}}S^{(\bar{m})}(N^{*}L;\Omega N^{*}L)\to 0\;.

We also get continuous inclusions

(2.2.8)

I^{(-m-n/4+\epsilon)}(M,L)\subset I^{m}(M,L)\subset I^{(-m-n/4-\epsilon)}(M,L)\;,

for all
m\in\mathbb{R}
and
\epsilon>0
(cf.
[
Mel96
, Eq. (6.2.5)]
,
[
MU08
, Eq. (9.35)]
). So

I(M,L)=\bigcup_{m}I^{m}(M,L)\;,\quad I^{(\infty)}(M,L)=I^{-\infty}(M,L):=\bigcap_{m}I^{m}(M,L)\;.

The spaces
I^{m}(M,L)
form what is called the
symbol-order filtration
of
I(M,L)
, or the
symbol-order inductive spectrum
defining
I(M,L)
.

2.2.4.
I(M,L)
for non-compact
M

If
M
is not assumed to be compact, the spaces and properties of
Sections

2.2.2
and
2.2.3
can be extended as follows
[
ÁLKL23
, Sections 4.2.2 and 4.3.3]
.

We can similarly define the LCHS
I^{(s)}_{{\cdot}/{\text{\rm c}}}(M,L)
by using
C^{-\infty}_{{\cdot}/{\text{\rm c}}}(M)
and
H^{s}_{\text{\rm loc/c}}(M)
. Every
I^{(s)}(M,L)
is a Fréchet space. We can describe
I^{(s)}_{\text{\rm c}}(M,L)=\bigcup_{K}I^{(s)}_{K}(M,L)
like in (
2.1.3
), which is a strict LF-space, and therefore
I_{\text{\rm c}}(M,L)=\bigcup_{s}I_{\text{\rm c}}^{(s)}(M,L)
is an LF-space; moreover
I_{\text{\rm c}}(M,L)=\bigcup_{K}I_{K}(M,L)
. We also have the LCHS
I^{(\infty)}_{\text{\rm c}}(M,L)=\bigcap_{s}I_{\text{\rm c}}^{(s)}(M,L)
. All of these spaces are modules over
C^{\infty}(M)
;
I_{\text{\rm c}}(M,L)
is a filtered module and
I^{(\infty)}_{\text{\rm c}}(M,L)
a submodule. The extension by zero defines a continuous inclusion
I_{\text{\rm c}}(U,L\cap U)\subset I_{\text{\rm c}}(M,L)
for any open
U\subset M
. We also define the space
I^{(\infty)}(M,L)
like in the compact case, as well as the space
\bigcup_{s}I^{(s)}(M,L)
, which consists of the conormal distributions with a Sobolev order. But now let (cf.
[
Hör85
, Definition 18.2.6]
)

(2.2.9)

I(M,L)=\{\,u\in C^{-\infty}(M)\mid C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(M)\,u\subset I_{\text{\rm c}}(M,L)\,\}\;,

which is a LCS with the topology like in (
2.1.1
). We have
I(M,L)=\bigcup_{s}I^{(s)}(M,L)
if and only if
L
is compact; thus the spaces
I^{(s)}(M,L)
form a filtration of
I(M,L)
just when
L
is compact. There is an extension of (
2.2.5
) for non-compact
M
, taking arbitrary/compact support; in particular,
I_{{\cdot}/{\text{\rm c}}}(M,L)
is Hausdorff. The density of the smooth functions with arbitrary/compact support is also true.

The definition of
I^{m}(M,L)
can be immediately extended assuming
\{U_{j}\}
is locally finite. We can similarly define
I^{m}_{K}(M,L)
for all compact
K\subset M
, and then define
I^{m}_{\text{\rm c}}(M,L)
like in (
2.1.3
). The space of conormal distributions with a symbol order is
\bigcup_{m}I^{m}(M,L)
, and let
I^{-\infty}_{{\cdot}/{\text{\rm c}}}(M,L)=\bigcap_{m}I^{m}_{{\cdot}/{\text{\rm c}}}(M,L)
. There are extensions of (
2.2.7
) and (
2.2.8
). So
\bigcup_{m}I^{m}(M,L)=\bigcup_{s}I^{(s)}(M,L)
,
I_{\text{\rm c}}(M,L)=\bigcup_{m}I^{m}_{\text{\rm c}}(M,L)
and
I^{(\infty)}_{{\cdot}/{\text{\rm c}}}(M,L)=I^{-\infty}_{{\cdot}/{\text{\rm c}}}(M,L)
.
\bigcup_{m}I^{m}(M,L)
and
I_{{\cdot}/{\text{\rm c}}}(M,L)
are acyclic Montel spaces, and
I(M,L)
is a Montel space.

If
M
is the domain of a given smooth submersion, the LCHS
I_{\text{\rm cv}}(M;E)
can be defined like
C^{-\infty}_{\text{\rm cv}}(M;E)
, using
I_{\text{\rm c}}(M;E)
instead of
C^{-\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(M;E)
.

2.2.5.
Pseudodifferential operators vs conormal distributions

Using the diagonal
\Delta\subset M^{2}
, the Schwartz kernel isomorphism (
2.1.9
) restricts to linear isomorphisms

\Psi^{m}(M;E,F)\xrightarrow{\cong}I^{m}(M^{2},\Delta;F\boxtimes(E^{*}\otimes\Omega M))\;,\quad A\mapsto K_{A}\;,

and a similar one for the whole of
\Psi(M;E,F)
. Via them,
\Psi^{m}(M;E,F)
and
\Psi(M;E,F)
become LCHSs satisfying the properties of the corresponding spaces of conormal distributional sections. In this case, we have
\bar{m}=m
in (
2.2.6
) and
\sigma_{m}(A)\equiv\sigma_{m}(K_{A})
for any
A\in\Psi^{m}(M;E,F)

[
Hör65
,
KN65
]
,
[
Hör85
, Chapter XVIII]
,
[
Sim90
, Chapter 6]
.

2.2.6.
Dirac sections at submanifolds

We have
\Omega NL\otimes\Omega L\equiv\Omega_{L}M
. The transpose of the restriction map
C^{\infty}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\cdot}}(M;E\otimes\Omega M)\to C^{\infty}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\cdot}}(L;E\otimes\Omega_{L}M)
is a continuous inclusion

(2.2.10)

\displaystyle C^{-\infty}_{{\cdot}/{\text{\rm c}}}(L;E\otimes\Omega^{-1}NL)\subset C^{-\infty}_{{\cdot}/{\text{\rm c}}}(M;E)\;,

\displaystyle u\mapsto\delta_{L}^{u}\;,\quad\langle\delta_{L}^{u},v\rangle=\langle u,v|_{L}\rangle\;,\quad v\in C^{\infty}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\cdot}}(M;E^{*}\otimes\Omega)\;.

By restriction of (
2.2.10
), we get a continuous inclusion
[
GS77
, p. 310]
,

(2.2.11)

C^{\infty}_{{\cdot}/{\text{\rm c}}}(L;E\otimes\Omega^{-1}NL)\subset C^{-\infty}_{{\cdot}/{\text{\rm c}}}(M;E)\;;

in this case, we can write
\langle\delta_{L}^{u},v\rangle=\int_{L}u\,v|_{L}
. This is the subspace of
\delta
-sections
or
Dirac sections
at
L
. Actually, the inclusion (
2.2.11
) induces a continuous injection
[
ÁLKL23
, Corollary 4.9]

(2.2.12)

C^{\infty}_{{\cdot}/{\text{\rm c}}}(L;E\otimes\Omega^{-1}NL)\subset H^{s}_{\text{\rm loc/c}}(M;E)\quad(s<-n^{\prime}/2)\;,

with

C^{\infty}_{{\cdot}/{\text{\rm c}}}(L;E\otimes\Omega^{-1}NL)\cap H^{-n^{\prime}/2}_{\text{\rm loc/c}}(M;E)=0\;.

For instance, for any
p\in M
and
u\in E_{p}\otimes\Omega_{p}^{-1}M
, we get
\delta_{p}^{u}\in H^{s}_{\text{\rm c}}(M;E)
if
s<-n/2
, with
\langle\delta_{p}^{u},v\rangle=u\cdot v(p)
for
v\in C^{\infty}(M;E^{*}\otimes\Omega)
, obtaining a continuous map

(2.2.13)

M\times C^{\infty}(M;E\otimes\Omega^{-1})\to H^{s}_{\text{\rm c}}(M;E)\;,\quad(p,u)\mapsto\delta_{p}^{u(p)}\;.

As a particular case, the Dirac mass at any
p\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is
\delta_{p}=\delta_{p}^{1\otimes|dx|^{-1}}\in H^{s}_{\text{\rm c}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

The Schwartz kernel of any
A\in L(C^{-\infty}(M;E),C^{\infty}(M;F))
has the following description: for all
q\in M
and
u\in E_{q}\otimes\Omega_{q}^{-1}
,

(2.2.14)

K_{A}(\cdot,q)(u)=A\delta_{q}^{u}\;.

2.2.7.
Differential operators on conormal distributional sections

Any
A\in\operatorname{Diff}^{k}(M;E)
induces continuous linear maps
[
Mel96
, Lemma 6.1.1]

(2.2.15)

A:I^{(s)}_{{\cdot}/{\text{\rm c}}}(M,L;E)\to I^{(s-k)}_{{\cdot}/{\text{\rm c}}}(M,L;E)\;,

which induce a continuous endomorphism
A
of
I_{{\cdot}/{\text{\rm c}}}(M,L;E)
. If
A\in\operatorname{Diff}(M,L;E)
, then it clearly induces a continuous endomorphism
A
of every
I^{(s)}_{{\cdot}/{\text{\rm c}}}(M,L;E)
.

By (
2.2.10
), for
A\in\operatorname{Diff}(M,L;E)
and
u\in C^{\infty}_{{\cdot}/{\text{\rm c}}}(L;E\otimes\Omega^{-1}NL)
, we have
[
ÁLKL23
, Eq. (4.17)]

(2.2.16)

A\delta_{L}^{u}=\delta_{L}^{A^{\prime}u}\;,\quad A^{\prime}=((A^{\text{\rm t}})|_{L})^{\text{\rm t}}\in\operatorname{Diff}(L;E\otimes\Omega^{-1}NL)\;,

where
A^{\text{\rm t}}\in\operatorname{Diff}(M,L;E^{*}\otimes\Omega)
and
(A^{\text{\rm t}})|_{L}\in\operatorname{Diff}(L,E^{*}\otimes\Omega_{L}M)
using the vector bundle versions of (
2.2.1
) and (
2.2.2
). By (
2.2.16
),
\operatorname{Diff}(M,L;E)
preserves the subspace of Dirac sections given by (
2.2.11
). Thus (
2.2.12
) induces a continuous inclusion

(2.2.17)

C^{\infty}_{{\cdot}/{\text{\rm c}}}(L;E\otimes\Omega^{-1}NL)\subset I^{(s)}_{{\cdot}/{\text{\rm c}}}(M,L;E)\quad(s<-n^{\prime}/2)\;.

2.2.8.
Pull-back of conormal distributions

If a smooth map
\phi:M^{\prime}\to M
is transverse to a regular submanifold
L\subset M
, which is a closed subset, then
L^{\prime}:=\phi^{-1}(L)\subset M^{\prime}
is a regular submanifold, which is a closed subset. The trivial-line-bundle version of (
2.1.13
) has continuous extensions

(2.2.18)

\phi^{*}:I^{m}(M,L)\to I^{m+k/4}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime})\quad(m\in\mathbb{R})\;,

where
k=\dim M-\dim M^{\prime}

[
Sim90
, Theorem 5.3.8]
,
[
Mel96
, Proposition 6.6.1]
. Taking inductive limits and using (
2.2.8
), we get a continuous linear map

(2.2.19)

\phi^{*}:I(M,L)\to I(M^{\prime},L^{\prime})\;.

If
\phi
is a submersion, this is a restriction of (
2.1.18
). In the case of a vector bundle
E
over
M
, we get

(2.2.20)

\phi^{*}:I(M,L;E)\to I(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\phi^{*}E)\;,

given by the
C^{\infty}(M)
-tensor product of the map (
2.2.19
) and the identity map on
C^{\infty}(M;E)
, using the versions of (
2.1.6
) and (
2.1.20
) for spaces of conormal distributions (see
Section

2.1.6
).

2.2.9.
Push-forward of conormal distributions

Let
\phi:M^{\prime}\to M
be a smooth submersion, and let
L\subset M
and
L^{\prime}\subset M^{\prime}
be regular submanifolds, which are closed subsets, such that
\phi(L^{\prime})\subset L
and the restriction
\phi:L^{\prime}\to L
is also a smooth submersion. Then (
2.1.14
) and (
2.1.15
) have continuous extensions

(2.2.21)

\phi_{*}:I^{m}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\text{\rm cv}}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Omega_{\text{\rm fiber}})\to I^{m+l/2-k/4}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\cdot}}(M,L)\quad(m\in\mathbb{R})\;,

where
k=\dim M^{\prime}-\dim M
and
l=\dim L^{\prime}-\dim L

[
Sim90
, Theorem 5.3.6]
,
[
Mel96
, Proposition 6.7.2]
. Taking inductive limits, we get a continuous linear map

(2.2.22)

\phi_{*}:I_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\text{\rm cv}}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Omega_{\text{\rm fiber}})\to I_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\cdot}}(M,L)\;,

which is a restriction of (
2.1.17
). In the case of a vector bundle
E
over
M
, we get

(2.2.23)

\phi_{*}:I_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\text{\rm cv}}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\phi^{*}E\otimes\Omega_{\text{\rm fiber}})\to I_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\cdot}}(M,L;E)\;,

is given by the
C^{\infty}(M)
-tensor product of (
2.2.22
) and the identity map on
C^{\infty}(M;E)
, using the obvious versions of (
2.1.6
) and (
2.1.21
) for spaces of conormal distributions (see
Section

2.1.6
). The map (
2.8.24
) is also a restriction of (
2.8.13
).

2.3.
Dual-conormal distributions

The dual space
I(M,L;E)^{\prime}

[
Mel96
, Chapter 6]
also plays an important role in our work. Again, the case of
I(M,L)^{\prime}
is considered first; its extension for any vector bundle
E
can be made like in
Section

2.2
, and will be considered without further comment.

2.3.1.
Dual-conormal distributions when
M
is compact

Consider the notation of
Sections

2.2.2
and
2.2.3
, where
M
is assumed to be compact. The space of
dual-conormal distributions
of
M
at
L
(or of
(M,L)
) is
[
Mel96
, Chapter 6]

(2.3.1)

I^{\prime}(M,L)=I(M,L;\Omega)^{\prime}\;.

Let also

(2.3.2)

I^{\prime\,(s)}(M,L)=I^{(-s)}(M,L;\Omega)^{\prime}\;,\quad I^{\prime\,m}(M,L)=I^{-m}(M,L;\Omega)^{\prime}\;.

I^{\prime}(M,L)
is a complete Montel space, and every
I^{\prime\,(s)}(M,L)
is bornological and barreled
[
ÁLKL23
, Corollaries 5.1 and 5.2]
.

Transposing the versions of (
2.2.4
) and (
2.2.7
) with
\Omega M
, we get continuous linear restriction maps, for
s^{\prime}<s
and
m<m^{\prime}
,

(2.3.3)

I^{\prime\,(s^{\prime})}(M,L)\leftarrow I^{\prime\,(s)}(M,L)\;,\quad I^{\prime\,m^{\prime}}(M,L)\leftarrow I^{\prime\,m}(M,L)\;.

These maps form projective spectra (the Sobolev-order and symbol-order spectra), giving rise to
\varprojlim I^{\prime\,(s)}(M,L)
as
s\uparrow+\infty
and
\varprojlim I^{\prime\,m}(M,L)
as
m\downarrow-\infty
. Similarly, from (
2.2.5
), we get continuous inclusions,

(2.3.4)

C^{-\infty}(M)\supset I^{\prime}(M,L)\supset C^{\infty}(M)\;,

and (
2.2.8
) gives rise to continuous linear restriction maps

(2.3.5)

I^{\prime\,(-m+n/4-\epsilon)}(M,L)\leftarrow I^{\prime\,m}(M,L)\leftarrow I^{\prime\,(-m+n/4+\epsilon)}(M,L)\;,

for all
m\in\mathbb{R}
and
\epsilon>0
. We also have
[
ÁLKL23
, Corollary 5.3]

(2.3.6)

I^{\prime}(M,L)\equiv\varprojlim I^{\prime\,(s)}(M,L)\equiv\varprojlim I^{\prime\,m}(M,L)\;,

as
s\uparrow+\infty
and
m\downarrow-\infty
, where the last equality follows from (
2.3.5
).

The left-hand-side maps of (
2.3.3
) have dense images, which follows from consequences of the Hahn-Banach theorem
[
NB11
, Theorems 7.7.5 and 7.7.7 (c)]
, using that their transposes are the analogs of the inclusions (
2.2.4
) with
\Omega M
by the reflexivity of the spaces
I^{(s)}(M,L;\Omega)
(
Section

2.2.2
). Similarly, the inclusions (
2.3.4
) are dense.

2.3.2.
Dual-conormal distributions when
M
is non-compact

If
M
is not supposed to be compact, the above concepts and properties can be extended as follows. We can similarly define the space
I^{\prime}_{K}(M,L)
of dual-conormal distributions supported in any compact
K\subset M
. Then define the LCHSs,
I^{\prime}_{\text{\rm c}}(M,L)=\bigcup_{K}I^{\prime}_{K}(M,L)
like in (
2.1.3
), and
I^{\prime}(M,L)
like in (
2.2.9
) using
I^{\prime}_{\text{\rm c}}(M,L)
instead of
I_{\text{\rm c}}(M,L)
. These spaces satisfy a version of (
2.3.1
), interchanging arbitrary/compact support like in (
2.1.5
).
I^{\prime}(M,L)
is a complete Montel space, and (
2.3.4
) is also true. Similarly, we can define the spaces
I^{\prime\,(s)}_{{\cdot}/{\text{\rm c}}}(M,L)
and
I^{\prime\,m}_{{\cdot}/{\text{\rm c}}}(M,L)
, which satisfy a version of (
2.3.2
) interchanging the support condition. Moreover (
2.3.5
) and (
2.3.6
) have obvious extensions.

If
M
is the domain of a given smooth submersion, the LCHS
I^{\prime}_{\text{\rm cv}}(M;E)
can be defined like
C^{-\infty}_{\text{\rm cv}}(M;E)
, using
I^{\prime}_{\text{\rm c}}(M;E)
instead of
C^{-\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(M;E)
.

2.3.3.
Conormal distributions vs dual-conormal distributions

Assume
M
is compact. Then
[
ÁLKL23
, Theorem 8.11]

I(M,L)\cap I^{\prime}(M,L)=C^{\infty}(M)\;.

2.3.4.
Differential operators on dual-conormal distributional sections

For any
A\in\operatorname{Diff}(M;E)
, the transpose of
A^{\text{\rm t}}
on
I_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\cdot}}(M,L;E^{*}\otimes\Omega)
(
Section

2.2.7
) is a continuous endomorphism
A
of
I^{\prime}_{{\cdot}/{\text{\rm c}}}(M,L;E)
, which is a continuous extension of
A
on
C^{\infty}(M;E)
, and a restriction of
A
on
C^{-\infty}(M;E)
(
Section

2.1.7
). By (
2.2.15
), if
A\in\operatorname{Diff}^{m}(M;E)
, we get induced continuous linear maps

(2.3.7)

A:I^{\prime\,(s)}_{{\cdot}/{\text{\rm c}}}(M,L;E)\to I^{\prime\,(s-m)}_{{\cdot}/{\text{\rm c}}}(M,L;E)\;,

If
A\in\operatorname{Diff}(M,L;E)
, the transpose of
A^{\text{\rm t}}
of
I^{(-s)}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\cdot}}(M,L;E^{*}\otimes\Omega)
is a continuous endomorphism
A
of
I^{\prime\,(s)}_{{\cdot}/{\text{\rm c}}}(M,L;E)
.

2.3.5.
Pull-back of dual-conormal distributions

With the notation and conditions of
Section

2.2.9
, transposing the compactly supported cases of (
2.2.21
) and (
2.2.22
) with
\Omega M
, we get continuous linear maps

\displaystyle\phi^{*}:I^{\prime\,m}(M,L)\to I^{\prime\,m+l/2-k/4}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime})\quad(m\in\mathbb{R})\;,

(2.3.8)

\displaystyle\phi^{*}:I^{\prime}(M,L)\to I^{\prime}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime})\;.

In the case of a vector bundle
E
over
M
, like in (
2.2.20
), we get

(2.3.9)

\phi^{*}:I^{\prime}(M,L;E)\to I^{\prime}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\phi^{*}E)\;.

The map (
2.3.9
) is an extension of (
2.1.13
) and a restriction of (
2.1.18
).

2.3.6.
Push-forward of dual-conormal distributions

With the notation and conditions of
Section

2.2.8
, suppose
\phi
is a submersion. Transposing the versions of (
2.2.18
) and (
2.2.19
) with
\Omega M
, and using an analog of (
2.1.16
), we get continuous linear maps,

\displaystyle\phi_{*}:I^{\prime\,m}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\text{\rm cv}}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime}\otimes\Omega_{\text{\rm fiber}})\to I^{\prime\,m-k/4}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\cdot}}(M,L)\quad(m\in\mathbb{R})\;,

(2.3.10)

\displaystyle\phi_{*}:I^{\prime}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\text{\rm cv}}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Omega_{\text{\rm fiber}})\to I^{\prime}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\cdot}}(M,L)\;.

In the case of a vector bundle
E
over
M
, like in (
2.2.23
), we get

(2.3.11)

\phi_{*}:I^{\prime}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\text{\rm cv}}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\phi^{*}E\otimes\Omega_{\text{\rm fiber}})\to I^{\prime}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\cdot}}(M,L;E)\;.

The map (
2.3.10
) is an extension of (
2.1.14
) and a restriction of (
2.1.17
).

2.4.
Bounded geometry

2.4.1.
Basic notation

The concepts recalled here become relevant when
M
is not compact. Equip
M
with a Riemannian metric
g
, and let
\nabla
denote its Levi-Civita connection,
R
its curvature tensor, and
\operatorname{inj}_{M}\geq 0
its injectivity radius (the infimum of the injectivity radius at all points). If
M
is connected, we have an induced distance function
d
. If
M
is not connected, we can also define
d
taking
d(p,q)=\infty
if
p
and
q
belong to different connected components. Observe that
M
is complete if
\operatorname{inj}_{M}>0
. For
r>0
,
p\in M
and
S\subset M
, let
B(p,r)
and
\overline{B}(p,r)
denote the open and closed
r
-balls centered at
p
, and
\operatorname{Pen}(S,r)
and
\overline{\operatorname{Pen}}(S,r)
denote the open and closed
r
-penumbras of
S
(defined by the conditions
d(\cdot,S)<r
and
d(\cdot,S)\leq r
, respectively). We may add the subscript “
M
” to this notation if needed, or a subscript “
a
” if we are referring to a family of Riemannian manifolds
M_{a}
.

2.4.2.
Manifolds and vector bundles of bounded geometry

Recall that
M
is said to be of
bounded geometry
if
\operatorname{inj}_{M}>0
and
\sup|\nabla^{m}R|<\infty
for every
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. This concept has the following chart description.

Theorem 2.4.1

(Eichhorn
[
Eic91
]
; see also
[
Roe88
,
Sch96
,
Sch01
]
)
.

M
is of bounded geometry if and only if, for some open ball
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
centered at
0
, there are normal coordinates
y_{p}:V_{p}\to B
at every
p\in M
such that the corresponding Christoffel symbols
\Gamma^{i}_{jk}
, as a family of functions on
B
parametrized by
i
,
j
,
k
and
p
, lie in a bounded set of the Fréchet space
C^{\infty}(B)
. This equivalence holds as well replacing the Cristoffel symbols with the metric coefficients
g_{ij}
.

Remark 2.4.2
.

Any non-connected Riemannian manifold of bounded geometry can be considered as a family of Riemannian manifolds (the connected components), which are of
equi-bounded geometry
in the sense that they satisfy the condition of bounded geometry with the same bounds.

Example 2.4.3
.

Typical examples of manifolds of bounded geometry are Lie groups with left invariant metrics, covering spaces of closed Riemannian manifolds and leaves of foliations on closed manifolds.

From now on in this section, assume
M
is of bounded geometry and consider the charts
y_{p}:V_{p}\to B
given by
2.4.1
. The radius of
B
will be denoted by
r_{0}
.

Proposition 2.4.4

(Schick
[
Sch96
, Theorem A.22]
,
[
Sch01
, Proposition 3.3]
)
.

For every multi-index
\alpha
, the function
|\partial_{I}(y_{q}y_{p}^{-1})|
is bounded on
y_{p}(V_{p}\cap V_{q})
, uniformly on
p,q\in M
.

Proposition 2.4.5

(Shubin
[
Shu92
, Appendix A1.1, Lemma 1.2]
)
.

For any
0<2r\leq r_{0}
, there is a subset
\{p_{k}\}\subset M
and some
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that the balls
B(p_{k},r)
cover
M
, and every intersection of
N+1
sets
B(p_{k},2r)
is empty.

A vector bundle
E
of rank
l
over
M
is said to be of
bounded geometry
when it is equipped with a family of local trivializations over the charts
(V_{p},y_{p})
, for small enough
r_{0}
, with corresponding defining cocycle
a_{pq}:V_{p}\cap V_{q}\to\operatorname{GL}(\mathbb{C},l)\subset\mathbb{C}^{l\times l}
, such that, for all multi-index
\alpha
, the function
|\partial_{I}(a_{pq}y_{p}^{-1})|
is bounded on
y_{p}(V_{p}\cap V_{q})
, uniformly on
p,q\in M
. When referring to local trivializations of a vector bundle of bounded geometry, we always mean that they satisfy this condition. If the corresponding defining cocycle is valued in
\operatorname{U}(l)
, then
E
is said to be of
bounded geometry
as a Hermitian vector bundle. Euclidean vector bundles of bounded geometry are similarly defined.

Example 2.4.6
.

The vector bundle
E
associated to the principal
\operatorname{O}(n)
-bundle
P
of orthonormal frames of
M
and any unitary representation of
\operatorname{O}(n)
is of bounded geometry in a canonical way. In particular, this applies to
T_{\mathbb{C}}M
and
\Lambda M
. If the representation is unitary, then bounded geometry holds as a Hermitian vector bundle. The same is true if we use any reduction
Q
of
P
with structural group
H\subset\operatorname{O}(n)
and any unitary representation of
H
.

Example 2.4.7
.

Bounded geometry is preserved by operations of vector bundles induced by operations of vector spaces, like dual vector bundles, direct sums, tensor products, exterior products, densities, etc.

Example 2.4.8
.

Let
E
be a vector bundle
E
over a closed Riemannian manifold
M
, and let
\widetilde{M}
be a covering of
M
. Then the lift
\widetilde{E}
of
E
to
\widetilde{M}
is of bounded geometry in a canonical way.

2.4.3.
Uniform spaces

For every
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, a function
u\in C^{m}(M)
is said to be
C^{m}
-uniformy bounded
if there is some
C_{m}\geq 0
with
|\nabla^{m^{\prime}}u|\leq C_{m}
on
M
for all
m^{\prime}\leq m
. These functions form the
uniform
C^{m}
space

C_{\text{\rm ub}}^{m}(M)
, which is a Banach space with the norm
\|{\cdot}\|_{C^{m}_{\text{\rm ub}}}
defined by the best constant
C_{m}
. As usual, we write
C_{\text{\rm ub}}(M)=C_{\text{\rm ub}}^{0}(M)=C(M)\cap L^{\infty}(M)
. Equivalently, we may take the norm
\|{\cdot}\|^{\prime}_{C^{m}_{\text{\rm ub}}}
defined by the best constant
C^{\prime}_{m}\geq 0
such that
|\partial_{I}(uy_{p}^{-1})|\leq C^{\prime}_{m}
on
B
for all
p\in M
and
|I|\leq m
; in fact, it is enough to consider any subset of points
p
so that
\{V_{p}\}
covers
M

[
Sch96
, Theorem A.22]
,
[
Sch01
, Proposition 3.3]
. The
uniform
C^{\infty}
space
is the Fréchet space
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M)=\bigcap_{m}C_{\text{\rm ub}}^{m}(M)
, with the semi-norms
\|{\cdot}\|_{C^{m}_{\text{\rm ub}}}
or
\|{\cdot}\|^{\prime}_{C^{m}_{\text{\rm ub}}}
. It consists of the functions
u\in C^{\infty}(M)
such that all functions
uy_{p}^{-1}
lie in a bounded set of
C^{\infty}(B)
.

The same definitions apply to functions with values in
\mathbb{C}^{l}
. Moreover the definition of uniform spaces with covariant derivative can be also considered for non-complete Riemannian manifolds.

Proposition 2.4.9

(Shubin
[
Shu92
, Appendix A1.1, Lemma 1.3]
; see also
[
Sch01
, Proposition 3.2]
)
.

Given
r
,
\{p_{k}\}
and
N
like in
2.4.5
there is a partition of unity
\{f_{k}\}
subordinated to the open covering
\{B(p_{k},r)\}
, which is bounded in the Fréchet space
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M)
.

For a Hermitian vector bundle
E
of bounded geometry over
M
, the
uniform
C^{m}
space

C_{\text{\rm ub}}^{m}(M;E)
can be defined by introducing
\|{\cdot}\|^{\prime}_{C^{m}_{\text{\rm ub}}}
like the case of functions, using local trivializations of
E
to consider every
uy_{p}^{-1}
in
C^{m}(B,\mathbb{C}^{l})
for all
u\in C^{m}(M;E)
. Then, as above, we get the
uniform
C^{\infty}
space

C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M;E)
, which consists of the sections
u\in C^{\infty}(M;E)
such that all functions
uy_{p}^{-1}
define a bounded set of
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(B;\mathbb{C}^{l})
. In particular,
\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M):=C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M;TM)
is a
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M)
-submodule and Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{X}(M)
.

The subset
\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm com}}(M)\subset\mathfrak{X}(M)
of complete vector fields satisfies
\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M)\subset\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm com}}(M)

[
ÁLKL20
, Proposition 3.8]
.

2.4.4.
Differential operators of bounded geometry

Like in
Section

2.1.7
, by using
\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M)
and
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M)
instead of
\mathfrak{X}(M)
and
C^{\infty}(M)
, we get the filtered subalgebra and
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M)
-submodule
\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M)\subset\operatorname{Diff}(M)
of differential operators of
bounded geometry
. Observe that

(2.4.1)

C^{m}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M)=\{\,u\in C^{m}(M)\mid\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm ub}}^{m}(M)\,u\subset L^{\infty}(M)\,\}\;.

The concept of
\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M)
can be extended to vector bundles of bounded geometry
E
and
F
over
M
by taking the
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M)
-tensor product with
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M;F\otimes E^{*})
, obtaining the filtered
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M)
-submodule
\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M;E,F)\subset\operatorname{Diff}(M;E,F)
(or
\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M;E)
if
E=F
). Bounded geometry of differential operators is preserved by compositions and by taking transposes, and by taking formal adjoints in the case of Hermitian vector bundles of bounded geometry; in particular,
\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M;E)
is a filtered subalgebra of
\operatorname{Diff}(M;E)
. Using local trivializations of
E
and
F
over the charts
(V_{p},y_{p})
, we get a local description of any operator in
\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm ub}}^{m}(M;E,F)
by requiring its local coefficients to define a bounded subset of the Fréchet space
C^{\infty}(B,\mathbb{C}^{l^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{l*})
, where
l
and
l^{\prime}
are the ranks of
E
and
F
(
Section

2.1.7
). If
E
is a line bundle of bounded geometry, then
[
ÁLKL23
, Eq. (2.24)]

(2.4.2)

\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm ub}}^{m}(M;E)\equiv\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm ub}}^{m}(M)\;.

Let
P_{\text{\rm ub}}(T^{*}M)\subset P(T^{*}M)
be the graded subalgebra generated by
P_{\text{\rm ub}}^{(0)}(T^{*}M)\equiv C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M)
and
P_{\text{\rm ub}}^{(1)}(T^{*}M)\equiv\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M)
, which is also a
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M)
-submodule. Restricting (
2.1.22
), we get a short exact sequence with
\sigma_{m}:\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm ub}}^{m}(M)\to P_{\text{\rm ub}}^{(m)}(T^{*}M)
. By taking the
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M)
-tensor product with
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M;F\otimes E^{*})
, we get
P_{\text{\rm ub}}^{(m)}(T^{*}M;F\otimes E^{*})
and a short exact sequence with
\sigma_{m}:\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm ub}}^{m}(M;E,F)\to P_{\text{\rm ub}}^{(m)}(T^{*}M;F\otimes E^{*})
.

Using the norms
\|{\cdot}\|^{\prime}_{C^{m}_{\text{\rm ub}}}
, it easily follows that every
A\in\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm ub}}^{m}(M;E,F)
defines bounded operators
A:C^{m+s}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M;E)\to C^{s}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M;F)
(
s\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
), which induce a continuous linear map
A:C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M;E)\to C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M;F)
.

Example 2.4.10
.

In Example
2.4.6
, the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla
induces a connection of bounded geometry on
E
, also denoted by
\nabla
. In particular,
\nabla
itself is of bounded geometry on
TM
, and induces a connection
\nabla
of bounded geometry on
\Lambda M
. This holds as well for the connection on
E
induced by any other Riemannian connection of bounded geometry on
TM
.

Example 2.4.11
.

Bounded geometry of connections is preserved by taking the induced connections in the operations with vector bundles of bounded geometry indicated in Example
2.4.7
.

Suppose
E
and
F
are Hermitian vector bundles of bounded geometry. Then any unitary connection
\nabla
of bounded geometry on
E
can be used to define an equivalent norm
\|{\cdot}\|_{C^{m}_{\text{\rm ub}}}
on every Banach space
C_{\text{\rm ub}}^{m}(M;E)
, like in the case of
C_{\text{\rm ub}}^{m}(M)
.

It is said that
A\in\operatorname{Diff}^{m}(M;E,F)
is
uniformly elliptic
if, given Hermitian metrics of bounded geometry on
E
and
F
, there is some
C\geq 1
such that, for all
p\in M
and
\xi\in T^{*}_{p}M
,

(2.4.3)

C^{-1}|\xi|^{m}\leq|\sigma_{m}(A)(p,\xi)|\leq C|\xi|^{m}\;.

This condition is independent of the choice of the Hermitian metrics of bounded geometry on
E
and
F
. Any
A\in\operatorname{Diff}^{m}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M;E,F)
satisfies the second inequality.

Example 2.4.12
.

In
2.4.8
, for any
A\in\operatorname{Diff}^{m}(M;E)
, its lift
\widetilde{A}\in\operatorname{Diff}^{m}(\widetilde{M};\widetilde{E})
is of bounded geometry in a canonical way. Moreover
\widetilde{A}
is uniformly elliptic if
A
is elliptic.

2.4.5.
Sobolev spaces of manifolds of bounded geometry

For any Hermitian vector bundle
E
of bounded geometry over
M
, any nonnegative symmetric uniformly elliptic
P\in\operatorname{Diff}^{2}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M;E)
can be used to define the Sobolev space
H^{s}(M;E)
(
s\in\mathbb{R}
) with a scalar product
\langle{\cdot},{\cdot}\rangle_{s}
(
Section

2.1.11
). Any choice of
P
defines the same Hilbertian space
H^{s}(M;E)
, which is a
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M)
-module. In particular,
L^{2}(M;E)
is the
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M)
-tensor product of
L^{2}(M;\Omega^{1/2})
and
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M;E\otimes\Omega^{-1/2})
, and
H^{s}(M;E)
is the
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M)
-tensor product of
H^{s}(M)
and
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M;E)
. For instance, we may take
P=\nabla^{*}\nabla
for any unitary connection
\nabla
of bounded geometry on
E
.

Example 2.4.13
.

In
2.4.8
and according to
2.4.12
,
H^{s}(\widetilde{M};\widetilde{E})
can be defined with the lift
\widetilde{P}
of any nonnegative symmetric uniformly elliptic
P\in\operatorname{Diff}^{2}(M;E)
.

For
s\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the Sobolev space
H^{s}(M)
can be also described with the scalar product

\langle u,v\rangle^{\prime}_{s}=\sum_{k}\sum_{|I|\leq s}\int_{B}f_{k}^{2}(x)\cdot\partial_{I}(uy_{p_{k}}^{-1})(x)\cdot\overline{\partial_{I}(vy_{p_{k}}^{-1})(x)}\,dx\;,

using the partition of unity
\{f_{k}\}
given by
2.4.5

[
Sch96
, Theorem A.22]
,
[
Sch01
, Propositions 3.2 and 3.3]
,
[
Shu92
, Appendices A1.2 and A1.3]
. A similar scalar product
\langle{\cdot},{\cdot}\rangle^{\prime}_{s}
can be defined for
H^{s}(M;E)
with the help of local trivializations defining the bounded geometry of
E
. Every
A\in\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm ub}}^{m}(M;E,F)
defines bounded operators
A:H^{m+s}(M;E)\to H^{s}(M;F)
(
s\in\mathbb{R}
), which induce continuous maps
A:H^{\pm\infty}(M;E)\to H^{\pm\infty}(M;F)
. For any almost everywhere positive
h\in C^{\infty}(M)
, we have
hH^{m}(M;E)=H^{m}(M;E)
if and only if
h>0
and
h^{\pm 1}\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M)
.

If
m^{\prime}>m+n/2
, then
H^{m^{\prime}}(M;E)\subset C_{\text{\rm ub}}^{m}(M;E)
, continuously, and therefore
H^{\infty}(M;E)\subset C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M;E)
, continuously
[
Roe88
, Proposition 2.8]
. The Schwartz kernel mapping,
A\mapsto K_{A}
, defines a continuous linear map
[
Roe88
, Proposition 2.9]

(2.4.4)

L(H^{-\infty}(M;E),H^{\infty}(M;F))\to C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M;F\boxtimes(E^{*}\otimes\Omega))\;.

Remark 2.4.14
.

By (
2.2.14
), for any
A\in L(H^{-\infty}(M;E),H^{\infty}(M;F))
and
r>0
,

\operatorname{supp}K_{A}\subset\{\,(p,q)\in M^{2}\mid d(p,q)\leq r\,\}

if and only if
\operatorname{supp}Au\subset\overline{\operatorname{Pen}}(\operatorname{supp}u,r)
for all
u\in H^{-\infty}(M;E)
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be the Fréchet space of rapidly decreasing functions on the real line. If
P\in\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm ub}}^{m}(M;E)
is uniformly elliptic and essentially self-adjoint, then the spectral theorem defines a continuous functional calculus

\mathcal{R}\to L(H^{-\infty}(M;E),H^{\infty}(M;E))\;,\quad\psi\mapsto\psi(P)\;.

Thus, by (
2.4.4
), the linear map

(2.4.5)

\mathcal{R}\to C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M;E\boxtimes(E^{*}\otimes\Omega))\;,\quad\psi\mapsto K_{\psi(P)}\;,

is continuous
[
Roe88
, Proposition 2.10]
.

2.4.6.
Maps of bounded geometry

For
a\in\{1,2\}
, let
M_{a}
be a Riemannian manifold of bounded geometry, of dimension
n_{a}
. Consider a normal chart
y_{a,p}:V_{a,p}\to B_{a}
at every
p\in M_{a}
satisfying the statement of
2.4.1
. Let
r_{a}
denote the radius of
B_{a}
. For
0<r\leq r_{a}
, let
B_{a,r}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n_{a}}
denote the ball centered at the origin with radius
r
. We have
B_{a}(p,r)=y_{a,p}^{-1}(B_{a,r})
.

A smooth map
\phi:M_{1}\to M_{2}
is said to be of
bounded geometry
if, for some
0<r<r_{1}
and all
p\in M_{1}
, we have
\phi(B_{1}(p,r))\subset V_{2,\phi(p)}
, and the compositions
y_{2,\phi(p)}\phi y_{1,p}^{-1}
define a bounded set in the Fréchet space
C^{\infty}(B_{1,r},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})
. This condition is preserved by the composition of maps. The set of smooth maps
M_{1}\to M_{2}
of bounded geometry is denoted by
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M_{1},M_{2})
.

Let
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M_{1},M_{2})
. For every
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\cup\{\infty\}
, using
\|{\cdot}\|^{\prime}_{C^{m}_{\text{\rm ub}}}
in the case where
m<\infty
(
Section

2.4.3
) it follows that
\phi^{*}
induces a continuous linear map
[
ÁLKL20
, Eq. (19)]

(2.4.6)

\phi^{*}:C_{\text{\rm ub}}^{m}(M_{2};\Lambda)\to C_{\text{\rm ub}}^{m}(M_{1};\Lambda)\;.

Recall that
\phi
is called
uniformly metrically proper
if, for any
s\geq 0
, there is some
t_{s}\geq 0
so that, for all
p,q\in M_{1}
,

d_{2}(\phi(p),\phi(q))\leq s\Rightarrow d_{1}(p,q)\leq t_{s}\;.

For all
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\cup\{\infty\}
, if
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M_{1},M_{2})
is uniformly metrically proper, then
\phi^{*}
induces a continuous linear map
[
ÁLKL20
, Eq. (21)]

(2.4.7)

\phi^{*}:H^{m}(M_{2};\Lambda)\to H^{m}(M_{1};\Lambda)\;.

If
\phi\in\operatorname{Diffeo}(M_{1},M_{2})
, and both
\phi
and
\phi^{-1}
are of bounded geometry, then
\phi
is uniformly metrically proper. In this case, (
2.4.7
) can be continuously extended to Sobolev spaces of order
-m
.

The pull-back of a vector bundle of bounded geometry by a map of bounded geometry is of bounded geometry.

Homomorphisms of bounded geometry between vector bundles of bounded geometry have an obvious definition, but we will not use them.

2.4.7.
Smooth families of bounded geometry

Let
T
be a manifold, and let
\operatorname{pr}_{1}:M\times T\to M
denote the first factor projection. A section
u\in C^{\infty}(M\times T;\operatorname{pr}_{1}^{*}E)
is called a
smooth family of smooth sections
of
E
(
parametrized by

T
), and we may use the notation
u=\{\,u_{t}\mid t\in T\,\}
, where
u_{t}=u(\cdot,t)\in C^{\infty}(M;E)
. Its
T
-support
is
\overline{\{\,t\in T\mid u_{t}\neq 0\,\}}
. If the
T
-support is compact, then
u
is said to be
T
-compactly supported
. It is said that
u
is
T
-locally
C^{\infty}
-uniformly bounded
if any
t\in T
is in some chart
(O,z)
of
T
such that the maps
u(y_{p}\times z)^{-1}
define a bounded subset of the Fréchet space
C^{\infty}(B\times z(O),\mathbb{C}^{l})
, using local trivializations of
E
over the normal charts
(V_{p},y_{p})
.

A
smooth family of differential operators
,
A=\{\,A_{t}\mid t\in T\,\}\subset\operatorname{Diff}(M;E,F)
, can be defined by using smooth families of
\mathbb{C}
-valued functions, tangent vector fields and sections of
C^{\infty}(M;F\otimes E^{*})
, like in
Section

2.1.7
. For this
A
, the
T
-support
and the property of being
T
-compactly supported
is defined like in the case of smooth families of sections. If the smooth families of functions, tangent vector fields and sections used to describe
A
are
T
-locally
C^{\infty}
-uniformly bounded, then it is said that
A
is of
T
-local bounded geometry
(cf.
Section

2.4.4
).

A smooth map
\phi:M_{1}\times T\to M_{2}
is called a
smooth family of smooth maps

M_{1}\to M_{2}
(with
parameters
in
T
). It may be denoted by
\phi=\{\,\phi^{t}\mid t\in T\,\}
, where
\phi^{t}=\phi(\cdot,t):M_{1}\to M_{2}
. It is said that
\phi
is of
T
-local bounded geometry
if every
t\in T
is in some chart
(O,z)
of
T
such that, for some
0<r<r_{1}
, we have
\phi(B_{1}(p,r)\times O)\subset V_{2,\phi(p)}
for all
p\in M_{1}
, and the compositions
y_{2,\phi(p)}\phi(y_{1,p}\times z)^{-1}
, for
p\in M_{1}
, define a bounded subset of the Fréchet space
C^{\infty}(B_{1,r}\times z(O),\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})
. The composition of smooth families of maps parametrized by
T
has the obvious sense and preserves the
T
-local bounded geometry condition. In particular, the
\mathbb{R}
-local bounded geometry condition makes sense for a flow
\phi=\{\phi^{t}\}
on
M
. Given
X\in\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm com}}(M)
with flow
\phi
, we have
X\in\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M)
if and only if
\phi
is of
\mathbb{R}
-local bounded geometry
[
ÁLKL20
, Proposition 3.18]
.

2.4.8.
Differential complexes of bounded geometry

With the notation of
Section

2.1.14
, assume that
M
,
E
and
d
are of bounded geometry (
Section

2.4.2
). Then we may also consider the topological complexes
(C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M;E),d)
and
(H^{\pm\infty}(M;E),d)
(
Sections

2.4.3
and
2.4.5
).

(E,d)
is said to be
uniformly elliptic
if
D
(or
\Delta
) is uniformly elliptic (
Section

2.4.4
); this is equivalent to the obvious extension of (
2.4.3
) for (
2.1.36
). In this case, a version of (
2.1.37
) is true for
(H^{\pm\infty}(M;E),d)
, where the reduced cohomology is used instead of the cohomology, and the closures of the images of
d
,
\delta
,
D
and
\Delta
are used instead of their images.

2.5.
Small b-calculus

R. Melrose introduced b-calculus, a way to extend calculus to manifolds with boundary
[
Mel93
,
Mel96
]
. We will only use a part of it, called small b-calculus. For the sake of simplicity, we consider only compact manifolds with boundary, and the concepts and notation given here can be extended to the non-compact case like in
Section

2.1
, using compactly supported versions or local versions; some non-compact manifolds with boundary will be used in the paper. For the same reason, several kinds of section spaces and operators will be only defined in the case of functions or half-b-densities. Their extension to arbitrary vector bundles can be defined with tensor product expressions, like in
Section

2.1
. Most of these extensions will be used without further comments.

2.5.1.
Some notions of b-geometry

Let
M
be a compact (smooth)
n
-manifold with boundary, whose interior is denoted by
\mathring{M}
. There exists a function
x\in C^{\infty}(M)
so that
x\geq 0
,
\partial M=\{x=0\}
and
dx\neq 0
on
\partial M
, which is called a
boundary-defining function
. Let
{}_{+}N\partial M\subset N\partial M
be the inward-pointing subbundle of the normal bundle to the boundary. There is a unique trivialization
\nu\in C^{\infty}(\partial M;{}_{+}N\partial M)
of
{}_{+}N\partial M
so that
dx(\nu)=1
. Take a collar neighborhood
T\equiv[0,\epsilon_{0})_{x}\times\partial M_{\varpi}
of
\partial M
. (In a product expression, every factor projection may be indicated as subscript of the corresponding factor.) Given coordinates
y=(y^{1},\dots,y^{n-1})
on some open
V\subset\partial M
, we get via
\varpi
coordinates
(x,y)=(x,y^{1},\dots,y^{n-1})
, adapted (to
\partial M
), on the open subset
U\equiv[0,\epsilon_{0})\times V\subset M
. There are vector bundles over
M
,
{}^{\text{\rm b}}TM
and
{}^{\text{\rm b}}T^{*}M
, called
b-tangent
and
b-cotangent
bundles, which have the same restrictions to
\mathring{M}
as
TM
and
T^{*}M
, and such that
x\partial_{x},\partial_{y^{1}},\dots,\partial_{y^{n-1}}
and
x^{-1}dx,dy^{1},\dots,dy^{n-1}
extend to local frames around boundary points. This gives rise to versions of induced vector bundles, like
{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{s}M:=\Omega^{s}({}^{\text{\rm b}}TM)
(
s\in\mathbb{R}
) and
{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega M:={}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1}M
. We have

(2.5.1)

C^{\infty}(M;\Omega^{s})\equiv x^{s}C^{\infty}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{s})\;.

Thus the integration operator
\int_{M}
is defined on
xC^{\infty}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega)
, and induces a pairing between
C^{\infty}(M)
and
xC^{\infty}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega)
.

At the points of
\partial M
, the local section
x\partial_{x}
is independent of the choice of adapted local coordinates, spanning a trivial line subbundle
{}^{\text{\rm b}}\!N\partial M\subset{}^{\text{\rm b}}T_{\partial M}M
with
T\partial M={}^{\text{\rm b}}T_{\partial M}M/{}^{\text{\rm b}}\!N\partial M
. So
{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{s}_{\partial M}M\equiv\Omega^{s}\partial M\otimes\Omega^{s}({}^{\text{\rm b}}\!N\partial M)
, and a restriction map
C^{\infty}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{s})\to C^{\infty}(\partial M;\Omega^{s})
is locally given by

u=a(x,y)\,\Big|\frac{dx}{x}dy\Big|^{s}\mapsto u|_{\partial M}=a(0,y)\,|dy|^{s}\;.

A Riemannian structure
g
on
{}^{\text{\rm b}}TM
is called a
b-metric
. Locally,

g=a_{0}\Big(\frac{dx}{x}\Big)^{2}+2\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}a_{0j}\,\frac{dx}{x}\,dy^{j}+\sum_{j,k=1}^{n-1}a_{jk}\,dy^{j}\,dy^{k}\;,

where
a_{0}
,
a_{0j}
and
a_{jk}
are
C^{\infty}
functions, provided that
g
is positive definite. If moreover
a_{0}=1+O(x^{2})
and
a_{0j}=O(x)
as
x\downarrow 0
, then
g
is called
exact
. In this case, the restriction of
g
to
\mathring{T}\equiv(0,\epsilon_{0})\times\partial M
is asymptotically cylindrical, and therefore the restriction of
g
to
\mathring{M}
is a complete Riemannian metric. This restriction is of bounded geometry if it is
cylindrical
around the boundary; i.e., taking
\epsilon_{0}
small enough, we have
g=(\frac{dx}{x})^{2}+h
on
\mathring{T}
for some Riemannian metric
h
on
\partial M
(considering
h\equiv\varpi^{*}h
).

2.5.2.
Supported and extendible smooth functions

Let
\breve{M}
be any closed manifold containing
M
as submanifold of dimension
n
(for instance,
\breve{M}
can be the double of
M
). Let
M^{\prime}=\breve{M}\setminus\mathring{M}
, which is another compact submanifold with boundary of
\breve{M}
, of dimension
n
and with
\partial M^{\prime}=M\cap M^{\prime}=\partial M
.

The concepts, notation and conventions of
Section

2.1.4
have straightforward extensions to manifolds with boundary, like the Fréchet space
C^{\infty}(M)
. Its elements are called
extendible functions
because the continuous linear restriction map

(2.5.2)

R:C^{\infty}(\breve{M})\to C^{\infty}(M)

is surjective; in fact, there is a continuous linear extension map
E:C^{\infty}(M)\to C^{\infty}(\breve{M})

[
See64
]
. Since
C^{\infty}(\breve{M})
and
C^{\infty}(M)
are Fréchet spaces, the map (
2.5.2
) is open by the open mapping theorem, and therefore it is a surjective topological homomorphism. Its null space is
C^{\infty}_{M^{\prime}}(\breve{M})
.

The Fréchet space of
supported functions
is the closed subspace of the smooth functions on
M
that vanish to all orders at the points of
\partial M
,

(2.5.3)

\dot{C}^{\infty}(M)=\bigcap_{m\geq 0}x^{m}C^{\infty}(M)\subset C^{\infty}(M)\;.

The extension by zero realizes
\dot{C}^{\infty}(M)
as the closed subspace of functions on
\breve{M}
supported in
M
,

(2.5.4)

\dot{C}^{\infty}(M)\equiv C^{\infty}_{M}(\breve{M})\subset C^{\infty}(\breve{M})\;.

By (
2.5.3
),

(2.5.5)

x^{m}\dot{C}^{\infty}(M)=\dot{C}^{\infty}(M)\quad(m\in\mathbb{R})\;,

and therefore, by (
2.5.1
),

(2.5.6)

\dot{C}^{\infty}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{s})\equiv\dot{C}^{\infty}(M;\Omega^{s})\quad(s\in\mathbb{R})\;.

We can similarly define Banach spaces
C^{k}(M)
and
\dot{C}^{k}(M)
(
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
) satisfying the analogs of (
2.5.2
)–(
2.5.4
), which in turn yield analogs of the first inclusions of (
2.1.7
), obtaining
C^{\infty}(M)=\bigcap_{k}C^{k}(M)
and
\dot{C}^{\infty}(M)=\bigcap_{k}\dot{C}^{k}(M)
.

2.5.3.
Supported and extendible distributions

The spaces of
supported
and
extendible distributions
on
M
are

\dot{C}^{-\infty}(M)=C^{\infty}(M;\Omega)^{\prime}\;,\quad C^{-\infty}(M)=\dot{C}^{\infty}(M;\Omega)^{\prime}\;.

These are barreled, ultrabornological, webbed, acyclic DF Montel spaces, and therefore complete, boundedly/compactly/sequentially retractive and reflexive
[
ÁLKL23
, Proposition 6.1]
. Transposing the version of (
2.5.2
) with
\Omega M
, we get
[
Mel96
, Proposition 3.2.1]

(2.5.7)

\dot{C}^{-\infty}(M)\equiv C^{-\infty}_{M}(\breve{M})\subset C^{-\infty}(\breve{M})\;.

Similarly, (
2.5.4
) and (
2.5.3
) give rise to continuous linear restriction maps

(2.5.8)

\displaystyle R:C^{-\infty}(\breve{M})\to C^{-\infty}(M)\;,

(2.5.9)

\displaystyle R:\dot{C}^{-\infty}(M)\to C^{-\infty}(M)\;,

which are surjective topological homomorphisms
[
ÁLKL23
, Proposition 6.2]
.
According to (
2.5.7
), the map (
2.5.9
) is a restriction of (
2.5.8
). There are continuous dense inclusions
[
Mel96
, Lemma 3.2.1]

(2.5.10)

C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(\mathring{M})\subset\dot{C}^{\infty}(M)\subset C^{\infty}(M)\subset\dot{C}^{-\infty}(M)\;,

the last one given by the integration pairing between
C^{\infty}(M)
and
C^{\infty}(M;\Omega)
. The restriction of this pairing to
\dot{C}^{\infty}(M;\Omega)
induces a continuous dense inclusion

(2.5.11)

C^{\infty}(M)\subset C^{-\infty}(M)\;.

Moreover (
2.5.9
) is the identity map on
C^{\infty}(M)
.

As before, from (
2.5.5
) and (
2.5.6
), we get

(2.5.12)

\displaystyle x^{m}C^{-\infty}(M)

\displaystyle=C^{-\infty}(M)\quad(m\in\mathbb{R})\;,

(2.5.13)

\displaystyle C^{-\infty}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{s})

\displaystyle\equiv C^{-\infty}(M;\Omega^{s})\quad(s\in\mathbb{R})\;.

The Banach spaces
C^{\prime-k}(M)
and
\dot{C}^{\prime-k}(M)
(
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
) are similarly defined. They satisfy the analogs of (
2.5.7
)–(
2.5.13
), and the analogs of the second inclusions of (
2.1.7
), obtaining
\bigcup_{k}C^{\prime-k}(M)=C^{-\infty}(M)
and
\bigcup_{k}\dot{C}^{\prime-k}(M)=\dot{C}^{-\infty}(M)
.

2.5.4.
Supported and extendible Sobolev spaces

The
supported Sobolev space
of order
s\in\mathbb{R}
is the closed subspace of the elements supported in
M
,

(2.5.14)

\dot{H}^{s}(M)=H^{s}_{M}(\breve{M})\subset H^{s}(\breve{M})\;.

On the other hand, using the map (
2.5.9
), the
extendible Sobolev space
of order
s
is
H^{s}(M)=R(H^{s}(\breve{M}))
with the inductive topology given by the linear map

(2.5.15)

R:H^{s}(\breve{M})\to H^{s}(M)\;.

The null space of (
2.5.15
) is
H^{s}_{M^{\prime}}(\breve{M})
. The analogs of (
2.1.31
)–(
2.1.34
) hold true in this setting using
\dot{C}^{\pm\infty}(M)
and
C^{\pm\infty}(M)
. Furthermore the spaces
\dot{H}^{s}(M)
and
H^{s}(M)
form compact spectra of Hilbertian spaces.

The following properties are satisfied
[
Mel96
, Proposition 3.5.1]
.
C^{\infty}(M)
is dense in
H^{s}(M)
, we have

(2.5.16)

\dot{H}^{s}(M)\equiv H^{-s}(M;\Omega)^{\prime}\;,\quad H^{s}(M)\equiv\dot{H}^{-s}(M;\Omega)^{\prime}\;,

and the map (
2.5.9
) has a continuous restriction

(2.5.17)

R:\dot{H}^{s}(M)\to H^{s}(M)\;,

which is surjective if
s\leq 1/2
, and injective if
s\geq-1/2
. In particular,
\dot{H}^{0}(M)\equiv H^{0}(M)\equiv L^{2}(M)
. The null space of (
2.5.17
) is
\dot{H}^{s}_{\partial M}(M)
.

2.5.5.
The space
\dot{C}^{-\infty}_{\partial M}(M)

The indicated properties of (
2.5.8
) and (
2.5.9
) mean that we have short exact sequences in the category of continuous linear maps between LCSs (see also
[
Mel96
, Proposition 3.3.1]
),

\displaystyle 0\to\dot{C}^{-\infty}(M^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\iota}C^{-\infty}(\breve{M})\xrightarrow{R}C^{-\infty}(M)\to 0\;,

(2.5.18)

\displaystyle 0\to\dot{C}^{-\infty}_{\partial M}(M)\xrightarrow{\iota}\dot{C}^{-\infty}(M)\xrightarrow{R}C^{-\infty}(M)\to 0\;.

From (
2.5.7
), we get

(2.5.19)

\dot{C}^{-\infty}_{\partial M}(M)\equiv C^{-\infty}_{\partial M}(\breve{M})\subset C^{-\infty}(\breve{M})\;.

The analogs of the second inclusion of (
2.1.7
), (
2.1.31
) and (
2.1.33
) hold true for the spaces
\dot{C}^{\prime\,-k}_{\partial M}(M)
and
\dot{H}^{s}_{\partial M}(M)
. Thus the spaces
\dot{C}^{\prime\,-k}_{\partial M}(M)
and
\dot{H}^{s}_{\partial M}(M)
form spectra with the same union; the spectrum of spaces
\dot{H}^{s}_{\partial M}(M)
is compact.

The following properties hold for
\dot{C}^{-\infty}_{\partial M}(M)

[
ÁLKL23
, Corollary 6.4 and 6.5]
: it is a limit subspace of the LF-space
\dot{C}^{-\infty}(M)
; and it is barreled, ultrabornological, webbed acyclic DF Montel space, and therefore complete, reflexive and boundedly/compactly/sequentially retractive. A description of
\dot{C}^{-\infty}_{\partial M}(M)
will be indicated in
2.6.1
.

2.5.6.
Differential operators acting on
C^{-\infty}(M)
and
\dot{C}^{-\infty}(M)

The notions of
Section

2.1.7
also have straightforward extensions to manifolds with boundary. The action of any
A\in\operatorname{Diff}(M)
on
C^{\infty}(M)
preserves
\dot{C}^{\infty}(M)
, giving rise to extended continuous actions of
A
on
C^{-\infty}(M)
and
\dot{C}^{-\infty}(M)
. They fit into commutative diagrams

(2.5.20)

\begin{CD}\dot{C}^{-\infty}(M)@>{A}>{}>\dot{C}^{-\infty}(M)\\
@V{R}V{}V@V{}V{R}V\\
C^{-\infty}(M)@>{A}>{}>C^{-\infty}(M)\end{CD}\qquad\begin{CD}C^{-\infty}(M)@>{A}>{}>C^{-\infty}(M)\\
@A{\iota}A{}A@A{}A{\iota}A\\
C^{\infty}(M)@>{A}>{}>C^{\infty}(M)\;.\hskip-5.69046pt\end{CD}

However the analogous diagram

(2.5.21)

\begin{CD}\dot{C}^{-\infty}(M)@>{A}>{}>\dot{C}^{-\infty}(M)\\
@A{\iota}A{}A@A{}A{\iota}A\\
C^{\infty}(M)@>{A}>{}>C^{\infty}(M)\end{CD}

may not be commutative. Using the notation
u\mapsto u_{\text{\rm c}}
for the injection
C^{\infty}(M)\subset\dot{C}^{-\infty}(M)
of (
2.5.10
), we have
A(u_{\text{\rm c}})-(Au)_{\text{\rm c}}\in C^{-\infty}_{\partial M}(M)
for all
u\in C^{\infty}(M)

[
Mel96
, Eq. (3.4.8)]
.

From (
2.5.2
) and its version for vector fields, we get a surjective restriction map

(2.5.22)

\operatorname{Diff}(\breve{M})\to\operatorname{Diff}(M)\;,\quad\breve{A}\mapsto\breve{A}|_{M}\;.

For any
\breve{A}\in\operatorname{Diff}(\breve{M})
with
\breve{A}|_{M}=A
, we have the commutative diagrams

(2.5.23)

\begin{CD}C^{-\infty}(\breve{M})@>{\breve{A}}>{}>C^{-\infty}(\breve{M})\\
@V{R}V{}V@V{}V{R}V\\
C^{-\infty}(M)@>{A}>{}>C^{-\infty}(M)\;,\hskip-5.69046pt\end{CD}\qquad\begin{CD}C^{-\infty}(\breve{M})@>{\breve{A}}>{}>C^{-\infty}(\breve{M})\\
@A{\iota}A{}A@A{}A{\iota}A\\
\dot{C}^{-\infty}(M)@>{A}>{}>\dot{C}^{-\infty}(M)\;,\hskip-5.69046pt\end{CD}

where the left-hand side square extends the left-hand side square of (
2.5.20
).

If
A\in\operatorname{Diff}^{m}(M)
(
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
), its actions on
\dot{C}^{-\infty}(M)
and
C^{-\infty}(M)
define continuous linear maps,

(2.5.24)

A:\dot{H}^{s}(M)\to\dot{H}^{s-m}(M)\;,\quad A:H^{s}(M)\to H^{s-m}(M)\;.

The maps (
2.5.17
) and (
2.5.24
) fit into a commutative diagram given by the left-hand side square of (
2.5.20
).

2.5.7.
Differential operators tangent to the boundary

The concepts of
Section

2.2
can be generalized to the case with boundary when
L=\partial M

[
Mel96
, Chapter 6]
(see also
[
Mel93
, Section 4.9]
), giving rise to the Lie subalgebra and
C^{\infty}(M)
-submodule
\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm b}}(M)\subset\mathfrak{X}(M)
of vector fields tangent to
\partial M
, called
b-vector fields
. We have
\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm b}}(M)\equiv C^{\infty}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}TM)
. Using
\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm b}}(M)
like in
Section

2.1.7
, we get the filtered
C^{\infty}(M)
-submodule and filtered subalgebra
\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}(M)\subset\operatorname{Diff}(M)
of
b-differential operators
; they are the operators
A\in\operatorname{Diff}(M)
such that (
2.5.21
) is commutative
[
Mel96
, Exercise 3.4.20]
. The definition of
\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}(M)
can be extended to arbitrary vector bundles like in
Section

2.1.7
. The condition of being tangent to the boundary is closed by taking transposes and formal adjoints. The restriction map (
2.5.22
) satisfies

(2.5.25)

\operatorname{Diff}(\breve{M},\partial M)|_{M}=\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}(M)\;.

For all
a\in\mathbb{R}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we have
[
Mel96
, Eqs. (4.2.7) and (4.2.8)]

(2.5.26)

\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}^{k}(M)\,x^{a}=x^{a}\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}^{k}(M)\;.

\operatorname{Diff}(M)
is spanned by
\partial_{x}
and
\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}(M)
as algebra, and therefore

(2.5.27)

\operatorname{Diff}^{k}(M)\,x^{a}\subset x^{a-k}\operatorname{Diff}^{k}(M)\;.

2.5.8.
Conormal distributions at the boundary

The spaces of
supported
and
extendible conormal distributions
at the boundary of Sobolev order
s\in\mathbb{R}
are the
C^{\infty}(M)
-modules and LCSs,

\displaystyle\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(s)}(M)

\displaystyle=\{\,u\in\dot{C}^{-\infty}(M)\mid\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}(M)\,u\subset\dot{H}^{s}(M)\,\}\;,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{(s)}(M)

\displaystyle=\{\,u\in C^{-\infty}(M)\mid\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}(M)\,u\subset H^{s}(M)\,\}\;,

with the topologies defined like in (
2.1.1
), which are totally reflexive Fréchet spaces
[
ÁLKL23
, Proposition 6.6]
. They satisfy the analogs of the continuous inclusions (
2.2.4
), giving rise to the filtered
C^{\infty}(M)
-modules and LCSs of
supported
and
extendible conormal distributions
at the boundary,

(2.5.28)

\dot{\mathcal{A}}(M)=\bigcup_{s}\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(s)}(M)\;,\quad\mathcal{A}(M)=\bigcup_{s}\mathcal{A}^{(s)}(M)\;,

which are barreled, ultrabornological and webbed
[
ÁLKL23
, Corollary 6.7]
. By definition, there are continuous inclusions

(2.5.29)

\dot{\mathcal{A}}(M)\subset\dot{C}^{-\infty}(M)\;,\quad\mathcal{A}(M)\subset C^{-\infty}(M)\;.

Thus
\dot{\mathcal{A}}(M)
and
\mathcal{A}(M)
are Hausdorff. We have

(2.5.30)

\bigcap_{s}\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(s)}(M)=\dot{C}^{\infty}(M)\;,\quad\bigcap_{s}\mathcal{A}^{(s)}(M)=C^{\infty}(M)\;,

obtaining continuous dense inclusions
[
Mel96
, Proposition 4.1.1 and Lemma 4.6.1]

(2.5.31)

\dot{C}^{\infty}(M)\subset\dot{\mathcal{A}}(M)\;,\quad C^{\infty}(M)\subset\mathcal{A}(M),\dot{\mathcal{A}}(M)\;.

By (
2.5.31
) and the density of the inclusions (
2.5.10
) and (
2.5.11
), it follows that the inclusions (
2.5.29
) are also dense. On the other hand, by elliptic regularity, we get continuous inclusions
[
Mel96
, Eq. (4.1.4)]

(2.5.32)

\dot{\mathcal{A}}(M)|_{\mathring{M}},\mathcal{A}(M)\subset C^{\infty}(\mathring{M})\;.

The maps (
2.5.17
) restrict to continuous linear maps

(2.5.33)

R:\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(s)}(M)\to\mathcal{A}^{(s)}(M)\;,

which are surjective for
s\leq 1/2
and injective for
s\geq-1/2
. If
s=0
, then (
2.5.33
) is a TVS-isomorphism because
\dot{H}^{0}(M)\equiv H^{0}(M)
. The maps (
2.5.33
) induce a surjective topological homomorphism
[
Mel96
, Proposition 4.1.1]
,
[
ÁLKL23
, Proposition 6.8]

(2.5.34)

R:\dot{\mathcal{A}}(M)\to\mathcal{A}(M)\;,

which is the identity on
C^{\infty}(M)
.

2.5.9.
The spaces
x^{m}L^{\infty}(M)

For
m\in\mathbb{R}
, consider the weighted space
x^{m}L^{\infty}(M)
(
Section

2.1.12
). There is a continuous inclusion

x^{m}L^{\infty}(M)\subset C^{-\infty}(M)\;.

For
m^{\prime}<m
, we also have a continuous inclusion

(2.5.35)

x^{m}L^{\infty}(M)\subset x^{m^{\prime}}L^{\infty}(M)\;,

and
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(\mathring{M})
is dense in
x^{m}L^{\infty}(M)
with the topology of
x^{m^{\prime}}L^{\infty}(M)

[
ÁLKL23
, Proposition 6.10]
.

2.5.10.
Filtration of
\mathcal{A}(M)
by bounds

For every
m\in\mathbb{R}
, let

\mathcal{A}^{m}(M)=\{\,u\in C^{-\infty}(M)\mid\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}(M)\,u\subset x^{m}L^{\infty}(M)\,\}\;.

This is another
C^{\infty}(M)
-module and Fréchet space with the topology like in (
2.1.1
). By (
2.5.35
), there is a continuous inclusion

(2.5.36)

\mathcal{A}^{m}(M)\subset\mathcal{A}^{m^{\prime}}(M)\quad(m^{\prime}<m)\;.

Moreover there are continuous inclusions
[
Mel96
, Proof of Proposition 4.2.1]

(2.5.37)

\mathcal{A}^{(s)}(M)\subset\mathcal{A}^{m}(M)\subset\mathcal{A}^{(\min\{m,0\})}(M)\quad(m<s-n/2-1)\;.

Hence

(2.5.38)

\mathcal{A}(M)=\bigcup_{m}\mathcal{A}^{m}(M)\;.

Despite of defining the same LF-space, the filtrations of
\mathcal{A}(M)
given by the spaces
\mathcal{A}^{(s)}(M)
and
\mathcal{A}^{m}(M)
are not equivalent because, in contrast with (
2.5.30
),

\dot{C}^{\infty}(M)=\bigcap_{m}\mathcal{A}^{m}(M)\;.

The following is true
[
ÁLKL23
, Corollaries 6.14–6.16 and 6.39 and Remark 6.41]
: the topologies of
\mathcal{A}(M)
and
C^{\infty}(\mathring{M})
coincide on every
\mathcal{A}^{m}(M)
(however the second inclusion of (
2.5.32
) is not a TVS-embedding);
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(\mathring{M})
is dense in every
\mathcal{A}^{m}(M)
, and therefore in every
\mathcal{A}^{(s)}(M)
and
\mathcal{A}(M)
; and
\mathcal{A}(M)
is an acyclic Montel space, and therefore complete, boundedly/compactly/sequentially retractive and reflexive.

2.5.11.
\dot{\mathcal{A}}(M)
and
\mathcal{A}(M)
vs
I(\breve{M},\partial M)

The restriction maps (
2.5.15
) define continuous linear maps

R:I^{(s)}(\breve{M},\partial M)\to\mathcal{A}^{(s)}(M)\;,

which induce a surjective topological homomorphism
[
ÁLKL23
, Proposition 6.18]

(2.5.39)

R:I(\breve{M},\partial M)\to\mathcal{A}(M)\;.

The null space of (
2.5.39
) is
I_{M^{\prime}}(\breve{M},\partial M)
. There are TVS-identities

(2.5.40)

\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(s)}(M)\equiv I^{(s)}_{M}(\breve{M},\partial M)\;,

inducing a TVS-isomorphism
[
ÁLKL23
, Corollary 6.20]

(2.5.41)

\dot{\mathcal{A}}(M)\xrightarrow{\cong}I_{M}(\breve{M},\partial M)\;.

Moreover
I_{M}(\breve{M},\partial M)
is a limit subspace of the LF-space
I(\breve{M},\partial M)

[
ÁLKL23
, Proposition 6.19]
.

2.5.12.
Filtration of
\dot{\mathcal{A}}(M)
by the symbol order

Like in (
2.5.40
), let

(2.5.42)

\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{m}(M)=I^{m}_{M}(\breve{M},\partial M)\subset I^{m}(\breve{M},\partial M)\quad(m\in\mathbb{R})\;,

which are closed subspaces satisfying the analogs of (
2.2.7
) and (
2.2.8
). Thus

\dot{\mathcal{A}}(M)=\bigcup_{m}\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{m}(M)\;,\quad\dot{C}^{\infty}(M)=\bigcap_{m}\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{m}(M)\;,

and the TVS-isomorphism (
2.5.41
) is also compatible with the symbol filtration.
\dot{\mathcal{A}}(M)
is an acyclic Montel space, and therefore complete, boundedly/compactly/sequentially retractive and reflexive
[
ÁLKL23
, Corollary 6.22]
.

2.5.13.
The space
\mathcal{K}(M)

The condition of being supported in
\partial M
defines the LCHSs and
C^{\infty}(M)
-modules

\mathcal{K}^{(s)}(M)=\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(s)}_{\partial M}(M)\;,\quad\mathcal{K}^{m}(M)=\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{m}_{\partial M}(M)\;,\quad\mathcal{K}(M)=\dot{\mathcal{A}}_{\partial M}(M)\;.

These are the null spaces of the corresponding restrictions of the map (
2.5.34
) to
\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(s)}(M)
,
\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{m}(M)
and
\dot{\mathcal{A}}(M)
. They satisfy the analogs of (
2.2.4
), (
2.2.7
) and (
2.2.8
), obtaining
\bigcup_{s}\mathcal{K}^{(s)}(M)=\bigcup_{m}\mathcal{K}^{m}(M)
.

The properties of (
2.5.34
) mean that the following sequence is exact in the category of continuous linear maps between LCSs:

(2.5.43)

0\to\mathcal{K}(M)\xrightarrow{\iota}\dot{\mathcal{A}}(M)\xrightarrow{R}\mathcal{A}(M)\to 0\;.

It is called the
conormal sequence at the boundary
. We have

\mathcal{K}^{(s)}(M)=\{\,u\in\dot{C}^{-\infty}_{\partial M}(M)\mid\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}(M)\,u\subset\dot{H}^{s}_{\partial M}(M)\,\}\;,

with the topology defined like in (
2.1.1
). The following properties hold
[
ÁLKL23
, Propositions 6.24 and 6.25 and Corollaries 6.26–6.28]
: every
\mathcal{K}^{(s)}(M)
is a totally reflexive Fréchet space;
\mathcal{K}(M)
is a limit subspace of the LF-space
\dot{\mathcal{A}}(M)
; and
\mathcal{K}(M)
is barreled, ultrabornological, webbed and an acyclic Montel space, and therefore complete, boundedly/compactly/sequentially retractive and reflexive.

The TVS-isomorphism (
2.5.41
) restricts to a TVS-identity

(2.5.44)

\mathcal{K}(M)\equiv I_{\partial M}(\breve{M},\partial M)\;,

which in turn restricts to identities between the LCHSs defining the Sobolev-order and symbol-order filtrations, according to (
2.5.40
) and (
2.5.42
).

A description of
\mathcal{K}^{(s)}(M)
and
\mathcal{K}(M)
will be indicated in
2.6.3
.

2.5.14.
Action of
\operatorname{Diff}(M)
on
\dot{\mathcal{A}}(M)
,
\mathcal{A}(M)
and
\mathcal{K}(M)

Any
A\in\operatorname{Diff}(M)
defines continuous endomorphisms
A
of
\dot{\mathcal{A}}(M)
,
\mathcal{A}(M)
and
\mathcal{K}(M)
. If
A\in\operatorname{Diff}^{k}(M)
, these maps also satisfy the analogs of (
2.2.15
). If
A\in\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}(M)
, then it defines continuous endomorphisms of
\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(s)}(M)
,
\mathcal{A}^{(s)}(M)
,
\mathcal{A}^{m}(M)
and
\mathcal{K}^{(s)}(M)
. All of these maps are restrictions of the endomorphisms
A
of
\dot{C}^{-\infty}(M)
,
C^{-\infty}(M)
and
C^{\infty}(\mathring{M})
, and extensions of the endomorphisms
A
of
\dot{C}^{\infty}(M)
and
C^{\infty}(M)
.

2.5.15.
Partial extension maps

Given linear subspaces,
X\subset\mathcal{A}(M)
and
Y\subset\dot{\mathcal{A}}(M
), a map
E:X\to Y
is called a
partial extension map
if
R(Y)\subset X
and
RE=1
on
X
. The surjectivity of (
2.5.34
) is given by the following result. Its proof is recalled here because it will play an important role in our work.

Proposition 2.5.1

(Cf.
[
Mel96
, Section 4.4]
)
.

For all
m\in\mathbb{R}
, there is a continuous linear partial extension map
E_{m}:\mathcal{A}^{m}(M)\to\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(s)}(M)
, where
s=0
if
m\geq 0
, and
m>s\in\mathbb{Z}^{-}
if
m<0
. For
m\geq 0
,
E_{m}:\mathcal{A}^{m}(M)\to\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(0)}(M)
is a continuous inclusion map.

Proof.

First, let us consider the non-compact
n
-manifold with boundary
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1}:=[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
, whose double is
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Consider the canonical coordinates on
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1}
given by the factor projections,
x:\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1}\to[0,\infty)
and
y:\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1}\to\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
. We use the obvious generalization to the non-compact case of the spaces of extendible and supported conormal distributions at the boundary, of Sobolev order
s
, whose definitions involve
H^{s}_{\text{\rm loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1})
and
\dot{H}^{s}_{\text{\rm loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1})
like in
Section

2.2.4
.

For
m\geq 0
, since
x^{m}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1})\subset L^{2}_{\text{\rm loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1})
, continuously, we get
\mathcal{A}^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1})\subset\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(0)}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1})
, continuously. This also follows from (
2.5.37
) using that
\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(0)}(\mathbb{R}_{1}^{n})\equiv\mathcal{A}^{(0)}(\mathbb{R}_{1}^{n})
. Thus
E_{m}
must be the inclusion map in this case.

Now fix
m<0
. For
0<\delta\leq 1
such that
m+\delta\leq 0
if
m\neq-1
, and
m+\delta<0
if
m=-1
, we have a continuous linear map
J:\mathcal{A}^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1})\to\mathcal{A}^{m+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1})
defined by

(2.5.45)

Ju(x,y)=\int_{1}^{x}u(\xi,y)\,d\xi\;.

So, for
-m<-s=:N\in\mathbb{N}
, we get the continuous linear maps (see
Section

2.5.14
)

\mathcal{A}^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1})\xrightarrow{J^{N}}\mathcal{A}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1})\xrightarrow{E_{0}}\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(0)}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1})\xrightarrow{\partial_{x}^{N}}\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(s)}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1})\;,

whose composition is the desired extension
E_{m}
. For all
u\in\mathcal{A}^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1})
, we have

(2.5.46)

\partial\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1}\cap\operatorname{supp}E_{m}u\subset\{0\}\times y(\operatorname{supp}u)\;.

Consider now a compact manifold with boundary
M
. Cover
\partial M
with a finite collection of adapted charts
(U_{j},(x_{j},y_{j}))
, and let
\{\lambda_{j},\mu\}
be a partition of unity subordinated to the open covering
\{U_{j},\mathring{M}\}
of
M
. By the case of
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1}
, we directly get
\mathcal{A}^{m}(U_{j})\subset\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(0)}(U_{j})
, continuously, if
m\geq 0
. By (
2.5.46
), if
m<0
and
-m<N\in\mathbb{N}
, we get a continuous linear partial extension map
E_{m,j}:\mathcal{A}^{m}(U_{j})\to\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(-N)}(U_{j})
, which preserves the condition of being compactly supported. Then the result follows with
E_{m}:\mathcal{A}^{m}(M)\to\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(s)}(M)
defined by
E_{m}u=\mu u+\sum_{j}E_{m,j}(\lambda_{j}u)
.
∎

Remark 2.5.2
.

Consider the case where
m<0
in the proof of
2.5.1
. Taking a collar neighborhood of the boundary,
T\equiv[0,\epsilon)_{x}\times\partial M_{\varpi}
, we can use adapted charts
(U_{j}\equiv[0,\epsilon)\times V_{j},(x,y_{j}))
defined by charts
(V_{j},y_{j})
of
\partial M
, like in
Section

2.5.1
. Then the operators
\partial_{x}\in\operatorname{Diff}(U_{j})
can be combined to define an operator
\partial_{x}\in\operatorname{Diff}(T)
, which indeed is the derivative operator on
C^{\infty}(T)\equiv C^{\infty}([0,\epsilon),C^{\infty}(\partial M))
. On the other hand, by integrating from
\epsilon
to
x
, like in (
2.5.45
), we get a continuous linear map
J:\mathcal{A}^{m}(T)\to\mathcal{A}^{m+\delta}(T)
; in fact, this defines a continuous endomorphism
J
of
C^{\infty}(\mathring{T})
. In this way, a continuous linear extension map
E_{m,T}:\mathcal{A}^{m}(T)\to\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(s)}(T)
can be defined like in the case of
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1}
. Then
E_{m}:\mathcal{A}^{m}(M)\to\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(s)}(M)
can be given by
E_{m}u=\mu u+E_{m,T}(\lambda u)
, where
\{\lambda,\mu\}
is a partition of unity subordinated to the open covering
\{T,\mathring{M}\}
of
M
.

Remark 2.5.3
.

A version of
2.5.1
with a vector bundle
E
over
M
can be achieved by taking the
C^{\infty}(M)
-tensor product with the identity map on
C^{\infty}(M;E\otimes E^{*})
. We can also adapt the proof as follows. With the notation of
2.5.2
, there is an identity
E_{T}\equiv\varpi^{*}E_{\partial M}\equiv[0,\epsilon)\times E_{\partial M}
over
T
, which induces trivializations
E_{U_{j}}\equiv[0,\epsilon)\times E_{V_{j}}\equiv[0,\epsilon)\times V_{j}\times\mathbb{C}^{l}
over domains
U_{j}\equiv[0,\epsilon)\times V_{j}
. Like in
2.5.2
, these local trivializations can be used to define
\partial_{x}\in\operatorname{Diff}^{1}(T;E)
, which is considered as the derivative operator on
C^{\infty}([0,\epsilon),C^{\infty}(\partial M;E))\equiv C^{\infty}(T;E)
. As usual, integration by parts shows that

(2.5.47)

\partial_{x}^{\text{\rm t}}=-\partial_{x}\in\operatorname{Diff}^{1}(T;E^{*}\otimes\Omega)\;.

If
E=\Lambda M
, then
\partial_{x}\in\operatorname{Diff}^{1}(T;\Lambda)
is the Lie derivative with respect to
\partial_{x}\in\mathfrak{X}(T)
.

Remark 2.5.4
.

By (
2.5.46
), all steps of the proof of
2.5.1
have obvious compactly supported versions. This also applies to
2.5.2
and
2.5.3
.

Given
m
and
s
satisfying the conditions of
2.5.1
, let us denote by
E_{m,s}
the partial extension map constructed in the proof of
2.5.1
. This notation will make it easier to analyze its dependence on
m
and
s
in the following result.

Proposition 2.5.5
.

Let
s^{\prime}\leq s
and
m^{\prime}\leq m
such that the maps
E_{m,s}
,
E_{m,s^{\prime}}
and
E_{m^{\prime},s^{\prime}}
are defined. Then
E_{m,s}u=E_{m^{\prime},s^{\prime}}u
for all
u\in\mathcal{A}^{m}(M)
.

Proof.

According to the proof of
2.5.1
, it is enough to consider the case of
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1}
.

If
m^{\prime}\geq 0
, there is nothing to prove.

In the case
m<0
, we have
s,s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{-}
with
m^{\prime}>s>s^{\prime}
. Let
N=-s
,
N^{\prime}=-s^{\prime}
and
k=s-s^{\prime}=N^{\prime}-N
in
\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. Since
\mathcal{A}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1})\subset L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1})
, the composition

\mathcal{A}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1})\xrightarrow{J^{k}}\mathcal{A}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1})\hookrightarrow\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(0)}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1})\xrightarrow{\partial_{x}^{k}}\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(-k)}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1})

is equal to the inclusion map
\mathcal{A}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1})\hookrightarrow\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(-k)}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1})
. So, for all
u\in\mathcal{A}^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1})
, since
J^{N}u\in\mathcal{A}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{1})
, we have

E_{m^{\prime},s^{\prime}}u=E_{m,s^{\prime}}u=\partial_{x}^{N^{\prime}}J^{N^{\prime}}u=\partial_{x}^{N}\partial_{x}^{k}J^{k}J^{N}u=\partial_{x}^{N}J^{N}u=E_{m,s}u\;.

In the case
m^{\prime}<0\leq m
, we have
s=0
and
m^{\prime}>s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{-}
. Then the result follows with a similar argument using
k=-s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
.
∎

Corollary 2.5.6
.

For all
s
and
m
such that the map
E_{m,s}
is defined, we have
E_{m,s}u=u
for all
u\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(\mathring{M})
.

Proof.

Use that
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(\mathring{M})\subset\mathcal{A}^{m}(M)
and apply
2.5.5
.
∎

Remark 2.5.7
.

The proof of
2.5.5
can be also applied to maps
E_{m,s}
with
m\geq 0
and
s\in\mathbb{Z}^{-}
, defined
E_{m}
like in the case
m<0
. Including these maps, the map
E_{m,s^{\prime}}
of the statement is always defined under the other assumptions.

Remark 2.5.8
.

2.5.5
and
2.5.6
are also true with the definitions of
E_{m}
given in
2.5.2
and
2.5.3
, with similar proofs.

2.5.16.
L^{2}
and
L^{\infty}
half-b-densities

We have

(2.5.48)

\displaystyle L^{2}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle\equiv x^{-\frac{1}{2}}L^{2}(M;\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\;,

(2.5.49)

\displaystyle L^{\infty}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle\equiv x^{-\frac{1}{2}}L^{\infty}(M;\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\;,

where (
2.5.48
) holds as Hilbert spaces, and (
2.5.49
) holds as LCHSs endowed with a family of equivalent Banach space norms
[
ÁLKL23
, Eqs. (6.51) and (6.52)]
.

Equip
M
with a b-metric
g
(
Section

2.5.1
), and endow
\mathring{M}
with the restriction of
g
, also denoted by
g
. With the corresponding Euclidean/Hermitean structures on
\Omega^{1/2}\mathring{M}
and
{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2}M
, we get
L^{\infty}(\mathring{M};\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\equiv L^{\infty}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})
as Banach spaces.

2.5.17.
b-Sobolev spaces

For
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the
b-Sobolev spaces
of
order

\pm m
are the
C^{\infty}(M)
-modules and Hilbertian spaces defined by the following analogs of (
2.1.29
) and (
2.1.30
):

\displaystyle H_{\text{\rm b}}^{m}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})=\{\,u\in L^{2}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\mid\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}^{m}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\,u\subset L^{2}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\,\}\;,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}^{m}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\,L^{2}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})=H_{\text{\rm b}}^{-m}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})=H_{\text{\rm b}}^{m}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})^{\prime}\;.

Any finite set of
C^{\infty}(M)
-generators of
\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}^{m}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})
defines a scalar product on
H_{\text{\rm b}}^{\pm m}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})
. The intersections/unions of the spaces
H_{\text{\rm b}}^{m}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})
(
m\in\mathbb{Z}
) are denoted by
H_{\text{\rm b}}^{\pm\infty}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})
. In particular,
H_{\text{\rm b}}^{\infty}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})=\mathcal{A}^{(0)}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})
.

2.5.18.
Weighted b-Sobolev spaces

We will also use the
weighted b-Sobolev space

x^{a}H_{\text{\rm b}}^{m}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})
(
a\in\mathbb{R}
), another Hilbertian space defined like in
Section

2.1.12
. We have
[
ÁLKL23
, Section 6.19]

\bigcap_{a,m}x^{a}H^{m}_{\text{\rm b}}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})=\dot{C}^{\infty}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\;.

2.5.19.
Action of
\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}^{m}(M)
on weighted b-Sobolev spaces

We have

(2.5.50)

\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}^{m}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\equiv\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}^{m}(M)\equiv\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}^{m}(M;\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\;,

like in (
2.1.23
). Moreover any
A\in\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}^{k}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})
defines continuous linear maps
[
Mel93
, Lemma 5.14]

A:x^{a}H^{m}_{\text{\rm b}}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\to x^{a}H^{m-k}_{\text{\rm b}}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\;,

which induce a continuous endomorphism
A
of
x^{a}H^{\pm\infty}_{\text{\rm b}}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})
.

2.5.20.
A description of
\mathcal{A}(M)

In this subsection, unless the contrary is indicated, assume the following properties:

(1)

\mathring{M}
is of bounded geometry with
g
.

(2)

The collar neighborhood
T
of
\partial M
can be chosen so that:

(a)

every
A\in\mathfrak{X}(\partial M)
has an extension
A^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm b}}(T)
such that
A^{\prime}
is
\varpi
-projectable to
A
, and
A^{\prime}|_{\mathring{T}}
is orthogonal to the
\varpi
-fibers; and

(b)

\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm ub}}(\mathring{M})|_{\mathring{T}}
is
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(\mathring{M})|_{\mathring{T}}
-generated by
x\partial_{x}
and the restrictions
A^{\prime}|_{\mathring{T}}
of the vector fields
A^{\prime}
of
2a
, for
A\in\mathfrak{X}(\partial M)
.

For instance,
1
and
2
are true if
\mathring{T}
is cylindrical with
g
(
Section

2.5.1
). The following properties hold
[
ÁLKL23
, Corollaries 6.32, 6.34, 6.35, 6.37, 6.38 and 6.40 and Propositions 6.33 and 6.36]
: the restriction to
\mathring{M}
defines a continuous injection
C^{\infty}(M)\subset C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(\mathring{M})
(thus
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(\mathring{M})
becomes a
C^{\infty}(M)
-module); as
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(\mathring{M})
-modules,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm ub}}^{m}(\mathring{M})

\displaystyle\equiv\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}^{m}(M)\otimes_{C^{\infty}(M)}C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(\mathring{M})\;,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm ub}}^{m}(\mathring{M};\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle\equiv\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}^{m}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\otimes_{C^{\infty}(M)}C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(\mathring{M})\;;

as
C^{\infty}(M)
-modules and Hilbertian spaces, for
m\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\displaystyle H^{m}(\mathring{M};\Omega^{1/2})

\displaystyle\equiv H_{\text{\rm b}}^{m}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})\;,
