\displaystyle\quad H^{m}(\mathring{M})

\displaystyle=x^{-1/2}H_{\text{\rm b}}^{m}(M)\;,

\displaystyle H^{\pm\infty}(\mathring{M};\Omega^{1/2})

\displaystyle\equiv H_{\text{\rm b}}^{\pm\infty}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})\;,

\displaystyle\quad H^{\pm\infty}(\mathring{M})

\displaystyle=x^{-1/2}H_{\text{\rm b}}^{\pm\infty}(M)\;;

as
C^{\infty}(M)
-modules and LCHSs, for
m\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{m}(M;\Omega^{1/2})\equiv x^{m+1/2}H_{\text{\rm b}}^{\infty}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})\;,

(2.5.51)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{m}(M)\equiv x^{m}H_{\text{\rm b}}^{\infty}(M)\equiv x^{m+1/2}H^{\infty}(\mathring{M})\;,

(2.5.52)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(M)\equiv\bigcup_{m}x^{m}H_{\text{\rm b}}^{\infty}(M)=\bigcup_{m}x^{m}H^{\infty}(\mathring{M})\;.

Actually, the first identities of (
2.5.51
) and (
2.5.52
) are independent of
g
, and therefore they hold true without the assumptions
1
and
2

[
ÁLKL23
, Remark 6.41]
.

2.5.21.
Dual-conormal distributions at the boundary

Consider the LCHSs
[
Hör85
, Section 18.3]
,
[
Mel96
, Chapter 4]
,

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(M)=\mathcal{K}(M;\Omega)^{\prime}\;,\quad\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(M)=\dot{\mathcal{A}}(M;\Omega)^{\prime}\;,\quad\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(M)=\mathcal{A}(M;\Omega)^{\prime}\;,

which are complete Montel spaces
[
ÁLKL23
, Proposition 6.42]
. The elements of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(M)
(resp.,
\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(M)
) will be called
extendible
(resp.,
supported
)
dual-conormal distributions
at the boundary. Consider also the LCHSs

\mathcal{K}^{\prime\,(s)}(M)=\mathcal{K}^{(-s)}(M;\Omega)^{\prime}\;,\quad\mathcal{K}^{\prime\,m}(M)=\mathcal{K}^{-m}(M;\Omega)^{\prime}\;,

and, similarly, define
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\,(s)}(M)
,
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\,m}(M)
,
\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime\,(s)}(M)
and
\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime\,m}(M)
. The spaces
\mathcal{K}^{\prime\,(s)}(M)
,
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\,(s)}(M)
and
\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime\,(s)}(M)
are bornological and barreled
[
ÁLKL23
, Corollary 6.43]
. The transpositions of the analogs of (
2.2.4
) and (
2.2.7
) for the spaces
\mathcal{K}^{(s)}(M;\Omega)
,
\mathcal{K}^{m}(M;\Omega)
,
\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(s)}(M;\Omega)
and
\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{m}(M;\Omega)
are continuous linear restriction maps

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{\prime\,(s^{\prime})}(M)\to\mathcal{K}^{\prime\,(s)}(M)\;,\quad\mathcal{K}^{\prime\,m}(M)\to\mathcal{K}^{\prime\,m^{\prime}}(M)\;,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\prime\,(s^{\prime})}(M)\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime\,(s)}(M)\;,\quad\mathcal{A}^{\prime\,m}(M)\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime\,m^{\prime}}(M)\;,

for
s<s^{\prime}
and
m<m^{\prime}
. These maps form projective spectra, giving rise to projective limits. The spaces
\mathcal{K}^{\prime\,(s)}(M)
,
\mathcal{K}^{\prime\,m}(M)
,
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\,(s)}(M)
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\,m}(M)
satisfy the analogs of (
2.3.5
) and (
2.3.6
)
[
ÁLKL23
, Corollary 6.44]
.

Similarly, transposing the analogs of (
2.2.4
) and (
2.5.36
) for the spaces
\mathcal{A}(M,\Omega M)
, we get continuous inclusions

\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime\,(s)}(M)\supset\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime\,(s^{\prime})}(M)\;,\quad\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime\,m}(M)\supset\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime\,m^{\prime}}(M)\;,

for
s<s^{\prime}
and
m<m^{\prime}
. The version of (
2.5.37
) with
\Omega M
yields continuous inclusions

(2.5.53)

\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime\,(s)}(M)\supset\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime\,m}(M)\supset\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime\,(\max\{m,0\})}(M)\quad(m>s+n/2+1)\;.

We also have
[
ÁLKL23
, Corollary 6.44]

(2.5.54)

\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(M)=\bigcap_{s}\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime\,(s)}(M)=\bigcap_{m}\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime\,m}(M)\;,

where the last equality is a consequence of (
2.5.53
).

Transposing the versions of (
2.5.3
), (
2.5.29
) and (
2.5.31
) with
\Omega M
, we get continuous inclusions
[
Mel96
, Section 4.6]

(2.5.55)

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(M)\subset\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(M)\subset C^{-\infty}(M),\dot{C}^{-\infty}(M)\;,

(2.5.56)

\displaystyle\dot{C}^{\infty}(M)\subset\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(M)\subset\dot{C}^{-\infty}(M),C^{-\infty}(M)\;,

and
R:\dot{C}^{-\infty}(M)\to C^{-\infty}(M)
restricts to the identity map on
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(M)
and
\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(M)
. The first inclusion of (
2.5.56
) is dense; in fact,
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(\mathring{M})
is dense in every
\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime\,m}(M)
, and therefore in
\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(M)

[
ÁLKL23
, Corollary 6.50 and Remark 6.51]
.

2.5.22.
Dual-conormal sequence at the boundary

Transposing maps in the version of (
2.5.43
) with
\Omega M
, we get the sequence

(2.5.57)

0\leftarrow\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(M)\xleftarrow{R^{\prime}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(M)\xleftarrow{\iota^{\prime}}\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(M)\leftarrow 0\;,

where
R^{\prime}=\iota^{\text{\rm t}}
and
\iota^{\prime}=R^{\text{\rm t}}
. It is called the
dual-conormal sequence at the boundary
of
M
, which is exact in the category of continuous linear maps between LCSs
[
ÁLKL23
, Proposition 6.45]
.

2.5.23.
\dot{\mathcal{A}}(M)
and
\mathcal{A}(M)
vs
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(M)

Using (
2.5.29
), (
2.5.31
) and (
2.5.55
), we have
[
Hör85
, Proposition 18.3.24]
,
[
Mel96
, Theorem 4.6.1]

(2.5.58)

\dot{\mathcal{A}}(M)\cap\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(M)=C^{\infty}(M)\;.

2.5.24.
A description of
\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(M)

If
1
and
2
are true, then, for
m\in\mathbb{R}

[
ÁLKL23
, Corollaries 6.48 and 6.49]
,

(2.5.59)

\displaystyle\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime\,m}(M)\equiv x^{m}H_{\text{\rm b}}^{-\infty}(M)=x^{m-\frac{1}{2}}H^{-\infty}(\mathring{M})\;,

(2.5.60)

\displaystyle\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(M)\equiv\bigcap_{m}x^{m}H_{\text{\rm b}}^{-\infty}(M)=\bigcap_{m}x^{m}H^{-\infty}(\mathring{M})\;.

The first identities of (
2.5.59
) and (
2.5.60
) are independent of
g
, and hold without the assumptions
1
and
2
.

2.5.25.
Action of
\operatorname{Diff}(M)
on
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(M)
,
\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(M)
and
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(M)

Any
A\in\operatorname{Diff}(M)
induces continuous linear endomorphisms
A
of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(M)
,
\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(M)
and
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(M)

[
Mel96
, Proposition 4.6.1]
, which are the transposes of
A^{\text{\rm t}}
on
\dot{\mathcal{A}}(M;\Omega)
,
\mathcal{A}(M;\Omega)
and
\mathcal{K}(M;\Omega)
(
Sections

2.1.5
and
2.5.14
). If
A\in\operatorname{Diff}^{k}(M)
, these maps satisfy the analogs of (
2.3.7
). If
A\in\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}(M)
, it induces continuous endomorphisms of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\,(s)}(M)
,
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\,m}(M)
,
\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime\,(s)}(M)
and
\mathcal{K}^{\prime\,(s)}(M)
.

2.5.26.
The b-stretched product

Let
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{r}
be the connected components of
\partial M
. Consider the submanifold
B:=\bigcup_{j=1}^{r}Y_{j}^{2}
of the
C^{\infty}
manifold with corners
M^{2}
. Its inward-pointing spherical normal bundle is
S_{+}NB={}_{+}NB/\mathbb{R}^{+}
, where
\mathbb{R}^{+}
acts on
{}_{+}NB
by multiplication. The
b-stretched product

M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}}
is the compact smooth manifold with corners obtained from
M^{2}
by blowing-up
B

[
Mel93
, Sections 4.1 and 4.2]
,
[
Mel96
, Chapter 4]
, with corresponding surjective smooth blow-down map
\beta_{\text{\rm b}}:M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}}\to M^{2}
; namely,
M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}}=S_{+}NB\sqcup(M^{2}\setminus B)
, and
\beta_{\text{\rm b}}
is the combination of the projection
S_{+}NB\to B
and the identity map on
M^{2}\setminus B
. The topology and
C^{\infty}
structure of
M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}}
can be described as follows.

For any
C^{1}
curve
\chi:[0,1]\to M^{2}
with
\chi(0)\in B
and
\chi((0,1])\subset M^{2}\setminus B
, let
\tilde{\chi}:[0,1]\to M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}}
be the lift of
\chi
so that
\tilde{\chi}(0)
is defined by
\chi^{\prime}(0)
. Then a subset
U\subset M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}}
is open if it has open intersections with
S_{+}NB
and
M^{2}\setminus B
, and, for any such curve
\chi
with
\tilde{\chi}([0,1])\subset U
, we have
\tilde{\eta}([0,1])\subset U
for all
C^{1}
curve
\eta:[0,1]\to M^{2}
of the same type as
\chi
and
C^{1}
-close enough to
\chi
.

Let
x
and
x^{\prime}
denote the lifts to
M^{2}
of the boundary-defining function
x
from the left and right factors. The
C^{\infty}
function

\tau:=\frac{x-x^{\prime}}{x+x^{\prime}}:M^{2}\setminus(\partial M)^{2}\to[-1,1]

has a continuous extension
\tau
to the open neighborhood
S_{+}NB\sqcup(M^{2}\setminus(\partial M)^{2})
of
S_{+}NB
in
M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}}
. Then
C^{\infty}(M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}})
is locally generated by
\tau
and
\beta_{\text{\rm b}}^{*}C^{\infty}(M^{2})
.

The manifold with corners
M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}}
has three boundary hypersurfaces,

\operatorname{ff}=\beta_{\text{\rm b}}^{-1}(B)\;,\quad\operatorname{lb}=\overline{\beta_{\text{\rm b}}^{-1}(\partial M\times\mathring{M})}\;,\quad\operatorname{rb}=\overline{\beta_{\text{\rm b}}^{-1}(\mathring{M}\times\partial M)}\;,

called the
front face
, and the
left
and
right boundaries
. They satisfy
\operatorname{lb}\cap\operatorname{rb}=\emptyset
. Another embedded, compact submanifold of
M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}}
is the
b-diagonal
,
\Delta_{\text{\rm b}}=\overline{\beta_{\text{\rm b}}^{-1}(\Delta\setminus B)}
, where
\Delta\subset M^{2}
is the diagonal. We have
\Delta_{\text{\rm b}}\pitchfork\operatorname{ff}
,
\Delta_{\text{\rm b},0}:=\Delta_{\text{\rm b}}\cap\operatorname{ff}=\partial\Delta_{\text{\rm b}}
and
\Delta_{\text{\rm b}}\cap\operatorname{lb}=\Delta_{\text{\rm b}}\cap\operatorname{rb}=\emptyset
. Moreover
\beta_{\text{\rm b}}:\Delta_{\text{\rm b}}\to\Delta\equiv M
is a diffeomorphism, where the last identity is given by the diagonal map.

Let also
x=\beta_{\text{\rm b}}^{*}x
and
x^{\prime}=\beta_{\text{\rm b}}^{*}x^{\prime}
on
M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}}
. Thus
r=x+x^{\prime}
is a defining function of
\operatorname{ff}
in
M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}}
(in the same sense as in
Section

2.5.1
for
\partial M
). For adapted local coordinates
(x,y)
, the lifts
y
and
y^{\prime}
of
y
to open subsets of
M^{2}
and
M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}}
are defined like
x
and
x^{\prime}
. Then
(r,\tau,y,y^{\prime})
or
(x,\tau,y,y^{\prime})
are local coordinates of
M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}}
around points of
\operatorname{ff}
, the submanifold
\Delta_{\text{\rm b}}
is locally described by the conditions
\tau=0
and
y=y^{\prime}
, and
\Delta_{\text{\rm b},0}
is locally described by the conditions
r=\tau=0
and
y=y^{\prime}
. Other local coordinates
(r,s,y,y^{\prime})
or
(x,s,y,y^{\prime})
of
M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}}
around points of
\mathring{\operatorname{ff}}
are defined using the function

s:=\frac{1+\tau}{1-\tau}=\frac{x}{x^{\prime}}:M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}}\setminus\operatorname{rb}\to(0,\infty)\;.

With the obvious extensions to manifolds with corners of some concepts of
Sections

2.5.1
and
2.5.7
, we get the following
[
Mel93
, Section 4.5]
. First,

\displaystyle{}^{\text{\rm b}}TM^{2}

\displaystyle\equiv({}^{\text{\rm b}}TM)^{2}\;,

\displaystyle\quad{}^{\text{\rm b}}TM^{2}_{\text{\rm b}}

\displaystyle\equiv\beta_{\text{\rm b}}^{*}({}^{\text{\rm b}}TM^{2})\;,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm b}}(M^{2})

\displaystyle\equiv C^{\infty}(M^{2};{}^{\text{\rm b}}TM^{2})\;,

\displaystyle\quad\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm b}}(M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}})

\displaystyle\equiv C^{\infty}(M^{2};{}^{\text{\rm b}}TM^{2}_{\text{\rm b}})\;.

Second, any vector field in
\mathfrak{X}(M^{2},B)
can be lifted to a vector field in
\mathfrak{X}(M^{2},\operatorname{ff})
; in particular, the lifts to
M^{2}
of
\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm b}}(M)
, from the left and right factors, generate
\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm b}}(M^{2})
over
C^{\infty}(M^{2})
. Third, there is a lifting map
\beta_{\text{\rm b}}^{*}:\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm b}}(M^{2})\to\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm b}}(M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}})
, whose image spans
\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm b}}(M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}})
over
C^{\infty}(M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}})
. It induces a lifting map
\beta_{\text{\rm b}}^{*}:\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}^{m}(X^{2})\to\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}^{m}(M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}})
, whose image spans
\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}^{m}(M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}})
over
C^{\infty}(M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}})

[
Mel93
, Exercise 4.11]
. For instance, using the above local coordinates, the lift of
x\partial_{x}
is
\frac{1}{2}(1+\tau)r\partial_{r}+\frac{1}{2}(1-\tau^{2})\partial_{\tau}
. Finally, the lift to
M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}}
of
\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm b}}(M)
from the left factor of
M^{2}
is a Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm b}}(M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}})
transverse to
\Delta_{\text{\rm b}}
, giving rise to natural isomorphisms
N\Delta_{\text{\rm b}}\cong{}^{\text{\rm b}}TM
and
N^{*}\Delta_{\text{\rm b}}\cong{}^{\text{\rm b}}T^{*}M

[
Mel93
, Lemmas 4.5 and 4.6]
. Thus there is a canonical isomorphism
{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2}(M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}})|_{\Delta_{\text{\rm b}}}\cong{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega M
(cf.
[
Mel93
, Eq. (4.125)]
).

2.5.27.
The b-pseudodifferential operators

A refinement of the Schwartz kernel theorem gives a bijection
[
Mel93
, Lemma 4.20]

\displaystyle L(\dot{C}^{\infty}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}),C^{-\infty}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}))\to C^{-\infty}(M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}};\beta_{\text{\rm b}}^{*}({}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}\boxtimes{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}))\;,

\displaystyle A\mapsto\kappa_{A}\;,\quad\langle Au,v\rangle=\langle\kappa_{A},\beta_{\text{\rm b}}^{*}(v\otimes u)\rangle\;,\quad u,v\in\dot{C}^{\infty}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\;.

The concept of conormal distributional sections can be also extended to submanifolds whose boundary is given by a transverse intersection with the boundary faces, like
\Delta_{\text{\rm b}}\subset M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}}
. Then a continuous linear map
A:\dot{C}^{\infty}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})\to C^{-\infty}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})
is called a
b-pseudodifferential operator
of
order
at most
m\in\mathbb{R}
if
\kappa_{A}\in I^{m}(M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}},\Delta_{\text{\rm b}};\beta_{\text{\rm b}}^{*}({}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2}\boxtimes{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2}))
and
\kappa_{A}
vanishes to all orders at
\operatorname{lb}\cup\operatorname{rb}

[
Mel93
, Definition 4.22]
. Such operators form a
C^{\infty}(M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}})
-module
\Psi_{\text{\rm b}}^{m}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})
, obtaining the filtered
C^{\infty}(M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}})
-module
\Psi_{\text{\rm b}}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})=\bigcup_{m}\Psi_{\text{\rm b}}^{m}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})
. The submodule
\Psi_{\text{\rm b}}^{-\infty}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2}):=\bigcap_{m}\Psi_{\text{\rm b}}^{m}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})
(resp.,
\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})
) of
\Psi_{\text{\rm b}}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})
consists of the operators
A\in\Psi_{\text{\rm b}}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})
with smooth
\kappa_{A}
(resp.,
\operatorname{supp}\kappa_{A}\subset\Delta_{\text{\rm b}}
). The obvious generalization of the definition of principal symbol, like in
Section

2.2.3
, now gives the
principal b-symbol
exact sequence,

0\to\Psi_{\text{\rm b}}^{m-1}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\hookrightarrow\Psi_{\text{\rm b}}^{m}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\xrightarrow{{}^{\text{\rm b}}\!\sigma_{m}}S^{(m)}({}^{\text{\rm b}}T^{*}M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}})\to 0\;.

The principal b-symbol is used to define
b-ellipticity
like ellipticity in the case of pseudodifferential operators (
Section

2.1.9
).

Omitting
{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2}
, if
A\in\Psi_{\text{\rm b}}^{-\infty}(M)
and
\kappa:=\kappa_{A}
is supported in the domain of a chart
(x,s,y,y^{\prime})
, then we can write
\kappa=\kappa^{\prime}(x,s,y,y^{\prime})\,s^{-1}ds\,dy^{\prime}
because
\kappa
is rapidly decreasing as
s\downarrow 0
and as
s\uparrow+\infty
, obtaining

(2.5.61)

Au(x,y)=\int_{\partial M}\int_{0}^{\infty}\kappa^{\prime}(x,s,y,y^{\prime})u\Big(\frac{x}{s},y^{\prime}\Big)\frac{ds}{s}\,dy^{\prime}\;,

for all
u\in\dot{C}^{\infty}(M)
supported in the domain of the chart
(x,y)
.

Any
A\in\Psi_{\text{\rm b}}^{m}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})
defines continuous endomorphisms
A
of
\dot{C}^{\infty}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})
and
C^{\pm\infty}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})

[
Mel93
, Propositions 4.29 and 4.34 and Exercise 4.33]
. In this sense,
\Psi_{\text{\rm b}}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})
becomes a filtered algebra with the operation of composition
[
Mel93
, Propositions 5.20]
, and the principal b-symbol map is multiplicative.

2.5.28.
The indicial family

Let
A\in\Psi_{\text{\rm b}}^{m}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})
(
m\in\mathbb{R}
) and write
\kappa=\kappa_{A}
. Roughly speaking, the
indicial family
of
A
is an entire family,
I_{\nu}(A,\lambda)\in\Psi^{m}(\partial M;\Omega^{1/2})
(
\lambda\in\mathbb{C})
, depending on the trivialization
\nu
of
{}_{+}N\partial M
(
Section

2.5.1
), defined by taking the “fiberwise” Mellin transform of certain conormal distributional section defined by
\kappa|_{\operatorname{ff}}
. Thus
I_{\nu}(A,\lambda)=0
for all
\lambda
just when
\kappa|_{\operatorname{ff}}=0
.

The indicial family can be also described as follows. For
z\in\mathbb{C}
and
m\in\mathbb{R}
, the mapping
A\mapsto x^{-z}Ax^{z}
defines an automorphism of
\Psi_{\text{\rm b}}^{m}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})

[
Mel93
, Proposition 5.7]
. Hence every
A\in\Psi_{\text{\rm b}}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})
defines a continuous endomorphism
A
of
x^{k}C^{\infty}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})
(
k\in\mathbb{N}
). Therefore a continuous endomorphism
A_{\partial}
of
C^{\infty}(\partial M;\Omega^{1/2})
is well defined by
A_{\partial}v=Au|_{\partial M}
if
u\in C^{\infty}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})
with
u|_{\partial M}=v

[
Mel93
, Eq. (5.31)]
. Then
[
Mel93
, Proposition 5.8]

(2.5.62)

I_{\nu}(A,\lambda)=(x^{-i\lambda}Ax^{i\lambda})_{\partial}\;.

We will only use the indicial family in the following cases, where
{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2}
is omitted for the sake of simplicity. First, if
A\in\Psi_{\text{\rm b}}^{-\infty}(M)
and
\kappa
is supported in the domain of a chart
(x,s,y,y^{\prime})
, as described in (
2.5.61
), then
I_{\nu}(A,\lambda)\in\Psi_{\text{\rm b}}^{-\infty}(\partial M;\Omega^{1/2})
is given by

(2.5.63)

K_{I_{\nu}(A,\lambda)}(y,y^{\prime})=\int_{0}^{\infty}s^{-i\lambda}\kappa^{\prime}(0,s,y,y^{\prime})\frac{ds}{s}\;.

The support condition can be obviously removed by using a partition of unity or a collar neighborhood of
\partial M
. Second, if
A\in\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}^{m}(M)
(
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
) is locally given by

A=\sum_{j+|I|\leq m}a_{j,I}(x,y)(xD_{x})^{j}D^{I}_{y}\;,

using adapted local coordinates
(x,y)
, then

(2.5.64)

I_{\nu}(A,\lambda)=\sum_{j+|I|\leq m}a_{j,I}(0,y)\lambda^{j}D^{I}_{y}\;.

The indicial family is multiplicative
[
Mel93
, Corollary of Proposition 5.20]
, and compatible with the operation of taking formal adjoints of b-differential operators (Cf.
[
Mel93
, Eq. (4.112)]
).

2.5.29.
The b-integral

The
b-integral
is a linear map
\!\!\!{\phantom{\int}}{}^{\nu}\!\!\!\int=\!\!\!{\phantom{\int}}^{\nu}\!\!\!\int_{M}:C^{1}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega)\to\mathbb{C}
, depending on
\nu
, defined by
[
Mel93
, Lemma 4.62]

\!\!\!\!\!{\phantom{\int}}{}^{\nu}\!\!\!\!\int u=\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\left(\int_{x\geq\epsilon}u+\ln\epsilon\int_{\partial M}u|_{L}\right)\;,

using a boundary-defining function
x
with
dx(\nu)=1
. Another trivialization
\mu\in C^{\infty}(M;{}_{+}N\partial M)
is of the form
\mu=a\nu
for some
0<a\in C^{\infty}(\partial M)
, and

\!\!\!\!\!{\phantom{\int}}{}^{\mu}\!\!\!\!\int u-\!\!\!\!\!{\phantom{\int}}^{\nu}\!\!\!\!\int u=\int_{\partial M}\ln a\cdot u|_{\partial M}\;.

Lemma 2.5.9
.

\!\!\!{\phantom{\int}}{}^{\nu}\!\!\!\int
is continuous with the
C^{1}
topology on
C^{1}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega)
.

Proof.

Consider a chart
(V,y)
of
\partial M
, and the adapted local coordinates
(x,y)
on
U\equiv[0,\epsilon_{0})_{x}\times V\subset M
(
\epsilon_{0}>0
). Since
\!\!\!{\phantom{\int}}{}^{\nu}\!\!\!\int\equiv\int_{M}
on
C^{1}_{\text{\rm c}}(\mathring{M};{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega)\equiv C^{1}_{\text{\rm c}}(\mathring{M};\Omega)
, it is easy to see that it is enough to prove the continuity of
\!\!\!{\phantom{\int}}{}^{\nu}\!\!\!\int
on
C^{1}_{\text{\rm c}}(U;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega)
. Every
u\in C^{1}_{\text{\rm c}}(U;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega)
is of the form
u(x,y)=a(x,y)\,|x^{-1}dx\,dy|
for some
a\in C^{1}_{\text{\rm c}}(U)
. Then

\!\!\!\!\!{\phantom{\int}}{}^{\nu}\!\!\!\!\int u=\ln\epsilon_{0}\cdot\int_{V}a(0,y)\,dy+\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\left(\int_{V}\int_{\epsilon}^{\epsilon_{0}}(a(x,y)-a(0,y))\,\frac{dx}{x}\,dy\right)\;.

Hence

\left|\!\!\!\!\!{\phantom{\int}}{}^{\nu}\!\!\!\!\int u\right|\leq\operatorname{vol}V\cdot\Big(\ln\epsilon_{0}\cdot\max_{y\in V}|a(0,y)|+\epsilon_{0}\cdot\max_{(\xi,y)\in U}|\partial_{x}a(\xi,y)|\Big)\;.\qed

Corollary 2.5.10
.

Let
T
and
T^{\prime}
be collar neighborhoods of
\partial M
in
M
with
\overline{T^{\prime}}\subset T
. For any sequence
u_{k}
in
C^{1}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega)
, if
u_{k}|_{T}\to 0
in
C^{1}(T;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega)
and
\lim_{k}\int_{M\setminus T^{\prime}}u_{k}=a\in\mathbb{C}
, then
\lim_{k}\!\!\!{\phantom{\int}}^{\nu}\!\!\!\int u_{k}=a
.

Proof.

Let
\{\lambda,\mu\}
be a smooth partition of unity of
M
subordinated to the open covering
\{T,M\setminus\overline{T^{\prime}}\}
. Then
\lim_{k}\lambda u_{k}=0
in
C^{1}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega)
, obtaining
\lim_{k}\!\!\!{\phantom{\int}}^{\nu}\!\!\!\int\lambda u_{k}=0
by
2.5.9
. Moreover
\lim_{k}\int_{M\setminus T^{\prime}}\lambda u_{k}=0
. Therefore

\lim_{k}\!\!\!\!\!{\phantom{\int}}^{\nu}\!\!\!\!\int u_{k}=\lim_{k}\!\!\!\!\!{\phantom{\int}}^{\nu}\!\!\!\!\int\mu u_{k}=\lim_{k}\int_{M\setminus T^{\prime}}\mu u_{k}=\lim_{k}\int_{M\setminus T^{\prime}}u_{k}=a\;.\qed

Remark 2.5.11
.

Consider a collar neighborhood of
\partial M
in
M
of the form
T\equiv[0,\epsilon)_{x}\times\partial M_{\varpi}
, and the intermediate space

\displaystyle C^{1}(T;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega)

\displaystyle\equiv C^{1}([0,\epsilon),C^{1}(\partial M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega_{\partial M}))

\displaystyle\subset C^{0,1}_{\varpi}(T;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega):=C^{1}([0,\epsilon),C^{0}(\partial M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega_{\partial M}))

\displaystyle\subset C^{0}(T;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega)\equiv C^{0}([0,\epsilon),C^{0}(\partial M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega_{\partial M}))\;.

Then
\!\!\!{\phantom{\int}}{}^{\nu}\!\!\!\int_{M}
is actually defined on

\{\,u\in C^{0}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega)\mid u|_{T}\in C^{0,1}_{\varpi}(T;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega)\,\}\;,

and the proof of
2.5.9
shows that it is continuous with the obvious topology defined by the topologies of
C^{0}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega)
and
C^{0,1}_{\varpi}(T;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega)
. So Corollary
2.5.10
is true with the weaker condition
u_{k}|_{T}\to 0
in
C^{0,1}_{\varpi}(T;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega)
.

2.5.30.
The b-trace

Any
A\in\Psi_{\text{\rm b}}^{-\infty}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})
is of trace class if and only if
A\in r\Psi_{\text{\rm b}}^{-\infty}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})
(i.e.,
\kappa_{A}|_{\operatorname{ff}}=0
). The
b-trace

{}^{\text{\rm b}}\!\operatorname{Tr}:\Psi_{\text{\rm b}}^{-\infty}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})\to\mathbb{C}
is an extension of the trace
\operatorname{Tr}:r\Psi_{\text{\rm b}}^{-\infty}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})\to\mathbb{C}
given by

{}^{\text{\rm b}}\!\operatorname{Tr}A=\!\!\!\!\!{\phantom{\int}}^{\nu}\!\!\!\!\int_{M}\kappa_{A}|_{\Delta_{\text{\rm b}}}\;,

using the canonical isomorphism
{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2}(M^{2}_{\text{\rm b}})|_{\Delta_{\text{\rm b}}}\cong{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega M
(
Section

2.5.26
). If
A,B\in\Psi^{-\infty}_{\text{\rm b}}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})
, then
[
Mel93
, Proposition 5.9]

(2.5.65)

{}^{\text{\rm b}}\!\operatorname{Tr}[A,B]=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\operatorname{Tr}(\partial_{\lambda}I_{\nu}(A,\lambda)\,I_{\nu}(B,\lambda))\,d\lambda\;.

This equality also holds if
A\in\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})
and
B\in\Psi_{\text{\rm b}}^{-\infty}(M;{}^{\text{\rm b}}\Omega^{1/2})

[
Mel93
, Lemma 5.10]
.

If
E
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded Hermitian vector bundle over
M
with degree involution
\mathsf{w}
(
\mathsf{w}u=(-1)^{k}u
for
u\in E^{k}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
), and
A\in\Psi^{-\infty}_{\text{\rm b}}(M;E)
is homogeneous of degree zero, then its
b-supertrace
is
{}^{\text{\rm b}}\!\operatorname{Str}A={}^{\text{\rm b}}\!\operatorname{Tr}(A\mathsf{w})
. This notion extends the supertrace
\operatorname{Str}(B)
of any homogeneous operator
B\in r\Psi^{-\infty}_{\text{\rm b}}(M;E)
of degree zero.

2.6.
Conormal sequence

In this section and the next one, for the sake of simplicity, we only consider submanifolds of codimension one because that is the only case we use. However, the results can be extended to submanifolds of arbitrary codimension with more work.

2.6.1.
Cutting along a submanifold

Again, for brevity reasons, we consider only the case of a closed manifold and the trivial line bundle. Like in other sections, the spaces of distributions we are going to define have obvious extensions to non-compact manifolds and arbitrary vector bundles, taking compact support or no support conditions, and taking regular submanifolds that are closed subspaces. We will consider those types of extensions without further comment.

Let
M
be a closed connected manifold, and
L\subset M
be a (possibly non-connected) regular closed submanifold of codimension one.
M\setminus L
may have several connected components. First assume also that
L
is transversely oriented. Then, like in the case with boundary (
Section

2.5.1
), there is some real-valued smooth function
x
on some tubular neighborhood
T
of
L
in
M
, with projection
\varpi:T\to L
, so that
L=\{x=0\}
and
dx\neq 0
on
L
. Any function
x
satisfying these conditions is called a
defining function
of
L
on
T
. We can suppose
T\equiv(-\epsilon,\epsilon)_{x}\times L_{\varpi}
, for some
\epsilon>0
. (If
M
and
L
were not compact, and
L
were a regular submanifold that is a closed subset, then the tubular neighborhood would have a more involved expression, using a smooth positive function
\epsilon(y)
on
L
instead of a fixed positive number
\epsilon
.) For any atlas
\{V_{j},y_{j}\}
of
L
, we get an atlas of
T
of the form
\{U_{j}\equiv(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\times V_{j},(x,y)\}
, whose charts are adapted to
L
. The corresponding local vector fields
\partial_{x}\in\mathfrak{X}(U_{j})
can be combined to define a vector field
\partial_{x}\in\mathfrak{X}(T)
; we can consider
\partial_{x}
as the derivative operator on
C^{\infty}(T)\equiv C^{\infty}((-\epsilon,\epsilon),C^{\infty}(L))
. For every
j
,
\operatorname{Diff}(U_{j},L\cap U_{j})
is spanned by
x\partial_{x},\partial_{j}^{1},\dots,\partial_{j}^{n-1}
using the operations of
C^{\infty}(U_{j})
-module and algebra, where
\partial_{j}^{k}=\partial/\partial y_{j}^{k}
. Using
T\equiv(-\epsilon,\epsilon)_{x}\times L
, any
A\in\operatorname{Diff}(L)
induces an operator
1\otimes A\in\operatorname{Diff}(T,L)
, such that
(1\otimes A)(u(x)v(y))=u(x)\,(Av)(y)
for
u\in C^{\infty}(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
and
v\in C^{\infty}(L)
. This defines a canonical injection
\operatorname{Diff}(L)\equiv 1\otimes\operatorname{Diff}(L)\subset\operatorname{Diff}(T,L)
so that
(1\otimes A)|_{L}=A
. (This also shows the surjectivity of (
2.2.2
) in this case.) Moreover
\operatorname{Diff}(T)
(resp.,
\operatorname{Diff}(T,L)
) is spanned by
\partial_{x}
(resp.,
x\partial_{x}
) and
1\otimes\operatorname{Diff}(L)
using the operations of
C^{\infty}(T)
-module and algebra. Clearly,

(2.6.1)

[\partial_{x},1\otimes\operatorname{Diff}(L)]=0\;,\quad[\partial_{x},x\partial_{x}]=\partial_{x}\;,

yielding

(2.6.2)

[\partial_{x},\operatorname{Diff}^{k}(T,L)]\subset\operatorname{Diff}^{k}(T,L)+\operatorname{Diff}^{k-1}(T,L)\,\partial_{x}\;.

\operatorname{Diff}^{k}(T,L)
and
\operatorname{Diff}^{k}(T)
satisfy the obvious versions of (
2.5.26
) and (
2.5.27
).

For a vector bundle
E
over
M
, there is an identity
E_{T}\equiv(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\times E_{L}
over
T\equiv(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\times L
, which can be used to define
\partial_{x}\in\operatorname{Diff}^{1}(T;E)
. With this interpretation of
\partial_{x}
and using tensor products like in (
2.1.6
), the vector bundle versions of the concepts and properties of this section are straightforward.

Let
\boldsymbol{M}
be the smooth manifold with boundary defined by “cutting”
M
along
L
; i.e., modifying
M
only on the tubular neighborhood
T\equiv(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\times L
, which is replaced with
\boldsymbol{T}=((-\epsilon,0]\sqcup[0,\epsilon))\times L
in the obvious way. (
\boldsymbol{M}
is the blowing-up
[M,L]
of
M
along
L

[
Mel96
, Chapter 5]
.) Thus
\partial\boldsymbol{M}\equiv L\sqcup L
because
L
is transversely oriented, and
\mathring{\boldsymbol{M}}\equiv M\setminus L
. A canonical projection
\boldsymbol{\pi}:\boldsymbol{M}\to M
is defined as the combination of the identity map
\mathring{\boldsymbol{M}}\to M\setminus L
and the map
\boldsymbol{T}\to T
given by the product of the canonical projection
(-\epsilon,0]\sqcup[0,\epsilon)\to(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
and
\operatorname{id}_{L}
. This projection realizes
M
as a quotient space of
\boldsymbol{M}
by the equivalence relation defined by the homeomorphism
h\equiv h_{0}\times\operatorname{id}
of
\partial\boldsymbol{M}\equiv\partial\boldsymbol{T}=(\{0\}\sqcup\{0\})\times L
, where
h_{0}
switches the two points of
\{0\}\sqcup\{0\}
. Moreover
\boldsymbol{\pi}:\boldsymbol{M}\to M
is a local embedding of a compact manifold with boundary to a closed manifold of the same dimension.

Like in
Section

2.1.6
, we have the continuous linear pull-back map

(2.6.3)

\boldsymbol{\pi}^{*}:C^{\infty}(M)\to C^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{M})\;,

which is clearly injective. The transpose of the version of (
2.6.3
) with
\Omega M
and
\Omega\boldsymbol{M}\equiv\boldsymbol{\pi}^{*}\Omega M
is the continuous linear push-forward map

(2.6.4)

\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}:\dot{C}^{-\infty}(\boldsymbol{M})\to C^{-\infty}(M)\;,

which is a surjective topological homomorphism
[
ÁLKL23
, Proposition 7.4]
.

After distinguishing a connected component
L_{0}
of
L
, let
\widetilde{M}
and
\widetilde{L}
be the quotients of
\boldsymbol{M}\sqcup\boldsymbol{M}\equiv\boldsymbol{M}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
\partial\boldsymbol{M}\sqcup\partial\boldsymbol{M}\equiv\partial\boldsymbol{M}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
by the equivalence relation generated by
(p,a)\sim(h(p),a)
if
\boldsymbol{\pi}(p)\in L\setminus L_{0}
and
(p,a)\sim(h(p),a+1)
if
\boldsymbol{\pi}(p)\in L_{0}
(
p\in\boldsymbol{\pi}^{-1}(L)=\partial\boldsymbol{M}
in both cases). Let us remark that
\widetilde{M}
may not be homeomorphic to the double of
\boldsymbol{M}
, which is the quotient of
\boldsymbol{M}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
by the equivalence relation generated by
(p,0)\sim(p,1)
, for
p\in\partial\boldsymbol{M}
. Note that
\widetilde{M}
is a closed connected manifold and
\widetilde{L}
is a closed regular submanifold. Moreover the quotient
\widetilde{T}
of
\boldsymbol{T}\sqcup\boldsymbol{T}
becomes a tubular neighborhood of
\widetilde{L}
in
\widetilde{M}
. The combination
\boldsymbol{\pi}\sqcup\boldsymbol{\pi}:\boldsymbol{M}\sqcup\boldsymbol{M}\to M
induces a two-fold covering map
\tilde{\pi}:\widetilde{M}\to M
, whose restrictions to
\widetilde{L}
and
\widetilde{T}
are trivial two-fold coverings of
L
and
T
, respectively; i.e.,
\widetilde{L}\equiv L\sqcup L
and
\widetilde{T}\equiv T\sqcup T
. The group of deck transformations of
\tilde{\pi}:\widetilde{M}\to M
is
\{\operatorname{id},\sigma\}
, where
\sigma:\widetilde{M}\to\widetilde{M}
is induced by the map
\sigma_{0}:\boldsymbol{M}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}\to\boldsymbol{M}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
defined by switching the elements of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. The composition of the injection
\boldsymbol{M}\to\boldsymbol{M}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
,
p\mapsto(p,0)
, with the quotient map
\boldsymbol{M}\sqcup\boldsymbol{M}\to\widetilde{M}
is a smooth embedding
\boldsymbol{M}\to\widetilde{M}
. This will be considered as an inclusion map of a regular submanifold with boundary, obtaining
\partial\boldsymbol{M}\equiv\widetilde{L}
.

Since
\tilde{\pi}
is a two-fold covering map, we have continuous linear maps (
Section

2.1.6
)

\displaystyle\tilde{\pi}_{*}:C^{\infty}(\widetilde{M})\to C^{\infty}(M)\;,\quad\tilde{\pi}^{*}:C^{\infty}(M)\to C^{\infty}(\widetilde{M})\;,

(2.6.5)

\displaystyle\tilde{\pi}^{*}:C^{-\infty}(M)\to C^{-\infty}(\widetilde{M})\;,\quad\tilde{\pi}_{*}:C^{-\infty}(\widetilde{M})\to C^{-\infty}(M)\;,

both pairs of maps satisfying

(2.6.6)

\tilde{\pi}_{*}\tilde{\pi}^{*}=2\;,\quad\tilde{\pi}^{*}\tilde{\pi}_{*}=A_{\sigma}\;,

where
A_{\sigma}:C^{\pm\infty}(\widetilde{M})\to C^{\pm\infty}(\widetilde{M})
is given by
A_{\sigma}u=u+\sigma_{*}u
. Using the continuous linear restriction and inclusion maps given by (
2.5.2
) and (
2.5.7
), we get the commutative diagrams

(2.6.7)

\begin{CD}C^{\infty}(\widetilde{M})@>{R}>{}>C^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{M})\\
@A{\tilde{\pi}^{*}}A{}A@A{}A{\boldsymbol{\pi}^{*}}A\\
C^{\infty}(M)=C^{\infty}(M)\;,\hskip-5.69046pt\end{CD}\qquad\begin{CD}\dot{C}^{-\infty}(\boldsymbol{M})@>{\iota}>{}>C^{-\infty}(\widetilde{M})\\
@V{\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}}V{}V@V{}V{\tilde{\pi}_{*}}V\\
C^{-\infty}(M)=C^{-\infty}(M)\;,\hskip-5.69046pt\end{CD}

the second one is the transpose of the density-bundles version of the first one.

2.6.2.
Lift of differential operators from
M
to
\widetilde{M}

For any
A\in\operatorname{Diff}(M)
, let
\widetilde{A}\in\operatorname{Diff}(\widetilde{M})
denote its lift via the covering map
\tilde{\pi}:\widetilde{M}\to M
. The action of
\widetilde{A}
on
C^{\pm\infty}(\widetilde{M})
corresponds to the action of
A
on
C^{\pm\infty}(M)
via
\tilde{\pi}^{*}:C^{\pm\infty}(M)\to C^{\pm\infty}(\widetilde{M})
and
\tilde{\pi}_{*}:C^{\pm\infty}(\widetilde{M})\to C^{\pm\infty}(M)
. According to (
2.5.22
),
\widetilde{A}|_{\boldsymbol{M}}\in\operatorname{Diff}(\boldsymbol{M})
is the lift of
A
via the local embedding
\boldsymbol{\pi}:\boldsymbol{M}\to M
, sometimes also denoted by
\widetilde{A}
. The action of
\widetilde{A}
on
C^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{M})
(resp.,
C^{-\infty}(\boldsymbol{M})
) corresponds to the action of
A
on
C^{\infty}(M)
(resp.,
C^{-\infty}(M)
) via
\boldsymbol{\pi}^{*}:C^{\infty}(M)\to C^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{M})
(resp.,
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}:C^{-\infty}(\boldsymbol{M})\to C^{-\infty}(M)
). If
A\in\operatorname{Diff}(M,L)
, then
\widetilde{A}\in\operatorname{Diff}(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{L})
and
\widetilde{A}|_{\boldsymbol{M}}\in\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}(\boldsymbol{M})
by (
2.5.25
).

2.6.3.
The spaces
C^{\pm\infty}(M,L)

Consider the closed subspaces,

(2.6.8)

C^{\infty}(M,L)\subset C^{\infty}(M)\;,\quad C^{k}(M,L)\subset C^{k}(M)\quad(k\in\mathbb{N}_{0})\;,

consisting of functions that vanish to all orders at the points of
L
in the first case, and that vanish up to order
k
at the points of
L
in the second case. Then let

C^{-\infty}(M,L)=C^{\infty}(M,L;\Omega)^{\prime}\;,\quad C^{\prime\,-k}(M,L)=C^{k}(M,L;\Omega)^{\prime}\;.

C^{-\infty}(M,L)
is a barreled, ultrabornological, webbed, acyclic DF Montel space, and therefore complete, boundedly/compactly/sequentially retractive and reflexive
[
ÁLKL23
, Corollary 7.1]
. Note that (
2.6.3
) restricts to TVS-isomorphisms

(2.6.9)

\boldsymbol{\pi}^{*}:C^{\infty}(M,L)\xrightarrow{\cong}\dot{C}^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{M})\;,\quad\boldsymbol{\pi}^{*}:C^{k}(M,L)\xrightarrow{\cong}\dot{C}^{k}(\boldsymbol{M})\;.

Taking the transposes of its versions with density bundles, it follows that (
2.6.4
) restricts to TVS-isomorphisms

(2.6.10)

\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}:C^{-\infty}(\boldsymbol{M})\xrightarrow{\cong}C^{-\infty}(M,L)\;,\quad\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}:C^{\prime\,-k}(\boldsymbol{M})\xrightarrow{\cong}C^{\prime\,-k}(M,L)\;.

So the spaces
C^{\infty}(M,L)
,
C^{k}(M,L)
,
C^{-\infty}(M,L)
and
C^{\prime\,-k}(M,L)
satisfy the analogs of (
2.1.7
) and (
2.1.8
).

On the other hand, there are Hilbertian spaces
H^{r}(M,L)
(
r>n/2
) and
H^{\prime\,s}(M,L)
(
s\in\mathbb{R}
), continuously included in
C^{0}(M,L)
and
C^{-\infty}(M,L)
, resp., such that the second map of (
2.6.9
) for
k=0
and the first map of (
2.6.10
) restrict to a TVS-isomorphisms

(2.6.11)

\boldsymbol{\pi}^{*}:H^{r}(M,L)\xrightarrow{\cong}\dot{H}^{r}(\boldsymbol{M})\;,\quad\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}:H^{s}(\boldsymbol{M})\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{\prime\,s}(M,L)\;.

For
s=0
, the second TVS-isomorphism of (
2.6.11
) becomes

(2.6.12)

\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}:L^{2}(\boldsymbol{M})\xrightarrow{\cong}L^{2}(M)\;.

By (
2.5.16
),

(2.6.13)

H^{\prime\,-r}(M,L)\equiv H^{r}(M,L;\Omega)^{\prime}\;,\quad H^{r}(M,L)\equiv H^{\prime\,-r}(M,L;\Omega)^{\prime}\;.

Now, the second identity of (
2.6.13
) can be used to extend the definition of
H^{r}(M,L)
for all
r\in\mathbb{R}
.

Alternatively, we may also use trace theorems
[
Ada75
, Theorem 7.53 and 7.58]
to define
H^{m}(M,L)
for
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, and then use the first identity of (
2.6.13
) to define
H^{\prime\,-m}(M,L)
.

From (
2.6.3
), (
2.6.4
), (
2.6.11
) and the analogs of (
2.1.32
)–(
2.1.34
) mentioned in
Section

2.5.4
, we get

(2.6.14)

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(M,L)

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{r}H^{r}(M,L)\;,

\displaystyle\quad C^{-\infty}(M,L)

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{s}H^{\prime\,s}(M,L)\;,

as well as a continuous inclusion and a continuous linear surjection,

(2.6.15)

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(M)

\displaystyle\subset\bigcap_{s}H^{\prime\,s}(M,L)\;,

\displaystyle\quad C^{-\infty}(M)

\displaystyle\leftarrow\bigcup_{r}H^{r}(M,L)\;.

By (
2.6.13
) and (
2.6.14
),

(2.6.16)

C^{\infty}(M,L)=C^{-\infty}(M,L;\Omega)^{\prime}\;.

The transpose of the version of the first inclusion of (
2.6.8
) with
\Omega M
is a surjective topological homomorphism
[
ÁLKL23
, Proposition 7.4]

(2.6.17)

R:C^{-\infty}(M)\to C^{-\infty}(M,L)\;,

whose restriction to
C^{\infty}(M)
is the identity. It can be also described as the composition

C^{-\infty}(M)\xrightarrow{\tilde{\pi}^{*}}C^{-\infty}(\widetilde{M})\xrightarrow{R}C^{-\infty}(\boldsymbol{M})\xrightarrow{\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}}C^{-\infty}(M,L)\;.

The canonical pairing between
C^{\infty}(M)
and
C^{\infty}(M,L;\Omega)
defines a continuous dense inclusion

(2.6.18)

C^{\infty}(M)\subset C^{-\infty}(M,L)

such that (
2.6.17
) is the identity on
C^{\infty}(M)
. We also get commutative diagrams

(2.6.19)

\begin{CD}C^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{M})@<{\iota}<{}<\dot{C}^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{M})\\
@A{\boldsymbol{\pi}^{*}}A{}A@A{\cong}A{\boldsymbol{\pi}^{*}}A\\
C^{\infty}(M)@<{\iota}<{}<C^{\infty}(M,L)\;,\hskip-5.69046pt\end{CD}\qquad\begin{CD}\dot{C}^{-\infty}(\boldsymbol{M})@>{R}>{}>C^{-\infty}(\boldsymbol{M})\\
@V{\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}}V{}V@V{\cong}V{\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}}V\\
C^{-\infty}(M)@>{R}>{}>C^{-\infty}(M,L)\;,\hskip-5.69046pt\end{CD}

the second one is the transpose of the density-bundles version of the first one.

2.6.4.
The space
C^{-\infty}_{L}(M)

The condition of being supported in
L
define closed subspaces,

C^{-\infty}_{L}(M)\subset C^{-\infty}(M)\;,\quad C^{\prime\,-k}_{L}(M)\subset C^{\prime\,-k}(M)\;,\quad H^{s}_{L}(M)\subset H^{s}(M)\;,

which are the null spaces of restrictions of (
2.6.17
). These spaces satisfy continuous inclusions analogous to (
2.1.7
), (
2.1.31
) and (
2.1.33
). The following properties hold
[
ÁLKL23
, Corollaries 7.2 and 7.3]
:
C^{-\infty}_{L}(M)
is a limit subspace of the LF-space
C^{-\infty}(M)
; and it is a barreled, ultrabornological, webbed, acyclic DF Montel space, and therefore complete, boundedly/compactly/sequentially retractive and reflexive.

According to (
2.5.19
) and
Section

2.6.1
, we have
[
ÁLKL23
, Eq. (7.19)]

(2.6.20)

\dot{C}^{-\infty}_{\partial\boldsymbol{M}}(\boldsymbol{M})\equiv C^{-\infty}_{L}(M)\oplus C^{-\infty}_{L}(M)\;,

The maps (
2.6.4
) and (
2.6.5
) have restrictions

(2.6.21)

\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}=\tilde{\pi}_{*}:\dot{C}^{-\infty}_{\partial\boldsymbol{M}}(\boldsymbol{M})\to C^{-\infty}_{L}(M)\;,\quad\tilde{\pi}^{*}:C^{-\infty}_{L}(M)\to\dot{C}^{-\infty}_{\partial\boldsymbol{M}}(\boldsymbol{M})\;.

Using (
2.6.20
), these maps are given by
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}(u,v)=u+v
and
\tilde{\pi}^{*}u=(u,u)
.

Moreover the right-hand side diagram of (
2.6.19
) can be completed to get the commutative diagram

(2.6.22)

\begin{CD}0\to\dot{C}^{-\infty}_{\partial\boldsymbol{M}}(\boldsymbol{M})@>{\iota}>{}>\dot{C}^{-\infty}(\boldsymbol{M})@>{R}>{}>C^{-\infty}(\boldsymbol{M})\to 0\\
@V{\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}}V{}V@V{\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}}V{}V@V{\cong}V{\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}}V\\
0\to C^{-\infty}_{L}(M)@>{\iota}>{}>C^{-\infty}(M)@>{R}>{}>C^{-\infty}(M,L)\to 0\;.\hskip-5.69046pt\end{CD}

The bottom row of this diagram is exact in the category of continuous linear maps between LCSs by the properties of (
2.6.17
).

2.6.5.
A description of
C^{-\infty}_{L}(M)

According to (
2.2.10
) and
Sections

2.1.7
and
2.6.1
, we have TVS-isomorphisms

(2.6.23)

\partial_{x}^{m}:C^{-\infty}(L;\Omega^{-1}NL)\xrightarrow{\cong}\partial_{x}^{m}C^{-\infty}(L;\Omega^{-1}NL)\subset C^{-\infty}_{L}(M)\;,

for
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, inducing TVS-isomorphisms
[
ÁLKL23
, Proposition 7.7]

(2.6.24)

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{m=0}^{\infty}C^{-\infty}(L;\Omega^{-1}NL)\xrightarrow{\cong}C^{-\infty}_{L}(M)\;,

(2.6.25)

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{m=0}^{k}C^{m-k}(L;\Omega^{-1}NL)\xrightarrow{\cong}C^{\prime\,-k}_{L}(M)\quad(k\in\mathbb{N}_{0})\;.

Remark 2.6.1

(See
[
Mel96
, Exercise 3.3.18]
)
.

In
Section

2.5.5
, for any compact manifold with boundary
M
, the analogs of (
2.6.24
) and (
2.6.25
) for
\dot{C}^{-\infty}_{\partial M}(M)
follows from their application to
C^{-\infty}_{\partial M}(\breve{M})
.

2.6.6.
Action of
\operatorname{Diff}(M)
on
C^{-\infty}(M,L)
and
C^{-\infty}_{L}(M)

For every
A\in\operatorname{Diff}(M)
,
A^{\text{\rm t}}
preserves
C^{\infty}(M,L;\Omega)
, and therefore
A
induces a continuous linear map
A=A^{tt}
on
C^{-\infty}(M,L)
. By locality, it restricts to a continuous endomorphism
A
of
C^{-\infty}_{L}(M)
.

2.6.7.
The space
J(M,L)

According to
Sections

2.5.8
and
2.6.3
, there is a LCHS
J(M,L)
, with a dense continuous inclusion

(2.6.26)

J(M,L)\subset C^{-\infty}(M,L)\;,

so that (
2.6.10
) restricts to a TVS-isomorphism

(2.6.27)

\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}:\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{M})\xrightarrow{\cong}J(M,L)\;,

where
\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{M})
is defined in (
2.5.28
). By (
2.5.32
), there is a continuous inclusion

J(M,L)\subset C^{\infty}(M\setminus L)\;.

We also get spaces
J^{(s)}(M,L)
and
J^{m}(M,L)
(
s,m\in\mathbb{R}
) corresponding to
\mathcal{A}^{(s)}(\boldsymbol{M})
and
\mathcal{A}^{m}(\boldsymbol{M})
via (
2.6.27
). Let
\boldsymbol{x}
be an extension of
|x|
to
M
so that it is positive and smooth on
M\setminus L
. Its lift
\boldsymbol{\pi}^{*}\boldsymbol{x}
is a boundary-defining function of
\boldsymbol{M}
, also denoted by
\boldsymbol{x}
. Using the first map of (
2.6.10
) and second map of (
2.6.11
), and according to
Section

2.6.2
, we can also describe

(2.6.28)

\displaystyle J^{(s)}(M,L)

\displaystyle=\{\,u\in C^{-\infty}(M,L)\mid\operatorname{Diff}(M,L)\,u\subset H^{\prime\,s}(M,L)\,\}\;,

\displaystyle J^{m}(M,L)

\displaystyle=\{\,u\in C^{-\infty}(M,L)\mid\operatorname{Diff}(M,L)\,u\subset\boldsymbol{x}^{m}L^{\infty}(M)\,\}\;,

with topologies like in (
2.1.1
). These spaces satisfy the analogs of (
2.2.4
), (
2.5.28
) and (
2.5.36
)–(
2.5.38
). Using (
2.6.15
) and (
2.6.28
), we get a continuous dense inclusion
[
ÁLKL23
, Corollary 7.14]

(2.6.29)

C^{\infty}(M)\subset J(M,L)\;.

In fact,
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(M\setminus L)
is dense in every
J^{(s)}(M,L)
and
J^{m}(M,L)
, and therefore in
J(M,L)

[
ÁLKL23
, Corollaries 7.14 and 7.17 and the analog of Remark 6.41 for
J(M,L)
]
. Moreover the following properties hold
[
ÁLKL23
, Corollaries 7.11–7.13 and 7.15]
: every
J^{(s)}(M,L)
is a totally reflexive Fréchet space;
J(M,L)
is barreled, ultrabornological, webbed and an acyclic Montel space, and therefore complete, boundedly/compactly/sequentially retractive and reflexive; and the topologies of
J(M,L)
and
C^{\infty}(M\setminus L)
coincide on every
J^{m}(M,L)
.

2.6.8.
A description of
J(M,L)

Take a b-metric
\boldsymbol{g}
on
\boldsymbol{M}
satisfying
1
and
2
(
Section

2.5.20
), and consider its restriction to
\mathring{\boldsymbol{M}}
. Consider also the boundary-defining function
\boldsymbol{x}
of
\boldsymbol{M}
(
Section

2.6.7
). Taking
m\in\mathbb{R}
, we have TVS-isomorphisms
[
ÁLKL23
, Corollaries 7.16 and 7.18]

(2.6.30)

\displaystyle J^{m}(M,L)\cong\boldsymbol{x}^{m}H_{\text{\rm b}}^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{M})\equiv\boldsymbol{x}^{m+1/2}H^{\infty}(\mathring{\boldsymbol{M}})\;,

(2.6.31)

\displaystyle J(M,L)\cong\bigcup_{m}\boldsymbol{x}^{m}H_{\text{\rm b}}^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{M})=\bigcup_{m}\boldsymbol{x}^{m}H^{\infty}(\mathring{\boldsymbol{M}})\;.

The first isomorphisms of (
2.6.30
) and (
2.6.31
) are independent of
\boldsymbol{g}
; thus they hold without assuming
1
and
2

[
ÁLKL23
, the analog of Remark 6.41 for
J(M,L)
]
.

2.6.9.
I(M,L)
vs
\dot{\mathcal{A}}(\boldsymbol{M})
and
J(M,L)

According to
Sections

2.2.8
and
2.2.9
, we have the continuous linear maps

(2.6.32)

\tilde{\pi}^{*}:I(M,L)\to I(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{L})\;,\quad\tilde{\pi}_{*}:I(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{L})\to I(M,L)\;,

which are restrictions of the maps (
2.6.5
), and therefore they satisfy (
2.6.6
). These maps are compatible with the symbol and Sobolev filtrations because
\tilde{\pi}:\widetilde{M}\to M
is a covering map (
Sections

2.2.8
and
2.2.9
).

Since (
2.5.41
) gives a TVS-embedding
\dot{\mathcal{A}}(\boldsymbol{M})\subset I(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{L})
, which preserves the Sobolev-order and symbol-order filtrations, the map
\tilde{\pi}_{*}
of (
2.6.32
) has the restriction

(2.6.33)

\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}:\dot{\mathcal{A}}(\boldsymbol{M})\to I(M,L)\;.

By (
2.6.12
) and according to
Section

2.6.2
, the map (
2.6.33
) restricts to a TVS-isomorphism

(2.6.34)

\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}:\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(0)}(\boldsymbol{M})\xrightarrow{\cong}I^{(0)}(M,L)\;.

On the other hand, the map (
2.6.17
) restricts to a continuous linear map

(2.6.35)

R:I(M,L)\to J(M,L)\;,

which is the identity on
C^{\infty}(M)
, and can be also described as the composition

I(M,L)\xrightarrow{\tilde{\pi}^{*}}I(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{L})\xrightarrow{R}\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{M})\xrightarrow{\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}}J(M,L)\;.

Both (
2.6.33
) and (
2.6.35
) are surjective topological homomorphisms
[
ÁLKL23
, Proposition 7.29]
, and therefore
C^{\infty}(M)
is dense in
J(M,L)

[
ÁLKL23
, Corollary 7.32]
.

2.6.10.
The space
K(M,L)

Like in
Section

2.5.13
, the condition of being supported in
L
defines the LCHSs and
C^{\infty}(M)
-modules

K^{(s)}(M,L)=I^{(s)}_{L}(M,L)\;,\quad K^{m}(M,L)=I^{m}_{L}(M,L)\;,\quad K(M,L)=I_{L}(M,L)\;.

These are closed subspaces of
I^{(s)}(M,L)
,
I^{m}_{L}(M,L)
and
I(M,L)
, respectively; more precisely, they are the null spaces of the corresponding restrictions of the map (
2.6.35
). The identity (
2.6.20
) restricts to a TVS-identity

(2.6.36)

\mathcal{K}(\boldsymbol{M})\equiv K(M,L)\oplus K(M,L)\;.

Furthermore the maps (
2.6.21
) induce continuous linear maps

(2.6.37)

\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}:\mathcal{K}(\boldsymbol{M})\to K(M,L)\;,\quad\tilde{\pi}^{*}:K(M,L)\to\mathcal{K}(\boldsymbol{M})\;.

Using (
2.6.36
), these maps are given by
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}(u,v)=u+v
and
\tilde{\pi}^{*}u=(u,u)
.

By (
2.5.40
) and (
2.5.42
),
K^{(s)}(M,L)
and
K^{m}(M,L)
satisfy analogs of (
2.6.36
), using
\mathcal{K}^{(s)}(\boldsymbol{M})
and
\mathcal{K}^{m}(\boldsymbol{M})
. The following properties hold true
[
ÁLKL23
, Corollaries 7.19–7.21 and 7.23]
:
K(M,L)
is a limit subspace of the LF-space
I(M,L)
; every
K^{(s)}(M,L)
is a totally reflexive Fréchet space; moreover it is barreled, ultrabornological and webbed, and therefore so is
K(M,L)
; and
K(M,L)
is an acyclic Montel space, and therefore complete, boundedly/compactly/sequentially retractive and reflexive.

Example 2.6.2
.

With the notation of
Section

2.1.9
,
\operatorname{Diff}(M)\equiv K(M^{2},\Delta)
becomes a filtered
C^{\infty}(M^{2})
-submodule of
\Psi(M)
, with the order filtration corresponding to the symbol filtration. In this way,
\operatorname{Diff}(M)
also becomes a LCHS satisfying the above properties. If
M
is compact, it is also a filtered subalgebra of
\Psi(M)
.

2.6.11.
A description of
K(M,L)

By (
2.2.17
) and (
2.2.15
), for
s<-1/2
, every isomorphism (
2.6.23
) restricts to a TVS-isomorphism

(2.6.38)

\partial_{x}^{m}:C^{\infty}(L;\Omega^{-1}NL)\xrightarrow{\cong}\partial_{x}^{m}C^{\infty}(L;\Omega^{-1}NL)\subset K^{(s-m)}(M,L)\;,

Then (
2.6.24
) restricts to a TVS-isomorphisms
[
ÁLKL23
, Proposition 7.26]

(2.6.39)

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{m=0}^{\infty}C^{\infty}(L;\Omega^{-1}NL)\xrightarrow{\cong}K(M,L)\;,

(2.6.40)

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{m<-s-\frac{1}{2}}C^{\infty}(L;\Omega^{-1}NL)\xrightarrow{\cong}K^{(s)}(M,L)\quad(s<-1/2)\;.

Remark 2.6.3
.

In
Section

2.5.13
, for any compact manifold with boundary
M
, the analogs of (
2.6.39
) and (
2.6.40
) for
\mathcal{K}(M)
follows from their application to
K(\breve{M},\partial M)
using (
2.5.44
).

2.6.12.
The conormal sequence

The diagram (
2.6.22
) has the restriction

(2.6.41)

\begin{CD}0\to\mathcal{K}(\boldsymbol{M})@>{\iota}>{}>\dot{\mathcal{A}}(\boldsymbol{M})@>{R}>{}>\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{M})\to 0\\
@V{\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}}V{}V@V{\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}}V{}V@V{\cong}V{\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}}V\\
0\to K(M,L)@>{\iota}>{}>I(M,L)@>{R}>{}>J(M,L)\to 0\;.\hskip-5.69046pt\end{CD}

The bottom row of (
2.6.41
) is exact in the category of continuous linear maps between LCSs
[
ÁLKL23
, Corollary 7.30]
; it will be called the
conormal sequence
of
M
at
L
(or of
(M,L)
).

The surjectivity of (
2.6.35
) can be realized with the partial extension maps given by the following consequence of
2.5.1
, whose proof is recalled by its relevance in
Chapters

5
and
6
.

Corollary 2.6.4

(
[
ÁLKL23
, Corollary 7.31]
)
.

For all
m\in\mathbb{R}
, there is a continuous linear partial extension map
E_{m}:J^{m}(M,L)\to I^{(s)}(M,L)
, where
s=0
if
m\geq 0
, and
m>s\in\mathbb{Z}^{-}
if
m<0
. For
m\geq 0
,
E_{m}:J^{m}(M,L)\to I^{(0)}(M)
is a continuous inclusion map.

Proof.

By the commutativity of (
2.6.41
) and using
2.5.1
, we can define
E_{m}:J^{m}(M,L)\to I^{(s)}(M,L)
as the composition

J^{m}(M,L)\xrightarrow{\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}^{-1}}\mathcal{A}^{m}(\boldsymbol{M})\xrightarrow{E_{m}}\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(s)}(\boldsymbol{M})\xrightarrow{\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}}I^{(s)}(M,L)\;.

The last assertion follows from
2.5.1
and
2.5.5
and (
2.6.34
).
∎

According to this proof,
2.5.2
,
2.5.3
,
2.5.4
,
2.5.5
,
2.5.6
,
2.5.7
and
2.5.8
have obvious versions for the maps given by
2.6.4
.

2.6.13.
Action of
\operatorname{Diff}(M)
on the conormal sequence

According to
Section

2.2.7
, every
A\in\operatorname{Diff}(M)
defines a continuous linear map
A
on
I(M,L)
, which preserves
K(M,L)
, and induces a continuous linear map
A
on
J(M,L)
. This map satisfies the analog of (
2.2.15
).

The map
A
on
J(M,L)
can be also described as a restriction of
A
on
C^{-\infty}(M,L)
(
Section

2.6.6
). On the other hand, according to
Section

2.5.14
, the lift
\widetilde{A}\in\operatorname{Diff}(\boldsymbol{M})
defines continuous linear maps on the top spaces of (
2.6.41
) which correspond to the operators defined by
A
on the bottom spaces via the maps
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}
. If
A\in\operatorname{Diff}(M,L)
, then it defines continuous endomorphisms
A
of
J^{(s)}(M,L)
and
J^{m}(M,L)
.

2.6.14.
Pull-back maps on the conormal sequence

Consider the notation and conditions of
Section

2.2.8
. By the exactness of the conormal sequences of
(M,L)
and
(M^{\prime},L^{\prime})
in the category of continuous linear maps between LCSs, the map (
2.2.19
) induces continuous linear maps,

(2.6.42)

\displaystyle\phi^{*}:K(M,L)\to K(M^{\prime},L^{\prime})\;,

(2.6.43)

\displaystyle\phi^{*}:J(M,L)\to J(M^{\prime},L^{\prime})\;.

The map (
2.6.42
) is the restriction of (
2.2.19
), which is well defined because the map (
2.2.19
) can be locally defined, and (
2.6.43
) is the induced map in the quotient. These maps are compatible with the maps
\iota
and
R
of the conormal sequences, and satisfy the analog of (
2.2.20
).

2.6.15.
Push-forward maps on the conormal sequence

Consider the notation and conditions of
Section

2.2.9
. Like in
Section

2.6.14
, the map (
2.2.22
) induces continuous linear maps,

(2.6.44)

\displaystyle\phi_{*}:K(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Omega_{\text{\rm fiber}})\to K(M,L)\;,

(2.6.45)

\displaystyle\phi_{*}:J(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Omega_{\text{\rm fiber}})\to J(M,L)\;.

They are also compatible with the maps
\iota
and
R
of the conormal sequences, and satisfy the analog of (
2.2.23
).

2.6.16.
Case where
L
is not transversely orientable

If
L
is not transversely orientable, we still have a tubular neighborhood
T
of
L
in
M
, but there is no defining function
x
of
L
in
T
trivializing the projection
\varpi:T\to L
. We can cut
M
along
L
as well to produce a bounded compact manifold,
\boldsymbol{M}
, with a projection
\boldsymbol{\pi}:\boldsymbol{M}\to M
and a boundary collar
\boldsymbol{T}
over
T
.

Using a boundary-defining function
\boldsymbol{x}
of
\boldsymbol{M}
, we get the same definitions, properties and descriptions of
C^{\pm\infty}(M,L)
and
J(M,L)
(
Sections

2.6.3
,
2.6.7
and
2.6.8
).
C^{-\infty}_{L}(M)
and
K(M,L)
also have the same definitions (
Sections

2.6.4
and
2.6.10
). However (
2.6.20
) and (
2.6.36
) are not true because the covering map
\boldsymbol{\pi}:\partial\boldsymbol{M}\to L
is not trivial, and the descriptions given in (
2.6.24
), (
2.6.25
), (
2.6.39
) and (
2.6.40
) need a slight modification. This problem can be solved as follows.

Let
\check{\pi}:\check{L}\to L
denote the two-fold covering of transverse orientations of
L
, and let
\check{\sigma}
denote its deck transformation different from the identity. Since the lift of
NL
to
\check{L}
is trivial,
\check{\pi}
on
\check{L}\equiv\{0\}\times\check{L}
can be extended to a two-fold covering
\check{\pi}:\check{T}:=(-\epsilon,\epsilon)_{x}\times\check{L}\to T
, for some
\epsilon>0
. Its deck transformation different from the identity is an extension of
\check{\sigma}
on
\check{L}\equiv\{0\}\times\check{L}
, also denoted by
\check{\sigma}
. Then
\check{L}
is transversely oriented in
\check{T}
; i.e., its normal bundle
N\check{L}
is trivial. Thus
C^{-\infty}_{\check{L}}(\check{T})
and
K(\check{T},\check{L})
satisfy (
2.6.20
), (
2.6.24
), (
2.6.25
), (
2.6.36
), (
2.6.39
) and (
2.6.40
). Since
N\check{L}\equiv\check{\pi}^{*}NL
, the map
\check{\sigma}
lifts to a homomorphism of
N\check{L}
, which induces a homomorphism of
\Omega^{-1}NL
also denoted by
\check{\sigma}
. Let
L_{-1}
be the union of non-transversely oriented connected components of
L
, and
L_{1}
the union of its transversely oriented components. Correspondingly, let
\check{L}_{\pm 1}=\check{\pi}^{-1}(L_{\pm 1})
and
\check{T}_{\pm 1}=(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\times\check{L}_{\pm 1}
. Since
\check{\sigma}^{*}x=\pm x
on
T_{\pm 1}
, the isomorphisms (
2.6.24
), (
2.6.25
), (
2.6.39
) and (
2.6.40
) become true in this case by replacing
C^{r}(L;\Omega^{-1}NL)
(
r\in\mathbb{Z}\cup\{\pm\infty\}
) with the direct sum of the spaces

\{\,u\in C^{r}(L_{\pm 1};\Omega^{-1}NL_{\pm 1})\mid\check{\sigma}^{*}u=\pm u\,\}\;.

The other results about
C^{-\infty}_{L}(M)
and
K(M,L)
(
Sections

2.6.4
,
2.6.5
,
2.6.10
and
2.6.11
) can be obtained by using these extensions of (
2.6.24
), (
2.6.25
), (
2.6.39
) and (
2.6.40
) instead of (
2.6.20
) and (
2.6.36
).
Sections

2.6.12
,
2.6.13
,
2.6.14
and
2.6.15
also have strightforward extensions.

2.7.
Dual-conormal sequence

2.7.1.
The spaces
K^{\prime}(M,L)
and
J^{\prime}(M,L)

Consider the notation of
Section

2.6
assuming that
L
is transversely oriented; the extension to the non-transversely orientable case can be made with the procedure of
Section

2.6.16
. Like in
Sections

2.3.1
and
2.5.21
, let

K^{\prime}(M,L)=K(M,L;\Omega)^{\prime}\;,\quad J^{\prime}(M,L)=J(M,L;\Omega)^{\prime}\;.

By (
2.6.27
) and (
2.6.36
),

(2.7.1)

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{M})\equiv K^{\prime}(M,L)\oplus K^{\prime}(M,L)\;,\quad\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{M})\equiv J^{\prime}(M,L)\;.

Let also

(2.7.2)

\left\{\begin{gathered}K^{\prime\,(s)}(M,L)=K^{(-s)}(M,L;\Omega)^{\prime}\;,\quad K^{\prime\,m}(M,L)=K^{-m}(M,L;\Omega)^{\prime}\;,\\
J^{\prime\,(s)}(M,L)=J^{(-s)}(M,L;\Omega)^{\prime}\;,\quad J^{\prime\,m}(M,L)=J^{-m}(M,L;\Omega)^{\prime}\;,\end{gathered}\right.

which satisfy the analog of (
2.7.1
). Like in
Section

2.5.21
, for
s<s^{\prime}
and
m<m^{\prime}
, we get continuous linear restriction maps

K^{\prime\,(s^{\prime})}(M,L)\to K^{\prime\,(s)}(M,L)\;,\quad K^{\prime\,m}(M,L)\to K^{\prime\,m^{\prime}}(M,L)\;,

and continuous injections

J^{\prime\,(s^{\prime})}(M,L)\subset J^{\prime\,(s)}(M,L)\;,\quad J^{\prime\,m^{\prime}}(M,L)\subset J^{\prime\,m}(M,L)\;,

forming projective spectra. By (
2.7.1
) and its analog for the spaces (
2.7.2
), and according to
Section

2.5.21
, we get that the spaces
K^{\prime\,(s)}(M,L)
and
K^{\prime\,m}(M,L)
satisfy the analogs of (
2.3.5
) and (
2.3.6
), and the spaces
J^{\prime\,(s)}(M,L)
and
J^{\prime\,m}(M,L)
satisfy the analogs of (
2.5.53
) and (
2.5.54
)
[
ÁLKL23
, Corollary 8.3]
. Furthermore,
K^{\prime}(M,L)
and
J^{\prime}(M,L)
are complete Montel spaces
[
ÁLKL23
, Corollary 8.1]
, and
K^{\prime\,(s)}(M,L)
and
J^{\prime\,(s)}(M,L)
are bornological and barreled
[
ÁLKL23
, Corollary 8.2]
.

Like in
Section

2.5.21
, the versions of (
2.6.16
), (
2.6.26
) and (
2.6.29
) with
\Omega M
induce continuous inclusions

(2.7.3)

C^{-\infty}(M)\supset J^{\prime}(M,L)\supset C^{\infty}(M,L)\;.

2.7.2.
A description of
J^{\prime}(M,L)

With the notation and conditions of
Section

2.6.8
, we have the following
[
ÁLKL23
, Corollaries 8.4 and 8.5]
:

(2.7.4)

\displaystyle J^{\prime\,m}(M,L)\cong\boldsymbol{x}^{m}H_{\text{\rm b}}^{-\infty}(\boldsymbol{M})=\boldsymbol{x}^{m-\frac{1}{2}}H^{-\infty}(\mathring{\boldsymbol{M}})\;,

(2.7.5)

\displaystyle J^{\prime}(M,L)\cong\bigcap_{m}\boldsymbol{x}^{m}H_{\text{\rm b}}^{-\infty}(\boldsymbol{M})=\bigcap_{m}\boldsymbol{x}^{m}H^{-\infty}(\mathring{\boldsymbol{M}})\;.

Actually, the first isomorphisms of (
2.7.4
) and (
2.7.5
) are independent of
g
, and hold true without the assumptions
1
and
2
. Furthermore
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(M\setminus L)
is dense in every
J^{\prime\,m}(M,L)
and in
J^{\prime}(M,L)

[
ÁLKL23
, Corollary 8.6]
. Therefore the right-hand side inclusion of (
2.7.3
) is also dense.

2.7.3.
Description of
K^{\prime}(M,L)

The transposes of the versions of (
2.6.39
) and (
2.6.40
) with
\Omega M
are TVS-isomorphisms
[
ÁLKL23
, Corollary 8.7]
,

(2.7.6)

\displaystyle K^{\prime}(M,L)\xrightarrow{\cong}\prod_{m=0}^{\infty}C^{-\infty}(L)\;,

(2.7.7)

\displaystyle K^{\prime\,(s)}(M,L)\xrightarrow{\cong}\prod_{m<s-1/2}C^{-\infty}(L)\quad(s>1/2)\;,

because

C^{\infty}(L;\Omega^{-1}NL\otimes\Omega M)^{\prime}=C^{\infty}(L;\Omega)^{\prime}=C^{-\infty}(L)\;.

2.7.4.
Dual-conormal sequence

The transpose of the density-bundles version of (
2.6.41
) is the commutative diagram

(2.7.8)

\begin{CD}0\leftarrow\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{M})@<{R^{\prime}}<{}<\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{M})@<{\iota^{\prime}}<{}<\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{M})\leftarrow 0\\
@A{\boldsymbol{\pi}^{*}}A{}A@A{\boldsymbol{\pi}^{*}}A{}A@A{\boldsymbol{\pi}^{*}}A{\cong}A\\
0\leftarrow K^{\prime}(M,L)@<{R^{\prime}}<{}<I^{\prime}(M,L)@<{\iota^{\prime}}<{}<J^{\prime}(M,L)\leftarrow 0\;,\hskip-5.69046pt\end{CD}

where
R^{\prime}=\iota^{\text{\rm t}}
and
\iota^{\prime}=R^{\text{\rm t}}
. Its bottom row is exact in the category of continuous linear maps between LCSs
[
ÁLKL23
, Proposition 8.8]
, and is called the
dual-conormal sequence
of
M
at
L
(or of
(M,L)
).

2.7.5.
Action of
\operatorname{Diff}(M)
on the dual-conormal sequence

With the notation of
Section

2.6.13
, consider the actions of
A^{\text{\rm t}}
and
\widetilde{A}^{\text{\rm t}}
on the bottom and top spaces of the version of (
2.6.41
) with
\Omega M
and
\Omega\boldsymbol{M}
. Taking transposes again, we get induced actions of
A
and
\widetilde{A}
on the bottom and top spaces of (
2.7.8
), which correspond one another via the linear maps
\boldsymbol{\pi}^{*}
. These maps satisfy the analogs of (
2.3.7
).

2.7.6.
Pull-back maps on the dual-conormal sequence

Consider the notation and conditions of
Section

2.3.5
(the same as in
Section

2.2.9
). Like in
Section

2.3.5
, transposing the compactly supported case of the analog of (
2.2.23
) for (
2.6.44
) and (
2.6.45
) with
E=\Omega M
, we get continuous linear maps,

(2.7.9)

\displaystyle\phi^{*}:K^{\prime}(M,L)\to K^{\prime}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime})\;,

(2.7.10)

\displaystyle\phi^{*}:J^{\prime}(M,L)\to J^{\prime}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime})\;.

They are compatible with the maps
\iota^{\prime}
and
R^{\prime}
of the dual-conormal sequences, and satisfy the analog of (
2.3.9
).

2.7.7.
Push-forward maps on the dual-conormal sequence

Consider the notation and conditions of
Section

2.3.6
(the same as in
Section

2.2.8
). Like in
Section

2.3.6
, transposing the analogs of (
2.2.20
) for (

) and (

) with

, and using an analog of (
2.1.16
), we get continuous linear maps,

(2.7.11)

\displaystyle\phi_{*}:K^{\prime}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Omega_{\text{\rm fiber}})\to K^{\prime}(M,L)\;,

(2.7.12)

\displaystyle\phi_{*}:J^{\prime}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Omega_{\text{\rm fiber}})\to J^{\prime}(M,L)\;.

They are compatible with the maps
\iota^{\prime}
and
R^{\prime}
of the dual-conormal sequences, and satisfy the analog of (

).

2.8.
Currents

Here, again, the manifold
M
may not be compact, and
L\subset M
is a regular submanifold that is a closed subset. When using
J(M,L;\Lambda)
or
K(M,L;\Lambda)
, it is also assumed that
L
is of codimension one.

2.8.1.
Differential forms and currents

Consider the space
C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda)
of smooth differential forms, and the space
C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda)
of currents. The most typical example of elliptic complex is given by the de Rham derivative
d
on
C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda)
, giving rise to the de Rham cohomology
H^{\bullet}(M)
. The extension of
d
to
C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda)
is another topological complex, which produces isomorphic cohomology
[
dR84
]
. We typically consider cohomology with complex coefficients without further comment; real cohomology classes are only considered in a few cases, where it is indicated; the same applies to other cohomologies that will be considered. The basic properties of
(C^{\pm\infty}(M;\Lambda),d)
and
H^{\bullet}(M)
can be seen in
[
dR84
,
BT82
]
; for instance, the general properties of elliptic complexes apply in this setup (
Section

2.1.14
). Some properties will be seen in
Section

2.9
with more generality.

A Riemannian metric
g
on
M
defines a Hermitian structure on
\Lambda M
, also denoted by
g
. Then we have the additional operators
\delta
(the
de Rham coderivative
),
D
and
\Delta
(the
Laplacian
) of
Section

2.1.14
. If needed, the subscript “
M
” may be added to this notation, and to other similar notation.

We may also consider the de Rham complex with coefficients in a flat vector bundle
\mathcal{F}
,
d=d^{\mathcal{F}}
on
C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda\otimes\mathcal{F})
. As above,
g
and a Hermitian structure on
\mathcal{F}
induce additional operators
\delta=\delta^{\mathcal{F}}
,
D=D^{\mathcal{F}}
and
\Delta=\Delta^{\mathcal{F}}
.

For any
V\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
, let
\iota_{V}
and
\mathcal{L}_{V}
denote the corresponding inner product and Lie derivative on
C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda)
. For
\eta=V^{\flat}\in C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda^{1})
, we write
{\eta\lrcorner}=-(\eta\wedge)^{*}=-\iota_{V}
. Let
\mathsf{w}
be the degree involution on
\Lambda M
. For the bundle of Clifford algebra of
T^{*}M
, we have the identity
\operatorname{Cl}(T^{*}M)\equiv\Lambda_{\mathbb{R}}M
defined by the symbol of filtered algebras. Via this identity, the left Clifford multiplication by
\eta
is
c(\eta)={\eta\wedge}+{\eta\lrcorner}
, and the composition of
\mathsf{w}
with the right Clifford multiplication by
\eta
is
\hat{c}(\eta)={\eta\wedge}-{\eta\lrcorner}
.

2.8.2.
Product of differential forms and currents

The exterior product of smooth differential forms has continuous extensions,
1
1

1

This holds with more generality under conditions on the wavefront set
[
Hör71
, Theorem 8.2.10]
, but we will not use it.

(2.8.1)

C^{\pm\infty}(M;\Lambda)\otimes C^{\mp\infty}(M;\Lambda)\to C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda)\;,

For example, with the notation of
Section

2.2.6
, assuming that
M
and
L
are oriented, it easily follows that, for
\alpha\in C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda)
and
\beta\in C^{\infty}(L;\Lambda\otimes\Omega^{-1}NL)
,

(2.8.2)

\alpha\wedge\delta_{L}^{\beta}=\delta_{L}^{\alpha|_{L}\wedge\beta}\;,\quad\delta_{L}^{\beta}\wedge\alpha=\delta_{L}^{\beta\wedge\alpha|_{L}}\;.

2.8.3.
Currents on oriented manifolds

Assume
M
is oriented. The orientation induces a canonical identity
\Omega M\equiv\Lambda^{n}M
. Then, for every degree
k
, the non-degenerate pairing
\Lambda^{k}M\otimes\Lambda^{n-k}M\to\Lambda^{n}M
defined by the wedge product induces a canonical identity

(2.8.3)

(\Lambda^{k}M)^{*}\otimes\Omega M\equiv\Lambda^{n-k}M\;.

By (
2.8.3
), the space (
2.1.5
) becomes

(2.8.4)

C^{-\infty}_{{\cdot}/{\text{\rm c}}}(M;\Lambda^{k})\equiv C^{\infty}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\cdot}}(M;\Lambda^{n-k})^{\prime}

in this case. This identity corresponds to a pairing

C^{\pm\infty}_{{\cdot}/{\text{\rm c}}}(M;\Lambda^{k})\otimes C^{\mp\infty}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\cdot}}(M;\Lambda^{n-k})\to\mathbb{C}\;,

which will be denoted with parentheses to distinguish it from the scalar product. This pairing can be given by the composition of (
2.8.1
) and the extension

C^{-\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(M;\Lambda^{n})\to\mathbb{C}\;,\quad\alpha\mapsto(\alpha,1)\;,

of
\int_{M}:C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(M;\Lambda M)\to\mathbb{C}
.

2.8.4.
Hodge operator on oriented manifolds

Continuing with the assumption of orientation, let
\star
on
\Lambda M
denote the
\mathbb{C}
-linear extension of the Hodge operator
\star
on the real forms, which is unitary, and let
\bar{\star}
denote its
\mathbb{C}
-antilinear extension. These operators are determined by the conditions, for
\alpha,\beta\in C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda)
,

\alpha\wedge\overline{\star\beta}=g(\alpha,\beta)\,\operatorname{dvol}=\alpha\wedge\bar{\star}\beta\;,

where
\operatorname{dvol}=\star 1
is the volume form. Recall that, on
C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda^{k})
,

(2.8.5)

\left\{\begin{gathered}\star^{2}=(-1)^{nk+k}\;,\quad\delta=(-1)^{nk+n+1}\star\,d\,\star\;,\quad{\eta\lrcorner}=(-1)^{nk+n+1}\star\,{\eta\wedge}\,\star\;,\\
d\,\star=(-1)^{k}\star\,\delta\;,\quad\delta\,\star=(-1)^{k+1}\star\,d\;,\quad\Delta\,\star=\star\,\Delta\;,\\
{\eta\wedge}\,\star=(-1)^{k}\,\star\,{\eta\lrcorner}\;,\quad{\eta\lrcorner}\,\star=(-1)^{k+1}\,\star\;{\eta\wedge}\;.\end{gathered}\right.

The equalities (
2.8.5
) are also true with
\bar{\star}
, and can be extended to
C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda)
.

For all
\alpha\in C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda^{k})
and
\beta\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(M;\Lambda^{n-k})
,

\alpha\wedge\beta=(-1)^{kn+k}\alpha\wedge\bar{\star}^{2}\beta=(-1)^{kn+k}g(\alpha,\bar{\star}\beta)\operatorname{dvol}\;,

yielding

(2.8.6)

(\alpha,\beta)=(-1)^{kn+k}\langle\alpha,\bar{\star}\beta\rangle\;.

2.8.5.
Pull-back and push-forward of currents

Given a smooth map
\phi:M^{\prime}\to M
, recall that its tangent map
T\phi=\phi_{*}:TM^{\prime}\to TM
defines a homomorphism
\phi_{*}:TM^{\prime}\to\phi^{*}TM
, which induces a homomorphism

(2.8.7)

\phi^{*}:\phi^{*}\Lambda M\to\Lambda M^{\prime}\;.

Then recall that the pull-back homomorphism

(2.8.8)

\phi^{*}:C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda)\to C^{\infty}(M^{\prime};\Lambda)

can be given as the composition

(2.8.9)

C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\phi^{*}}C^{\infty}(M^{\prime};\phi^{*}\Lambda M)\xrightarrow{\phi^{*}}C^{\infty}(M^{\prime};\Lambda)\;,

where the first map
\phi^{*}
is given by (
2.1.13
), and the second map
\phi^{*}
is induced by (
2.8.7
).

Now, suppose
\phi
is a submersion and its vertical subbundle
\mathcal{V}
is oriented. Let
\pi_{\text{\rm top}}:\Lambda\mathcal{V}\to\Lambda^{\text{\rm top}}\mathcal{V}
denote the canonical projection. The orientation of
\mathcal{V}
gives a canonical identity
\Omega_{\text{\rm fiber}}\equiv\Lambda^{\text{\rm top}}\mathcal{V}
. So

\phi^{*}\Lambda M\otimes\Omega_{\text{\rm fiber}}\equiv\phi^{*}\Lambda M\otimes\Lambda^{\text{\rm top}}\mathcal{V}\subset\phi^{*}\Lambda M\otimes\Lambda\mathcal{V}\equiv\Lambda M^{\prime}\;.

Moreover,
\pi_{\text{\rm top}}:\Lambda\mathcal{V}\to\Lambda^{\text{\rm top}}\mathcal{V}
induces a projection

(2.8.10)

\pi_{\text{\rm top}}:\Lambda M^{\prime}\to\phi^{*}\Lambda M\otimes\Omega_{\text{\rm fiber}}\;.

The push-forward homomorphism or
integration along the fibers

[
BT82
, Section I.6]
,

(2.8.11)

\phi_{*}:C^{\infty}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\text{\rm cv}}}(M^{\prime};\Lambda)\to C^{\infty}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\cdot}}(M;\Lambda)\;,

can be described as the composition

(2.8.12)

C^{\infty}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\text{\rm cv}}}(M^{\prime};\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\pi_{\text{\rm top}}}C^{\infty}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\text{\rm cv}}}(M^{\prime};\phi^{*}\Lambda M\otimes\Omega_{\text{\rm fiber}})\xrightarrow{\phi_{*}}C^{\infty}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\cdot}}(M;\Lambda)\;,

where
\pi_{\text{\rm top}}
is induced by (
2.8.10
), and
\phi_{*}
is given by (
2.1.14
) with
E=\Lambda M
.

We also get the push-forward and pull-back maps on currents,

(2.8.13)

\displaystyle\phi_{*}:C^{-\infty}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\text{\rm cv}}}(M^{\prime};\Lambda)\to C^{-\infty}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\cdot}}(M;\Lambda)\;,

(2.8.14)

\displaystyle\phi^{*}:C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda)\to C^{-\infty}(M^{\prime};\Lambda)\;,

given by the compositions

(2.8.15)

\displaystyle C^{–\infty}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\text{\rm cv}}}(M^{\prime};\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\pi_{\text{\rm top}}}C^{–\infty}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\text{\rm cv}}}(M^{\prime};\phi^{*}\Lambda M\otimes\Omega_{\text{\rm fiber}})\xrightarrow{\phi_{*}}C^{\infty}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\cdot}}(M;\Lambda)\;,

(2.8.16)

\displaystyle C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\phi^{*}}C^{-\infty}(M^{\prime};\phi^{*}\Lambda M)\xrightarrow{\phi^{*}}C^{-\infty}(M^{\prime};\Lambda)\;,

where
\phi_{*}
and the first map
\phi^{*}
are given by (
2.1.17
)–(
2.1.19
) with
E=\Lambda M
, and
\pi_{\text{\rm top}}
is induced by (
2.8.10
). The notation
\fint_{\phi}
is also used for
\phi_{*}
, or
\fint_{F}
if
\phi
is a trivial bundle with typical fiber
F
.

Proposition 2.8.1
.

The compactly supported case of (
2.8.13
) is the transpose of (
2.8.8
), and (
2.8.14
) is the transpose of the compactly supported case of (
2.8.11
).

Proof.

By passing to double covers of orientations, we can assume
M
and
M^{\prime}
are oriented, and therefore we can use (
2.8.4
). By the density of the space of smooth forms in the space of currents (
Section

2.1.4
), it is enough to check the statement on smooth forms, where it is given by
[
BT82
, Proposition 6.15 (b)]
: for
\alpha\in C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda)
and
\beta\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(M^{\prime};\Lambda)
,

(\phi^{*}\alpha,\beta)=\int_{M^{\prime}}\phi^{*}\alpha\wedge\beta=\int_{M}\alpha\wedge\phi_{*}\beta=(\alpha,\phi_{*}\beta)\;.\qed

2.8.6.
Homotopy operators

Recall that any smooth homotopy,
H:M^{\prime}\times I\to M
(
I=[0,1]
), induces a continuous homotopy operator
\mathsf{h}:C^{\infty}(M^{\prime};\Lambda)\to C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda)
(a linear map, which is homogeneous of degree
-1
, and satisfies
H_{1}^{*}-H_{0}^{*}=\mathsf{h}d+d\mathsf{h}
, where
H_{t}=H(\cdot,t):M^{\prime}\to M
). For instance, we can take
\mathsf{h}
equal to the composition
[
BT82
, Section 4]

(2.8.17)

C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda)\xrightarrow{H^{*}}C^{\infty}(M^{\prime}\times I;\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\fint_{I}}C^{\infty}(M^{\prime};\Lambda)\;.

With the notations and conditions of

2.2.8
, the map (

2.8.10.
Push-forward of dual-conormal currents

With the notations and conditions of

\phi
is oriented. Then the map (

) has a continuous extension

(2.8.33)

\phi_{*}:I^{\prime}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\text{\rm cv}}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)\to I^{\prime}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\cdot}}(M,L;\Lambda)\;,

which can be described as the composition

I^{\prime}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\text{\rm cv}}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\pi_{\text{\rm top}}}I^{\prime}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\text{\rm cv}}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\phi^{*}\Lambda M\otimes\Omega_{\text{\rm fiber}})\xrightarrow{\phi_{*}}I^{\prime}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\cdot}}(M,L;\Lambda)\;,

using (
2.3.11
) like in (
2.8.25
). The map (
2.8.33
) is also a restriction of (
2.8.13
).

Similarly, if
L
is of codimension one, there are continuous homomorphisms,

(2.8.34)

\displaystyle\phi_{*}:K^{\prime}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\text{\rm cv}}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)\to K^{\prime}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\cdot}}(M,L;\Lambda)\;,

(2.8.35)

\displaystyle\phi_{*}:J^{\prime}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\text{\rm cv}}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)\to J^{\prime}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\cdot}}(M,L;\Lambda)\;,

which can be described as the compositions

\displaystyle K^{\prime}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\text{\rm cv}}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\pi_{\text{\rm top}}}K^{\prime}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\text{\rm cv}}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\phi^{*}\Lambda M\otimes\Omega_{\text{\rm fiber}})\xrightarrow{\phi_{*}}K^{\prime}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\cdot}}(M,L;\Lambda)\;,

\displaystyle J^{\prime}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\text{\rm cv}}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\pi_{\text{\rm top}}}J^{\prime}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\text{\rm cv}}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\phi^{*}\Lambda M\otimes\Omega_{\text{\rm fiber}})\xrightarrow{\phi_{*}}J^{\prime}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\cdot}}(M,L;\Lambda)\;,

using the analogs of (
2.3.11
) for (
2.7.11
) and (
2.7.12
) like in (
2.8.28
) and (
2.8.29
).

Proposition 2.8.2
.

The compact-support cases of (
2.8.33
)–(
2.8.35
) are transposes of (
2.8.18
), (
2.8.20
) and (
2.8.21
); and (
2.8.30
)–(
2.8.32
) are transposes of the compact-support cases of (
2.8.24
), (
2.8.26
) and (
2.8.27
).

Proof.

We have the commutative diagrams

\begin{CD}I_{\text{\rm c}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)@>{\phi_{*}}>{}>I_{\text{\rm c}}(M,L;\Lambda)\\
@A{}A{}A@A{}A{}A\\
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(M^{\prime};\Lambda)@>{\phi_{*}}>{}>C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(M;\Lambda)\end{CD}\qquad\begin{CD}I^{\prime}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)@<{\phi^{*}}<{}<I^{\prime}(M,L;\Lambda)\phantom{\;.}\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
C^{-\infty}(M^{\prime};\Lambda)@<{\phi^{*}}<{}<C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda)\;,\end{CD}

where the vertical arrows are continuous dense inclusions given by (
2.2.5
) and (
2.3.4
) with
\Lambda M
. By
2.8.1
, the transpose of the first diagram is

\begin{CD}I^{\prime}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)@<{(\phi_{*})^{\text{\rm t}}}<{}<I^{\prime}(M,L;\Lambda)\phantom{\;.}\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
C^{-\infty}(M^{\prime};\Lambda)@<{\phi^{*}}<{}<C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda)\;,\end{CD}

where the vertical arrows are again inclusion maps. Comparing the second and third diagrams, we get

(2.8.36)

(\phi_{*})^{\text{\rm t}}=\phi^{*}:I^{\prime}(M,L;\Lambda)\to I^{\prime}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)\;.

The analogous argument with the commutative diagrams

\begin{CD}I(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)@<{\phi^{*}}<{}<I(M,L;\Lambda)\\
@A{}A{}A@A{}A{}A\\
C^{\infty}(M^{\prime};\Lambda)@<{\phi^{*}}<{}<C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda)\end{CD}\qquad\begin{CD}I^{\prime}_{\text{\rm c}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)@>{\phi_{*}}>{}>I^{\prime}_{\text{\rm c}}(M,L;\Lambda)\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
C^{-\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(M^{\prime};\Lambda)@>{\phi_{*}}>{}>C^{-\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(M;\Lambda)\end{CD}

shows that

(2.8.37)

(\phi^{*})^{\text{\rm t}}=\phi_{*}:I^{\prime}_{\text{\rm c}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)\to I^{\prime}_{\text{\rm c}}(M,L;\Lambda)\;.

Next, consider the commutative diagrams

\begin{CD}K_{\text{\rm c}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)@>{\phi_{*}}>{}>K_{\text{\rm c}}(M,L;\Lambda)\\
@V{\iota}V{}V@V{}V{\iota}V\\
I_{\text{\rm c}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)@>{\phi_{*}}>{}>I_{\text{\rm c}}(M,L;\Lambda)\end{CD}\qquad\begin{CD}K^{\prime}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)@<{\phi^{*}}<{}<K^{\prime}(M,L;\Lambda)\phantom{\;.}\\
@A{R^{\prime}}A{}A@A{}A{R^{\prime}}A\\
I^{\prime}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)@<{\phi^{*}}<{}<I^{\prime}(M,L;\Lambda)\;.\end{CD}

As above, comparing the second one with the transposition of the first one, and using (
2.8.36
) and the surjectivity of
R^{\prime}:I^{\prime}(M,L;\Lambda)\to K^{\prime}(M,L;\Lambda)
(
Section

2.7.4
), we get

(\phi_{*})^{\text{\rm t}}=\phi^{*}:K^{\prime}(M,L;\Lambda)\to K^{\prime}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)\;.

A similar argument with the commutative diagrams

\begin{CD}K(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)@<{\phi^{*}}<{}<K(M,L;\Lambda)\\
@V{\iota}V{}V@V{}V{\iota}V\\
I(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)@<{\phi^{*}}<{}<I(M,L;\Lambda)\end{CD}\qquad\begin{CD}K^{\prime}_{\text{\rm c}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)@>{\phi_{*}}>{}>K^{\prime}_{\text{\rm c}}(M,L;\Lambda)\phantom{\;,}\\
@A{R^{\prime}}A{}A@A{}A{R^{\prime}}A\\
I^{\prime}_{\text{\rm c}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)@>{\phi_{*}}>{}>I^{\prime}_{\text{\rm c}}(M,L;\Lambda)\;,\end{CD}

using (
2.8.37
), shows that

(\phi^{*})^{\text{\rm t}}=\phi_{*}:K^{\prime}_{\text{\rm c}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)\to K^{\prime}_{\text{\rm c}}(M,L;\Lambda)\;.

Now, consider the commutative diagrams

\begin{CD}J_{\text{\rm c}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)@>{\phi_{*}}>{}>J_{\text{\rm c}}(M,L;\Lambda)\\
@A{R}A{}A@A{}A{R}A\\
I_{\text{\rm c}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)@>{\phi_{*}}>{}>I_{\text{\rm c}}(M,L;\Lambda)\end{CD}\qquad\begin{CD}J^{\prime}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)@<{\phi^{*}}<{}<J^{\prime}(M,L;\Lambda)\phantom{\;.}\\
@V{\iota^{\prime}}V{}V@V{}V{\iota^{\prime}}V\\
I^{\prime}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)@<{\phi^{*}}<{}<I^{\prime}(M,L;\Lambda)\;.\end{CD}

Again, comparing the second one with the transposition of the first one, and using (
2.8.36
) and the injectivity of
\iota^{\prime}:J^{\prime}(M,L;\Lambda)\to I^{\prime}(M,L;\Lambda)
(
Section

2.7.4
), we get

(\phi_{*})^{\text{\rm t}}=\phi^{*}:J^{\prime}(M,L;\Lambda)\to J^{\prime}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)\;.

Finally, the same argument with the commutative diagrams

\begin{CD}J(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)@<{\phi^{*}}<{}<J(M,L;\Lambda)\\
@A{R}A{}A@A{}A{R}A\\
I(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)@<{\phi^{*}}<{}<I(M,L;\Lambda)\end{CD}\qquad\begin{CD}J^{\prime}_{\text{\rm c}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)@>{\phi_{*}}>{}>J^{\prime}_{\text{\rm c}}(M,L;\Lambda)\phantom{\;,}\\
@V{\iota^{\prime}}V{}V@V{}V{\iota^{\prime}}V\\
I^{\prime}_{\text{\rm c}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)@>{\phi_{*}}>{}>I^{\prime}_{\text{\rm c}}(M,L;\Lambda)\;,\end{CD}

using (
2.8.37
), gives

(\phi^{*})^{\text{\rm t}}=\phi_{*}:J^{\prime}_{\text{\rm c}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda)\to J^{\prime}_{\text{\rm c}}(M,L;\Lambda)\;.\qed

2.9.
Witten’s perturbation of the de Rham complex

2.9.1.
Witten’s complex

The notation
z=\mu+i\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
(
i=\sqrt{-1}
) will be used for a complex parameter. Any closed real
\eta\in C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda^{1})
induces the
Witten’s operators
on
C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda)
, depending on the parameter
z\in\mathbb{C}

[
Wit82
,
Nov81
,
Nov82
,
Paj87
,
BF97
]
,

(2.9.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} d_{z}&=d+z\,\eta\wedge\;,\quad\delta_{z}=d_{z}^{*}=\delta-\bar{z}\,{\eta\lrcorner}\;,\\
D_{z}&=d_{z}+\delta_{z}=D+\mu\hat{c}(\eta)+i\lambda c(\eta)\;,\\
\Delta_{z}&=D_{z}^{2}=d_{z}\delta_{z}+\delta_{z}d_{z}=\Delta+\mu\mathsf{H}_{\eta}-i\lambda\mathsf{J}_{\eta}+|z|^{2}|\eta|^{2}\;,\end{aligned}\right.

where
\mathsf{H}_{\eta}=\mathcal{L}_{V}+\mathcal{L}_{V}^{*}
is of order zero and
\mathsf{J}_{\eta}=\mathcal{L}_{V}-\mathcal{L}_{V}^{*}
is of order one. Here,
d_{z}
is an elliptic complex, giving rise to the
twisted cohomology

H_{z}^{\bullet}(M)
, whose isomorphism class depends only on the real class
\xi:=[\eta]\in H^{1}(M)
and
z\in\mathbb{C}
. The more explicit notation
d_{z\eta}
,
\delta_{z\eta}
,
D_{z\eta}
and
\Delta_{z\eta}
may be used if needed.

Suppose the manifold
M
is closed, and let
n=\dim M
