e(\mathcal{F},g_{\mathcal{F}})\in C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda^{n^{\prime\prime}}\mathcal{F})\equiv C^{\infty}(M;\Omega\mathcal{F})
is defined by the Euler form of the leaves (
Section

2.9.10
). When
\mathcal{F}
is not oriented,
e(\mathcal{F},g_{\mathcal{F}})
is defined as an element of
C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda^{n^{\prime\prime}}\mathcal{F}\otimes o(\mathcal{F}))\equiv C^{\infty}(M;\Omega\mathcal{F})
.

3.2.8.
Leafwise currents

We may also consider the continuous extension of
d_{\mathcal{F}}
to
C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})
(
Section

2.1.7
), defining another topological complex whose cohomology and reduced cohomology are denoted by
H^{\bullet}C^{-\infty}(\mathcal{F})
and
\bar{H}^{\bullet}C^{-\infty}(\mathcal{F})
(see
Section

3.2.1
). The elements of
C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})
are called
leafwise currents
. In general,
C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})\hookrightarrow C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})
does not induce an isomorphism in cohomology or reduced cohomology (consider a foliation by points).

Like in (
2.8.1
), the exterior product has continuous extensions,

C^{\pm\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})\otimes C^{\mp\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})\to C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})\;,

with a corresponding extension of the property of
d_{\mathcal{F}}
to be a derivation. Given a leafwise metric
g_{\mathcal{F}}
on
M
, we can also consider the continuous extension
\delta_{\mathcal{F}}
to
C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})

The concept of leafwise currents with coefficients in any
\mathcal{F}
-flat vector bundle
E
can be also considered, and the obvious notation is used for the corresponding topological complex and its cohomology and reduced cohomology. In particular,
E
can be any vector bundle associated with
N\mathcal{F}
.

3.2.9.
Bigrading of currents

Consider also the bigrading of
C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda)
induced by the bigrading of
\Lambda M
, and the continuous extensions to
C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda)
of the operators
d_{i,1-i}
, which satisfy (
3.2.8
) and (
3.2.9
). Given a metric
g
on
M
, we can also consider the continuous extensions of the operators
\delta_{-i,i-1}
to
C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda)
.

If
M
is oriented, then (
2.8.3
), (
2.8.4
) and (
2.9.8
) for
z=0
give

(3.2.16)

\displaystyle(\Lambda^{u,v}M)^{*}\otimes\Omega M\equiv\Lambda^{n^{\prime}-u,n^{\prime\prime}-v}M\;,\

(3.2.17)

\displaystyle C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda^{u,v})\equiv C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(M;\Lambda^{n^{\prime}-u,n^{\prime\prime}-v})^{\prime}\;,

(3.2.18)

\displaystyle d_{0,1}\equiv(-1)^{u+v+1}\,d_{0,1}^{\text{\rm t}}:C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda^{u,v})\to C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda^{u,v+1})\;.

When
M
is not oriented, these identities hold after adding the tensor product with
o(M)
to the exterior bundles in the right-hand sides, or working locally, or passing to the double cover of orientations. By (
3.2.9
), if
u=n^{\prime}
, then (
3.2.18
) agrees with (
2.9.8
) for
z=0
on
C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda^{n^{\prime},v})
. By (
3.2.3
), (
3.2.8
) and (
3.2.9
), if
u=0
, then (
3.2.16
)–(
3.2.18
) become

(3.2.19)

\displaystyle(\Lambda^{v}\mathcal{F})^{*}\otimes\Omega M\equiv\Lambda^{n^{\prime},n^{\prime\prime}-v}M\equiv\Lambda^{n^{\prime\prime}-v}\mathcal{F}\otimes\Lambda^{n^{\prime}}N\mathcal{F}\equiv\Lambda^{n^{\prime\prime}-v}\mathcal{F}\otimes\Omega N\mathcal{F}\;,

(3.2.20)

\displaystyle C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda^{v}\mathcal{F})\equiv C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(M;\Lambda^{n^{\prime},n^{\prime\prime}-v})^{\prime}\equiv C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(M;\Lambda^{n^{\prime\prime}-v}\mathcal{F}\otimes\Omega N\mathcal{F})^{\prime}\;,

(3.2.21)

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{F}}\equiv(-1)^{v+1}\,d^{\text{\rm t}}\equiv(-1)^{v+1}\,d_{\mathcal{F}}^{\text{\rm t}}:C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda^{v}\mathcal{F})\to C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda^{v+1}\mathcal{F})\;.

3.2.10.
Pull-back of leafwise forms

Let
\phi\in C^{\infty}(M^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime};M,\mathcal{F})
. Like in (
2.8.8
) and (
2.8.9
), the homomorphisms
\phi_{*}:T\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\to T\mathcal{F}
and
\phi_{*}:N\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\to N\mathcal{F}
induce continuous homomorphisms,

(3.2.22)

\displaystyle\phi^{*}:(C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F}\otimes\Lambda N\mathcal{F}),d_{\mathcal{F}})\to(C^{\infty}(M^{\prime};\Lambda\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\otimes\Lambda N\mathcal{F}^{\prime}),d_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}})\;,

(3.2.23)

\displaystyle\phi^{*}:(C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F}),d_{\mathcal{F}})\to(C^{\infty}(M^{\prime};\Lambda\mathcal{F}^{\prime}),d_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}})\;,

the second one is a restriction of the first one according to (
3.2.1
).

On the other hand,
\phi^{*}:C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda)\to C^{\infty}(M^{\prime};\Lambda)
has restrictions

\phi^{*}:C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda^{\geq u,\cdot})\to C^{\infty}(M^{\prime};\Lambda^{\geq u,\cdot})\;,

which induce (
3.2.22
) using (
3.2.5
).

3.2.11.
Bihomogeneous components of pull-back homomorphisms

For any smooth map
\phi:M^{\prime}\to M
, the homomorphism
\phi^{*}:C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda)\to C^{\infty}(M^{\prime};\Lambda)
decomposes into bihomogeneous components,

\phi^{*}=\cdots+\phi^{*}_{-1,1}+\phi^{*}_{0,0}+\phi^{*}_{1,-1}+\cdots

If
\phi\in C^{\infty}(M^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime};M,\mathcal{F})
, then
\phi^{*}_{i,-i}=0
for
i<0
. Moreover, via (
3.2.3
),

(3.2.24)

\phi^{*}_{0,0}\equiv\phi^{*}\;,

where the right-hand side is (
3.2.22
).

3.2.12.
Bihomogeneous components of the Lie derivative

For any
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
, by comparing bidegrees in Cartan’s formula,
\mathcal{L}_{X}=d\iota_{X}+\iota_{X}d
, we get a decomposition into bi-homogeneous components,

\mathcal{L}_{X}=\mathcal{L}_{X,-1,1}+\mathcal{L}_{X,0,0}+\mathcal{L}_{X,1,-1}+\mathcal{L}_{X,2,-2}\;.

For instance,

(3.2.25)

\mathcal{L}_{X,0,0}=d_{0,1}\iota_{\boldsymbol{V}X}+\iota_{\boldsymbol{V}X}d_{0,1}+d_{1,0}\iota_{\boldsymbol{H}X}+\iota_{\boldsymbol{H}X}d_{1,0}\;,

where
\boldsymbol{V}:TM\to T\mathcal{F}
and
\boldsymbol{H}:TM\to\boldsymbol{H}
denote the projections defined by (
3.2.2
). By comparing bidegrees in the derivation formula of
\mathcal{L}_{X}
, we also get that every
\mathcal{L}_{X,i,-i}
(
i\in\{-1,0,1,2\}
) satisfies the same derivation formula. Thus
\mathcal{L}_{X,-1,1}
,
\mathcal{L}_{X,1,-1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{X,2,-2}
are of order zero. For the sake of simplicity, we will write
\Theta_{X}=\mathcal{L}_{X,0,0}
, which is of order 1.

If
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M,\mathcal{F})
, then
\mathcal{L}_{X,-1,1}=0
, obtaining

(3.2.26)

\Theta_{X}d_{0,1}=d_{0,1}\Theta_{X}

by comparing bi-degrees in the formula
\mathcal{L}_{X}d=d\mathcal{L}_{X}
. On the other hand, by (
3.2.25
), if
X\in C^{\infty}(M;\boldsymbol{H})
, then, for all
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
,

(3.2.27)

\Theta_{fX}=f\Theta_{X}

on
C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda^{0,\bullet})\equiv C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})
. If
d_{1,0}f=0
, then (
3.2.27
) holds on
C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda)
by (
3.2.25
) and the derivation formula of
\Theta_{X}
.

3.2.13.
Local descriptions

Let
(U,x)
be a foliated chart of
\mathcal{F}
, with
x=(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})
, like in (
3.1.1
). To emphasize the difference between the coordinates
x^{\prime}
and
x^{\prime\prime}
, we use the following notation on
U
or
x(U)
. Let
x^{\prime i}=x^{i}
and
\partial_{i}^{\prime}=\partial_{i}
for
i\leq n^{\prime}
, and
x^{\prime\prime i}=x^{i}
and
\partial_{i}^{\prime\prime}=\partial_{i}
for
i>n^{\prime}
. Thus, when using
x^{\prime i}
or
\partial_{i}^{\prime}
, it will be understood that
i
runs in
\{1,\dots,n^{\prime}\}
, and, when using
x^{\prime\prime i}
or
\partial_{i}^{\prime\prime}
, it will be understood that
i
runs in
\{n^{\prime}+1,\dots,n\}
. For multi-indices of the form
J=\{j_{1},\dots,j_{r}\}
with
1\leq j_{1}<\dots<j_{r}\leq n
, let
dx^{J}=dx^{j_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge dx^{j_{r}}
be denoted by
dx^{\prime J}
or
dx^{\prime\prime J}
if
J
only contains indices in
\{1,\dots,n^{\prime}\}
or
\{n^{\prime}+1,\dots,n\}
, respectively. Using functions
f_{I}\in C^{\infty}(U)
,
d_{\mathcal{F}}
on
U
can be described by

(3.2.28)

d_{\mathcal{F}}(f_{I}\,dx^{\prime\prime I})=\partial^{\prime\prime}_{j}f_{I}\,dx^{\prime\prime j}\wedge dx^{\prime\prime I}\;.

Since the forms
dx^{\prime J}
are basic, (
3.2.8
) on
U
means that

(3.2.29)

d_{0,1}(f_{IJ}\,dx^{\prime\prime I}\wedge dx^{\prime J})=d_{\mathcal{F}}(f_{IJ}\,dx^{\prime\prime I})\wedge dx^{\prime J}\;,

using functions
f_{IJ}\in C^{\infty}(U)
.

Given a metric
g
on
M
, the local description

(3.2.30)

\delta_{0,-1}(f_{IJ}\,dx^{\prime\prime I}\wedge dx^{\prime J})=\delta_{\mathcal{F}}(f_{IJ}\,dx^{\prime\prime I})\wedge dx^{\prime J}

is satisfied just when
g
is bundle-like
[
ÁLK01
, Lemma 3.4]
; in fact this is a local expression of (
3.2.8
).

From (
3.2.25
), we also get that, on
C^{\infty}(U,\Lambda^{0,{\bullet}})
,

(3.2.31)

d_{1,0}={dx^{\prime i}\wedge}\,\Theta_{\boldsymbol{H}\partial^{\prime}_{i}}\;.

Since
d_{1,0}
is an anti-derivation, it follows that

(3.2.32)

d_{1,0}(f_{IJ}\,dx^{\prime\prime I}\wedge dx^{\prime J})=(-1)^{|I|}\Theta_{\boldsymbol{H}\partial^{\prime}_{i}}(f_{IJ}\,dx^{\prime\prime I})\wedge dx^{\prime i}\wedge dx^{\prime J}\;.

3.2.14.
Bigrading of leafwise forms

Suppose
\mathcal{F}
is subfoliation of another smooth foliation
\mathcal{G}
on
M
. Like in
Section

3.2.2
, for any choice of a complement
\boldsymbol{G}
of
T\mathcal{F}
in
T\mathcal{G}
, we have
\Lambda\mathcal{G}=\Lambda\mathcal{F}\otimes\Lambda\boldsymbol{G}
, obtaining a bigrading of
\Lambda\mathcal{G}
defined by
\Lambda^{u,v}\mathcal{G}=\Lambda^{v}\mathcal{F}\otimes\Lambda^{u}\boldsymbol{G}
, and a corresponding bigrading of
C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{G})
. The decomposition (
3.2.6
) has an obvious version for
d_{\mathcal{G}}
satisfying analogous properties.

3.2.15.
Push-forward and pull-back of leafwise currents

With the notation of
Section

2.8.5
, assume
\phi:M^{\prime}\to M
is a smooth submersion and
\mathcal{V}
oriented. Using any complement
\mathcal{H}
of
\mathcal{V}
in
TM^{\prime}
, we get a corresponding bigrading of
\Lambda M^{\prime}
with
\phi^{*}\Lambda M\otimes\Omega_{\text{\rm fiber}}M^{\prime}\equiv\Lambda^{\bullet,{\text{\rm top}}}M^{\prime}
. Suppose
M
is equipped with a smooth foliation
\mathcal{F}
, and let
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\phi^{*}\mathcal{F}
. Choose complements,
\boldsymbol{H}
of
T\mathcal{F}
in
TM
and
\boldsymbol{H}^{\prime}
of
T\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
in
TM^{\prime}
. The tangent map
\phi_{*}
defines an identity
\boldsymbol{H}^{\prime}\equiv\phi^{*}\boldsymbol{H}
. Consider the bigradings of
\Lambda M
and
\Lambda M^{\prime}
induced by
(\mathcal{F},\boldsymbol{H})
and
(\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{H}^{\prime})
. Then the maps (
2.8.11
)–(
2.8.14
) have restrictions compatible with
d_{0,1}
,

\displaystyle\phi_{*}:C^{\pm\infty}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\text{\rm cv}}}(M^{\prime};\Lambda^{u,\bullet})\to C^{\pm\infty}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\cdot}}(M;\Lambda^{u,\bullet-p})\quad(p=\dim\mathcal{V})\;,

\displaystyle\phi^{*}:C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda^{u,\bullet})\to C^{-\infty}(M^{\prime};\Lambda^{u,\bullet})\;.

For
u=0
, by (
3.2.1
), they are continuous homomorphisms,

(3.2.33)

\displaystyle\phi_{*}:(C^{\pm\infty}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\text{\rm cv}}}(M^{\prime};\Lambda\mathcal{F}^{\prime}),d_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}})\to(C^{\pm\infty}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\cdot}}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F}),d_{\mathcal{F}})\;,

(3.2.34)

\displaystyle\phi^{*}:(C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F}),d_{\mathcal{F}})\to(C^{-\infty}(M^{\prime};\Lambda\mathcal{F}^{\prime}),d_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}})\;.

Like in (
2.8.13
)–(
2.8.16
), the maps (
3.2.33
) and (
3.2.34
) can be also defined as the compositions

(3.2.35)

\displaystyle C^{\pm\infty}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\text{\rm cv}}}(M^{\prime};\Lambda\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\pi_{\text{\rm top}}}C^{\pm\infty}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\text{\rm cv}}}(M^{\prime};\phi^{*}\Lambda\mathcal{F})\xrightarrow{\phi_{*}}C^{\pm\infty}_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\cdot}}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})\;,

(3.2.36)

\displaystyle C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})\xrightarrow{\phi^{*}}C^{-\infty}(M^{\prime};\phi^{*}\Lambda\mathcal{F})\xrightarrow{\phi^{*}}C^{-\infty}(M^{\prime};\Lambda\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\;.

We can directly extend the definition of (
3.2.33
) to the case where
M^{\prime}
is a manifold with boundary, assuming
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is tangent or transverse to the boundary. It is a cochain map when
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is tangent to the boundary. If
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is transverse to the boundary and
\phi|_{\partial M^{\prime}}:\partial M^{\prime}\to M
is a submersion, the Stokes’ formula gives

(3.2.37)

\phi_{*}d_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}-d_{\mathcal{F}}\phi_{*}=(\phi|_{\partial M^{\prime}})_{*}\iota^{*}:C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(M^{\prime};\Lambda\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\to C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})\;,

where
\iota:\partial M^{\prime}\hookrightarrow M^{\prime}
.

3.2.16.
Leafwise homotopy operators

With the notation of
Section

2.8.6
, suppose
M
and
M^{\prime}
are equipped with respective smooth foliations
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
,
H
is a leafwise homotopy, and consider
H_{t}^{*}:C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})\to C^{\infty}(M^{\prime};\Lambda\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
(
t\in I
). Then we similarly get a continuous linear map
\mathsf{h}:C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})\to C^{\infty}(M^{\prime};\Lambda\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
, called a
leafwise homotopy operator
, which is homogeneous of degree
-1
and satisfies
H_{1}^{*}-H_{0}^{*}=\mathsf{h}d_{\mathcal{F}}+d_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}\mathsf{h}
. By using (
3.2.23
), (
3.2.37
) and (
3.2.33
),
\mathsf{h}
can be given as the composition

(3.2.38)

C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})\xrightarrow{H^{*}}C^{\infty}(M^{\prime}\times I;\Lambda(\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\times I))\xrightarrow{\fint_{I}}C^{\infty}(M^{\prime};\Lambda\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\;.

So
H_{0}
and
H_{1}
induce the same homomorphisms
H^{\bullet}C^{\infty}(\mathcal{F})\to H^{\bullet}C^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
and
\bar{H}^{\bullet}C^{\infty}(\mathcal{F})\to\bar{H}^{\bullet}C^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
.

Suppose
H
is transverse to
\mathcal{F}
and
H^{*}\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\times I
. Let
\operatorname{pr}_{1}:M^{\prime}\times I\to M^{\prime}
denote the first-factor projection. Consider the bigradings defined by
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
, and complements
\boldsymbol{H}
and
\boldsymbol{H}^{\prime}
of their tangent bundles. So
H_{*}
defines a homomorphism
\operatorname{pr}_{1}^{*}\boldsymbol{H}^{\prime}\to\boldsymbol{H}
whose restrictions to the fibers are isomorphisms. Then (
3.2.38
) is the bihomogeneous component of bidegree
(0,-1)
of (
2.8.17
).

If moreover
H
is a submersion, then (
3.2.33
) and (
3.2.34
) give a continuous extension of the maps of (
3.2.38
),

C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})\xrightarrow{H^{*}}C^{-\infty}(M^{\prime}\times I;\Lambda(\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\times I))\xrightarrow{\fint_{I}}C^{-\infty}(M^{\prime};\Lambda\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\;.

Their composition,
\mathsf{h}:C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})\to C^{-\infty}(M^{\prime};\Lambda\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
, satisfies
H_{1}^{*}-H_{0}^{*}=\mathsf{h}d_{\mathcal{F}}+d_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}\mathsf{h}
. Thus
H_{0}
and
H_{1}
also induce the same homomorphisms
H^{\bullet}C^{-\infty}(\mathcal{F})\to H^{\bullet}C^{-\infty}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
and
\bar{H}^{\bullet}C^{-\infty}(\mathcal{F})\to\bar{H}^{\bullet}C^{-\infty}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
.

3.3.
Witten’s perturbation on foliated manifolds

The operators acting on differential forms on foliated manifolds (
Section

3.2
) are extended now by taking Witten’s perturbations (
Section

2.9
).

3.3.1.
Perturbation vs bigrading

Using the notation of
Sections

2.9.1
and
3.2
, write
3
3

3

In
[
ÁLKL20
, Section 11]
, we took
\eta\in C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda^{0,1})
. However a general
\eta
is needed, and therefore additional work is required in
Sections

3.3
and
3.5
.

\eta=\eta_{0}+\eta_{1}
with
\eta_{0}\in C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda^{0,1})\equiv C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda^{1}\mathcal{F})
and
\eta_{1}\in C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda^{1,0})
. The condition
d\eta=0
means

(3.3.1)

d_{0,1}\eta_{0}=d_{1,0}\eta_{1}=d_{1,0}\eta_{0}+d_{0,1}\eta_{1}=0\;.

Like in (
3.2.6
) and (
3.2.10
), we get

d_{z}=d_{z,0,1}+d_{z,1,0}+d_{2,-1}\;,\quad\delta_{z}=\delta_{z,0,-1}+\delta_{z,-1,0}+\delta_{-2,1}\;,

where

\displaystyle d_{z,0,1}

\displaystyle=d_{0,1}+z\,{\eta_{0}\wedge}\;,

\displaystyle\quad d_{z,1,0}

\displaystyle=d_{1,0}+z\,{\eta_{1}\wedge}\;,

\displaystyle\delta_{z,0,-1}

\displaystyle=\delta_{0,1}-\bar{z}\,{\eta_{0}\lrcorner}\;,

\displaystyle\quad\delta_{z,-1,0}

\displaystyle=\delta_{-1,0}-\bar{z}\,{\eta_{1}\lrcorner}\;.

We will also use the perturbed versions of the operators (
3.2.12
), denoted by
D_{z,0}
,
D_{z,\perp}
and
\Delta_{z,0}
, defined with the operators
d_{z,i,1-i}
and
\delta_{z,i,i-1}
.

There is an obvious analog of (
3.2.7
) for the operators
d_{z,i,1-i}
, giving rise to analogous relations for the operators
\delta_{z,i,i-1}
. In particular,
d_{z,0,1}
and
\delta_{z,0,-1}
define leafwise differential complexes. By (
2.9.7
), the expressions (
3.2.14
) and (
3.2.15
) have direct extensions to this setting as well.

Concerning uniform leafwise/transverse ellipticity, symmetry and being non-negative, the perturbations
d_{z,0,1}
,
\delta_{z,0,-1}
,
D_{z,0}
,
D_{z,\perp}
and
\Delta_{z,0}
satisfy the same properties as
d_{0,1}
,
\delta_{0,-1}
,
D_{0}
,
D_{\perp}
and
\Delta_{0}
.

By (
3.2.31
), on a foliated chart
(U,x)
, we get

d_{1,0}\eta_{0}=dx^{\prime i}\wedge\Theta_{\boldsymbol{H}\partial_{i}}\eta_{0}=-\Theta_{\boldsymbol{H}\partial_{i}}\eta_{0}\wedge dx^{\prime i}\;.

But, writing
\eta_{1}=h_{i}\,dx^{\prime j}
, by (
3.3.1
),

d_{1,0}\eta_{0}=-d_{0,1}\eta^{1}=-\partial^{\prime\prime}_{j}h_{i}\,dx^{\prime\prime j}\wedge dx^{\prime i}\;.

So

\Theta_{\boldsymbol{H}\partial_{i}}\eta_{0}=\partial^{\prime\prime}_{j}h_{i}\,dx^{\prime\prime j}\;.

Then, since
\Theta_{\boldsymbol{H}\partial_{i}}
is a derivation, on
C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})
,

(3.3.2)

[\Theta_{\boldsymbol{H}\partial_{i}},{\eta_{0}\wedge}]={(\Theta_{\boldsymbol{H}\partial_{i}}\eta_{0})\wedge}=\partial^{\prime\prime}_{j}h_{i}\,{dx^{\prime\prime j}\wedge}=(d_{\mathcal{F}}h_{i})\wedge=[d_{\mathcal{F}},h_{i}]\;.

Thus (
3.2.26
) has the following change in this setting:

[\Theta_{\boldsymbol{H}\partial_{i}},d_{z,0,1}]=z[d_{\mathcal{F}},h_{i}]\;.

3.3.2.
Perturbation of the leafwise complex

Consider also the perturbed leafwise complex,
d_{\mathcal{F},z}=d_{\mathcal{F}}+z\eta_{0}\wedge
on
C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})
, or on
C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F}\otimes\Lambda N\mathcal{F})
, as well as its formal adjoint
\delta_{\mathcal{F},z}=\delta_{\mathcal{F}}-\bar{z}\,{\eta_{0}\lrcorner}
, and the induced perturbations,
D_{\mathcal{F},z}
of
D_{\mathcal{F}}
and
\Delta_{\mathcal{F},z}
of
\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}
. They satisfy the obvious versions of (
3.2.8
) and (
3.2.29
). If
g
is bundle-like, they also satisfy the obvious versions of (
3.2.11
) and (
3.2.30
).

3.3.3.
Perturbation with two parameters

For
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}
, the operators
D_{0,z,z^{\prime}}
and
\Delta_{0,z,z^{\prime}}
are defined like
D_{z,z^{\prime}}
and
\Delta_{z,z^{\prime}}
(
Section

2.9.7
), by using
d_{z,0,1}
and
\delta_{z^{\prime},0,-1}
instead of
d_{z}
and
\delta_{z^{\prime}}
. In other words,
D_{0,z,z^{\prime}}
is the component of
D_{z,z^{\prime}}
that preserves the transverse degree, and
\Delta_{z,z^{\prime}}=D_{z,z^{\prime}}^{2}
. They are uniformly leafwise elliptic, with a symmetric leading symbol.

The operators
D_{\mathcal{F},z,z^{\prime}}
and
\Delta_{\mathcal{F},z,z^{\prime}}
on
C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})
, or on
C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F}\otimes\Lambda N\mathcal{F})
, are defined like
D_{z,z^{\prime}}
and
\Delta_{z,z^{\prime}}
, by using
d_{\mathcal{F},z}
and
\delta_{\mathcal{F},z^{\prime}}
instead of
d_{z}
and
\delta_{z^{\prime}}
. They are also uniformly leafwise elliptic, with a symmetric leading symbol. If
g
is bundle-like, they also agree with
D_{z,z^{\prime}}
and
\Delta_{z,z^{\prime}}
via (
3.2.3
).

3.3.4.
Perturbation vs foliated maps

With the notation of
Sections

2.9.2
and
2.9.4
for a smooth foliated map
\phi:(M,\mathcal{F})\to(M,\mathcal{F})
, let
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
and
\tilde{\eta}_{j}
(
j=1,2
) be the lifts of
\mathcal{F}
and
\eta_{j}
to
\widetilde{M}
. Thus
\tilde{\eta}_{0}=d_{0,1}F\equiv d_{\mathcal{F}}F
and
\tilde{\eta}_{1}=d_{1,0}F
. Any lift
\tilde{\phi}
of
\phi
to
\widetilde{M}
is a foliated diffeomorphism of
(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}})
. The endomorphism
\phi^{t*}_{z}
of
(C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda),d_{z})
decomposes into the sum of bihomogeneous components
\phi^{*}_{z,i,-i},
like in
Section

3.2.11
, whose lifts to
C^{\infty}(\widetilde{M};\Lambda)
are
e^{z(\tilde{\phi}^{*}F-F)}\tilde{\phi}^{*}_{i,-i}
. Then
\phi^{*}_{z,0,0}
is an endomorphism of
(C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda),d_{z,0,1})
.

Similarly, the endomorphism
\phi^{*}
of
(C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F}\otimes\Lambda N\mathcal{F}),d_{\mathcal{F}})
given by (
3.2.22
) has a perturbation
\phi^{*}_{z}
, which is an endomorphism of
(C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F}\otimes\Lambda N\mathcal{F}),d_{\mathcal{F},z})
. We have
\phi^{*}_{z,0,0}\equiv\phi^{*}_{z}
like in (
3.2.24
). By restriction using (
3.2.1
), we get an endomorphism
\phi^{*}_{z}
of
(C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F}),d_{\mathcal{F},z})
, which is a perturbation of the endomorphism
\phi^{*}
of
(C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F}),d_{\mathcal{F}})
given by (
3.2.23
).

3.4.
Analysis on Riemannian foliations of bounded geometry

In this section,
\mathcal{F}
is a Riemannian foliation on a possibly open manifold
M
, equipped with a bundle-like metric
g
. We adopt the notation of
Section

2.4
for the metric concepts of
M
.

3.4.1.
Riemannian foliations of bounded geometry

The vector subbundle
\boldsymbol{H}:=T\mathcal{F}^{\perp}\subset TM
is called
horizontal
, giving rise to the concepts of
horizontal
vectors, vector fields and frames. Consider the corresponding splitting (
3.2.2
), obtaining orthogonal projections
\boldsymbol{V}:TM\to T\mathcal{F}
and
\boldsymbol{H}:TM\to\boldsymbol{H}
. The O’Neill tensors
[
O’N66
]
of the local Riemannian submersions defining
\mathcal{F}
can be combined to produce
(1,2)
-tensors
\mathsf{T}
and
\mathsf{A}
on
M
, defined by

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}_{E}F

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{H}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{V}E}(\boldsymbol{V}F)+\boldsymbol{V}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{V}E}(\boldsymbol{H}F)\;,

\displaystyle\mathsf{A}_{E}F

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{H}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{H}E}(\boldsymbol{V}F)+\boldsymbol{V}\nabla_{\boldsymbol{H}E}(\boldsymbol{H}F)\;,

for
E,F\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
. By
[
O’N66
, Theorem 4]
, if
M
is connected, given
g
and any
p\in M
, the foliation
\mathcal{F}
is determined by
\mathsf{T}
,
\mathsf{A}
and
T_{p}\mathcal{F}
.

The
adapted
Riemannian connection
\mathring{\nabla}
on
M
is defined by

\mathring{\nabla}_{E}F=\boldsymbol{V}\nabla_{E}(\boldsymbol{V}F)+\boldsymbol{H}\nabla_{E}(\boldsymbol{H}F)\;,

for
E,F\in\mathfrak{X}(M)

[
ÁLT91
]
. It satisfies the following properties
[
ÁLKL14
, Section 3]
,
[
ÁLKL20
, Section 5]
: for
V\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{F})
and
X\in C^{\infty}(M;\boldsymbol{H})
,

(3.4.1)

\displaystyle\nabla_{V}-\mathring{\nabla}_{V}=\mathsf{T}_{V}\;,\quad\nabla_{X}-\mathring{\nabla}_{X}=\mathsf{A}_{X}\;,

(3.4.2)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{V}([X,V])=\mathring{\nabla}_{X}V-\mathsf{T}_{V}X\;.

Moreover, the leaves are
\mathring{\nabla}
-totally geodesic, the
\mathring{\nabla}
-geodesics in the leaves are the
\nabla^{\mathcal{F}}
-geodesics, and
\mathring{\nabla}
and
\nabla
have the same geodesics orthogonal to the leaves.

Let
x^{\prime}:U\to\Sigma
be a distinguished submersion around any
p\in M
. Consider the Riemannian metric on
\Sigma
such that
x^{\prime}
is a Riemannian submersion, and let
\check{\nabla}
and
\check{\exp}
denote the corresponding Levi-Civita connection and exponential map of
\Sigma
. For all horizontal
X,Y\in\mathfrak{X}(U,\mathcal{F}|_{U})
, we have
\mathring{\nabla}_{X}Y\in\mathfrak{X}(U,\mathcal{F}|_{U})
and
\overline{\mathring{\nabla}_{X}Y}=\check{\nabla}_{\overline{X}}\overline{Y}

[
O’N66
, Lemma 1 (3)]
.

Let
\mathring{\exp}
denote the exponential map of the geodesic spray of
\mathring{\nabla}
(see e.g.
[
Poo81
, pp. 96–99]
). The maps
\mathring{\exp}
and
\check{\exp}
restrict to diffeomorphisms of some open neighborhoods,
V
of
0
in
T_{p}M
and
\check{V}
of
0
in
T_{x^{\prime}(p)}\Sigma
, to some open neighborhoods,
O
of
p
in
M
and
\check{O}
of
x^{\prime}(p)
in
\Sigma
. Moreover we can suppose
O\subset U
,
x^{\prime}_{*}(V)\subset\check{V}
and
x^{\prime}(O)\subset\check{O}
, and we have
x^{\prime}\,\mathring{\exp}=\check{\exp}\,x^{\prime}_{*}
on
V\cap T_{p}\mathcal{F}^{\perp}
. Let
\kappa=\kappa_{p}
be the smooth map of some neighborhood
W
of
0
in
T_{p}M
to
M
defined by

\kappa_{p}(X)=\mathring{\exp}_{q}(\mathring{P}_{\boldsymbol{H}X}\boldsymbol{V}X)\;,

where
q=\mathring{\exp}_{p}(\boldsymbol{H}X)
, and
\mathring{P}_{\boldsymbol{H}X}:T_{p}M\to T_{q}M
denotes the
\mathring{\nabla}
-parallel transport along the
\mathring{\nabla}
-geodesic
t\mapsto\mathring{\exp}_{p}(t\boldsymbol{H}X)
,
0\leq t\leq 1
, which is orthogonal to the leaves. Assume
W\subset V
and
\kappa(W)\subset O
, and therefore
x^{\prime}_{*}(W)\subset\check{V}
and
x^{\prime}\kappa(W)\subset\check{O}
. For
X,Y\in W
, we have
X-Y\in T_{p}\mathcal{F}
if and only if
\kappa(X)
and
\kappa(Y)
belong to the same plaque in
U

[
ÁLKL14
, Proposition 6.1]
. Moreover
x^{\prime}\kappa(X)=\check{\exp}\,x^{\prime}_{*}(X)
for all
X\in W\cap T_{p}\mathcal{F}^{\perp}
, and
\kappa
defines a diffeomorphism of some neighborhood of
0
in
T_{p}M
to some neighborhood of
p
in
M
with
\kappa_{*}\equiv\operatorname{id}:T_{0}(TM)\equiv T_{p}M\to T_{p}M

[
ÁLKL14
, Proposition 6.2 and Corollary 6.3]
. Consider identities
T_{p}\mathcal{F}^{\perp}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}}
and
T_{p}\mathcal{F}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime\prime}}
given by the choice of horizontal and vertical orthonormal frames at
p
. Then, for some open balls centered at the origin,
B^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}}
and
B^{\prime\prime}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime\prime}}
, we can assume
\kappa
is a diffeomorphism of
B^{\prime}\times B^{\prime\prime}
to some open neighborhood of
p
, obtaining foliated coordinates
x=(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}):=\kappa^{-1}:U:=\kappa(B^{\prime}\times B^{\prime\prime})\to B^{\prime}\times B^{\prime\prime}
, which are said to be
normal
. As usual,
g_{ij}
denotes the corresponding metric coefficients and
(g^{ij})=(g_{ij})^{-1}
. It is said that
\mathcal{F}
has
positive injectivity bi-radius
if there are normal foliated coordinates
x_{p}:U_{p}\to B^{\prime}\times B^{\prime\prime}
at every
p\in M
such that the balls
B^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime\prime}
are independent of
p
. Then
\mathcal{F}
is said to be of
bounded geometry
if it has positive injectivity bi-radius, and the functions
|\nabla^{m}R|
,
|\nabla^{m}\mathsf{T}|
and
|\nabla^{m}\mathsf{A}|
are uniformly bounded on
M
for every
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

[
ÁLKL14
, Definition 8.1]
.

Example 3.4.1
.

Let
H
be a connected Lie group,
L\vartriangleleft H
a normal connected Lie subgroup and
\Gamma\subset H
a discrete subgroup. Then the projection of the translates of
L
to
\Gamma\backslash H
are the leaves of a Riemannian foliation of bounded geometry with the bundle-like metric induced by any left invariant metric on
H
.

The following chart characterization of bounded geometry for Riemannian foliations is connected with another definition given by Sanguiao
[
San08
, Definition 1.7]
.

Theorem 3.4.2

(
[
ÁLKL14
, Theorem 8.4]
)
.

With the above notation,
\mathcal{F}
is of bounded geometry if and only if there is a normal foliated chart
x_{p}:U_{p}\to B^{\prime}\times B^{\prime\prime}
at every
p\in M
, such that the balls
B^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime\prime}
are independent of
p
, and the corresponding coefficients
g_{ij}
and
g^{ij}
, as family of smooth functions on
B^{\prime}\times B^{\prime\prime}
parametrized by
i
,
j
and
p
, lie in a bounded subset of the Fréchet space
C^{\infty}(B^{\prime}\times B^{\prime\prime})
.

For the rest of
Section

3.4
, let us assume that
\mathcal{F}
is of bounded geometry. Then
M
and the disjoint union of the leaves are of bounded geometry
[
ÁLKL14
, Remark 8.2 and Proposition 8.6]
. Consider the foliated charts
y_{p}:V_{p}\to B
and foliated charts
x_{p}:U_{p}\to B^{\prime}\times B^{\prime\prime}
given by
2.4.1
and
3.4.2
. Let
r_{0}
,
r^{\prime}_{0}
and
r^{\prime\prime}_{0}
denote the radii of the balls
B
,
B^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime\prime}
. For
0<r\leq r_{0}
,
0<r^{\prime}\leq r^{\prime}_{0}
and
0<r^{\prime\prime}\leq r^{\prime\prime}_{0}
, let
R_{r}
,
B^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}
and
B^{\prime\prime}_{r^{\prime\prime}}
denote the balls in
\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}}
and
\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime\prime}}
centered at the origin with radii
r
,
r^{\prime}
and
r^{\prime\prime}
, respectively. If
r
is small enough, then
V_{p,r}:=x_{p}^{-1}(B_{r})\subset U_{p}
for all
p

[
ÁLKL14
, Proposition 8.6]
. On the other hand, if
r^{\prime}+r^{\prime\prime}\leq r_{0}
, then
U_{p,r^{\prime},r^{\prime\prime}}:=x_{p}^{-1}(B^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}\times B^{\prime\prime}_{r^{\prime\prime}})\subset V_{p}
for all
p
by the triangle inequality. Then the following subsets are bounded in the corresponding Fréchet spaces
[
ÁLKL20
, Proposition 5.6 and 5.7]
:

(3.4.3)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \{\,x_{p}y_{p}^{-1}\mid p\in M\,\}&\subset C^{\infty}(B,\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime\prime}})\;,\\
\{\,y_{p}x_{p}^{-1}\mid p\in M\,\}&\subset C^{\infty}(B^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}\times B^{\prime\prime}_{r^{\prime\prime}},\mathbb{R}^{n})\;.\end{aligned}\right.

Let
E
be the Hermitian vector bundle of bounded geometry associated to the principal
\operatorname{O}(n)
-bundle of orthonormal frames on
M
and a unitary representation of
\operatorname{O}(n)
(Example
2.4.6
). Since
\nabla
on
TM
is of bounded geometry, it follows from (
3.4.1
) that
\mathring{\nabla}
is also of bounded geometry. Thus we get induced connections
\nabla
and
\mathring{\nabla}
of bounded geometry on
E
(Example
2.4.10
). By (
3.4.1
), we also get that
\mathring{\nabla}
can be used instead of
\nabla
to define equivalent versions of
\|{\cdot}\|_{C_{\text{\rm ub}}^{m}}
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{m}
in the spaces
C_{\text{\rm ub}}^{m}(M;E)
and
H^{m}(M;E)
. Since the subsets (
3.4.3
) are bounded, if
B^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime\prime}
are small enough, then we can use the coordinates
(U_{p},x_{p})
instead of coordinates of
(V_{p},y_{p})
to define equivalent versions of
\|{\cdot}\|^{\prime}_{C_{\text{\rm ub}}^{m}}
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle^{\prime}_{m}
. Similarly, given another bundle
F
like
E
, we can use the coordinates
(U_{p},x_{p})
instead of
(V_{p},y_{p})
to describe
\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm ub}}^{m}(M;E,F)
by requiring that the local coefficients form a bounded subset of the Fréchet space
C^{\infty}(B^{\prime}\times B^{\prime\prime};\mathbb{C}^{l^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{l*})
, where
l
and
l^{\prime}
are the ranks of
E
and
F
.

The condition of being leafwise differential operators of bounded geometry is preserved by compositions, and by taking transposes and formal adjoints. They form a filtered
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M)
-submodule
\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm ub}}(\mathcal{F};E,F)\subset\operatorname{Diff}(\mathcal{F};E,F)
. The notation
\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm ub}}(\mathcal{F};E)
is used if
E=F
; this is a filtered subalgebra of
\operatorname{Diff}(\mathcal{F};E)
. The concepts of
uniform leafwise ellipticity
for operators in
\operatorname{Diff}^{m}(\mathcal{F};E,F)
can be defined like uniform ellipticity (
Section

2.4.4
), and can be extended to leafwise differential complexes of order
m
like in
Section

2.1.14
. The same applies to
uniform transverse ellipticity
for operators in
\operatorname{Diff}^{m}(M;E,F)
and for differential complexes of order
m
. If
P\in\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm ub}}^{2}(\mathcal{F};E)
is uniformly leafwise elliptic and
Q\in\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm ub}}^{2}(M;E)
is uniformly transversely elliptic, and both
P
and
Q
are symmetric and non-negative, then
H^{s}(M;E)
(
s\in\mathbb{R}
) can be described with the scalar product
\langle u,v\rangle_{s}=\langle((1+P)^{s}+(1+Q)^{s})u,v\rangle
.

Let
\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm ub}}(\mathcal{F})
and
\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M,\mathcal{F})
denote the intersections of
\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M)
with
\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{F})
and
\mathfrak{X}(M,\mathcal{F})
, respectively. Then
\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm ub}}(\mathcal{F})
can be also described like in
Section

2.1.7
, using
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M)
and
\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm ub}}(\mathcal{F})
instead of
C^{\infty}(M)
and
\mathfrak{X}(M)
, and
\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm ub}}(\mathcal{F};E,F)
can be also described as the
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M)
-tensor product of
\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm ub}}(\mathcal{F})
and
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M;E,F)
.

3.4.2.
Operators of bounded geometry on differential forms

Since
\nabla
and
\mathring{\nabla}
are of bounded geometry on
TM
, the induced connections
\nabla
and
\mathring{\nabla}
on
\Lambda M
are of bounded geometry as well (
2.4.10
). Using
2.4.6
and
2.4.10
, we get that
\boldsymbol{H}
and
T\mathcal{F}
are also of bounded geometry, and the restrictions of
\mathring{\nabla}
to
\boldsymbol{H}
and
T\mathcal{F}
are of bounded geometry
[
ÁLKL20
, Section 6]
. Thus every
\Lambda^{u,v}M
is of bounded geometry (
2.4.7
), and
\mathring{\nabla}
is of bounded geometry on
\Lambda^{u,v}M
(
2.4.11
). So this also applies to
\Lambda\mathcal{F}\equiv\Lambda^{0,\bullet}M
.

By using
\mathring{\nabla}
instead of
\nabla
in the definitions of
\|{\cdot}\|_{C_{\text{\rm ub}}^{m}}
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{s}
(
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
), it follows that the spaces
C_{\text{\rm ub}}^{m}(M;\Lambda)
and
H^{s}(M;\Lambda)
inherit the bigrading of
\Lambda M
, and therefore
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M;\Lambda)
and
H^{\pm\infty}(M;\Lambda)
have an induced bigrading.

The following properties hold
[
ÁLK01
, Section 3]
,
[
ÁLKL20
, Section 6]
: the canonical projections
\Lambda M\to\Lambda^{u,v}M
, the operators
\star
,
\star_{\mathcal{F}}
or
\star_{\perp}
(under appropriate orientability assumptions), and the operators of (
3.2.6
), (
3.2.10
) and (
3.2.12
) are of bounded geometry; the differential complexes
d_{0,1}
and
\delta_{0,-1}
are uniformly leafwise elliptic; the differential operators
D_{0}
and
\Delta_{0}
are symmetric and uniformly leafwise elliptic; the differential operator
D_{\perp}
is uniformly transversely elliptic; and there is an endomorphism of bounded geometry,
K
of
\Lambda M
, such that
4
4

4

In
[
ÁLKL20
, Eq. (55)]
,
D_{0}
should be
\delta_{0,-1}
, like in
[
ÁLK01
, Proposition 3.1]
.

(3.4.4)

D_{\perp}\delta_{0,-1}+\delta_{0,-1}D_{\perp}=K\delta_{0,-1}+\delta_{0,-1}K\;.

Let us recall the definition of
K
and the proof of (
3.4.4
) because an extension will be needed, which is slightly more general than the extension considered in
[
ÁLKL20
, Section 11]
. Let
\Theta:\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{F})\to C^{\infty}(M;\boldsymbol{H}^{*}\otimes T\mathcal{F})
be the differential operator defined by
\Theta_{X}V=\boldsymbol{V}([X,V])
(the expression (
3.4.2
)), which induces a differential operator
\Theta:C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})\to C^{\infty}(M;\boldsymbol{H}^{*}\otimes\Lambda\mathcal{F})
. If

, then
\Theta_{X}
on

agrees with

on

via (

) (

). A homomorphism

can be locally defined by

\Xi_{X}=(-1)^{(n^{\prime\prime}-v)v}[\Theta_{X},\star_{\mathcal{F}}]\star_{\mathcal{F}}

on

is defined with any choice of local orientation of

on any normal foliated chart

defines an endomorphism of

. A computation using (

), (

), (

) gives

yielding (

) for the operators

3.4.3.
Foliated maps of bounded geometry

For

be a Riemannian foliation of bounded geometry on a manifold

with a bundle-like metric. To refer to each

3.4.4.
Leafwise functional calculus

Consider the notation of
Sections

3.4.1
and
3.4.2
. Like in (
2.9.10
) and (
2.9.11
), the hyperbolic equation

(3.4.8)

\partial_{t}\alpha_{t}=iD_{0}\alpha_{t}\;,\quad\alpha_{0}=\alpha\;,

has a unique solution on any open subset of
M
and for
t
in any interval containing zero, which satisfies
[
Che73
, Theorem 1.3]
,
[
Roe87
, Proposition 1.2]

(3.4.9)

\operatorname{supp}\alpha_{t}\subset\operatorname{Pen}_{\mathcal{F}}(\operatorname{supp}\alpha,|t|)\;.

The operators
D_{0}
and
\Delta_{0}
, with domain
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(M;\Lambda)
, are essentially self-adjoint in
L^{2}(M;\Lambda)

[
Che73
, Theorem 2.2]
, and their self-adjoint extensions are also denoted by
D_{0}
and
\Delta_{0}
. The functional calculus of
D_{0}
, given by the spectral theorem, assigns a (bounded) operator
\psi(D_{0})
to every (bounded) measurable function
\psi
on
\mathbb{R}
; in particular, we have a unitary operator
e^{itD_{0}}
and a bounded self-adjoint operator
e^{-t\Delta_{0}}
on
L^{2}(M;\Lambda)
. The notation
\Pi_{0}=e^{-\infty\Delta_{0}}
is used for the orthogonal projection of
L^{2}(M;\Lambda)
to
\ker D_{0}=\ker\Delta_{0}
in
L^{2}(M;\Lambda)
.

If
\alpha\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(M;\Lambda)
, the solution of (
3.4.8
) is given by
\alpha_{t}=e^{itD_{0}}\alpha
. For every
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, there is some
C_{m}\geq 0
such that
[
Tay81
, Section IV.2]
,
[
Roe87
, Proposition 1.4]
,
[
ÁLKL20
, Proposition 7.1]

(3.4.10)

\|e^{itD_{0}}\alpha\|_{m}\leq e^{C_{m}|t|}\|\alpha\|_{m}\;,

for all
\alpha\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(M;\Lambda)
.

On the other hand, like in (
2.9.12
), for
\psi\in\mathcal{S}
, we get

(3.4.11)

\psi(D_{0})=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\hat{\psi}(\xi)e^{i\xi D_{0}}\,d\xi\;.

Taking
\psi\in\mathcal{A}
(
Section

2.9.8
), it follows from (
2.9.15
), (
3.4.10
) and (
3.4.11
) that, for every
m\in\mathbb{Z}\cup\{\pm\infty\}
, the functional calculus
\psi\mapsto\psi(D_{0})
restricts to a continuous homomorphisms of
\mathbb{C}[z]
-modules and algebras
[
Roe87
, Proposition 4.1]
,
[
ÁLKL20
, Proposition 7.2]
,

(3.4.12)

\mathcal{A}\to\operatorname{End}(H^{m}(M;\Lambda))\;,\quad\mathcal{A}\to\operatorname{End}(H^{\infty}(M;\Lambda))\;.

By taking coefficients in
o(M)
and transposition (see
Section

3.2.9
),
\psi\mapsto\psi(D_{0})
also induces continuous homomorphisms of
\mathbb{C}[z]
-modules and algebras,

(3.4.13)

\mathcal{A}\to\operatorname{End}(H^{-m}(M;\Lambda))\;,\quad\mathcal{A}\to\operatorname{End}(H^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda))\;.

3.4.5.
Leafwise Hodge decomposition

According to (
3.4.12
), the operator
e^{-t\Delta_{0}}
(
t>0
) restricts to a continuous endomorphism of
H^{\infty}(M;\Lambda)
. As pointed out in
[
San08
]
, using the bounded geometry and uniform leafwise/transverse ellipticity of the operators considered in

3.4.2
, and applying (
3.4.4
) and (
3.4.12
), the arguments of
[
ÁLK01
]
can be adapted to show the following, where

is considered on

[
San08
]
,
[
ÁLKL20
, Theorem 7.3 and Corollary 7.4]
: there is a TVS-direct-sum decomposition,

(3.4.14)

H^{\infty}(M;\Lambda)=\ker\Delta_{0}\oplus\overline{\operatorname{im}d_{0,1}}\oplus\overline{\operatorname{im}\delta_{0,-1}}\;,

whose terms are orthogonal in
L^{2}(M;\Lambda)
; the map

(3.4.15)

[0,\infty]\times H^{\infty}(M;\Lambda)\to H^{\infty}(M;\Lambda)\;,\quad(t,\alpha)\mapsto e^{-t\Delta_{0}}\alpha\;,

is well-defined and continuous; and

induces a TVS-isomorphism

(3.4.16)

\bar{H}(H^{\infty}(M;\Lambda),d_{0,1})\xrightarrow{\cong}\ker\Delta_{0}\;,

whose inverse is induced by
\ker\Delta_{0}\hookrightarrow H^{\infty}(M;\Lambda)
. The analogs of (
3.4.14
)–(
3.4.16
) with
H^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda)
are also true.

By (

) and (

), we can consider

as a topological subcomplex of

and

is used for its cohomology and reduced cohomology. By (

),

on

is also given by
\delta_{0,-1}
. Thus we get the operators

and
\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}=D_{\mathcal{F}}^{2}=\delta_{\mathcal{F}}d_{\mathcal{F}}+d_{\mathcal{F}}\delta_{\mathcal{F}}
on
H^{\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})
, which are essentially self-adjoint in

. Let

be the orthogonal projection to

in

. Then (

)–(
3.4.16
) have obvious versions with

,
\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}
,
\bar{H}^{\bullet}H^{\infty}(\mathcal{F})
and
\Pi_{\mathcal{F}}

[
San08
]
,
[
ÁLKL20
, Section 7]
.

3.4.7.
Description of some Schwartz kernels

In
Section

3.4.6
,
\overline{Z}
defines the structure of a transversely complete
\mathbb{R}
-Lie foliation on
\mathcal{F}
, and therefore we can consider also the notation of
Section

3.1.11
. Then the lift
\tilde{g}
of
g
to
\widetilde{M}
is a bundle-like metric of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}=\pi^{*}\mathcal{F}
, and
\widetilde{Z}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm ub}}(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}})
. Assume
D_{*}\widetilde{Z}=\partial_{x}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathbb{R})
and
\bar{\phi}^{t}(x)=t+x
; hence
\phi^{t}
preserves every leaf of
\mathcal{F}
if and only if
t\in\operatorname{Hol}\mathcal{F}
(
Section

3.1.11
).

For any
\psi\in\mathcal{A}
and
f\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(\mathbb{R})
, we have the smoothing operator
P
given by (
3.4.17
), and a similar smoothing operator
\widetilde{P}
is defined by using
\tilde{\phi}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
instead of
\phi
and
\mathcal{F}
. We are going to describe their Schwartz kernels.

Let
\mathfrak{G}=\operatorname{Hol}(M,\mathcal{F})
and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}}=\operatorname{Hol}(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}})
, whose source and range maps are denoted by
\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{r}:\mathfrak{G}\to M
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{r}}:\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}}\to\widetilde{M}
(
Section

3.1.4
). Since the leaves of
\mathcal{F}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
have trivial holonomy groups, the smooth immersions
(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{s}):\mathfrak{G}\to M^{2}
and
(\tilde{\boldsymbol{r}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}):\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}}\to\widetilde{M}^{2}
are injective, with images
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{F}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}
. Via these injections, the restriction
\pi\times\pi:\mathcal{R}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}\to\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{F}}
corresponds to the Lie groupoid homomorphism
\pi_{\mathfrak{G}}:=\operatorname{Hol}(\pi):\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}}\to\mathfrak{G}
(
Section

3.1.7
), which is a covering map with
\operatorname{Aut}(\pi_{\mathfrak{G}})\equiv\Gamma
. In fact, since
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
is defined by the fiber bundle
D
, we get that
\mathcal{R}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}
is a regular submanifold of
\widetilde{M}^{2}
, and
(\tilde{\boldsymbol{r}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}):\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}}\to\mathcal{R}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}
is a diffeomorphism. We may write
\mathfrak{G}\equiv\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{F}}
and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}}\equiv\mathcal{R}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}
.

Consider the
C^{\infty}
vector bundles,
S=\boldsymbol{r}^{*}\Lambda\mathcal{F}\otimes\boldsymbol{s}^{*}(\Lambda\mathcal{F}\otimes\Omega\mathcal{F})
over
\mathfrak{G}
and
\widetilde{S}={\tilde{\boldsymbol{r}}^{*}\Lambda\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}\otimes{\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{*}(\Lambda\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\otimes\Omega\widetilde{\mathcal{F}})}
over
\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}}
. Note that
\widetilde{S}\equiv\pi_{\mathfrak{G}}^{*}S
, and any
k\in C^{\infty}(\mathfrak{G};S)
lifts via
\pi_{\mathfrak{G}}
to a section
\tilde{k}\in C^{\infty}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}};\widetilde{S})
. Since
\pi
restricts to diffeomorphisms of the leaves of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
to the leaves of
\mathcal{F}
, it follows that
\tilde{k}\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm p}}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{G}};\widetilde{S})
if and only if
k\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm p}}(\mathfrak{G};S)
.

For any
\psi\in\mathcal{R}
, the collection of Schwartz kernels
k_{L}:=K_{\psi(D_{L})}
, for all leaves
L
of
\mathcal{F}
, defines a section
k=k_{\psi}
of
S
. This also applies to the operators
\psi(D_{\widetilde{L}})
on the leaves
\widetilde{L}
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
, obtaining a section
\tilde{k}=\tilde{k}_{\psi}
of
\widetilde{S}
.

If
\hat{\psi}\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(\mathbb{R})
, then
k_{\psi}\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm p}}(\mathfrak{G};S)
, and the global action of
k_{\psi}
on
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})
(
Section

3.1.5
) agrees with the restriction of the operator
\psi(D_{\mathcal{F}})
on

defined by the version of (

) for
D_{\mathcal{F}}

[
ÁLKL20
, Proposition 10.1]
. Precisely, if
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\psi}\subset[-R,R]

for all

by Remark

.

Let
\widetilde{\Lambda}=D^{*}dx\equiv dx
, which is an invariant transverse volume form of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
defining the same transverse orientation as

. Since

. These

|\widetilde{\Lambda}|
and
|\Lambda|
of

and
\mathcal{F}
.

Let
\tilde{p},\tilde{q}\in\widetilde{M}
over

, and write
t_{\tilde{p},\tilde{q}}=D(\tilde{q})-D(\tilde{p})
. If
\psi\in\mathcal{A}
, then
6
6

6

There is an error in the statement of

).

(3.4.21)

K_{P}(p,q)\equiv\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}T_{\gamma}^{*}\,\tilde{\phi}^{t_{\tilde{p},\tilde{q}}-h(\gamma)*}\tilde{k}\big(T_{\gamma}\tilde{\phi}^{t_{\tilde{p},\tilde{q}}-h(\gamma)}(\tilde{p}),\tilde{q}\big)\,f(t_{\tilde{p},\tilde{q}}-h(\gamma))\,|\Lambda|(q)\;,

defining a convergent series in

ÁLKL20
, Proposition 10.3]
. Here, the identity

is used, and the leafwise part of the density of

at

is given by the density of
\tilde{k}(\cdot,\tilde{q})
at
\tilde{q}
.

3.5.
Witten’s operators on Riemannian foliations of bounded geometry

Consider the notation of
Section

3.3.1
with our assumption that
\mathcal{F}
is Riemannian of bounded geometry. Suppose also that
\eta\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M;\Lambda^{1})
, and therefore
\eta_{0}\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M;\Lambda^{0,1})\equiv C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M;\Lambda^{1}\mathcal{F})
and
\eta_{1}\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M;\Lambda^{1,0})
. Thus the operators
d_{z,i,1-i}
,
\delta_{z,i,i-1}
,
D_{z,0}
,
D_{z,\perp}
and
\Delta_{z,0}
are of bounded geometry. Arguing like in (
3.4.4
), we get

(d_{1,0}\,{\eta_{0}\lrcorner}+{\eta_{0}\lrcorner}\,d_{1,0})(f_{IJ}\,dx^{\prime\prime I}\wedge dx^{\prime J})\\
\begin{aligned} &=(K\,{\eta_{0}\lrcorner}+{\eta_{0}\lrcorner}\,K)(f_{IJ}\,dx^{\prime\prime I}\wedge dx^{\prime J})\\
&\phantom{={}}{}+(-1)^{(n^{\prime\prime}+1)|I|+n^{\prime\prime}}\big(\star_{\mathcal{F}}[\Theta_{\boldsymbol{H}\partial^{\prime}_{i}},{\eta_{0}\wedge}]\star_{\mathcal{F}}(f_{IJ}\,dx^{\prime\prime I})\big)\wedge dx^{\prime i}\wedge dx^{\prime\prime J}\;.\end{aligned}

Using (
3.2.30
) and (
3.3.2
), it follows that

(d_{1,0}\,{\eta_{0}\lrcorner}+{\eta_{0}\lrcorner}\,d_{1,0}-K\,{\eta_{0}\lrcorner}-{\eta_{0}\lrcorner}\,K)(f_{IJ}\,dx^{\prime\prime I}\wedge dx^{\prime J})\\
\begin{aligned} &=(-1)^{(n^{\prime\prime}+1)|I|+n^{\prime\prime}}\big(\star_{\mathcal{F}}[d_{\mathcal{F}},h_{i}]\star_{\mathcal{F}}(f_{IJ}\,dx^{\prime\prime I})\big)\wedge dx^{\prime i}\wedge dx^{\prime\prime J}\\
&=-(-1)^{|I|}\big(\delta_{\mathcal{F}}(h_{i}f_{IJ}\,dx^{\prime\prime I})\big)\wedge dx^{\prime i}\wedge dx^{\prime\prime J}\\
&\phantom{={}}{}+(-1)^{|I|}\big(\delta_{\mathcal{F}}(f_{IJ}\,dx^{\prime\prime I})\big)\wedge\eta_{1}\wedge dx^{\prime\prime J}\\
&=-\delta_{0,-1}\big(\eta_{1}\wedge f_{IJ}\,dx^{\prime\prime I}\wedge dx^{\prime\prime J}\big)-\eta_{1}\wedge\delta_{0,-1}\big(f_{IJ}\,dx^{\prime\prime I}\wedge dx^{\prime\prime J}\big)\;.\end{aligned}

This shows that

(3.5.1)

d_{1,0}\,{\eta_{0}\lrcorner}+{\eta_{0}\lrcorner}\,d_{1,0}=K\,{\eta_{0}\lrcorner}+{\eta_{0}\lrcorner}\,K-\delta_{0,-1}\,{\eta_{1}\wedge}-{\eta_{1}\wedge}\,\delta_{0,-1}\;.

Combining (
3.4.5
) and (
3.5.1
), and using that
{\eta_{0}\lrcorner}
is an anti-derivation, we compute

d_{z,1,0}\delta_{z,0,-1}+\delta_{z,0,-1}d_{z,1,0}\\
\begin{aligned} &=K\delta_{0,-1}+\delta_{0,-1}K-\bar{z}(K\,{\eta_{0}\lrcorner}+{\eta_{0}\lrcorner}\,K-\delta_{0,-1}\,{\eta_{1}\wedge}-{\eta_{1}\wedge}\,\delta_{0,-1})\\
&\phantom{={}}{}+z({\eta_{1}\wedge}\,\delta_{0,-1}+\delta_{0,-1}\,{\eta_{1}\wedge})+|z|^{2}({\eta_{1}\wedge}\,{\eta_{0}\lrcorner}+{\eta_{0}\lrcorner}\,{\eta_{1}\wedge})\\
&=K\delta_{z,0,-1}+\delta_{z,0,-1}K+2\Re z\,({\eta_{1}\wedge}\,\delta_{0,-1}+\delta_{0,-1}\,{\eta_{1}\wedge})\\
&\phantom{={}}{}-2\Re z\,\bar{z}({\eta_{1}\wedge}\,{\eta_{0}\lrcorner}+{\eta_{0}\lrcorner}\,{\eta_{1}\wedge})\\
&=K_{z}\delta_{z,0,-1}+\delta_{z,0,-1}K_{z}\;,\end{aligned}

where
K_{z}=K+2\Re z\,{\eta_{1}\wedge}
is an endomorphism of
\Lambda M
of bounded geometry. Using also the analog of (
3.2.7
) for the operators
d_{z,i,1-i}
, it follows that
7
7

7

The equality

d_{-\bar{z},1,0}\delta_{z,0,-1}+\delta_{z,0,-1}d_{-\bar{z},1,0}=K\delta_{z,0,-1}+\delta_{z,0,-1}K

is also true, but this does not fit the analog of (
3.2.7
).

(3.5.2)

D_{z,\perp}\delta_{z,0,-1}+\delta_{z,0,-1}D_{z,\perp}=K_{z}\delta_{z,0,-1}+\delta_{z,0,-1}K_{z}\;.

Using this key equality, we get straightforward generalizations of all results in
Sections

3.4.4
and
3.4.5
for
d_{z,0,1}
,
\delta_{z,0,-1}
,
D_{z,0}
and
\Delta_{z,0}
, which also have obvious versions for
d_{\mathcal{F},z}
,
\delta_{\mathcal{F},z}
,
D_{\mathcal{F},z}
and
\Delta_{\mathcal{F},z}
. Let
\Pi_{0,z}
and
\Pi_{\mathcal{F},z}
denote the corresponding versions of
\Pi_{0}
and
\Pi_{\mathcal{F}}
.

Let
\phi:(M,\mathcal{F})\to(M,\mathcal{F})
be a smooth foliated map of bounded geometry. Since
\eta\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M;\Lambda)
, we get versions of the continuity of (

) and (

) for
\phi^{*}_{z}
, assuming

is uniformly metrically proper for the second one (
Section

2.4.6
). In particular, this applies to any foliated flow of
\mathbb{R}
-local bounded geometry (
Section

2.4.7
),
\phi=\{\phi^{t}\}
on
(M,\mathcal{F})
, using its unique lift
\tilde{\phi}=\{\tilde{\phi}^{t}\}
to
\widetilde{M}
. Then the definitions and results of

and

have obvious twisted extensions using

,

and

. The subscript “

,

,

,

,

and

in this setting.

Recall that

and

are uniformly leafwise elliptic with a symmetric leading symbol (

). Moreover, they are of bounded geometry. Then the obvious version of (

) with

has a unique solution, which satisfies the obvious analogs of (

(

) can be defined by the analog of (

and

and

and

. The double subscript “

” may be added to the notation

,

,

,

and

,

are not symmetric, and therefore the results of

Chapter 4
Foliations with simple foliated flows

4.1.
Simple foliated flows

4.1.1.
Simple flows

Let
\phi:\Omega\to M
be a smooth local flow, where
\Omega
is an open neighborhood of
M\times\{0\}
in
M\times\mathbb{R}
. Let
Z\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
be the infinitesimal generator. For
p\in M
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
, let

\Omega_{p}=\{\,t\in\mathbb{R}\mid(p,t)\in\Omega\,\}\;,\quad\Omega^{t}=\{\,q\in M\mid(q,t)\in\Omega\,\}\;,

and let
\phi^{t}=\phi(\cdot,t):\Omega^{t}\to M
. The fixed point set is

\operatorname{Fix}(\phi)=\{\,p\in M\mid p\in\operatorname{Fix}(\phi^{t})\ \forall t\in\Omega_{p}\ \text{close enough to $0$}\,\}\;,

which equals the zero set of
Z
. Recall that a fixed point
p
of
\phi
is called
simple
(or
transverse
) if it is a simple fixed point of
\phi^{t}
for all
t\neq 0
close enough to
0
in
\Omega_{p}
(see
Section

2.9.11
). In this case, the associated number
\epsilon_{p}(\phi^{t})
, defined in (
2.9.23
), is independent of
t>0
close enough to
0
in
\Omega_{p}
, and is denoted by
\epsilon_{p}=\epsilon_{p}(\phi)
. If the fixed points of
\phi
are simple, then
\operatorname{Fix}(\phi)
is a discrete subset of
M
. For a fixed point
p
, we can write
\phi^{t}_{*}=e^{tA}
on
T_{p}M
for some endomorphism
A
of
T_{p}M
. Then
p
is simple just when
A
is an automorphism.

Now, assume
Z
is complete, and therefore we can take
\Omega=M\times\mathbb{R}
. On
M\setminus\operatorname{Fix}(\phi)
, let
N\phi
denote the normal bundle of the foliation defined by the orbits of
\phi
; i.e.,
N_{p}\phi=T_{p}M/\mathbb{R}Z(p)
for every
p\in M\setminus\operatorname{Fix}(\phi)
. Let
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}(\phi)
denote the set of closed orbits of
\phi
(without including fixed points). For any
c\in\mathcal{C}
, let
\ell(c)
denote its minimum positive period. For every subset
I\subset\mathbb{R}
, let

\mathcal{C}_{I}=\mathcal{C}_{I}(\phi)=\{\,c\in\mathcal{C}\mid\ell(c)\in I\,\}\;.

The nonzero periods of all closed orbits form the set

\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}(\phi)=\{\,k\ell(c)\mid c\in\mathcal{C},\ k\in\mathbb{Z}^{\times}\,\}\;.

For all
c\in\mathcal{C}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
p\in c
, let
\phi^{k\ell(c)}_{*}:N_{p}\phi\to N_{p}\phi
be the homomorphism induced by
\phi^{k\ell(c)}_{*}:T_{p}M\to T_{p}M
. Recall that
c
is called
simple
when the eigenvalues of
\phi^{k\ell(c)}_{*}:N_{p}\phi\to N_{p}\phi
are different from
1
for some (and therefore for all)
p\in c
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{\times}
; in this case, let

\epsilon_{c}(k)=\epsilon_{c}(k,\phi)=\operatorname{sign}\det\big(\operatorname{id}-\phi^{k\ell(c)}_{*}:N_{p}\phi\to N_{p}\phi\big)\in\{\pm 1\}\;.

Every simple closed orbit
c
, there are neighborhoods,
V
where
c
in
M
and
I
of
\ell(c)
in
\mathbb{R}
, such that
c
is the only closed orbit whose first positive period is in
I
, and moreover that
V\cap\operatorname{Fix}(\phi)=\emptyset
.

The flow
\phi
is called
simple
if all of its fixed points and closed orbits are simple. If moreover
M
is closed, then
\operatorname{Fix}(\phi)
is finite, and
\mathcal{C}_{I}(\phi)
are finite for all compact
I\subset\mathbb{R}
. Therefore
\mathcal{P}(\phi)
is a discrete subset of
\mathbb{R}
.

4.1.2.
Transversely simple foliated flows

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a transversely oriented smooth foliation of codimension one on a closed manifold
M
. We assume
M
is closed for the sake of simplicity, but the concepts and properties recalled here also have obvious versions when
M
is a manifold with boundary, where both
\mathcal{F}
and
\phi
are tangent to
\partial M
. Some generalizations to non-compact manifolds will be also indicated and needed.

Let
\phi=\{\phi^{t}\}
be a foliated flow on
M
and let
Z\in\mathfrak{X}(M,\mathcal{F})
be its infinitesimal generator (
Section

3.1.7
). Let
M^{0}
be the union of leaves preserved by
\phi
, and let
M^{1}=M\setminus M^{0}
. The
\phi
-invariant set
M^{0}
is compact because it is the zero set of
\overline{Z}\in\overline{\mathfrak{X}}(M,\mathcal{F})\subset C^{\infty}(M;N\mathcal{F})
. Therefore the
\phi
-invariant set
M^{1}
is open in
M
. Moreover
\phi
is transverse to the leaves on
M^{1}
. So there is a canonical isomorphism
N\phi\cong T\mathcal{F}
on
M^{1}
, and
\mathcal{F}
is transitive at every point of
M^{1}
(
Section

3.1.9
); in particular, the leaves in
M^{1}
have no holonomy. Consider the notation of
Sections

3.1.2
,
3.1.3
and
3.1.7
, using the notation
(x_{k},y_{k})
instead of
(x^{\prime}_{k},x^{\prime\prime}_{k})
because
\operatorname{codim}\mathcal{F}=1
. Let
\bar{\phi}
be the local flow on
\Sigma
generated by
\overline{Z}\in\mathfrak{X}(\Sigma,\mathcal{H})
. Via the homeomorphism
M/\mathcal{F}\to\Sigma/\mathcal{H}
induced by the coordinates
x_{k}:U_{k}\to\Sigma_{k}
, the leaves preserved by
\phi
correspond to the
\mathcal{H}
-orbits preserved by
\bar{\phi}
, which indeed form
\operatorname{Fix}(\bar{\phi})
because the
\mathcal{H}
-orbits are totally disconnected.
\overline{Z}
is
\mathcal{H}
-invariant, and
\bar{\phi}
is
\mathcal{H}
-equivariant in an obvious sense.

Since
\dim\Sigma=1
, for all simple
\bar{p}\in\operatorname{Fix}(\bar{\phi})
, there is some
\varkappa=\varkappa_{\bar{p}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
such that
\bar{\phi}^{t}_{*}\equiv e^{\varkappa t}
on
T_{\bar{p}}\Sigma\equiv\mathbb{R}
. By the
\mathcal{H}
-equivariance of
\bar{\phi}
, we get
\varkappa_{\bar{p}}=\varkappa_{\bar{q}}
for all
\bar{q}\in\mathcal{H}(\bar{p})\subset\operatorname{Fix}(\bar{\phi})
. Thus we can use the notation
\varkappa_{L}=\varkappa_{\bar{p}}
if
\mathcal{H}(\bar{p})
corresponds to a leaf
L
.

The leaves preserved by
\phi
that correspond to simple fixed points of
\bar{\phi}
are said to be
transversely simple
. If all leaves preserved by
\phi
are transversely simple, then
\phi
(or
Z
) is called
transversely simple
; if moreover its closed orbits are simple, then
\phi
(or
Z
) is said to be
weakly simple
. If
\phi
is weakly simple, every closed orbit is contained either in
M^{0}
or in
M^{1}
, and its (possibly non-simple) fixed points belong to
M^{0}
.

Suppose
\phi
is transversely simple unless otherwise stated. Then
M^{0}
is a finite union of compact leaves because every fixed point of
\bar{\phi}
is isolated. For any point
p
in a preserved leaf
L
, there are foliated coordinates
(x,y):U\to\Sigma\times B
, where
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}
is an open interval containing
0
, so that
[
ÁLKL22
, Lemma 3.2]

(4.1.1)

x(p)=0\;,\quad\overline{Z}=\varkappa_{L}x\partial_{x}\;,\quad\bar{\phi}^{t}(x)=e^{\varkappa_{L}t}x\;.

Hence the following properties hold
[
ÁLKL22
, Propositions 3.4 and 3.5]
:

(3)

\mathcal{F}
is almost without holonomy with finitely many leaves with holonomy.

(4)

The holonomy groups of the compact leaves are groups of germs at
0
of homotheties on
\mathbb{R}
, for some choice of
\{U_{k},(x_{k},y_{k})\}
.

According to
3
and Remark
3.1.6

2
, we can consider Hector’s description with this choice of
M^{0}
and
M^{1}
, even though there may be leaves without holonomy in
M^{0}
. With the notation of
Section

3.1.12
, since
\boldsymbol{\pi}:(M_{l},\mathcal{F}_{l})\to(M,\mathcal{F})
is a foliated local embedding and
\boldsymbol{\pi}:\partial M_{l}\to M^{0}
a local diffeomorphism, any
A\in\mathfrak{X}(M,M^{0})
has a lift
A_{l}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm b}}(M_{l})
. Moreover
A_{l}\in\mathfrak{X}(M_{l},\mathcal{F}_{l})
if
A\in\mathfrak{X}(M,\mathcal{F})
. Thus any (foliated) flow
\zeta=\{\zeta^{t}\}
on
(M,\mathcal{F})
preserving
M^{0}
can be lifted via
\boldsymbol{\pi}
to a (foliated) flow
\zeta_{l}=\{\zeta_{l}^{t}\}
on
(M_{l},\mathcal{F}_{l})
preserving
\partial M_{l}
. If a foliated flow
\zeta
on
(M,\mathcal{F})
is weakly simple, then
\zeta_{l}
is also weakly simple (on the foliated manifold with boundary
(M_{l},\mathcal{F}_{l})
). The restrictions
A_{l}|_{\mathring{M}_{l}}\equiv A|_{M^{1}_{l}}
and
\zeta_{l}|_{\mathring{M}_{l}}\equiv\zeta|_{M^{1}_{l}}
are also denoted by
A_{l}
and
\zeta_{l}
. In particular, this notation applies to
Z
and
\phi
, obtaining
Z_{l}
and
\phi_{l}=\{\phi_{l}^{t}\}
, which induces the structure of a complete
\mathbb{R}
-Lie foliation on
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{l}
. According to
Section

3.1.12
, we consider the transverse orientation of every
\mathcal{F}_{l}
so that
\boldsymbol{\pi}:(M_{l},\mathcal{F}_{l})\to(M,\mathcal{F})
is compatible with the transverse orientations. However, we will consider the transverse orientation of every
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{l}
defined by
\overline{Z}_{l}
. Now Hector’s description has the following more specific cases
[
ÁLKL22
, Section 3]
:

(3)

\mathcal{F}
is given by a fiber bundle
M\to S^{1}
with connected fibers.

(4)

\mathcal{F}
is an
\mathbb{R}
-Lie foliation with dense leaves.

(5)

M^{0}\neq\emptyset
,
\operatorname{Hol}L\cong\mathbb{Z}
for all leaves
L\subset M^{0}
, and the foliations
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{l}
are given by fiber bundles
M^{1}_{l}\to S^{1}
with connected fibers.

(6)

M^{0}\neq\emptyset
,
\operatorname{Hol}L
is a finitely generated abelian group of rank
>1
for all leaves
L\subset M^{0}
, and all foliations
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{l}
are minimal
\mathbb{R}
-Lie foliations.

The case
3
can be considered as a model
1
with empty boundary, avoiding the use of models
0
, or it can be cut into models
0
by adding a finite number of leaves without holonomy to
M^{0}
. Except in this case,
M^{1}
is just the transitive point set of
\mathcal{F}
, and
\mathfrak{X}(M,\mathcal{F})
spans
\mathfrak{X}(M,M^{0})
as
C^{\infty}(M)
-module by (
4.1.1
).

For every leaf
L\subset M^{0}
, its holonomy homomorphism
\boldsymbol{h}=\boldsymbol{h}_{L}
is induced by a homomorphism
\hat{h}=\hat{h}_{L}:\pi_{1}L\to\operatorname{Diffeo}^{+}(\mathbb{R},0)
whose image consists of homotheties; i.e., writing
\Gamma=\Gamma_{L}=\pi_{1}L/\ker\hat{h}
,
\hat{h}
induces a monomorphism
h=h_{L}:\Gamma\to\operatorname{Diffeo}^{+}(\mathbb{R},0)
,
\gamma\mapsto h_{\gamma}
, with
h_{\gamma}(x)=a_{\gamma}x
for some monomorphism
\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}^{+}\equiv(\mathbb{R}^{+},\times)
,
\gamma\mapsto a_{\gamma}=a_{L,\gamma}
. The restriction of
\mathcal{F}
to some neighborhood of
L
can be described as the suspension of
h
(
Section

3.1.2
); its definition for this case will be recalled in
Section

4.2.1
. On the other hand, every
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{l}
has a Fedida’s description, which will be better analyzed in
Section

4.3.4
.

Remark 4.1.1
.

The concepts recalled in this subsection do not need the compactness of
M
. Only the completeness of
Z
and compactness of
M^{0}
are needed to extend the indicated notions and properties.

4.1.3.
Existence of simple foliated flows

For a transversely oriented foliation
\mathcal{F}
of codimension one on a closed foliated manifold
M
, the following conditions are equivalent
[
ÁLKL22
, Propositions 6.1 and 6.3 and Theorem 6.9]
:

(7)

It satisfies
3
and
4
.

(8)

There is a transversely simple foliated flow.

(9)

There is a weakly simple foliated flow.

(10)

There is a simple foliated flow.

Moreover the families of foliated flows
\phi
satisfying
8
,
9
or
10
induce the same family of local flows
\bar{\phi}
on
\Sigma
. In the case
9
, it can be also assumed that
M^{0}\subset\operatorname{Fix}(\phi)
and there are no closed orbits in some neighborhood of
M^{0}
, obtaining the same family of local flows
\bar{\phi}
on
\Sigma
.

A more precise description of the foliations satisfying these equivalent conditions is given in
[
ÁLKL22
, Theorem 6.9]
, but it will not be needed here.

4.2.
Case of suspension foliations

4.2.1.
Suspension foliations defined with homotheties

For a pointed connected closed manifold
(L,p)
, let
\hat{h}:\pi_{1}L=\pi_{1}(L,p)\to\operatorname{Diffeo}^{+}(\mathbb{R},0)
be a homomorphism whose image consists of homotheties, like in
Section

4.1
. Therefore, writing
\Gamma=\pi_{1}L/\ker\hat{h}
,
\hat{h}
induces a monomorphism
h:\Gamma\to\operatorname{Diffeo}^{+}(\mathbb{R},0)
,
\gamma\mapsto h_{\gamma}
, where
h_{\gamma}(x)=a_{\gamma}x
for some monomorphism
\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
\gamma\mapsto a_{\gamma}
; in particular,
\Gamma
is abelian, torsion free and finitely generated. Let
\pi=\pi_{L}:(\widetilde{L},\tilde{p})\to(L,p)
be the pointed regular covering map with
\pi_{1}\widetilde{L}=\pi_{1}(\widetilde{L},\tilde{p})\equiv\ker\hat{h}
, and therefore
\operatorname{Aut}(\pi)\equiv\Gamma
. Like in
Sections

2.9.2
and
2.9.9
, the canonical left action of every
\gamma\in\Gamma
on
\widetilde{L}
is denoted by
T_{\gamma}
or
\tilde{y}\mapsto\gamma\cdot\tilde{y}
, and write
[\tilde{y}]=\pi(\tilde{y})
for
\tilde{y}\in\widetilde{L}
. For the diagonal left
\Gamma
-action on
\widetilde{M}=\mathbb{R}\times\widetilde{L}
,
\gamma\cdot(x,\tilde{y})=(a_{\gamma}x,\gamma\cdot\tilde{y})
, the orbit space
M=\Gamma\backslash\widetilde{M}
is called a
suspension manifold
. The canonical projection
\pi_{M}:\widetilde{M}\to M
is a
\Gamma
-cover with deck transformations
h_{\gamma}\times T_{\gamma}
(
\gamma\in\Gamma
). Write
[x,\tilde{y}]=\pi_{M}(x,\tilde{y})
for
(x,\tilde{y})\in\widetilde{M}
.

Let
\widetilde{\varpi}:\widetilde{M}\to\widetilde{L}
denote the second-factor projection, and let
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
be the foliation on
\widetilde{M}
with leaves
\{x\}\times\widetilde{L}
(
x\in\mathbb{R}
). Since
\widetilde{\varpi}
is
\Gamma
-equivariant, we get an induced fiber bundle map
\varpi:M\to L
, defined by
\varpi([x,\tilde{y}])=\pi(\tilde{y})
. On the other hand, since
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
and its canonical transverse orientation are
\Gamma
-invariant, we also get an induced transversely oriented foliation
\mathcal{F}
on
M
, called a
suspension foliation
, which is transverse to the fibers of
\varpi
. The typical fiber of
\varpi
is
\mathbb{R}
because the corresponding fibers of
\widetilde{\varpi}
and
\varpi
can be identified via
\pi_{M}
. Since
0
is fixed by the
\Gamma
-action on
\mathbb{R}
, the leaf
\{0\}\times\widetilde{L}\equiv\widetilde{L}
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
is
\Gamma
-invariant, and
\pi_{M}(\{0\}\times\widetilde{L})\equiv L
is a compact leaf of
\mathcal{F}
. The other leaves of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
are diffeomorphic via
\pi_{M}
to the corresponding leaves of
\mathcal{F}
because the elements of
\Gamma\setminus\{e\}
have no fixed points in
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
. Given any
\tilde{p}\in\widetilde{L}
with
\pi(\tilde{p})=p\in L
, the fiber
\varpi^{-1}(p)\equiv\widetilde{\varpi}^{-1}(\tilde{p})=\mathbb{R}\times\{\tilde{p}\}\equiv\mathbb{R}
is a global transversal of
\mathcal{F}
through
p\equiv[0,\tilde{p}]
. Note that the holonomy homomorphism
\boldsymbol{h}:\pi_{1}L\to\operatorname{Hol}L
is induced by
h
, and therefore
\widetilde{L}^{\text{\rm hol}}\equiv\widetilde{L}
(
Section

3.1.2
). The standard orientation of
\mathbb{R}
induces a transverse orientation of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
, which is
\Gamma
-invariant because the image of
h
consists of orientation preserving homotheties, giving rise to a transverse orientation of
\mathcal{F}
. Let
\boldsymbol{H}\subset TM
and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{H}}\subset T\widetilde{M}
be the linear subbundles of vectors tangent to the fibers of
\varpi
and
\widetilde{\varpi}
, which induce bigradings of
\Lambda M
and
\Lambda\widetilde{M}
satisfying
d_{2,-1}=0
(
Sections

3.2.2
and
3.2.3
). For
\tilde{p}\in\widetilde{M}
and
p\in M
, we will use the identities
\Lambda_{\tilde{p}}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\equiv\Lambda_{\widetilde{\varpi}(\tilde{p})}\widetilde{L}
and
\Lambda_{p}\mathcal{F}\equiv\Lambda_{\varpi(p)}L
induced by
\widetilde{\varpi}
and
\varpi
.

4.2.2.
Transversely simple flows on suspension foliations

Let
\phi=\{\phi^{t}\}
be any transversely simple foliated flow on
M
and let
Z\in\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm com}}(M,\mathcal{F})
be its infinitesimal generator. Let us recall the notation of
Section

4.1.2
in this case (see also
Section

3.1.12
). Without loss of generality, we can assume
M^{0}=\pi_{M}(\{0\}\times\widetilde{L})\equiv L
for the description around a compact leaf. By (
4.1.1
), we can suppose the lifts of
\phi
and
Z
to
\widetilde{M}
, denoted by
\tilde{\phi}
and
\widetilde{Z}
, are of the form

(4.2.1)

\tilde{\phi}^{t}(x,\tilde{y})=(e^{\varkappa t}x,\tilde{\phi}_{x}^{t}(\tilde{y}))\;,\quad\widetilde{Z}=(\varkappa x\partial_{x},\widetilde{Z}_{x})\;,

for some
\varkappa\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
, and smooth families,
\{\,\widetilde{\phi}^{t}_{x}\mid x,t\in\mathbb{R}\,\}\subset\operatorname{Diffeo}(\widetilde{L})
and
\{\,\widetilde{Z}_{x}\mid x\in\mathbb{R}\,\}\subset\mathfrak{X}(\widetilde{L})
, with
\tilde{\phi}_{x}^{0}=\operatorname{id}_{\widetilde{L}}
. In particular,
\widetilde{Z}_{0}
and
\tilde{\phi}_{0}^{t}
are the restrictions of
\widetilde{Z}
and
\tilde{\phi}^{t}
to
\widetilde{L}\equiv\{0\}\times\widetilde{L}
. Thus
\widetilde{Z}_{0}
is
\Gamma
-invariant and
\tilde{\phi}_{0}=\{\tilde{\phi}_{0}^{t}\}
is
\Gamma
-equivariant, inducing the restrictions of
Z
and
\phi^{t}
to
L
, denoted by
Z_{0}
and
\phi_{0}=\{\phi_{0}^{t}\}
; we may also use the notation
Z_{L}=Z_{0}
and
\phi_{L}=\{\phi_{L}^{t}\}=\{\phi_{0}^{t}\}
. The
\Gamma
-equivariance of
\tilde{\phi}^{t}
and the
\Gamma
-invariance of
\widetilde{Z}
mean that, for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
x,t\in\mathbb{R}
,

(4.2.2)

T_{\gamma}\tilde{\phi}_{x}^{t}=\tilde{\phi}_{a_{\gamma}x}^{t}T_{\gamma}\;,\quad T_{\gamma*}\widetilde{Z}_{x}=\widetilde{Z}_{a_{\gamma}x}\;.

The only preserved leaf of
\tilde{\phi}
,
\{0\}\times\widetilde{L}\equiv\widetilde{L}
, is transversely simple. Now
\widetilde{M}^{0}=\{0\}\times\widetilde{L}
and
\widetilde{M}^{1}=\widetilde{M}\setminus\widetilde{M}^{0}=\mathbb{R}^{\times}\times\widetilde{L}
, which has two connected components,
\widetilde{M}^{1}_{\pm}=\mathbb{R}^{\pm}\times\widetilde{L}
. In this case,
\widetilde{M}_{\pm}=(\mathbb{R}^{\pm}\cup\{0\})\times\widetilde{L}
, with
\mathring{\widetilde{M}}_{\pm}=\widetilde{M}^{1}_{\pm}
and
\partial\widetilde{M}_{\pm}=\widetilde{M}^{0}\equiv\widetilde{L}
. The connected components of
M^{1}=M\setminus M^{0}
are
M^{1}_{\pm}=\pi_{M}(\widetilde{M}^{1}_{\pm})
, and we have
M_{\pm}=\pi_{M}(\widetilde{M}_{\pm})
, with
\mathring{M}_{\pm}=M^{1}_{\pm}
and
\partial M_{\pm}=M^{0}\equiv L
. The restriction
\pi_{M}:\widetilde{M}_{\pm}\to M_{\pm}
will be denoted by
\pi_{M_{\pm}}
. The foliations
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{\pm}=\mathring{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}_{\pm}
on
\widetilde{M}^{1}_{\pm}=\mathring{\widetilde{M}}_{\pm}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm}
on
\widetilde{M}_{\pm}
are restrictions of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
, and the foliations
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{\pm}=\mathring{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm}
on
M^{1}_{\pm}=\mathring{M}_{\pm}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\pm}
on
M_{\pm}
are restrictions of
\mathcal{F}
. We have
\boldsymbol{M}=M_{+}\sqcup M_{-}
(resp.,
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{M}}=\widetilde{M}_{+}\sqcup\widetilde{M}_{-}
), equipped with the combination
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}
(resp.,
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}}
) of
\mathcal{F}_{+}
and
\mathcal{F}_{-}
(resp.,
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{+}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{-}
). The restriction of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}
to
\mathring{\boldsymbol{M}}
is denoted by
\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}}
. Now the map
\boldsymbol{\pi}:\boldsymbol{M}\to M
(resp.,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\pi}}:\widetilde{\boldsymbol{M}}\to\widetilde{M}
) is the combination of the inclusion maps
M_{\pm}\hookrightarrow M
(resp.,
\widetilde{M}_{\pm}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{M}
). The combination of the maps
\pi_{M_{\pm}}
is a covering projection
\pi_{\boldsymbol{M}}:\widetilde{\boldsymbol{M}}\to\boldsymbol{M}
. Moreover
\varpi
(resp.,
\widetilde{\varpi}
) restricts to global collar neighborhoods of the boundaries,
\varpi_{\pm}:M_{\pm}\to\partial M_{\pm}\equiv L
(resp.,
\widetilde{\varpi}_{\pm}:\widetilde{M}_{\pm}\to\partial\widetilde{M}_{\pm}\equiv\widetilde{L}
), whose combination is a global collar neighborhood of the boundary,
\boldsymbol{\varpi}:\boldsymbol{M}\to\partial\boldsymbol{M}\equiv L\sqcup L
(resp.,
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varpi}}:\widetilde{\boldsymbol{M}}\to\partial\widetilde{\boldsymbol{M}}\equiv\widetilde{L}\sqcup\widetilde{L}
). Like in
Section

4.2.1
, for
\tilde{p}\in\widetilde{M}_{\pm}
and
p\in M_{\pm}
, we have canonical identities
\Lambda_{\tilde{p}}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm}\equiv\Lambda_{\widetilde{\varpi}_{\pm}(\tilde{p})}\widetilde{L}
and
\Lambda_{p}\mathcal{F}_{\pm}\equiv\Lambda_{\varpi_{\pm}(p)}L
.

Recall also that any
A\in\mathfrak{X}(M,\mathcal{F})
, with foliated flow
\zeta=\{\zeta^{t}\}
, induces vector fields
A_{\pm}\in\mathfrak{X}(M_{\pm},\mathcal{F}_{\pm})\subset\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm b}}(M_{\pm})
, with foliated flows
\zeta_{\pm}=\{\zeta_{\pm}^{t}\}
, whose restrictions to
\mathring{M}_{\pm}\equiv M^{1}_{\pm}
are denoted in the same way. In particular, we get
Z_{\pm}
with flow
\phi_{\pm}=\{\phi_{\pm}^{t}\}
. The same kind of notation is used for vector fields and flows induced by elements of
\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm com}}(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}})
. Then
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{\pm}\equiv\mathring{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm}
on
M^{1}_{\pm}\equiv\mathring{M}_{\pm}
is a transversely complete
\mathbb{R}
-Lie foliation with the structure defined by
Z_{\pm}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm com}}(M^{1}_{\pm},\mathcal{F}^{1}_{\pm})
(see Remark
4.1.1
). In its Fedida’s description (
Section

3.1.9
),
\widetilde{M}^{1}_{\pm}
is the holonomy covering of
M^{1}_{\pm}
, whose group of deck transformations is also
\Gamma
, the developing map
D_{\pm}:\widetilde{M}^{1}_{\pm}\to\mathbb{R}
is given by
D_{\pm}(x,y)=\varkappa^{-1}\ln|x|=:t
, the holonomy monomorphism
h_{\pm}:\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
is given by
h_{\pm}(\gamma)=\varkappa^{-1}\ln a_{\gamma}
, and therefore
\operatorname{Hol}\mathcal{F}_{\pm}=\{\,\varkappa^{-1}\ln a_{\gamma}\mid\gamma\in\Gamma\,\}
. Thus
\widetilde{Z}_{\pm}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm com}}(\widetilde{M}^{1}_{\pm},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{\pm})
is
D_{\pm}
-projectable and
(D_{\pm})_{*}\widetilde{Z}_{\pm}=\partial_{t}
. Furthermore
\phi^{t}_{\pm}
preserves every leaf of
\mathcal{F}_{\pm}
if and only if
t=h_{\pm}(\gamma)=\varkappa^{-1}\ln a_{\gamma}
for some
\gamma\in\Gamma
(
Section

3.1.11
).

Let
\tilde{\xi}=\{\tilde{\xi}^{t}\}
be the weakly simple foliated flow on
(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}})
, with infinitesimal generator is
\widetilde{Y}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm com}}(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}})
, given by

(4.2.3)

\tilde{\xi}^{t}(x,\tilde{y})=(e^{\varkappa t}x,\tilde{y})\;,\quad\widetilde{Y}=(\varkappa x\partial_{x},0)\;.

We have
\overline{\widetilde{Y}}=\overline{\widetilde{Z}}\equiv\varkappa x\partial_{x}
,
\operatorname{Fix}(\tilde{\xi}^{t})=\widetilde{L}
, and the orbits of
\tilde{\xi}^{t}
on
\mathring{\widetilde{M}}_{\pm}
are the fibers of the restriction
\widetilde{\varpi}:\mathring{\widetilde{M}}_{\pm}\to\widetilde{L}
. Since
\tilde{\xi}^{t}
is
\Gamma
-equivariant and
\widetilde{Y}
is
\Gamma
-invariant, they project to
M
obtaining a weakly simple foliated flow
\xi^{t}
on
(M,\mathcal{F})
and its infinitesimal generator
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M,\mathcal{F})
. We have
\overline{Y}=\overline{Z}\equiv\varkappa x\partial_{x}
,
\operatorname{Fix}(\xi^{t})=L
, and the orbits of
\xi^{t}
on
\mathring{M}_{\pm}
are the fibers of the restriction
\varpi:\mathring{M}_{\pm}\to L
.

On the one hand, we consider the restriction of the transverse orientations of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
and
\mathcal{F}
to
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\pm}
, and, on the other hand, we consider the transverse orientations of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{\pm}
and
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{\pm}
induced by
\widetilde{Z}_{\pm}
and
Z_{\pm}
, which corresponds to the standard orientation of
\mathbb{R}
by
D_{\pm}
(
Sections

3.1.12
and
4.1.2
). They agree on
\widetilde{M}^{1}_{+}
and
M^{1}_{+}
(resp.,
\widetilde{M}^{1}_{-}
and
M^{1}_{-}
) if and only if
\kappa>0
(resp.,
\kappa<0
).

4.2.3.
A defining form of
\mathcal{F}

For
k=\operatorname{rank}\Gamma
, fix generators
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{k}
of
\Gamma
. Let
c_{i}
be a piecewise smooth loop in
L
based at
p
such that
[c_{i}]\in\pi_{1}(L,p)
projects to
\gamma_{i}
, and let
a_{i}=a_{\gamma_{i}}
. By the universal coefficients and Hurewicz theorems, there are closed
1
-forms
\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{k}
on
L
such that
\delta_{ij}=\langle[\beta_{i}],[c_{j}]\rangle=\int_{0}^{1}c_{j}^{*}\beta_{i}
and
\langle[\beta_{i}],\ker\hat{h}\rangle=0
. Every
\pi^{*}\beta_{i}
is exact on
\widetilde{L}
. Let
\eta=-\ln(a_{1})\,\beta_{1}-\dots-\ln(a_{k})\,\beta_{k}
and
\tilde{\eta}=\pi^{*}\eta=dF
for some
F\in C^{\infty}(\widetilde{L})
. Note that
h(\Gamma)\cong\Gamma
is the group of periods of
\eta
. With some abuse of notation, write also
F\equiv\widetilde{\varpi}^{*}F\in C^{\infty}(\widetilde{M})
, and

\displaystyle\eta\equiv\varpi^{*}\eta\in C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda^{0,1})\equiv C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda^{1}\mathcal{F})\;,

\displaystyle\tilde{\eta}\equiv\widetilde{\varpi}^{*}\tilde{\eta}=\pi_{M}^{*}\eta=dF\in C^{\infty}(\widetilde{M};\Lambda^{0,1})\equiv C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda^{1}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}})\;,

using (
3.2.5
). Thus
\eta=\eta_{0}
in this case, with the notation of
Section

3.3.1
. It is easy to check that

(4.2.4)

T_{\gamma}^{*}F=F-\ln a_{\gamma}

for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
, yielding
T_{\gamma}^{*}e^{F}=a_{\gamma}^{-1}e^{F}
. It easily follows that the
1
-form
\tilde{\omega}=|\varkappa|^{-1}e^{F}\,dx
on
\widetilde{M}
is
\Gamma
-invariant. (Recall that the
\Gamma
-action on
\widetilde{M}
is given by
\gamma\cdot(x,\tilde{y})=(a_{\gamma}x,\gamma\cdot\tilde{y})
.) Furthermore
T\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}=\ker\tilde{\omega}
and
\tilde{\omega}
defines the transverse orientation of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
. Therefore
\tilde{\omega}
induces a
1
-form
\omega
on
M
satisfying
T\mathcal{F}=\ker\omega
and defining the transverse orientation of
\mathcal{F}
. On the other hand, it is easy to compute
d\tilde{\omega}=\tilde{\eta}\wedge\tilde{\omega}
, yielding
d\omega=\eta\wedge\omega
. The vector field
\widetilde{X}=(|\varkappa|e^{-F}\partial_{x},0)\equiv|\varkappa|e^{-F}\partial_{x}\in C^{\infty}(\widetilde{M};\widetilde{\boldsymbol{H}})
is determined by
\tilde{\omega}(\widetilde{X})=1
. Thus
\widetilde{X}
is
\Gamma
-invariant and induces the vector field
X\in C^{\infty}(M;\boldsymbol{H})
satisfying
\omega(X)=1
. So
\widetilde{X}
and
X
also define the transverse orientations of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
and
\mathcal{F}
. On the other hand,
\tilde{\omega}(\widetilde{Z})=\operatorname{sign}(\varkappa)e^{F}x
by (
4.2.1
), yielding
\operatorname{sign}\tilde{\omega}(\widetilde{Z}_{\pm})=\pm\operatorname{sign}(\varkappa)
, and therefore

\operatorname{sign}\omega(Z_{\pm})=\pm\operatorname{sign}(\varkappa)\;.

So the transverse orientation of
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{\pm}
is also defined by the restrictions to
M^{1}_{\pm}
of
\pm\operatorname{sign}(\varkappa)\overline{X}
or
\pm\operatorname{sign}(\varkappa)\omega
.

4.2.4.
A defining function of
M^{0}

Let
\tilde{\rho}=e^{F}x
, which is a defining function of
\widetilde{M}^{0}\equiv\widetilde{L}
on the whole of
\widetilde{M}
. Moreover
\tilde{\rho}
is
\Gamma
-invariant by (
4.2.4
), and therefore it induces a defining function
\rho
of
M^{0}\equiv L
on the whole of
M
. It is easy to compute

d\tilde{\rho}=e^{F}(x\tilde{\eta}+dx)=\tilde{\rho}\tilde{\eta}+|\varkappa|\tilde{\omega}\;,

yielding

(4.2.5)

d\rho=\rho\eta+|\varkappa|\omega\;,

and therefore

(4.2.6)

d\rho\wedge\omega=\rho\eta\wedge\omega\;.

Since
\tilde{\xi}^{t*}\tilde{\rho}=e^{\varkappa t}\tilde{\rho}
by (
4.2.3
), we also get

(4.2.7)

\xi^{t*}\rho=e^{\varkappa t}\rho\;.

The global tubular neighborhood
\widetilde{\varpi}:\widetilde{M}\to\widetilde{L}\equiv\widetilde{M}^{0}
can be trivialized with
\tilde{\rho}
, obtaining
\widetilde{M}\equiv\mathbb{R}_{\tilde{\rho}}\times\widetilde{L}_{\widetilde{\varpi}}
besides
\widetilde{M}=\mathbb{R}_{x}\times\widetilde{L}_{\widetilde{\varpi}}
. Thus the global tubular neighborhood
\varpi:M\to L\equiv M^{0}
can be trivialized with
\rho
, obtaining
M\equiv\mathbb{R}_{\rho}\times L_{\varpi}
. According to
Section

2.6
, we have corresponding vector fields
\partial_{x},\partial_{\tilde{\rho}}\in\mathfrak{X}(\widetilde{M})
and
\partial_{\rho}\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
, and operators
\partial_{x},\partial_{\tilde{\rho}}\in\operatorname{Diff}^{1}(\widetilde{M};\Lambda)
and
\partial_{\rho}\in\operatorname{Diff}^{1}(M;\Lambda)
. We compute

|\varkappa|\partial_{\tilde{\rho}}=|\varkappa|\partial_{\tilde{\rho}}(x)\,\partial_{x}=|\varkappa|\partial_{\tilde{\rho}}(e^{-F}\tilde{\rho})\,\partial_{x}=|\varkappa|e^{-F}\,\partial_{x}=\widetilde{X}\in\mathfrak{X}(\widetilde{M})\;.

It easily follows that

|\varkappa|\partial_{\tilde{\rho}}=|\varkappa|e^{-F}\,\partial_{x}\in\operatorname{Diff}^{1}(M;\Lambda)\;.

But, with the notation of
Section

3.2.12
, it is easy to check that

\partial_{x}=\Theta_{\partial_{x}}\in\operatorname{Diff}^{1}(\widetilde{M};\Lambda)\;.

Since
\widetilde{X}\in C^{\infty}(\widetilde{M};\widetilde{\boldsymbol{H}})
and
d_{1,0}F=0
, we can apply (
3.2.27
) on
C^{\infty}(\widetilde{M};\Lambda)
to get

\Theta_{\widetilde{X}}=|\varkappa|e^{-F}\Theta_{\partial_{x}}=|\varkappa|e^{-F}\partial_{x}=|\varkappa|\partial_{\tilde{\rho}}\in\operatorname{Diff}^{1}(\widetilde{M};\Lambda)\;.

So, by derivation formula of
\Theta_{\widetilde{X}}
, (
3.2.26
) and (
3.2.27
), and since
\partial_{x}\in\mathfrak{X}(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}})\cap C^{\infty}(\widetilde{M};\widetilde{\boldsymbol{H}})
and
\mathcal{L}_{\widetilde{X}}\tilde{\eta}=0
,

\displaystyle[d_{0,1},\Theta_{\widetilde{X}}]

\displaystyle=|\varkappa|[d_{0,1},e^{-F}\Theta_{\partial_{x}}]=|\varkappa|[d_{0,1},e^{-F}]\Theta_{\partial_{x}}

\displaystyle=-|\varkappa|e^{-F}{\tilde{\eta}\wedge}\,\Theta_{\partial_{x}}=-{\tilde{\eta}\wedge}\,\Theta_{\widetilde{X}}=-\Theta_{\widetilde{X}}\,{\tilde{\eta}\wedge}\;.

Hence
|\varkappa|\partial_{\rho}=X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
, and

(4.2.8)

\displaystyle|\varkappa|\partial_{\rho}=\Theta_{X}\in\operatorname{Diff}^{1}(M;\Lambda)\;,

(4.2.9)

\displaystyle[\Theta_{X},d_{0,1}]={\eta\wedge}\,\Theta_{X}=\Theta_{X}\,{\eta\wedge}\in\operatorname{Diff}^{1}(M;\Lambda)\;.

Note also that
\Theta_{\widetilde{X}}\tilde{\omega}=0
, and therefore
\Theta_{X}\omega=0
. Moreover,
\Theta_{X}\eta=0
.

For any
\epsilon>0
, the restriction
\widetilde{\varpi}:\widetilde{T}_{\epsilon}:=\{|\tilde{\rho}|<\epsilon\}\to\widetilde{L}
is a smaller tubular neighborhood of
\widetilde{L}
in
\widetilde{M}
