, which induces a smaller tubular neighborhood
\varpi:T_{\epsilon}:=\{|\rho|<\epsilon\}\to L
of
L
in
M
. Let
\widetilde{T}^{1}_{\epsilon}=\widetilde{T}_{\epsilon}\cap\widetilde{M}^{1}
,
\widetilde{T}^{1}_{\pm,\epsilon}=\widetilde{T}_{\epsilon}\cap\widetilde{M}^{1}_{\pm}
,
T^{1}_{\epsilon}=T_{\epsilon}\cap M^{1}
and
T^{1}_{\pm,\epsilon}=T_{\epsilon}\cap M^{1}_{\pm}
.

4.2.5.
A boundary-defining function of
M_{\pm}

Now consider the boundary-defining function
\tilde{\rho}_{\pm}=e^{F}|x|=\pm\tilde{\rho}=|\tilde{\rho}|
on
\widetilde{M}_{\pm}
, which is
\Gamma
-invariant, and therefore it induces a boundary-defining function
\rho_{\pm}
on
M_{\pm}
satisfying

(4.2.10)

\rho_{\pm}=\pm\rho=|\rho|\;.

If there is no danger of confusion, with some abuse of notation, these boundary-defining functions may be simply denoted by
\tilde{\rho}
and
\rho
. Furthermore, we have the boundary-defining function
\tau=\tau_{\pm}:=|x|
on
\widetilde{M}_{\pm}
.

The global collar neighborhood
\widetilde{\varpi}:\widetilde{M}_{\pm}\to\partial\widetilde{M}_{\pm}\equiv\widetilde{L}
can be trivialized with, either
\tau
, or
\tilde{\rho}
, obtaining

(4.2.11)

\displaystyle\widetilde{M}_{\pm}

\displaystyle\equiv[0,\infty)_{\tau}\times\widetilde{L}_{\widetilde{\varpi}}

(4.2.12)

\displaystyle\equiv[0,\infty)_{\tilde{\rho}}\times\widetilde{L}_{\widetilde{\varpi}}\;.

So the global collar neighborhood
\varpi:M_{\pm}\to\partial M\equiv L
can be trivialized with
\rho
, obtaining

(4.2.13)

M_{\pm}\equiv[0,\infty)_{\rho}\times L_{\varpi}\;.

By (
4.2.5
), (
4.2.8
), (
4.2.9
) and (
4.2.10
),

(4.2.14)

\displaystyle d\rho=\rho\eta\pm|\varkappa|\omega\;,

(4.2.15)

\displaystyle[\partial_{\rho},d_{0,1}]={\eta\wedge}\,\partial_{\rho}\in\operatorname{Diff}^{1}(M_{\pm};\Lambda)\;,

using the operator
\partial_{\rho}\in\operatorname{Diff}^{1}(M_{\pm};\Lambda)
introduced in
2.5.2
and
2.5.3
. We have
\partial_{\rho}\omega=0
, and therefore
\partial_{\rho}\eta=0
.

Observe that
\tilde{\rho}^{-1}\tilde{\omega}=|\varkappa x|^{-1}dx
is a basic form of
\mathring{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}_{\pm}
, and therefore
\rho^{-1}\omega
is a basic form of
\mathring{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm}
. The transverse orientation of
\mathring{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm}=\mathcal{F}^{1}_{\pm}
is also defined by the basic form

(4.2.16)

\omega_{\text{\rm b},\pm}=\operatorname{sign}(\varkappa)\,\rho^{-1}\omega=\pm\operatorname{sign}(\varkappa)\,\rho_{\pm}^{-1}\omega=\operatorname{sign}(\omega(Z_{\pm}))\,\rho_{\pm}^{-1}\omega\;,

whose lift to
\mathring{\widetilde{M}}_{\pm}
is
\tilde{\omega}_{\text{\rm b},\pm}=(\varkappa x)^{-1}\,dx
; in fact,
\tilde{\omega}_{\text{\rm b},\pm}(\widetilde{Z}_{\pm})=1
, and therefore
\omega_{\text{\rm b},\pm}(Z_{\pm})=1
. By (
4.2.14
) and (
4.2.16
),

(4.2.17)

\rho_{\pm}^{-1}\,d\rho_{\pm}=\eta+\varkappa\,\omega_{\text{\rm b},\pm}\;.

Let
\nu=\nu_{\pm}
be the unique smooth trivialization of
{}_{+}N\partial M_{\pm}
so that
d\rho(\nu)=1
(
Section

2.5.1
). By (
4.2.14
),
\nu
is represented by the restriction of
\pm|\varkappa|^{-1}X
to
L\equiv\partial M_{\pm}
.

The combination of
\rho_{+}
and
\rho_{-}
is a boundary-defining function
\boldsymbol{\rho}
on
\boldsymbol{M}
, and the combination of
\nu_{+}
and
\nu_{-}
is the unique smooth trivialization
\boldsymbol{\nu}
of
{}_{+}N\partial\boldsymbol{M}
so that
d\boldsymbol{\rho}(\boldsymbol{\nu})=1
. Similarly, we define
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
on
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{M}}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
on
\partial\widetilde{\boldsymbol{M}}
.

For
\epsilon>0
, the restriction
\widetilde{\varpi}_{\pm}=\widetilde{\varpi}:\widetilde{T}_{\pm,\epsilon}:=\{\tilde{\rho}_{\pm}<\epsilon\}\to\widetilde{L}
is a smaller collar neighborhood of the boundary in
\widetilde{M}_{\pm}
, which induces a smaller collar neighborhood
\varpi_{\pm}=\varpi:T_{\pm,\epsilon}:=\{\rho_{\pm}<\epsilon\}\to L
of the boundary in
M_{\pm}
. By combination, we get smaller collar neighborhoods of the boundaries,
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\varpi}}:\widetilde{\boldsymbol{T}}_{\epsilon}:=\{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\rho}}<\epsilon\}\to\partial\widetilde{\boldsymbol{M}}=\widetilde{L}\sqcup\widetilde{L}
and
\boldsymbol{\varpi}:\boldsymbol{T}_{\epsilon}:=\{\boldsymbol{\rho}<\epsilon\}\to\partial\boldsymbol{M}=L\sqcup L
. We have
\mathring{\widetilde{T}}_{\epsilon}\equiv\widetilde{T}^{1}_{\epsilon}
,
\mathring{\widetilde{T}}_{\pm,\epsilon}\equiv\widetilde{T}^{1}_{\pm,\epsilon}
,
\mathring{T}_{\epsilon}\equiv T^{1}_{\epsilon}
and
\mathring{T}_{\pm,\epsilon}\equiv T^{1}_{\pm,\epsilon}
.

4.2.6.
The metric
g_{M}

Take a Riemannian metric
g_{L}
on
L
, and let
g_{\widetilde{L}}
be its lift to
\widetilde{L}
. Consider leafwise metrics,
g_{\mathcal{F}}=\varpi^{*}g_{L}
for
\mathcal{F}
and
g_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}=\widetilde{\varpi}^{*}g_{\widetilde{L}}
for
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
; their restrictions to
\mathcal{F}_{\pm}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm}
may be denoted by
g_{\mathcal{F}_{\pm}}
and
g_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm}}
. Consider also the metric
g_{M}=\omega^{2}+g_{\mathcal{F}}
on
M
. The lift of
g_{M}
to
\widetilde{M}
is

g_{\widetilde{M}}=\tilde{\omega}^{2}+g_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}=|\varkappa|^{-2}e^{2F}\,(dx)^{2}+g_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}\;.

With respect to
g_{M}
, the transverse volume form is
\omega
,
X
is unitary and orthogonal to
\mathcal{F}
, and
T\mathcal{F}^{\perp}=\boldsymbol{H}
.

4.2.7.
The b-metrics
g_{\text{\rm b},\pm}

Define also the metric
g_{\text{\rm b},\pm}=\rho_{\pm}^{-2}\omega^{2}+g_{\mathcal{F}}=\omega_{\text{\rm b},\pm}^{2}+g_{\mathcal{F}}
on
\mathring{M}_{\pm}=M^{1}_{\pm}
, where the last equality uses (
4.2.16
). It is bundle-like for
\mathring{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm}=\mathcal{F}^{1}_{\pm}
, and its lift to
\mathring{\widetilde{M}}_{\pm}
is

(4.2.18)

\tilde{g}_{\text{\rm b},\pm}=\tilde{\rho}_{\pm}^{-2}\,\tilde{\omega}^{2}+g_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}=\tilde{\omega}_{\text{\rm b},\pm}^{2}+g_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}=(\varkappa x)^{-2}\,(dx)^{2}+g_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}\;.

With respect to
g_{\text{\rm b},\pm}
, the transverse volume form is
\omega_{\text{\rm b},\pm}
,
Z_{\pm}
is unitary and orthogonal to
\mathring{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm}
, and
T\mathring{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm}^{\perp}=\boldsymbol{H}|_{\mathring{M}_{\pm}}
. The metrics
\tilde{g}_{\text{\rm b},\pm}
and
g_{\text{\rm b},\pm}
on
\mathring{\widetilde{M}}_{\pm}
and
\mathring{M}_{\pm}
are restrictions of b-metrics on
\widetilde{M}_{\pm}
and
M_{\pm}
, also denoted by
\tilde{g}_{\text{\rm b},\pm}
and
g_{\text{\rm b},\pm}
. In the rest of this subsection,
\mathring{\widetilde{M}}_{\pm}
and
\mathring{M}_{\pm}
(resp.,
\widetilde{M}_{\pm}
and
M_{\pm}
) are assumed to be endowed with the metrics (resp., b-metrics)
\tilde{g}_{\text{\rm b},\pm}
and
g_{\text{\rm b},\pm}
. By (
4.2.17
), if
\eta\neq 0
or
\varkappa^{2}\neq 1
, then the b-metrics
\tilde{g}_{\text{\rm b},\pm}
and
g_{\text{\rm b},\pm}
are not exact (
Section

2.5
).

Proposition 4.2.1
.

\mathring{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm}
is of bounded geometry.

Proof.

\mathring{\widetilde{M}}_{\pm}
is of bounded geometry because it is the Riemannian product of
(\mathbb{R}^{\pm},(\varkappa x)^{-2}(dx)^{2})
and
(\widetilde{L},g_{\widetilde{L}})
, which are of bounded geometry since
L
is compact and the change of coordinate
t=\varkappa^{-1}\ln|x|
defines an isometry between
(\mathbb{R}^{\pm},(\varkappa x)^{-2}(dx)^{2})
and
(\mathbb{R},(dt)^{2})
. Via this isometry,
\tilde{\omega}_{\text{\rm b},\pm}\equiv(\varkappa x)^{-1}\,dx
on
\mathbb{R}^{\pm}
is the pull-back of
dt
on
\mathbb{R}
. On the other hand, the leaves of
\mathring{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}_{\pm}
are the fibers
\{x\}\times\widetilde{L}
(
x\in\mathbb{R}^{\pm}
), and the O’Neill tensors of
\mathring{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}_{\pm}
with
\tilde{g}_{\text{\rm b},\pm}
vanish. Hence, on
\mathring{M}_{\pm}
with
\mathring{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm}
and
g_{\text{\rm b},\pm}
, all covariant derivatives of the curvature tensor are uniformly bounded, and the O’Neill tensors vanish.

Finally, the bi-injectivity radius of
\mathring{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}_{\pm}
with
\tilde{g}_{\text{\rm b},\pm}
is positive because a normal foliated chart centered at any
\tilde{p}=(x_{0},\tilde{q})
is given by

\tilde{\chi}_{\tilde{p},\pm}=(t_{x_{0}},\tilde{y}_{\tilde{q}}):\widetilde{U}_{\tilde{p},\pm}=\mathbb{R}^{\pm}\times B_{\widetilde{L}}(\tilde{q},r)\to\mathbb{R}\times B\;,

where
t_{x_{0}}=\varkappa^{-1}(\ln|x|-\ln|x_{0}|)
,
r=\operatorname{inj}_{L}\leq\operatorname{inj}_{\widetilde{L}}
,
B
is the open ball in
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
of radius
r
and center
0
, and
\tilde{y}_{\tilde{q}}:B_{\widetilde{L}}(\tilde{q},r)\to B
is a normal chart of
\widetilde{L}
. Let
q=\pi(\tilde{q})
,
p=\pi_{M}(\tilde{p})
and
U_{p,\pm}=\pi_{M}(\widetilde{U}_{\tilde{p},\pm}
). Then
\pi:B_{\widetilde{L}}(\tilde{q},r)\to B_{L}(q,r)
is a diffeomorphism, obtaining a normal chart
y_{q}:B_{L}(q,r)\to B
of
L
that corresponds to
\tilde{y}_{\tilde{q}}
via
\pi
. So
\pi_{M}:\widetilde{U}_{\tilde{p},\pm}\to U_{p,\pm}
is also a diffeomorphism, and
\tilde{\chi}_{\tilde{p},\pm}
induces via
\pi_{M}
a normal foliated chart of
\mathring{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm}
with
g_{\text{\rm b},\pm}
, centered at
p
,

\chi_{p,\pm}\equiv(t_{x_{0}},y_{q}):U_{p,\pm}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{\pm}\times B_{L}(q,r)\to\mathbb{R}\times B\;.

This shows that the injectivity bi-radius of
\mathring{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm}
with
g_{\text{\rm b},\pm}
is positive.
∎

By
4.2.1
and according to
Section

3.4.1
,
\mathring{M}_{\pm}
is of bounded geometry (the property
1
of
Section

2.5.20
).

Proposition 4.2.2
.

M_{\pm}
satisfies the property
2
of
Section

2.5.20
.

Proof.

According to the proof of
4.2.1
, it is easy to check that
(\widetilde{M}_{\pm},\tilde{g}_{\text{\rm b},\pm})
satisfies
2
on the whole of
\widetilde{M}_{\pm}
with
\tilde{\rho}\partial_{\tilde{\rho}}
and the extensions
B^{\prime}=(B,0)\in\mathfrak{X}(\widetilde{M}_{\pm})
of vector fields
B\in\mathfrak{X}(\widetilde{L})
. It follows that
(M_{\pm},g_{\text{\rm b},\pm})
also satisfies
2
on the whole of
M_{\pm}
with
\rho\partial_{\rho}
and the extensions
A^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{X}(M_{\pm})
of vector fields
A\in\mathfrak{X}(L)
defined as follows. For every
A\in\mathfrak{X}(L)
, let
\widetilde{A}
denote its lift to
\widetilde{L}
. Then
\widetilde{A}^{\prime}=(\widetilde{A},0)\in\mathfrak{X}(\widetilde{M}_{\pm})
is
\Gamma
-invariant, and therefore it is
\pi_{M_{\pm}}
-projectable to a vector field
A^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{X}(M_{\pm})
.
∎

Proposition 4.2.3
.

We have
d(\ln\rho)\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(\mathring{M}_{\pm};\Lambda^{1})
.

Proof.

On the one hand, by the compactness of
L
and the definition of
g_{\text{\rm b},\pm}
, we have
\eta\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(\mathring{M}_{\pm};\Lambda^{0,1})
. On the other hand,
\rho^{-1}\omega\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(\mathring{M}_{\pm};\Lambda^{1,0})
by
4.2.1
, since
\omega_{\text{\rm b},\pm}=\operatorname{sign}(\varkappa)\rho^{-1}\omega
is the
g_{\text{\rm b},\pm}
-transverse volume form. Hence
d(\ln\rho)=\eta\pm|\varkappa|\rho^{-1}\omega\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(\mathring{M}_{\pm};\Lambda^{1})
by (
4.2.14
).
∎

Let
|{\cdot}|:\Gamma\to\mathbb{N}_{0}
be the word length function given by a finite generating set. There is some
c_{0}>0
so that, for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
,

(4.2.19)

|\ln a_{\gamma}|\leq c_{0}\,|\gamma|\;.

By (
2.9.16
), (
4.2.3
) and (
4.2.18
), for all
\tilde{p}\in\mathring{\widetilde{M}}_{\pm}
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
,

c_{1}^{-1}|\gamma|\leq d_{\mathring{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}_{\pm}}\big(\gamma^{-1}\cdot\xi^{h_{\pm}(\gamma)}(\tilde{p}),\tilde{p}\big)\leq c_{1}|\gamma|\;,

using the holonomy homomorphism
h_{\pm}:\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
of
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{\pm}\equiv\mathring{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm}
(
Section

4.2.2
).

Lemma 4.2.4
.

There are
C>0
and
c\geq 1
so that, for all
\tilde{y},\tilde{y}^{\prime}\in\widetilde{L}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
,

d_{\widetilde{L}}(\tilde{y},\tilde{y}^{\prime})\geq C\ln\frac{\rho([x,\tilde{y}])}{c\rho([x,\tilde{y}^{\prime}])}\;.

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{F}\subset\widetilde{L}
be a fundamental domain. Without loss of generality, we can assume
\tilde{y}\in\boldsymbol{F}
. Take some
\gamma\in\Gamma
such that

\gamma\cdot\tilde{y}^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{F}
. Then

\rho([x,\tilde{y}])=e^{F(\tilde{y})}x\;,\quad\rho([x,\tilde{y}^{\prime}])=\rho([a_{\gamma}x,\gamma\cdot\tilde{y}^{\prime}])=e^{F(\gamma\cdot\tilde{y}^{\prime})}a_{\gamma}x\;.

There is some
C_{0}\geq 1
such that, for all
\tilde{y}_{1},\tilde{y}_{2}\in\boldsymbol{F}
,

C_{0}^{-1}e^{F(\tilde{y}_{1})}\leq e^{F(\tilde{y}_{2})}\leq C_{0}e^{F(\tilde{y}_{1})}\;.

So, using (
2.9.17
) with
\boldsymbol{K}=\boldsymbol{F}^{2}
and (
4.2.19
),

\frac{\rho([x,\tilde{y}])}{\rho([x,\tilde{y}^{\prime}])}=\frac{e^{F(\tilde{y})}}{e^{F(\gamma\cdot\tilde{y}^{\prime})}a_{\gamma}}\leq C_{0}a_{\gamma^{-1}}\leq C_{0}e^{c_{0}\,|\gamma^{-1}|}\leq C_{0}e^{c_{0}c_{1}(d_{\widetilde{L}}(\tilde{y}^{\prime},\tilde{y})+c_{2})}\;.\qed

Corollary 4.2.5
.

For
R,\epsilon>0
, we have
\operatorname{Pen}_{\mathcal{F}}(T_{\epsilon},R)\subset T_{ce^{R/C}\epsilon}
.

Lemma 4.2.6
.

For
p\in\mathring{M}_{\pm}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
, if
\phi^{t}(L_{p})=L_{p}
, then
d_{\mathcal{F}}(\phi^{t}(p),p)\geq\operatorname{inj}_{L}
.

Proof.

We have
p=[x_{0},\tilde{q}]
for some
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{\pm}
and
\tilde{q}\in\widetilde{L}
, and let
q=\pi(\tilde{q})\in L
. For
r=\operatorname{inj}_{L}
, take normal charts
\tilde{y}_{\tilde{q}}:B_{\widetilde{L}}(\tilde{q},r)\to B
and
y_{q}:B_{L}(q,r)\to B
like in the proof of
4.2.1
. We have the foliated chart of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
,

\tilde{\chi}_{\tilde{q}}=(x,\tilde{y}_{\tilde{q}}):\widetilde{U}_{\tilde{q}}=\mathbb{R}\times B_{\widetilde{L}}(\tilde{q},r)\to\mathbb{R}\times B\;,

which induces via
\pi_{M}
a foliated chart of
\mathcal{F}
,

\chi_{q}=(x,y_{q}):U_{q}=\pi_{M}(\widetilde{U}_{\tilde{q}})\equiv\mathbb{R}\times B_{L}(q,r)\to\mathbb{R}\times B\;.

On the one hand, if
\phi^{t}(p)\in U_{q}
, then
\chi_{q}\phi^{t}(p)=(e^{\varkappa t}x_{0},\tilde{\phi}^{t}_{x_{0}}(\tilde{q}))
by (
4.2.1
), with
e^{\varkappa t}x_{0}\neq x_{0}
because
t\neq 0
. So
p
and
\phi^{t}(p)
lie in different plaques of
(U_{q},\chi_{q})
. On the other hand, if
\phi^{t}(p)\not\in U_{q}
, then a fortiori
\phi^{t}(p)
is not in the plaque of
(U_{q},\chi_{q})
through
p
. In any case,
d_{\mathcal{F}}(\phi^{t}(p),p)\geq r
because the plaque through
p
is
B_{L_{p}}(p,r)\equiv B_{\widetilde{L}}(\tilde{q},r)
.
∎

Proposition 4.2.7
.

If
Z_{\pm}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M^{1}_{\pm},\mathcal{F}^{1}_{\pm})
, then, for any compact
I\subset\mathbb{R}
, there are
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that, for all
p\in\mathring{M}_{\pm}
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
with
h_{\pm}(\gamma)\in I
,

d_{\mathcal{F}}\big(\phi^{h_{\pm}(\gamma)}(p),p\big)\geq c_{1}^{-1}|\gamma|-c_{2}\;.

Proof.

Since
I
is compact and
\phi_{\pm}
is of
\mathbb{R}
-local bounded geometry on
\mathring{M}_{\pm}
(
Section

2.4.7
), there is some
R>0
such that
d_{\widetilde{L}}(\tilde{\phi}^{t}_{x}(\tilde{y}),\tilde{y})\leq R
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{\pm}
,
t\in I
and
\tilde{y}\in\widetilde{L}
. Given any fundamental domain
\boldsymbol{F}\subset\widetilde{L}
, let
\boldsymbol{K}=\overline{\operatorname{Pen}}_{\widetilde{L}}(\boldsymbol{F},R)\times\boldsymbol{F}
. By (
2.9.17
), there are
c_{1}\geq 1
and
c_{2}\geq 0
such that

(4.2.20)

d_{\widetilde{L}}(\gamma\cdot\tilde{\phi}^{t}_{x}(\tilde{y}),\tilde{y}^{\prime})\geq c_{1}^{-1}|\gamma|-c_{2}

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{\pm}
,
t\in I
,
\tilde{y},\tilde{y}^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{F}
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
, because
(\tilde{\phi}^{t}_{x}(\tilde{y}),\tilde{y}^{\prime})\in\boldsymbol{K}
.

Any
p\in\mathring{M}_{\pm}
is of the form
p=[x,\tilde{y}]
for some
x\in\mathbb{R}^{\pm}
and
\tilde{y}\in\boldsymbol{F}
. Let
\gamma\in\Gamma
with
t:=h_{\pm}(\gamma)=\varkappa^{-1}\ln a_{\gamma}\in I
. Then
\phi^{t}(L_{p})=L_{p}
(
Section

4.2.1
), and, by (
4.2.20
),

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{F}}(\phi^{t}(p),p)

\displaystyle=d_{\mathcal{F}}([e^{\varkappa t}x,\tilde{\phi}^{t}_{x}(\tilde{y})],[x,\tilde{y}])=d_{\mathcal{F}}([a_{\gamma}x,\tilde{\phi}^{t}_{x}(\tilde{y})],[x,\tilde{y}])

\displaystyle=d_{\mathcal{F}}([x,\gamma^{-1}\cdot\tilde{\phi}^{t}_{x}(\tilde{y})],[x,\tilde{y}])=d_{\widetilde{L}}(\gamma^{-1}\cdot\tilde{\phi}^{t}_{x}(\tilde{y}),\tilde{y})\geq c_{1}^{-1}|\gamma|-c_{2}\;.\qed

Corollary 4.2.8
.

If
Z_{\pm}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M^{1}_{\pm},\mathcal{F}^{1}_{\pm})
, then, for any compact
I\subset\mathbb{R}^{\times}
, there is some
c_{3}>0
such that, for all
p\in\mathring{M}_{\pm}
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
with
h_{\pm}(\gamma)\in I
,

d_{\mathcal{F}}\big(\phi^{h_{\pm}(\gamma)}(p),p\big)\geq c_{3}|\gamma|\;.

Proof.

By
4.2.6
and
4.2.7
, the result follows taking
c_{3}>0
such that, for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
,

c_{3}|\gamma|\leq\begin{cases}\operatorname{inj}_{L}&\text{if $|\gamma|\leq c_{1}c_{2}$}\\
c_{1}^{-1}|\gamma|-c_{2}&\text{if $|\gamma|>c_{1}c_{2}$}\;.\qed\end{cases}

Proposition 4.2.9
.

If
Z_{\pm}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M^{1}_{\pm},\mathcal{F}^{1}_{\pm})
, then, for any compact
I\subset\mathbb{R}
, there exists some
c^{\prime}>0
such that
\phi^{t}(T_{\epsilon})\subset T_{c^{\prime}\epsilon}
for all
t\in I
and
\epsilon>0
.

Proof.

Take some
R>0
like in the proof of
4.2.7
. Let
\tilde{y}\in\widetilde{L}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{\pm}
such that
\rho([x,\tilde{y}])<\epsilon
. If
\boldsymbol{c}:[0,1]\to\widetilde{L}
is a minimizing geodesic segment from
\tilde{y}
to
\tilde{\phi}^{t}_{x}(\tilde{y})
, then

F(\tilde{\phi}^{t}_{x}(\tilde{y}))-F(\tilde{y})=\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{c}^{*}dF=\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{c}^{*}\tilde{\eta}\leq\|\tilde{\eta}\|_{L^{\infty}}d_{\widetilde{L}}(\tilde{y},\tilde{\phi}^{t}_{x}(\tilde{y}))\leq\|\eta\|_{L^{\infty}}R\;.

Take also some
c^{\prime}_{1}>0
such that
e^{\varkappa t}\leq c^{\prime}_{1}
for all
t\in I
. Then

\rho(\phi^{t}([x,\tilde{y}]))=e^{F(\tilde{\phi}^{t}_{x}(\tilde{y}))-F(\tilde{y})+\varkappa t}\rho([x,\tilde{y}])\leq e^{\|\eta\|_{L^{\infty}}R}c^{\prime}_{1}\epsilon\;.\qed

Proposition 4.2.10
.

Suppose
\Gamma
is nontrivial. For any
\epsilon>0
, there is some
0<\epsilon^{\prime}<\epsilon
such that, for all leaf
L^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{F}
, if a connected component
W
of
L^{\prime}\cap T_{\epsilon}
meets
T_{\epsilon^{\prime}}
, then
L\subset\overline{W}
.

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{F}\subset\widetilde{L}
be a fundamental domain. We can choose
0<\epsilon^{\prime}<\epsilon
such that
e^{F(\tilde{y})-F(\tilde{y}^{\prime})}\epsilon^{\prime}<\epsilon
for all
\tilde{y},\tilde{y}^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{F}
. Let
W
be a connected component of
L^{\prime}\cap T_{\epsilon}
that meets
T_{\epsilon^{\prime}}
at some point
[x,\tilde{y}]
. We can assume
\tilde{y}\in\boldsymbol{F}
. For every
\tilde{y}^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{F}
, we have
[x,\tilde{y}^{\prime}]\in L^{\prime}
and

|\rho([x,\tilde{y}^{\prime}])|=e^{F(\tilde{y}^{\prime})}|x|=e^{F(\tilde{y}^{\prime})-F(\tilde{y})}|\rho([x,\tilde{y}])|<e^{F(\tilde{y}^{\prime})-F(\tilde{y})}\epsilon^{\prime}<\epsilon\;.

So
\pi_{M}(\{x\}\times\boldsymbol{F})\subset W
because
\boldsymbol{F}
is connected. Since
\Gamma
is nontrivial and
h
is injective, there is some
\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
\gamma\cdot\boldsymbol{F}\cap\boldsymbol{F}\neq\emptyset
and
a_{\gamma}>1
. Then
W_{0}:=\bigcup_{m=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{m}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}
is connected in
\widetilde{L}
. Moreover, by (
4.2.4
), for all
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\tilde{y}^{\prime}\in\boldsymbol{F}
,

|\rho([x,\gamma^{m}\tilde{y}^{\prime}])|=e^{F(\gamma^{m}\tilde{y}^{\prime})}|x|=a_{\gamma}^{-m}e^{F(\tilde{y}^{\prime})}|x|=a_{\gamma}^{-m}|\rho([x,\tilde{y}^{\prime}])|<a_{\gamma}^{-m}\epsilon\;,

which is
{}<\epsilon
and converges to
0
as
m\to\infty
. Since
[x,\gamma^{m}\tilde{y}^{\prime}]\in\varpi^{-1}([\tilde{y}^{\prime}])
, it follows that
[x,\gamma^{m}\tilde{y}^{\prime}]\to[0,\tilde{y}^{\prime}]\equiv[\tilde{y}^{\prime}]
as
m\to\infty
. Hence
\pi_{M}(\{x\}\times W_{0})\subset W
and
L\subset\overline{\pi_{M}(\{x\}\times W_{0})}
.
∎

4.2.8.
The b-metrics
g_{\text{\rm c},\pm}

Using (
4.2.13
), we can also define the metric
g_{\text{\rm c},\pm}\equiv(\varkappa\rho)^{-2}\,(d\rho)^{2}+g_{L}
on
\mathring{M}_{\pm}
and its lift
\tilde{g}_{\text{\rm c},\pm}\equiv(\varkappa\tilde{\rho})^{-2}\,(d\rho)^{2}+g_{\widetilde{L}}
on
\mathring{\widetilde{M}}_{\pm}
. These are restrictions to the interiors of b-metrics, also denoted by
g_{\text{\rm c},\pm}
and
\tilde{g}_{\text{\rm c},\pm}
. The b-metrics
\varkappa^{2}g_{\text{\rm c},\pm}
and
\varkappa^{2}\tilde{g}_{\text{\rm c},\pm}
are exact and cylindrical around the boundary (
Section

2.5.1
); in particular, the level hypersurfaces of
\rho
and
\tilde{\rho}
are totally geodesic for
g_{\text{\rm c},\pm}
and
\tilde{g}_{\text{\rm c},\pm}
.

Proposition 4.2.11
.

The metrics
g_{\text{\rm c},\pm}
and
g_{\text{\rm b},\pm}
are quasi-isometric on
\mathring{M}_{\pm}
; more precisely,

|{\cdot}|_{g_{\text{\rm c},\pm}}\leq\sqrt{2(1+\varkappa^{-2}\|\eta\|_{L^{\infty}})}\,|{\cdot}|_{g_{\text{\rm b},\pm}}\;,\quad|{\cdot}|_{g_{\text{\rm b},\pm}}\leq\sqrt{2}\,|{\cdot}|_{g_{\text{\rm c},\pm}}\;.

Proof.

Take any
p\in\mathring{M}_{\pm}
and
u\in T_{p}\mathring{M}_{\pm}\equiv T_{p}M=\boldsymbol{H}_{p}\oplus\boldsymbol{V}_{p}
, and let
v=\boldsymbol{V}u
and
w=\boldsymbol{H}u
. Then

|u|_{g_{\text{\rm b},\pm}}^{2}=\rho^{-2}\omega(w)^{2}+|v|_{g_{L}}^{2}\;.

By (
4.2.14
) and since
\omega(X)=1
, it follows that
u
is the sum of the vectors

v\mp|\varkappa|^{-1}\rho\eta(v)X_{p}\in\ker(d\rho)_{p}\;,\quad w\pm|\varkappa|^{-1}\rho\eta(v)X_{p}\in\boldsymbol{H}_{p}=\ker\varpi_{*p}\;,

obtaining

\displaystyle|u|_{g_{\text{\rm c},\pm}}^{2}

\displaystyle=(\varkappa\rho)^{-2}\big(\pm|\varkappa|\omega(w)+\rho\eta(v)\big)^{2}+|v|_{g_{L}}^{2}

\displaystyle=|u|_{g_{\text{\rm b},\pm}}^{2}+\varkappa^{-2}\eta(v)^{2}\pm 2(|\varkappa|\rho)^{-1}\omega(w)\eta(v)\;.

Then

\displaystyle|u|_{g_{\text{\rm c},\pm}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq|u|_{g_{\text{\rm b},\pm}}^{2}+2\varkappa^{-2}\eta(v)^{2}+\rho^{-2}\omega(w)^{2}\leq 2(1+\varkappa^{-2}\|\eta\|_{L^{\infty}})|u|_{g_{\text{\rm b},\pm}}^{2}\;,

\displaystyle|u|_{g_{\text{\rm b},\pm}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq|u|_{g_{\text{\rm c},\pm}}^{2}+\rho^{-2}\omega(w)^{2}\leq 2|u|_{g_{\text{\rm c},\pm}}^{2}\;.\qed

4.2.9.
Vector fields

Assume again that
\mathring{\widetilde{M}}_{\pm}
and
\mathring{M}_{\pm}
are endowed with
\tilde{g}_{\text{\rm b},\pm}
and
g_{\text{\rm b},\pm}
. Recall that any
A\in\mathfrak{X}(M,\mathcal{F})
induces a vector field
A_{\pm}\in\mathfrak{X}(M,\mathcal{F})
, whose restriction to
\mathring{M}_{\pm}
is also denoted by
A_{\pm}
(
Sections

4.1.2
and
4.2.1
).

Proposition 4.2.12
.

Y_{\pm}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm ub}}(\mathring{M}_{\pm},\mathring{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm})
.

Proof.

This follows from the proof of
4.2.1
because
\partial_{t}
corresponds to
\varkappa x\partial_{x}
by the change of coordinate
t=\varkappa^{-1}\ln|x|
.
∎

Lemma 4.2.13
.

If
V\in\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm c}}(\mathcal{F})
, then
V_{\pm}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm ub}}(\mathring{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm})
.

Proof.

Consider the normal foliated charts of
\mathring{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm}
,
\chi_{p,\pm}=(t_{x_{0}},y_{q})=(t,y)
on
U_{p,\pm}
, like in the proof of
4.2.1
, and the foliated charts of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
,
\chi_{q}=(x,y_{q})=(x,y)
on
U_{q}
, like in the proof of
4.2.6
. Then
U_{q}=\varpi^{-1}(B_{L}(q,r))
,
U_{p,\pm}=U_{q}\cap\mathring{M}_{\pm}
and
x=e^{\varkappa t}x_{0}
. Let
\partial_{i}=\partial_{y^{i}}
and
\partial_{I}=\partial_{i_{1}}\cdots\partial_{i_{m}}
for any multi-index
I=(i_{1},\dots,i_{m})
. Take a partition of unity subordinated to a finite open cover of the compact manifold
L
by balls
B_{L}(q,r)
(
q\in L
). By using the
\varpi
-lift of this partition of unity to
M
, it easily follows that we can assume
V
is supported in some
U_{q}
. Thus we can write
V=f^{i}(x,y)\partial_{i}
on
U_{q}
for functions
f^{i}\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(\mathbb{R}\times B)\equiv C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(U_{q})
, and write
V_{\pm}=h^{i}(t,y)\partial_{i}
on
U_{p,\pm}
for functions
h^{i}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{\pm}\times B)
. We have

(4.2.21)

\partial_{I}h^{i}(t,y)=\partial_{I}f^{i}(e^{\varkappa t}x_{0},y)\;.

Claim 4.2.14
.

For
l\leq k
in
\mathbb{N}
, there are
c_{k,l}\in\mathbb{N}
such that, on
U_{p,\pm}
,

\partial_{t}^{k}=\sum_{l}c_{k,l}x^{l}\partial_{x}^{l}=\sum_{l}(\pm 1)^{l}c_{k,l}\rho^{l}X^{l}\;.

To simplify the notation, we define
c_{k,l}
for all
k,l\in\mathbb{Z}
by setting
c_{k,l}=0
if
\min\{k,l\}<0
,
c_{0,0}=1
, and
c_{k,l}=\varkappa(lc_{k-1,l}+c_{k-1,l-1})
if
\max\{k,l\}>0
. Note that
c_{k,l}=0
if
l\leq 0<k
or
l>k
.

The first equality of
4.2.14
follows by induction on
k
. The case
k=1
is true because
\partial_{t}=\varkappa x\partial_{x}
. If
k>1
and the first equality holds for
k-1
, then

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{k}

\displaystyle=\varkappa x\partial_{x}\sum_{l}c_{k-1,l}x^{l}\partial_{x}^{l}=\sum_{l}\varkappa c_{k-1,l}\big(x^{l+1}\partial_{x}^{l+1}+x\big[\partial_{x},x^{l}\big]\partial_{x}^{l}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l}\varkappa c_{k-1,l}\big(x^{l+1}\partial_{x}^{l+1}+lx^{l}\partial_{x}^{l}\big)=\sum_{l}\varkappa(lc_{k-1,l}+c_{k-1,l-1})x^{l}\partial_{x}^{l}\;.

The second equality of
4.2.14
holds because

\tilde{\rho}^{l}\widetilde{X}^{l}=e^{lF(y)}|x|^{l}(e^{-F(y)}\partial_{x})^{l}=|x|^{l}\partial_{x}^{l}=(\pm 1)^{l}x^{l}\partial_{x}^{l}\;.

By (
4.2.21
) and Claim
4.2.14
,

\partial_{t}^{k+1}\partial_{I}h^{i}(t,y)=\sum_{l=1}^{k}(\pm 1)^{l}c_{k,l}\rho^{l}X^{l}\partial_{I}f^{i}(e^{\varkappa t}x_{0},y)\;.

Thus every function
|\partial_{t}^{k+1}\partial_{I}h^{i}|
is uniformly bounded on
\mathbb{R}_{\pm}\times B
because
f^{i}\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(\mathbb{R}\times B)\equiv C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(U_{q})
and
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
.
∎

Proposition 4.2.15
.

For any
\epsilon>0
, there is some
A\in\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm com}}(M,\mathcal{F})
such that
A_{\pm}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm ub}}(\mathring{M}_{\pm},\mathring{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm})
and
A=Z
on
T_{\epsilon}
.

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{V}=(0,\widetilde{Z}_{x})\in\mathfrak{X}(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}})
, which projects to a vector field
V\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{F})
by (
4.2.2
). For any
\lambda\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(M)
such that
0\leq\lambda\leq 1
and
\lambda=1
on
T_{\epsilon}
, we have
V^{\prime}:=\lambda V\in\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm c}}(\mathcal{F})
. Then
V^{\prime}_{\pm}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm ub}}(\mathring{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm})
by
4.2.13
,
A:=Y+V^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm com}}(M,\mathcal{F})
,
A=Z
on
T_{\epsilon}
, and
A_{\pm}=Y_{\pm}+V^{\prime}_{\pm}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm ub}}(\mathring{M}_{\pm},\mathring{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm})
by
4.2.12
.
∎

4.3.
Global objects on foliations with simple foliated flows

Consider the notation of
Sections

3.1.12
and
4.1.2
, where
M
is compact,
\mathcal{F}
is transversely oriented, and
\phi
is transversely simple.

4.3.1.
Tubular neighborhoods of
M^{0}

In the following, for
L\in\pi_{0}M^{0}
(the set of leaves in
M^{0}
), we have corresponding objects
\hat{h}_{L}
,
h_{L}
,
\Gamma_{L}
,
\pi_{L}:\widetilde{L}\to L
,
\varkappa_{L}
and
a_{L,\gamma}
(
Section

4.1.2
). Consider also the corresponding suspension foliated manifold,
(M^{\prime}_{L},\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{L})
, and all other associated objects (
Sections

4.2.1
,
4.2.2
,
4.2.3
,
4.2.4
,
4.2.5
,
4.2.6
,
4.2.7
and
4.2.8
). A prime and the subscript “
L
” is added to their notation; for instance, we have
\xi^{\prime}_{L}=\{\xi^{\prime\,t}_{L}\}
,
Y^{\prime}_{L}
,
M^{\prime\,0}_{L}
,
M^{\prime\,1}_{L}
,
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\,1}_{L}
,
\varpi^{\prime}_{L}
,
\rho^{\prime}_{L}
,
T^{\prime}_{L,\epsilon}
,
T^{\prime\,1}_{L,\epsilon}
,
X^{\prime}_{L}
,
\omega^{\prime}_{L}
,
\eta^{\prime}_{L}
,
g_{M^{\prime}_{L}}
and
g_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{L}}
. The corresponding disjoint unions or combinations, with
L
running in
\pi_{0}M^{0}
, are denoted by
M^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
,
\xi^{\prime}=\{\xi^{\prime\,t}\}
,
Y^{\prime}
,
M^{\prime\,0}
,
M^{\prime\,1}
,
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\,1}
,
\varpi^{\prime}
,
\rho^{\prime}
,
T^{\prime}_{\epsilon}
,
T^{\prime\,1}_{\epsilon}
,
X^{\prime}
,
\omega^{\prime}
,
\eta^{\prime}
,
g_{M^{\prime}}
and
g_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
, removing the subscript “
L
”.

By the Reeb’s local stability, if
\epsilon>0
is small enough, there is a tubular neighborhood of every
L
in
M
,
\varpi_{L}:T_{L,\epsilon}\to L
, such that
T_{L,\epsilon}
is diffeomorphic to
T^{\prime}_{L,\epsilon}
, with
\varpi_{L}
and
\mathcal{F}|_{T_{L,\epsilon}}
corresponding to
\varpi^{\prime}_{L}
and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{L}|_{T^{\prime}_{L,\epsilon}}
; we simply write
\varpi_{L}\equiv\varpi^{\prime}_{L}
and
\mathcal{F}\equiv\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{L}
on
T_{L,\epsilon}\equiv T^{\prime}_{L,\epsilon}
. We can assume the closures
\overline{T_{L,\epsilon}}
are disjoint one another. Then the combination of the maps
\varpi_{L,\epsilon}
is a tubular neighborhood of
M^{0}
in
M
,

\varpi\equiv\varpi^{\prime}:T_{\epsilon}:=\bigcup_{L}T_{L,\epsilon}\equiv T^{\prime}_{\epsilon}\to M^{0}\equiv M^{\prime\,0}\;.

4.3.2.
Collar neighborhoods of every
\partial M_{l}

Given any connected component
M^{1}_{l}
of
M^{1}
, consider only leaves
L\in\pi_{0}(M^{0}\cap\overline{M^{1}_{l}})\equiv\pi_{0}(\partial M_{l})
. The notation
(M^{\prime}_{L,l},\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{L,l})
is used for
(M^{\prime}_{L,+},\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{L,+})
(resp.,
(M^{\prime}_{L,-},\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{L,-})
) if the transverse orientation of
\mathcal{F}_{l}
along
L
points inwards (resp., outwards), like the transverse orientation along
L
of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{L,+}
(resp.,
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{L,-}
). This kind of change is applied to the rest of notation concerning these foliated manifolds with boundary (
Sections

4.2.2
,
4.2.5
,
4.2.6
,
4.2.7
,
4.2.8
and
4.2.9
). For instance, we obtain
\xi^{\prime}_{L,l}=\{\xi^{\prime\,t}_{L,l}\}
,
Y^{\prime}_{L,l}
,
\varpi^{\prime}_{L,l}
,
\rho^{\prime}_{L,l}
,
\nu^{\prime}_{L,l}
,
T^{\prime}_{L,l,\epsilon}
,
\omega^{\prime}_{\text{\rm b},L,l}
,
\eta^{\prime}_{L,l}
,
g^{\prime}_{\text{\rm b},L,l}
and
g^{\prime}_{\text{\rm c},L,l}
. Similarly, we have
(M^{\prime\,1}_{L,l},\mathcal{F}^{\prime\,1}_{L,l})\equiv(\mathring{M}^{\prime}_{L,l},\mathring{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}_{L,l})
, whose Molino’s description involves
\widetilde{M}^{\prime\,1}_{L,l}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime\,1}_{L,l}
,
h^{\prime}_{L,l}:\Gamma_{L}\to\mathbb{R}
and
D^{\prime}_{L,l}:\widetilde{M}^{\prime\,1}_{L,l}\to\mathbb{R}
. We have
\mathring{T}^{\prime}_{L,l,\epsilon}\equiv T^{\prime}_{L,\epsilon}\cap M^{\prime\,1}_{l}=:T^{\prime\,1}_{L,l,\epsilon}
. The corresponding disjoint unions or combinations, with
L
running in
\pi_{0}(\partial M_{l})
, are denoted by
M^{\prime}_{l}
,
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{l}
,
\xi^{\prime}_{l}=\{\xi^{\prime\,t}_{l}\}
,
Y^{\prime}_{l}
,
\varpi^{\prime}_{l}
,
\rho^{\prime}_{l}
,
\nu^{\prime}_{L,l}
,
T^{\prime}_{l,\epsilon}
,
\omega^{\prime}_{\text{\rm b},l}
,
\eta^{\prime}_{l}
,
g^{\prime}_{\text{\rm b},l}
and
g^{\prime}_{\text{\rm c},l}
, deleting the subscript “
L
”. In the same way, we have
M^{\prime\,1}_{l}
,
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\,1}_{l}
and
T^{\prime\,1}_{l,\epsilon}=T^{\prime}_{\epsilon}\cap M^{\prime\,1}_{l}\equiv\mathring{T}^{\prime}_{l,\epsilon}
.

Next, we delete “
l
” from this notation and use boldface for the corresponding disjoint unions or combinations for all
l
, obtaining
\boldsymbol{M}^{\prime}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}
,
\boldsymbol{\varpi}^{\prime}
,
\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\prime}
,
\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}
,
\boldsymbol{T}^{\prime}_{\epsilon}
,
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}_{\text{\rm b}}
,
\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}
,
\boldsymbol{g}^{\prime}_{\text{\rm b}}
and
\boldsymbol{g}^{\prime}_{\text{\rm c}}
.

On the other hand,
\varpi_{L}:T_{L,\epsilon}\to L
induces a collar neighborhood
\varpi_{L,l}:T_{L,l,\epsilon}\to L
of the boundary component
L
of
M_{l}
, and the identity
T_{L,\epsilon}\equiv T^{\prime}_{L,\epsilon}
induces an identity
T_{L,l,\epsilon}\equiv T^{\prime}_{L,l,\epsilon}
, and we have
\varpi_{L,l}\equiv\varpi^{\prime}_{L,l}
and
\mathcal{F}_{l}\equiv\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{L,l}
on
T_{L,l,\epsilon}\equiv T^{\prime}_{L,l,\epsilon}
. Moreover
T^{\prime\,1}_{L,l,\epsilon}\equiv T^{1}_{L,l,\epsilon}:=T_{L,\epsilon}\cap M^{1}_{l}\equiv\mathring{T}_{L,l,\epsilon}
and
T^{\prime\,1}_{L,l,\epsilon}\equiv T^{1}_{l,\epsilon}:=T_{\epsilon}\cap M^{1}_{l}\equiv\mathring{T}_{L,l,\epsilon}
.

The combination of the maps
\varpi_{L,l}
, with
L
running in
\pi_{0}M_{l}
, is a collar neighborhood
\varpi_{l}\equiv\varpi^{\prime}_{l}:T_{l,\epsilon}\equiv T^{\prime}_{l,\epsilon}\to\partial M_{l}\equiv\partial M^{\prime}_{l}
of the boundary in
M_{l}
, where
\mathcal{F}_{l}\equiv\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{l}
. In turn, the combination of the maps
\varpi_{l}
is a collar neighborhood

\boldsymbol{\varpi}\equiv\boldsymbol{\varpi}^{\prime}:\boldsymbol{T}_{\epsilon}:=\bigsqcup_{l}T_{l,\epsilon}\equiv\boldsymbol{T}^{\prime}_{\epsilon}\to\partial\boldsymbol{M}\equiv M^{0}\sqcup M^{0}

of the boundary in
\boldsymbol{M}
, and we have
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}\equiv\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}
on
\boldsymbol{T}_{\epsilon}\equiv\boldsymbol{T}^{\prime}_{\epsilon}
.

4.3.3.
Globalization

For fixed
0<\epsilon<\epsilon_{0}
small enough, we can construct the following objects with standard arguments, using a partition of unity subordinated to the open cover
\{T_{\epsilon_{0}},M\setminus\overline{T_{\epsilon}}\}
of
M
:

(5)

For any
A^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm com}}(M^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
with
\overline{A^{\prime}}=\overline{Y^{\prime}}
, there is some
A\in\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm com}}(M,\mathcal{F})
with
\overline{A}=\overline{Z}
,
A\equiv A^{\prime}
on
T_{\epsilon}\equiv T^{\prime}_{\epsilon}
and
A=Z
on
M\setminus T_{\epsilon_{0}}
. Moreover
A
induces a vector field
A_{l}\in\mathfrak{X}(M_{l},\mathcal{F}_{l})
(
Section

4.1.2
), whose restriction to
\mathring{M}_{l}\equiv M^{1}_{l}
is denoted in the same way. In particular, this applies to
Y^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm com}}(M^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
, obtaining
Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M,\mathcal{F})
with flow
\xi=\{\xi^{t}\}
and
Y_{l}\in\mathfrak{X}(M_{l},\mathcal{F}_{l})
with flow
\xi_{l}=\{\xi^{t}_{l}\}
. We have
\operatorname{Fix}(\xi)=M^{0}
, and the orbits of
\xi
agree with the fibers of
\varpi
on
T_{\epsilon}\cap M^{1}
. Thus
\xi
has no closed orbit in
T_{\epsilon}\cap M^{1}
.

(6)

Some
Z^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm com}}(M^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
, with flow
\phi^{\prime}=\{\phi^{\prime\,t}\}
, such that
\overline{Z^{\prime}}=\overline{Y^{\prime}}
,
Z^{\prime}\equiv Z
on
T_{\epsilon}\equiv T^{\prime}_{\epsilon}
, and
Z^{\prime}=Y^{\prime}
on
M^{\prime}\setminus T^{\prime}_{\epsilon_{0}}
. This
Z^{\prime}
induces vector fields
Z^{\prime}_{L,l}\in\mathfrak{X}(M^{\prime}_{L,l},\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{L,l})
with flow
\phi^{\prime}_{L,l}=\{\phi^{\prime\,t}_{L,l}\}
, and
Z^{\prime}_{l}\in\mathfrak{X}(M^{\prime}_{l},\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{l})
with flow
\phi^{\prime}_{l}=\{\phi^{\prime\,t}_{l}\}
.

(7)

A bundle-like metric
g_{\text{\rm b},l}
of every
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{l}\equiv\mathring{\mathcal{F}}_{l}
on
M^{1}_{l}\equiv\mathring{M}_{l}
such that
g_{\text{\rm b},l}\equiv g^{\prime}_{\text{\rm b},l}
on
T^{1}_{l,\epsilon}\equiv T^{\prime\,1}_{l,\epsilon}
. Thus
g_{\text{\rm b},l}
is the restriction to
\mathring{M}_{l}
of a b-metric on
M_{l}
, also denoted by
g_{\text{\rm b},l}
. Let
\omega_{\text{\rm b},l}
be the
g_{\text{\rm b},l}
-transverse volume form, defining the transverse orientation given by
\overline{Z_{l}}
; thus
\omega_{\text{\rm b},l}\equiv\omega^{\prime}_{\text{\rm b},l}
on
T^{1}_{l,\epsilon}\equiv T^{\prime\,1}_{l,\epsilon}
. Since
\omega^{\prime}_{\text{\rm b},l}(Y^{\prime}_{l})=\omega^{\prime}_{\text{\rm b},l}(Z^{\prime}_{l})=1
(
Section

4.2.5
), we can assume
\omega_{\text{\rm b},l}(Y_{l})=\omega_{\text{\rm b},l}(Z_{l})=1
.

(8)

A Riemannian metric
g_{\text{\rm c},l}
on every
M^{1}_{l}\equiv\mathring{M}_{l}
such that
g_{\text{\rm c},l}\equiv g^{\prime}_{\text{\rm c},l}
on
T^{1}_{l,\epsilon}\equiv T^{\prime\,1}_{l,\epsilon}
. Thus
g_{\text{\rm c},l}
is the restriction to
\mathring{M}_{l}
of a b-metric on
M_{l}
, also denoted by
g_{\text{\rm c},l}
, and the b-metric
\varkappa_{L}^{2}g_{\text{\rm c},l}
is exact and cylindrical around every boundary component
L
of
M_{l}
.

(9)

A Riemannian metric
g_{M}
on
M
such that
g_{M}\equiv g_{M^{\prime}}
on
T_{\epsilon}\equiv T^{\prime}_{\epsilon}
,
g_{M}=g_{\text{\rm b},l}
on every
M^{1}_{l}\setminus T_{\epsilon_{0}}
, and
g_{M}
defines the same orthogonal complement of
T\mathcal{F}
as
g_{\text{\rm b},l}
on every
M^{1}_{l}
. We consider the bigrading of
\Lambda M
defined by the
g_{M}
-orthogonal complement of the leaves (
Section

3.2
).

(10)

A leafwise Riemannian metric
g_{\mathcal{F}}
of
\mathcal{F}
such that
g_{\mathcal{F}}\equiv g_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}
on
T_{\epsilon}\equiv T^{\prime}_{\epsilon}
. We can assume it is induced by
g_{M}
on
M
, and by
g_{\text{\rm b},l}
and
g_{\text{\rm c},l}
on every
M^{1}_{l}
. It induces a leafwise metric
g_{\mathcal{F}_{l}}
for every
\mathcal{F}_{l}
.

(11)

Differential forms,
\omega\in C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda^{1,0})
and
\eta\in C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda^{0,1})
, such that
\omega
is the transverse volume form of
\mathcal{F}
with respect to
g_{M}
, and
d\omega=\eta\wedge\omega
. Thus
\ker\omega=T\mathcal{F}
,
\eta=0
on
M\setminus T_{\epsilon_{0}}
, and they extend the forms
\omega
and
\eta
we had on
T_{\epsilon}
. For every
L\in\pi_{0}M^{0}
, we may use the notation
\eta_{L}=\eta|_{L}
and
\tilde{\eta}_{L}=\pi_{L}^{*}\eta_{L}=d_{\widetilde{L}}F_{L}
for some
F_{L}\in C^{\infty}(\widetilde{L})
. Moreover,
\eta=\eta_{0}
on
T_{\epsilon}
with the notation of
Section

3.3.1
because this is true for every
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{L}
.

(12)

A defining function
\rho\equiv\rho^{\prime}
of
M^{0}
in
T_{\epsilon_{0}}\equiv T^{\prime}_{\epsilon_{0}}
.

(13)

A boundary-defining function
\rho=\rho_{l}
on every
M_{l}
such that
\rho_{l}\equiv\rho^{\prime}_{l}
on
T_{l,\epsilon}\equiv T^{\prime}_{l,\epsilon}
, and
\rho_{l}=1
on
M^{1}_{l}\setminus T^{1}_{l,\epsilon_{0}}
. The level hypersurfaces of
\rho_{l}
in
T^{1}_{l,\epsilon}
are totally geodesic with respect to
g_{\text{\rm c},l}
. Let
\nu=\nu_{l}
be the unique smooth trivialization of
{}_{+}N\partial M_{l}
with
d\rho_{l}(\nu_{l})=1
(
Section

2.5.1
). Thus
\nu_{l}\equiv\nu^{\prime}_{l}
via
T_{l,\epsilon}\equiv T^{\prime}_{l,\epsilon}
.

From
4.2.1
,
4.2.2
,
4.2.3
,
4.2.12
and
4.2.15
, it easily follows that
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{l}
is of bounded geometry,
(M_{l},g_{\text{\rm b},l})
satisfies the properties
1
and
2
of
Section

2.5.18
,
d(\ln\rho_{l})\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm ub}}(\mathring{M}_{l};T^{*}\mathring{M}_{l})
, and
Y_{l}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M^{1}_{l},\mathcal{F}^{1}_{l})
with respect to
g_{\text{\rm b},l}
, and we can assume
Z^{\prime}_{L,l}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M^{\prime\,1}_{L,l},\mathcal{F}^{\prime\,1}_{L,l})
with respect to
g^{\prime}_{\text{\rm b},L,l}
. So
Z_{l}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm ub}}(M^{1}_{l},\mathcal{F}^{1}_{l})
with respect to
g_{\text{\rm b},l}
. By
4.2.11
and since
M^{1}_{l}\setminus T^{1}_{l,\epsilon}
is compact, we also get that the metrics
g_{\text{\rm b},l}
and
g_{\text{\rm c},l}
are quasi-isometric on
M^{1}_{l}
; this also follows because both of these metrics are restrictions to
\mathring{M}_{l}
of b-metrics on the compact manifold with boundary
M_{l}
.

By (
4.2.16
), we have
\omega=\operatorname{sign}(\omega(Z_{l}))\,\rho_{l}\omega_{\text{\rm b},l}
on
\mathring{M}_{l}\cap\boldsymbol{T}_{\epsilon}\equiv M^{1}_{l}\cap T_{\epsilon}
. This equality is also true on
M^{1}_{l}\setminus T_{\epsilon_{0}}
, where
g_{M}=g_{\text{\rm b},l}
and
\rho_{l}=1
. Indeed, we can choose
\rho_{l}
so that this equality holds on the whole of
\mathring{M}_{l}\equiv M^{1}_{l}
. So

d\omega=\operatorname{sign}(\omega(Z_{l}))\,d\rho_{l}\wedge\omega_{\text{\rm b},l}=d\rho_{l}\wedge\rho_{l}^{-1}\omega=d(\ln\rho_{l})\wedge\omega

on
\mathring{M}_{l}\equiv M^{1}_{l}
, yielding

(4.3.1)

\eta_{0}=d_{0,1}(\ln\rho_{l})\equiv d_{\mathcal{F}_{l}}(\ln\rho_{l})\;.

Taking combinations of the above objects on the manifolds
M_{l}
, we get a boundary-defining function
\boldsymbol{\rho}
on
\boldsymbol{M}
, a trivialization
\boldsymbol{\nu}
of
{}_{+}N\partial\boldsymbol{M}
, real
1
-forms
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\text{\rm b}}
and
\boldsymbol{\eta}
, and b-metrics
\boldsymbol{g}_{\text{\rm b}}
and
\boldsymbol{g}_{\text{\rm c}}
. They agree with
\boldsymbol{\rho}^{\prime}
,
\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\prime}
,
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}_{\text{\rm b}}
,
\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}
,
\boldsymbol{g}^{\prime}_{\text{\rm b}}
and
\boldsymbol{g}^{\prime}_{\text{\rm c}}
on
\boldsymbol{T}_{\epsilon}\equiv\boldsymbol{T}^{\prime}_{\epsilon}
.

4.3.4.
The components of
M^{1}

Recall that every
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{l}\equiv\mathring{\mathcal{F}}_{l}
on
M^{1}_{l}\equiv\mathring{M}_{l}
is a transversely complete
\mathbb{R}
-Lie foliation, where this transverse structure is defined by
Z_{l}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\text{\rm com}}(M^{1}_{l},\mathcal{F}^{1}_{l})
. Of course, the transverse orientation of
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{l}
defined by
Z_{l}
may not agree with the original transverse orientation of
\mathcal{F}
.

The Fedida’s description of
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{l}
is given by a regular covering
\pi_{l}:\widetilde{M}^{1}_{l}\to M^{1}_{l}
with group of deck transformations
\Gamma_{l}
, a holonomy monomorphism
h_{l}:\Gamma_{l}\to\mathbb{R}
and a developing map
D_{l}:\widetilde{M}^{1}_{l}\to\mathbb{R}
(
Sections

3.1.9
and
3.1.11
). Note that
\Gamma_{l}
has finite rank because
M^{1}_{l}\equiv\mathring{M}_{l}
and
M_{l}
is compact. Recall that the action of any
\gamma\in\Gamma_{l}
on
\widetilde{M}^{1}_{l}
is denoted by
\tilde{p}\mapsto\gamma\cdot\tilde{p}
or by
T_{\gamma}
.

Let
Y_{l}
and
\xi_{l}=\{\xi_{l}^{t}\}
denote the restrictions of
Y
and
\xi
to every
M^{1}_{l}
. Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{l}
,
\widetilde{Y}_{l}
,
\widetilde{Z}_{l}
,
\tilde{\xi}_{l}=\{\tilde{\xi}_{l}^{t}\}
and
\tilde{\phi}_{l}=\{\tilde{\phi}_{l}^{t}\}
be the lifts to
\widetilde{M}^{1}_{l}
of
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{l}
,
Z_{l}
and
\phi_{l}
, respectively. Recall from
Section

3.4.7
that
\widetilde{Z}_{l}
is
\Gamma_{l}
-invariant and
D_{l}
-projectable, and
\tilde{\phi}_{l}
is
\Gamma_{l}
-equivariant. Moreover we can assume
D_{l*}\widetilde{Z}_{l}=\partial_{x}
, where
x
denotes the canonical global coordinate of
\mathbb{R}
, and therefore
\tilde{\phi}_{l}
corresponds via
D_{l}
to the flow
\bar{\phi}_{l}
on
\mathbb{R}
defined by
\bar{\phi}_{l}^{t}(x)=t+x
. So
D_{l}
restricts to diffeomorphisms between the orbits of
\tilde{\phi}_{l}
and
\mathbb{R}
.

Proposition 4.3.1
.

Given any leaf
L_{l}
of
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{l}
, there is a left action of
\Gamma_{l}
on
L_{l}
and there is an identity
\widetilde{M}^{1}_{l}\equiv\mathbb{R}\times L_{l}
such that:

(1)

D_{l}
is the left-factor projection;

(2)

\widetilde{Y}_{l}\equiv(\partial_{x},0)
and
\tilde{\xi}_{l}^{t}(x,y)=(t+x,y)
;

(3)

the action of
\Gamma_{l}
on
\widetilde{M}^{1}_{l}
is given by
\gamma\cdot(x,y)=(h_{l}(\gamma)+x,\gamma\cdot y)
; and

(4)

there is some compact
K_{l}\subset M^{1}_{l}
so that, if
\gamma\cdot y=y
for some
\gamma\in\Gamma_{l}
and
y\in L_{l}\setminus K_{l}
, then
\gamma=e
.

Proof.

Since
\widetilde{Y}_{l}
is projectable by
D_{l}
to
\partial_{x}
because
\overline{\widetilde{Y}_{l}}=\overline{\widetilde{Z}_{l}}
, it follows that
D_{l}
also restricts to diffeomorphisms of the
\tilde{\xi}_{l}
-orbits to
\mathbb{R}
. So, given any leaf
\widetilde{L}_{l}
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{l}
over
L_{l}
, we get
\widetilde{M}^{1}_{l}\equiv\mathbb{R}\times\widetilde{L}_{l}\equiv\mathbb{R}\times L_{l}
such that
1
and
2
hold.

The action of every
\gamma\in\Gamma_{l}
on
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}\times L_{l}\equiv\widetilde{M}^{1}_{l}
can be written as
\gamma\cdot(x,y)=(h_{l}(\gamma)+x,T_{\gamma}(x,y))
for some smooth map
T_{\gamma}:\mathbb{R}\times L_{l}\to L_{l}
. Then, since the flow
\tilde{\xi}_{l}^{t}
is
\Gamma_{l}
-equivariant, it easily follows that
T_{\gamma}(x,y)=T_{\gamma}(t+x,y)
. So
T_{\gamma}(x,y)
is independent of
x
, and therefore it can be written as
\gamma\cdot y
. It is easy to check that this defines a left
\Gamma_{l}
-action on
L_{l}
, and
3
follows.

Let us prove
4
. If
\gamma\cdot y=y
for some
\gamma\in\Gamma_{l}\setminus\{e\}
and
y\in L_{l}
, then we easily compute
\gamma\cdot\tilde{\xi}^{t}(x,y)=\tilde{\xi}^{h_{l}(\gamma)+t}(x,y)
for all
x,t\in\mathbb{R}
. Thus the
\tilde{\xi}^{t}
-orbit of
(x,y)
is invariant by the action of
\gamma
, and therefore the
\xi^{t}
-orbit of
[x,y]
is closed because
\gamma\neq e
. Since
Y\equiv Y^{\prime}
on
T_{\epsilon}\equiv T^{\prime}_{\epsilon}
, it follows that
y\in L_{l}\setminus T_{\epsilon}
, and
M^{1}_{l}\setminus T_{\epsilon}
is compact in
M^{1}_{l}
.
∎

Remark 4.3.2
.

In
4.3.1
, the projection
L_{l}\to\Gamma_{l}\backslash L_{l}
may not be a covering map, and therefore
(M^{1}_{l},\mathcal{F}^{1}_{l})
may not be given by a suspension. According to its proof, a point
y\in L_{l}
is fixed by some
\gamma\in\Gamma_{l}\setminus\{e\}
just when
\mathbb{R}\times\{y\}
projects to a closed orbit of
\xi^{t}
in
M^{1}_{l}
whose group of periods contains
h_{l}(\gamma)
.

According to
4.3.1
, we may use the notation
[x,y]=\pi_{l}(x,y)\in M^{1}_{l}
for
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}\times L_{l}\equiv\widetilde{M}^{1}_{l}
, and the action of every
\gamma\in\Gamma_{l}
on
L_{l}
may be also denoted by
T_{\gamma}
. Like in (
4.2.1
) and (
4.2.2
), we get

(4.3.2)

\tilde{\phi}_{l}^{t}(x,\tilde{y})=(t+x,\tilde{\phi}_{l,x}^{t}(\tilde{y}))\;,\quad\widetilde{Z}_{l}=(\partial_{x},\widetilde{Z}_{l,x})\;,

for some smooth families,
\{\,\tilde{\phi}_{l,x}^{t}\mid x,t\in\mathbb{R}\,\}\subset\operatorname{Diffeo}(L_{l})
and
\{\,\widetilde{Z}_{l,x}\mid x\in\mathbb{R}\,\}\subset\mathfrak{X}(L_{l})
, such that

T_{\gamma}\tilde{\phi}_{l,x}^{t}=\tilde{\phi}_{l,h_{l}(\gamma)+x}^{t}T_{\gamma}\;,\quad T_{\gamma*}\widetilde{Z}_{x}=\widetilde{Z}_{h_{l}(\gamma)+x}\;.

Let
c
be a closed orbit of
\phi_{l}
with period
t_{0}
, and let
p=[x,y]\in c
and
\tilde{p}=(x,y)\in\widetilde{M}^{1}_{l}\equiv\mathbb{R}\times L_{l}
. Then
k=t_{0}/\ell(c)\in\mathbb{Z}
and there is a unique
\gamma_{0}\in\Gamma_{l}
such that
\tilde{\phi}_{l}^{t_{0}}(\tilde{p})=\gamma_{0}\cdot\tilde{p}
. Using (
4.3.2
) and
4.3.1

3
, it easily follows that
t_{0}=h_{l}(\gamma_{0})
and
\tilde{\phi}_{l,x}^{t_{0}}(y)=\gamma_{0}\cdot y
; i.e.,
y
is a fixed point of the diffeomorphism
T_{\gamma_{0}}^{-1}\tilde{\phi}^{t_{0}}_{l,x}
of
L_{l}
. Moreover
y
is simple if and only if
c
is simple, and, in this case,
\epsilon_{y}(T_{\gamma_{0}}\tilde{\phi}_{l,x}^{t_{0}})=\epsilon_{c}(k,\phi)=\epsilon_{c}(k)
.

We have
\tilde{\omega}_{\text{\rm b},l}:=\pi_{l}^{*}\omega_{\text{\rm b},l}=D_{l}^{*}dx\equiv dx
because
D_{l*}\widetilde{Z}_{l}=\partial_{x}
and
\omega_{\text{\rm b},l}(Z_{l})=1
(
Section

4.3.3
).

4.3.5.
Metric properties of the components of
M^{1}

With the notation of
Sections

4.3.2
,
4.3.3
and
4.3.4
, for leaves
L\subset M^{0}\cap\overline{M^{1}_{l}}
and
0<\epsilon^{\prime}\leq\epsilon
, the open subsets

\widetilde{T}^{\prime\,1}_{L,l,\epsilon^{\prime}}=\pi_{M^{\prime}_{L,l}}^{-1}(T^{\prime\,1}_{L,l,\epsilon^{\prime}})\subset\widetilde{M}^{\prime\,1}_{L,l}\;,\quad\widetilde{T}^{1}_{L,l,\epsilon^{\prime}}=\pi_{l}^{-1}(T^{1}_{L,l,\epsilon^{\prime}})\subset\widetilde{M}^{1}_{l}\;,

are invariant by
\Gamma_{L}
and
\Gamma_{l}
, respectively. Let
\tilde{\rho}_{l}=\pi_{l}^{*}\rho_{l}
and
M^{1}_{l,\epsilon^{\prime}}=M^{1}_{l}\setminus T_{l,\epsilon^{\prime}}
, which is a connected compact smooth submanifold with boundary of
M^{1}_{l}
. Then
\widetilde{T}^{1}_{l,\epsilon^{\prime}}:=\pi_{l}^{-1}(T^{1}_{l,\epsilon^{\prime}})=\{\tilde{\rho}_{l}<\epsilon^{\prime}\}
is a
\Gamma_{l}
-invariant open subspace of
\widetilde{M}^{1}_{l}
, and
\pi_{l}:\widetilde{M}^{1}_{l,\epsilon^{\prime}}:=\widetilde{M}^{1}_{l}\setminus\widetilde{T}^{1}_{l,\epsilon^{\prime}}\to M^{1}_{l,\epsilon^{\prime}}
is a regular
\Gamma_{l}
-covering.

Let
d_{l}
denote the length-metric on
M^{1}_{l}
defined by
g_{\text{\rm b},l}
. Let
\tilde{g}_{\text{\rm b},l}
and
\tilde{g}_{\text{\rm c},l}
be the lifts to
\widetilde{M}^{1}_{l}
of
g_{\text{\rm b},l}
and
g_{\text{\rm c},l}
. Both of them induce the same leafwise metric
g_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{l}}
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{l}
, which is the lift of
g_{\mathcal{F}_{l}}
. Let
\tilde{d}_{l}
and
\tilde{d}_{l,\epsilon^{\prime}}
denote the length-metrics on
\widetilde{M}^{1}_{l}
and
\widetilde{M}^{1}_{l,\epsilon^{\prime}}
defined by
\tilde{g}_{\text{\rm b},l}
. Similarly, let
\tilde{d}_{\text{\rm c},l}
and
\tilde{d}_{\text{\rm c},l,\epsilon^{\prime}}
be the length-metrics on
\widetilde{M}^{1}_{l}
and
\widetilde{M}^{1}_{l,\epsilon^{\prime}}
defined by
\tilde{g}_{\text{\rm c},l}
. Since
g_{\text{\rm b},l}
and
g_{\text{\rm c},l}
are quasi-isometric (
Section

4.3.3
), the metrics
\tilde{g}_{\text{\rm b},l}
and
\tilde{g}_{\text{\rm c},l}
are also quasi-isometric. Therefore there is some
C_{1}\geq 1
such that, for all
\tilde{p},\tilde{q}\in\widetilde{M}^{1}_{l}
,

(4.3.3)

C_{1}^{-1}\tilde{d}_{l}(\tilde{p},\tilde{q})\leq\tilde{d}_{c,l}(\tilde{p},\tilde{q})\leq C_{1}\tilde{d}_{l}(\tilde{p},\tilde{q})\;.

On the other hand,
\tilde{d}_{l}\leq\tilde{d}_{l,\epsilon^{\prime}}
on
\widetilde{M}^{1}_{l,\epsilon^{\prime}}
.

Lemma 4.3.3
.

We have
\tilde{d}_{\text{\rm c},l}=\tilde{d}_{\text{\rm c},l,\epsilon^{\prime}}
on
\widetilde{M}^{1}_{l,\epsilon^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

It is enough to show that any
\tilde{g}_{\text{\rm c},l}
-geodesic segment with end-points in
\widetilde{M}^{1}_{l,\epsilon^{\prime}}
is contained in
\widetilde{M}^{1}_{l,\epsilon^{\prime}}
(
\widetilde{M}^{1}_{l,\epsilon^{\prime}}
is
\tilde{g}_{\text{\rm c},l}
-convex). This follows easily using that the level hypersurfaces of
\tilde{\rho}_{l}
are
\tilde{g}_{\text{\rm c},l}
-totally geodesic because the level hypersurfaces of
\rho_{l}
in
T^{1}_{l,\epsilon}
are
g_{\text{\rm c},l}
-totally geodesic (
13
of
Section

4.3.3
).
∎

Let
|{\cdot}|=|{\cdot}|_{l}:\Gamma_{l}\to\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
|{\cdot}|=|{\cdot}|_{L}:\Gamma_{L}\to\mathbb{N}_{0}
be the word length functions induced by any choice of finite sets of generators of
\Gamma_{l}
and
\Gamma_{L}
. By the compactness of
M^{1}_{l,\epsilon^{\prime}}
, there is some
C_{2}=C_{2}(\epsilon^{\prime})\geq 1
such that, for all
\gamma\in\Gamma_{l}
and
\tilde{p}\in\widetilde{M}^{1}_{l,\epsilon^{\prime}}
,

(4.3.4)

C_{2}^{-1}|\gamma|\leq\tilde{d}_{l,\epsilon^{\prime}}(\tilde{p},\gamma\cdot\tilde{p})\leq C_{2}|\gamma|\;.

Since
g_{\text{\rm b},l}
and
g_{\text{\rm c},l}
are quasi-isometric on
M^{1}_{l}
, it follows from (
4.3.3
), (
4.3.4
) and
4.3.3
that there is some
C_{3}=C_{3}(\epsilon^{\prime})\geq 1
such that, for all
\gamma\in\Gamma_{l}
and
\tilde{p}\in\widetilde{M}^{1}_{l,\epsilon^{\prime}}
,

(4.3.5)

C_{3}^{-1}|\gamma|\leq\tilde{d}_{l}(\tilde{p},\gamma\cdot\tilde{p})\leq C_{3}|\gamma|\;.

Remark 4.3.4
.

For any leaf
L\subset M^{0}\cap\overline{M^{1}_{l}}
, the given descriptions of
\mathcal{F}
on
T^{1}_{L,\epsilon}
and
M^{1}_{l}
have the following relation, whose proof is omitted because it will not be used. There is a monomorphism
H_{L,l}:\Gamma_{L}\to\Gamma_{l}
such that, for every connected component
\widetilde{T}^{1}_{L,l,\epsilon^{\prime},0}
of
\widetilde{T}^{1}_{L,l,\epsilon^{\prime}}
, the identity
T^{\prime\,1}_{L,l,\epsilon^{\prime}}\equiv T^{1}_{L,l,\epsilon^{\prime}}
can be lifted to an
H_{L,l}
-equivariant identity
\widetilde{T}^{\prime\,1}_{L,l,\epsilon^{\prime}}\equiv\widetilde{T}^{1}_{L,l,\epsilon^{\prime},0}
, which is locally equivariant with respect to the local flows defined by
\tilde{\xi}^{\prime}_{L}
on
\widetilde{T}^{\prime\,1}_{L,\epsilon^{\prime},\pm}
and
\tilde{\xi}_{l}
on
\widetilde{T}^{1}_{L,l,\epsilon^{\prime},0}
, and so that
D_{l}
corresponds to
D^{\prime}_{L,l}
.

Chapter 5
Conormal leafwise reduced cohomology

5.1.
Conormal sequence of leafwise currents

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a transversely orientable smooth foliation of codimension one on a closed manifold
M
satisfying the conditions
3
and
4
of
Section

4.1.2
. Then
M^{0}
is determined by
\mathcal{F}
in the cases
4
–
6
of
Section

4.1.2
, whereas
M^{0}
must be also given in the case
3
. The compactness condition on
M
is assumed for the sake of simplicity, but all concepts, results and arguments of this section have straightforward extensions to the case where
M
is not compact and
M^{0}
is compact, using compactly supported versions or versions without support restrictions of the spaces of leafwise currents that will be considered. The compactly supported versions, in the non-compact case, will be used in the arguments.

Since
\operatorname{Diff}^{1}(\mathcal{F};\Lambda\mathcal{F})\subset\operatorname{Diff}^{1}(M,M^{0};\Lambda\mathcal{F})
, the graded LCHS

I(\mathcal{F})=I\Lambda^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F}):=I(M,M^{0};\Lambda\mathcal{F})

becomes a topological complex with
d_{\mathcal{F}}
(
Sections

2.2.7
and
3.2.1
). If we take coefficients in some leafwise flat vector bundle
E
, then the notation
I(\mathcal{F};E)
will be used, and all other notations will be modified in the same way. We may even consider
I(\mathcal{F};E)
for an arbitrary vector bundle
E
, missing the leafwise differential map
d_{\mathcal{F}}
.

The topological complex
(I(\mathcal{F}),d_{\mathcal{F}})
produces the
conormal leafwise cohomology
and
conormal leafwise reduced cohomology
of
\mathcal{F}
(or of
(\mathcal{F},M^{0})
when
M^{0}
is not determined by
\mathcal{F}
), denoted by
H^{\bullet}I(\mathcal{F})
and
\bar{H}^{\bullet}I(\mathcal{F})
, which are LCSs (
Section

2.1.13
). The image and kernel of
d_{\mathcal{F}}
in
I(\mathcal{F})
are denoted by
BI(\mathcal{F})
and
ZI(\mathcal{F})
, and we write
\bar{B}I(\mathcal{F})=\overline{BI(\mathcal{F})}
.

The LCHSs

I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})=I^{(s)}\Lambda^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F}):=I^{(s)}(M,M^{0};\Lambda\mathcal{F})\quad(s\in\mathbb{R})

also become topological complexes with
d_{\mathcal{F}}
(
Section

2.2.7
). The notation
H^{\bullet}I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
,
\bar{H}^{\bullet}I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
,
BI^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
,
ZI^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
and
\bar{B}I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
is used as before. We have continuous inclusion maps (
Section

2.2.2
)

(5.1.1)

j_{s}:I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})\hookrightarrow I(\mathcal{F})\;,\quad j_{s,s^{\prime}}:I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})\hookrightarrow I^{(s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F})\quad(s^{\prime}\leq s)\;.

The induced homomorphism in cohomology and reduced cohomology are denoted by
j_{s*}
,
j_{s,s^{\prime}*}
,
\bar{\jmath}_{s*}
and
\bar{\jmath}_{s,s^{\prime}*}
. The homomorphism
j_{s,s^{\prime}*}
and
\bar{\jmath}_{s*}
form inductive spectra, giving rise to inductive limits as
s\downarrow-\infty
. The maps
j_{s*}
and
\bar{\jmath}_{s*}
induce canonical continuous linear isomorphisms (
Section

5.2
),

(5.1.2)

\left\{\begin{gathered}\tilde{\jmath}_{*}:=\varinjlim j_{s*}:\widetilde{H}^{\bullet}I(\mathcal{F}):=\varinjlim H^{\bullet}I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{\bullet}I(\mathcal{F})\;,\\
\hat{\jmath}_{*}:=\varinjlim\bar{\jmath}_{s*}:\widehat{H}^{\bullet}I(\mathcal{F}):=\varinjlim\bar{H}^{\bullet}I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})\xrightarrow{\cong}\bar{H}^{\bullet}I(\mathcal{F})\;.\end{gathered}\right.

The canonical maps of the steps to the inductive limits are denoted by

\tilde{\jmath}_{s*}:H^{\bullet}I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})\to\widetilde{H}^{\bullet}I(\mathcal{F})\;,\quad\hat{\jmath}_{s*}:\bar{H}^{\bullet}I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})\to\widehat{H}^{\bullet}I(\mathcal{F})\;.

The graded LCHSs,

J(\mathcal{F})=J\Lambda^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F}):=J(M,M^{0};\Lambda\mathcal{F})\;,\quad K(\mathcal{F})=K\Lambda^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F}):=K(M,M^{0};\Lambda\mathcal{F})\;,

also become topological complexes with
d_{\mathcal{F}}
(
Section

2.6.13
). The above kind of notation is also used for the induced spaces:
BJ(\mathcal{F})
,
ZJ(\mathcal{F})
,
\bar{B}J(\mathcal{F})
and
H^{\bullet}J(\mathcal{F})
,
\bar{H}^{\bullet}J(\mathcal{F})
, and the same for
K(\mathcal{F})
.

Similarly, we have topological complexes
J^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
,
J^{m}(\mathcal{F})
and
K^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
(
s,m\in\mathbb{R}
) with
d_{\mathcal{F}}
(
Section

2.6.13
). The analogs of the inclusion maps (
5.1.1
) for the spaces
J^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
and
K^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
are denoted in the same way. The induced homomorphisms in cohomology and reduced cohomology form inductive spectra. Their inductive limits, denoted by
\widetilde{H}^{\bullet}K(\mathcal{F})
,
\widehat{H}^{\bullet}K(\mathcal{F})
,
\widetilde{H}^{\bullet}J(\mathcal{F})
and
\widehat{H}^{\bullet}J(\mathcal{F})
, satisfy analogs of (
5.1.2
) (proved with the same arguments). In fact, in the case of
K(\mathcal{F})
, we have canonical TVS-identities (
5.3.2
),

(5.1.3)

\left\{\begin{gathered}H^{\bullet}K(\mathcal{F})\equiv\bar{H}^{\bullet}K(\mathcal{F})\;,\quad H^{\bullet}K^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})\equiv\bar{H}^{\bullet}K^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})\;,\\
\widetilde{H}^{\bullet}K^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})\equiv H^{\bullet}K(\mathcal{F})\;,\quad\widehat{H}^{\bullet}K^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})\equiv\bar{H}^{\bullet}K(\mathcal{F})\;.\end{gathered}\right.

There are also continuous inclusion maps (
Section

2.6.7
)

(5.1.4)

\left\{\begin{gathered}j_{m}:J^{m}(\mathcal{F})\hookrightarrow J(\mathcal{F})\;,\quad j_{m,m^{\prime}}:J^{m}(\mathcal{F})\hookrightarrow J^{m^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F})\quad(m^{\prime}\leq m)\;,\\
j_{s,m}:J^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})\hookrightarrow J^{m}(\mathcal{F})\quad(m<s-n/2-1)\;,\\
j_{m,s}:J^{m}(\mathcal{F})\hookrightarrow J^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})\quad(s\leq m,0)\;,\end{gathered}\right.

denoted like in (
5.1.1
) with some abuse of notation. The homomorphisms induced by the maps
j_{m,m^{\prime}}
in cohomology and reduced cohomology form inductive spectra whose inductive limits as
m\downarrow-\infty
agree with the previous ones for
J(\mathcal{F})
, and the maps
j_{m}
induce a continuous linear isomorphism analogous to (
5.1.2
).

There are similar constructions for the spaces of the symbol-order filtration of
I(\mathcal{F})
and
K(\mathcal{F})
, with similar properties, but they will not be used here.

The
leafwise conormal exact sequence
of
\mathcal{F}
is the bottom row of (
2.6.41
) with
\Lambda\mathcal{F}
,

(5.1.5)

0\to K(\mathcal{F})\xrightarrow{\iota}I(\mathcal{F})\xrightarrow{R}J(\mathcal{F})\to 0\;.

Besides being exact in the category of continuous linear maps between LCSs, it is compatible with
d_{\mathcal{F}}
. The exactness of the induced sequences,

(5.1.6)

\displaystyle 0\to H^{\bullet}K(\mathcal{F})\xrightarrow{\iota_{*}}H^{\bullet}I(\mathcal{F})\xrightarrow{R_{*}}H^{\bullet}J(\mathcal{F})\to 0\;,

(5.1.7)

\displaystyle 0\to H^{\bullet}K(\mathcal{F})\xrightarrow{\bar{\iota}_{*}}\bar{H}^{\bullet}I(\mathcal{F})\xrightarrow{\bar{R}_{*}}\bar{H}^{\bullet}J(\mathcal{F})\to 0\;,

will be proved in
Section

5.5
; in particular, this shows
1.3.3
.

Concerning notation, the subscript “
s
” may be added to the notation of cochain maps between the topological complexes
K^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
,
I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
or
J^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
, like

(5.1.8)

\iota_{s}=\iota:K^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})\to I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})\;,\quad R_{s}=R:I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})\to J^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})\;.

The subscript “
s
” may be also added to the elements of their cohomologies or reduced cohomologies:
[\alpha]_{s}\in H^{\bullet}I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
and
\overline{[\alpha]}_{s}\in\bar{H}^{\bullet}I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
for
\alpha\in ZI^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
.

5.2.
Injective limits in cohomology and reduced cohomology

The purpose of this section is to prove that the maps (
5.1.2
) are isomorphisms. The details are given for the case of
\bar{H}^{\bullet}I(\mathcal{F})
. Some remarks indicate how to modify the arguments to show the simpler case of
H^{\bullet}I(\mathcal{F})
.

5.2.1.
Injectivity of
\hat{\jmath}_{*}

Take any element in
\ker\hat{\jmath}_{*}
, which is of the form
\hat{\jmath}_{s*}(\overline{[\alpha]}_{s})
for some
\overline{[\alpha]}_{s}\in\bar{H}^{\bullet}I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
. Then there is some net
\varphi_{l}\in I(\mathcal{F})
such that
\alpha=\lim_{l}d_{\mathcal{F}}\varphi_{l}
in
I(\mathcal{F})
. We can assume
\varphi_{l}\in C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})
by the density of
C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})
in
I(\mathcal{F})
(
Section

2.2.2
). The set
\{\alpha,d_{\mathcal{F}}\varphi_{l}\}_{l}
is compact in
I(\mathcal{F})
. Then
\{\alpha,d_{\mathcal{F}}\beta_{l}\}_{l}
is contained and compact in some step
I^{(s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F})
(
s^{\prime}\leq s
) because
I(\mathcal{F})
is compactly retractive (
Section

2.2.2
). Thus
\alpha=\lim_{l}d_{\mathcal{F}}\varphi_{l}
in
I^{(s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F})
; otherwise, using that
\{\alpha,d_{\mathcal{F}}\varphi_{l}\}_{l}
is compact in
I^{(s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F})
, it is easy to find a subnet
d_{\mathcal{F}}\varphi_{l_{k}}
convergent to some
\beta\neq\alpha
in
I^{(s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F})
, which contradicts the continuity of
j_{s^{\prime}}:I^{(s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F})\to I(\mathcal{F})
and the convergence
d_{\mathcal{F}}\varphi_{l}\to\alpha
in
I(\mathcal{F})
. (Indeed, we can assume
d_{\mathcal{F}}\phi_{l}
is a sequence because
I^{(s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F})
is a Fréchet space.) So
\overline{[\alpha]}_{s^{\prime}}=0
in
\bar{H}^{\bullet}I^{(s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F})
, and therefore
\hat{\jmath}_{s*}(\overline{[\alpha]}_{s})=\hat{\jmath}_{s^{\prime}*}(\overline{[\alpha]}_{s^{\prime}})=0
.

Remark 5.2.1
.

To prove injectivity of
\tilde{\jmath}_{*}
, take some
\tilde{\jmath}_{s*}(\overline{[\alpha]}_{s})
in
\ker\tilde{\jmath}_{*}
. Now modify the above argument by using cohomology classes, and taking an element
\varphi\in I(\mathcal{F})
with
d_{\mathcal{F}}\varphi=\alpha
instead of a net
\varphi_{l}
. Then
\varphi
and
\alpha
are in some step
I^{(s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F})
(
s^{\prime}\leq s
), yielding
[\alpha]_{s^{\prime}}=0
in
H^{\bullet}I^{(s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F})
, and therefore
\hat{\jmath}_{s*}([\alpha]_{s})=\hat{\jmath}_{s^{\prime}*}([\alpha]_{s^{\prime}})=0
.

5.2.2.
Surjectivity of
\hat{\jmath}_{*}

For any
\overline{[\alpha]}\in\bar{H}^{\bullet}I(\mathcal{F})
, there is some
s
such that
\alpha\in I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
, and therefore
\alpha\in ZI^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
. Hence the element
\overline{[\alpha]}_{s}\in\bar{H}I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
is defined, and the element
\hat{\jmath}_{s*}(\overline{[\alpha]}_{s})\in\widehat{H}^{\bullet}I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
is mapped to
\overline{[\alpha]}
by
\hat{\jmath}_{*}
.

Remark 5.2.2
.

To prove the surjectivity of
\tilde{\jmath}_{*}
, simply modify the argument by using cohomology classes instead of reduced cohomology classes.

5.3.
Description of
H^{\bullet}K(\mathcal{F})

Consider also the notation of
Section

4.3.3
. For every
z\in\mathbb{C}
, we have the Witten’s complex
d_{z}=d+z\,{\eta\wedge}
on
C^{\infty}(M^{0};\Lambda)
, whose cohomology is denoted by
H^{\bullet}_{z}(M^{0})
(
Section

2.9.1
). Consider also the trivialization of the flat line bundle
\Omega^{z}NM^{0}=\Omega^{z}N\mathcal{F}|_{M^{0}}
defined by
|\omega|^{z}
. Then, by (
2.9.5
) and since
d\omega=\eta\wedge\omega
(
11
of
Section

4.3.3
),

\displaystyle C^{\pm\infty}(M^{0};\Lambda\otimes\Omega^{z}NM^{0})\equiv C^{\pm\infty}(M^{0};\Lambda)\otimes\mathbb{R}|\omega|^{z}\equiv C^{\pm\infty}(M^{0};\Lambda)\;,

\displaystyle d\equiv d_{z}\otimes 1\equiv d_{z}\;,\quad H^{\bullet}(M^{0};\Omega^{z}NM^{0})\equiv H_{z}(M^{0})\;.

These identities will be applied without further comment. By Reeb’s local stability, the following result follows from the case of a suspension foliation, which will be proved in
Section

5.6.1
(Corollary
5.6.2
).

Proposition 5.3.1
.

We have identities of topological complexes,

\displaystyle K(\mathcal{F})\equiv\bigoplus_{k}C^{\infty}(M^{0};\Lambda)\equiv\bigoplus_{k}C^{\infty}(M^{0};\Lambda\otimes\Omega^{-k-1}NM^{0})\;,

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{F}}\equiv\bigoplus_{k}d_{-k-1}\equiv\bigoplus_{k}d\;,

where
k
runs in
\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Moreover the subcomplex
K^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})\subset K(\mathcal{F})
corresponds to the finite direct sum with
k<-s-1/2
.

Corollary 5.3.2
.

We have TVS-identities,

H^{\bullet}K(\mathcal{F})\equiv\bigoplus_{k}H_{-k-1}^{\bullet}(M^{0})\equiv\bigoplus_{k}H^{\bullet}(M^{0},\Omega^{-k-1}NM^{0})\;.

Moreover
H^{\bullet}K^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
is the topological vector subspace of
H^{\bullet}K(\mathcal{F})
given by the finite direct sum with
k<-s-1/2
. In particular, (
5.1.3
) is satisfied.

Remark 5.3.3
.

The differential complexes on
M^{0}
used in
5.3.1
obviously split into direct sums of the same complexes given by leaves
L\subset M^{0}
. The same applies to their cohomologies in Corollary
5.3.2
.

Remark 5.3.4
.

Like in
5.3.1
, the isomorphism (
2.6.24
) gives

C^{-\infty}_{M^{0}}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})\equiv\bigoplus_{k}C^{-\infty}(M^{0};\Lambda)\equiv\bigoplus_{k}C^{-\infty}(M^{0};\Lambda\otimes\Omega^{-k-1}NM^{0})\;.

5.4.
Description of
\bar{H}^{\bullet}J(\mathcal{F})

With the notation of
Sections

3.1.12
and
4.1.2
, by (
2.6.30
) and (
2.6.31
), for
m\in\mathbb{R}
,

(5.4.1)

\displaystyle J^{m}(\mathcal{F})\cong\boldsymbol{\rho}^{m}H_{\text{\rm b}}^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{M};\Lambda\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}})\equiv\boldsymbol{\rho}^{m+\frac{1}{2}}H^{\infty}(\mathring{\boldsymbol{M}};\Lambda\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}})\;,

(5.4.2)

\displaystyle J(\mathcal{F})\cong\bigcup_{m}\boldsymbol{\rho}^{m}H_{\text{\rm b}}^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{M};\Lambda\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}})=\bigcup_{m}\boldsymbol{\rho}^{m}H^{\infty}(\mathring{\boldsymbol{M}};\Lambda\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}})\;,

as topological complexes with
d_{\mathcal{F}}
,
d_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}}
or
d_{\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}}}
, using the b-metric
\boldsymbol{g}
to define
H_{\text{\rm b}}^{\infty}(\boldsymbol{M};\Lambda\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}})
, and using
\boldsymbol{g}|_{\mathring{\boldsymbol{M}}}
to define
H^{\infty}(\mathring{\boldsymbol{M}};\Lambda\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}})
.

On the other hand, since
\boldsymbol{\eta}=d_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}}(\ln\boldsymbol{\rho})
on
\mathring{\boldsymbol{M}}
by (
4.3.1
), we get isomorphisms of topological complexes,

(5.4.3)

\boldsymbol{\rho}^{-m-\frac{1}{2}}:\big(\boldsymbol{\rho}^{m+\frac{1}{2}}H^{\infty}(\mathring{\boldsymbol{M}};\Lambda\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}}),d_{\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}}}\big)\xrightarrow{\cong}\big(H^{\infty}(\mathring{\boldsymbol{M}};\Lambda\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}}),d_{\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}},m+\frac{1}{2}}\big)\;,

by the leafwise version of (
2.9.4
) (
Section

3.5
).

By (
5.4.1
) and (
5.4.3
), and the analog of (
3.4.16
) for
\Delta_{\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}},m+\frac{1}{2}}
in
H^{\infty}(\mathring{\boldsymbol{M}};\Lambda\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}})
(
Section

3.5
), we get induced TVS-isomorphisms

(5.4.4)

\displaystyle\bar{H}^{\bullet}J^{m}(\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\cong\bar{H}^{\bullet}\big(\boldsymbol{\rho}^{m+\frac{1}{2}}H^{\infty}(\mathring{\boldsymbol{M}};\Lambda\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}}),d_{\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}}}\big)

(5.4.5)

\displaystyle\cong\bar{H}^{\bullet}\big(H^{\infty}(\mathring{\boldsymbol{M}};\Lambda\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}}),d_{\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}},m+\frac{1}{2}}\big)

(5.4.6)

\displaystyle\cong\ker\Delta_{\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}},m+\frac{1}{2}}\;.

By the analog of (
5.1.2
) for
J(\mathcal{F})
and (
5.4.6
), the LCHS
\bar{H}^{\bullet}J(\mathcal{F})
is an inductive limit of Hilbertian spaces. The isomorphisms (
5.4.4
) and (
5.4.5
) are also true in cohomology.

1.3.2
follows from the analog of (
5.1.2
) for
J(\mathcal{F})
and (
5.4.1
)–(
5.4.3
).

5.5.
Short exact sequence of conormal reduced cohomology

The goal of this section is to prove
1.3.3
; i.e., the exactenss (
5.1.7
). Some remarks will indicate how to modify the argument to get also the exactness of (
5.1.6
). To begin with, we choose appropriate partial extension maps.

5.5.1.
Compatibility of the maps
E_{m}
with
d_{\mathcal{F}}

For
m\in\mathbb{R}
, take
s\in\mathbb{R}
such that
s=0
if
m\geq 0
, and
m>s\in\mathbb{Z}^{-}
if
m<0
. For fixed
0<\epsilon<1
, using the tubular neighborhood
T:=T_{\epsilon}
of
M^{0}
in
M
(
Section

4.3.1
), consider the continuous inclusions of
I^{(s)}_{\text{\rm c}}(\mathcal{F}|_{T})\subset I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
and
J^{m}_{\text{\rm c}}(\mathcal{F}|_{T})\subset J^{m}(\mathcal{F})
, using the extension by zero. Let
E_{m,T}:J^{m}_{\text{\rm c}}(\mathcal{F}|_{T})\to I^{(s)}_{\text{\rm c}}(\mathcal{F}|_{T})
be the continuous linear partial extension map constructed in the proofs of the compactly supported versions of
2.5.1
and
2.6.4
with
\Lambda\mathcal{F}|_{T}
(see
2.5.3
and
2.5.4
). By the Reeb’s local stability, the following result follows from its case for suspension foliations, which will be proved in
Section

5.6.3
(
5.6.6
).

Proposition 5.5.1
.

E_{m,T}d_{\mathcal{F}}=d_{\mathcal{F}}E_{m,T}
.

Let
\{\lambda,\mu\}
be a smooth partition of unity of
\mathbb{R}
subordinated to the open cover
\{(-\epsilon,\epsilon),\mathbb{R}^{\times}\}
, which induces the smooth partition of unity
\{\lambda(\rho),\mu(\rho)\}
of
M
subordinated to the open cover
\{T,M^{1}\}
, where
\lambda(\rho)
(resp.,
\mu(\rho)
) is extended by
0
(resp.,
1
) to the whole of
M
. According to
2.5.2
and
2.5.3
, take the continuous linear partial extension map
E_{m}:J^{m}(\mathcal{F})\to I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
defined by

(5.5.1)

E_{m}\alpha=E_{m,T}(\lambda(\rho)\,\alpha)+\mu(\rho)\,\alpha\;.

Corollary 5.5.2
.

E_{m}d_{\mathcal{F}}=d_{\mathcal{F}}E_{m}
.

Proof.

By the version of
2.5.6
for
E_{m,T}
(
Section

2.6.12
), and since
d_{\mathcal{F}}\lambda(\rho)=-d_{\mathcal{F}}\mu(\rho)
is supported in
M^{1}
, we get, for
\alpha\in J^{m}(\mathcal{F})
,

\displaystyle E_{m}d_{\mathcal{F}}\alpha

\displaystyle=E_{m,T}(\lambda(\rho)\,d_{\mathcal{F}}\alpha)+\mu(\rho)\,d_{\mathcal{F}}\alpha

\displaystyle=E_{m,T}d_{\mathcal{F}}(\lambda(\rho)\,\alpha)-E_{m,T}(d_{\mathcal{F}}\lambda(\rho)\wedge\alpha)

\displaystyle\phantom{={}}{}+d_{\mathcal{F}}(\mu(\rho)\,\alpha)-d_{\mathcal{F}}\mu(\rho)\wedge\alpha\wedge\omega

\displaystyle=d_{\mathcal{F}}E_{m,T}(\lambda(\rho)\,\alpha)-d_{\mathcal{F}}\lambda(\rho)\wedge\alpha

\displaystyle\phantom{={}}{}+d_{\mathcal{F}}(\mu(\rho)\,\alpha)-d_{\mathcal{F}}\mu(\rho)\wedge\alpha

\displaystyle=d_{\mathcal{F}}E_{m,T}(\lambda(\rho)\,\alpha)+d_{\mathcal{F}}(\mu(\rho)\,\alpha)=d_{\mathcal{F}}E_{m}\alpha\;.\qed

5.5.2.
The maps
F_{m}

For
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
m<s-n/2-1
, we can consider
R:I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})\to J^{m}(\mathcal{F})
by the analog of (
2.5.37
) for
J(\mathcal{F})
(
Section

2.6.7
). Taking
s^{\prime}=0
if
m\geq 0
, and
m>s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{-}
if
m<0
, let
E_{m}:J^{m}(\mathcal{F})\to I^{(s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F})
be defined like in
Section

5.5.1
.We can also consider

(5.5.2)

E_{m}=E_{m}j_{s,m}:J^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})\to I^{(s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F})\;.

Then define the continuous linear map

F_{m}:=1-E_{m}R:I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})\to K^{(s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F})\;.

Note that

(5.5.3)

\displaystyle E_{m}R_{s}+\iota_{s^{\prime}}F_{m}=j_{s,s^{\prime}}:I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})\to I^{(s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F})\;,

(5.5.4)

\displaystyle F_{m}\iota_{s}=j_{s,s^{\prime}}:K^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})\to K^{(s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F})\;.

Moreover, by
5.5.2
,

(5.5.5)

F_{m}d_{\mathcal{F}}=d_{\mathcal{F}}F_{m}\;.

Take smaller numbers,
s_{1}<s
,
m_{1}<m
and
s^{\prime}_{1}<s^{\prime}
, satisfying the same inequalities as
s
,
m
and
s^{\prime}
. Then, with (
5.5.2
), the version of
2.5.5
with
\Lambda\mathcal{F}
(see
2.5.8
) gives

(5.5.6)

j_{s^{\prime},s^{\prime}_{1}}E_{m}=E_{m_{1}}j_{s,s_{1}}\;.

Then, using the definition of
F_{m}
, we also get

(5.5.7)

j_{s^{\prime},s^{\prime}_{1}}F_{m}=F_{m_{1}}j_{s,s_{1}}\;.

Remark 5.5.3
.

According to
6.1.1
, we can also define

F_{m}:I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F};\Omega M)\to K^{(s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F};\Omega M)

satisfying similar properties, using
d_{\mathcal{F}}^{\text{\rm t}}
.

5.5.3.
The equality
\ker\bar{R}_{*}=\operatorname{im}\bar{\iota}_{*}

We already know that
\ker\bar{R}_{*}\supset\operatorname{im}\bar{\iota}_{*}
. To prove that
\ker\bar{R}_{*}\subset\operatorname{im}\bar{\iota}_{*}
, take any class
\overline{[\alpha]}\in\ker\bar{R}_{*}
in
\bar{H}^{\bullet}I(\mathcal{F})
. Hence there is some net
\varphi_{l}\in J(\mathcal{F})
such that
R\alpha=\lim_{l}d_{\mathcal{F}}\varphi_{l}
in
J(\mathcal{F})
. We can assume
\varphi_{l}\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(M^{1};\Lambda\mathcal{F})
by the density of
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(M^{1};\Lambda\mathcal{F})
in
J(\mathcal{F})
(
Section

2.6.7
).

Using that
J(\mathcal{F})
is compactly retractive (
Section

2.6.7
) and arguing like in
Section

5.2.1
, we get that
\{R\alpha,d_{\mathcal{F}}\beta_{l}\}_{l}
is contained in some step
J^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
, and
R\alpha=\lim_{l}d_{\mathcal{F}}\varphi_{l}
in
J^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
. Moreover, we can assume
d_{\mathcal{F}}\phi_{l}
is a sequence because
J^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
is a Fréchet space.

Consider the notation of
Section

5.5.2
. We have
R\alpha=\lim_{l}d_{\mathcal{F}}\varphi_{l}
in
J^{m}(\mathcal{F})
by the version of (
2.5.36
) for
J(\mathcal{F})
(
Section

2.6.7
). We have
\beta:=F_{m}\alpha\in ZJ^{(s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F})\subset ZJ(\mathcal{F})
by (
5.5.5
), obtaining a class
\overline{[\beta]}\in\bar{H}^{\bullet}J(\mathcal{F})
.

Since the sequence
\varphi_{l}
is in
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(M^{1};\Lambda\mathcal{F})
, it is also in
J^{m}(\mathcal{F})
and in
I^{(s^{\prime})}I(\mathcal{F})
, and we have
E_{m}\varphi_{l}=\varphi_{l}
by the version of
2.5.6
with
J^{m}(\mathcal{F})
. Hence, by
5.5.2
,

\beta=\alpha-E_{m}R\alpha=\alpha-\lim_{l}E_{m}d_{\mathcal{F}}\varphi_{l}=\alpha-\lim_{l}d_{\mathcal{F}}E_{m}\varphi_{l}=\alpha-\lim_{l}d_{\mathcal{F}}\varphi_{l}

in
I^{(s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F})
, and therefore also in
I(\mathcal{F})
. This shows that
\bar{\iota}_{*}(\overline{[\beta]})=\overline{[\alpha]}
in
\bar{H}^{\bullet}I(\mathcal{F})
.

Remark 5.5.4
.

Using cohomology instead of reduced cohomology and a single element
\varphi
instead of a net
\varphi_{l}
, the analogous argument gives
\ker R_{*}\subset\operatorname{im}\iota_{*}
, obtaining
\ker R_{*}=\operatorname{im}\iota_{*}
.

5.5.4.
Injectivity of
\bar{\iota}_{*}

Take any
\overline{[\alpha]}\in H^{\bullet}K(\mathcal{F})
with
\bar{\iota}_{*}(\overline{[\alpha]})=0
in
\bar{H}^{\bullet}I(\mathcal{F})
. Since
I(\mathcal{F})
is compactly retractive,
C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})
is dense in
I(\mathcal{F})
and every
I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
is a Fréchet space (
Section

2.2.2
), we get as above that there is some
s
and a sequence
\varphi_{l}
in
C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})
such that
\alpha\in K^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
and
\alpha=\lim_{l}d_{\mathcal{F}}\varphi_{l}
in
I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
.

Consider again the notation of
Section

5.5.2
. By (
5.5.4
),

\alpha=F_{m}\alpha=\lim_{l}F_{m}d_{\mathcal{F}}\varphi_{l}=\lim_{l}d_{\mathcal{F}}F_{m}\varphi_{l}

in
K^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
, and therefore in
K(\mathcal{F})
. So
\alpha\in\bar{B}K(\mathcal{F})=BK(\mathcal{F})
by (
5.1.3
), and therefore
[\alpha]=0
in
H^{\bullet}K(\mathcal{F})
.

Remark 5.5.5
.

Like in
5.5.6
, we also get the injectivity of
\iota_{*}
.

5.5.5.
Surjectivity of
\bar{R}_{*}

For any class
\overline{[\alpha]}\in\bar{H}^{\bullet}J(\mathcal{F})
, the representative
\alpha
is in some step
J^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
, and therefore it is also in
ZJ^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
. With the notation of
Section

5.5.2
,
\beta:=E_{m}\alpha\in ZI^{(s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F})\subset ZI(\mathcal{F})
by
5.5.2
, and we have
R\beta=\alpha
. This shows that
\overline{[\alpha]}=\bar{R}(\overline{[\beta]})
.

Remark 5.5.6
.

Using cohomology instead of reduced cohomology, the analogous argument gives the surjectivity of
R_{*}
.

5.6.
Computations in the case of a suspension foliation

Consider the notation of
Sections

4.2.1
to
4.2.5
, where the case of a suspension foliation with a simple foliated flow was considered.

5.6.1.
Description of
(K(\mathcal{F}),d_{\mathcal{F}})

For every
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
s<-1/2
, consider the injection defined by (
2.6.38
) for the vector bundle
\Lambda\mathcal{F}
,

(5.6.1)

C^{\infty}(L;\Lambda\otimes\Omega^{-1}NL)\to K^{(s-m)}(M,L;\Lambda\mathcal{F})\;,\quad\alpha\mapsto\partial_{\rho}^{m}\delta_{L}^{\alpha}\;.

Proposition 5.6.1
.

Via (
5.6.1
), the operator
d_{\mathcal{F}}
on
K^{(s-m)}(M,L;\Lambda\mathcal{F})
corresponds to the operator
d_{L}-m{\eta\wedge}
on
C^{\infty}(L;\Lambda\otimes\Omega^{-1}NL)
.

Proof.

Consider first the case
m=0
. According to (
3.2.20
), for some degree
v
, take
\alpha\in C^{\infty}(L;\Lambda^{v}\otimes\Omega^{-1}NL)
and
\beta\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(M;\Lambda^{1,n-1-v})
. We can write
\alpha=\alpha_{0}\otimes|\omega|^{-1}
and
\beta=\beta_{0}\wedge\omega
for some
\alpha_{0}\in C^{\infty}(L;\Lambda^{v})
and
\beta_{0}\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(M;\Lambda^{0,n-1-v})
. By (
2.9.5
), (
2.8.4
), (
2.9.8
) (or (
2.9.2
) and the Stokes’ theorem), (
3.2.20
) and (
3.2.21
), and since
d\omega=\eta\wedge\omega
,

\displaystyle\langle d_{\mathcal{F}}\delta_{L}^{\alpha},\beta\rangle

\displaystyle=-(-1)^{v}\langle\delta_{L}^{\alpha},d\beta\rangle=-(-1)^{v}\langle\delta_{L}^{\alpha},(d\beta_{0}+(-1)^{n-1-v}\beta_{0}\wedge\eta)\wedge\omega\rangle

\displaystyle=-(-1)^{v}\int_{L}\alpha_{0}\wedge((d+\eta\wedge)\beta_{0})|_{L}=-(-1)^{v}\int_{L}\alpha_{0}\wedge(d+\eta\wedge)(\beta_{0}|_{L})

\displaystyle=\int_{L}(d_{L}-\eta\wedge)\alpha_{0}\wedge\beta_{0}|_{L}=\int_{L}d_{L}\alpha\wedge\beta|_{L}=\langle\delta_{L}^{d_{L}\alpha},\beta\rangle\;.

The general case follows from the previous case because
d_{\mathcal{F}}\partial_{\rho}=\partial_{\rho}(d_{\mathcal{F}}-{\eta\wedge})
on
C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})
by (
4.2.8
) and (
4.2.9
), and
\eta\wedge\delta_{L}^{\alpha}=\delta_{L}^{\eta\wedge\alpha}
by (
2.8.2
).
∎

Corollary 5.6.2
.

5.3.1
is true in this case.

Proof.

Apply (
2.6.39
), (
2.6.40
) and
5.6.1
.
∎

5.6.2.
A partial extension map on
M_{\pm}

The notation of
Sections

2.5.8
,
2.5.9
,
2.5.10
,
2.5.11
,
2.5.12
and
2.5.13
, concerning conormal distributions at the boundary, is also used here. By
2.5.1
, there is a continuous linear partial extension map,

E_{m,\pm}:\mathcal{A}^{m}(M_{\pm};\Lambda\mathcal{F}_{\pm})\to\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(s)}(M_{\pm};\Lambda\mathcal{F}_{\pm})\;,

where
s=0
if
m\geq 0
, and
m>s\in\mathbb{Z}^{-}
if
m<0
.
According to the proof of
2.5.1
and
2.5.2
and
2.5.3
, in the case
0>m>s\in\mathbb{Z}^{-}
, the homomorphism
E_{m,\pm}
can be given by the composition

\mathcal{A}^{m}(M_{\pm};\Lambda\mathcal{F}_{\pm})\xrightarrow{J^{N}}\mathcal{A}^{0}(M_{\pm};\Lambda\mathcal{F}_{\pm})\xrightarrow{E_{0,\pm}}\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(0)}(M_{\pm};\Lambda\mathcal{F}_{\pm})\xrightarrow{\partial_{\rho}^{N}}\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(s)}(M_{\pm};\Lambda\mathcal{F}_{\pm})\;,

where
N=-s\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
,
E_{0,\pm}
is a continuous inclusion map, and
J
is the endomorphism of
C^{\infty}(M_{\pm};\Lambda\mathcal{F}_{\pm})
given by

J\alpha(\rho,y)=\int_{1}^{\rho}\alpha(\rho_{1},y)\,d\rho_{1}\;,

using (
2.5.32
), (
4.2.13
) and the identity
\Lambda_{(\rho,y)}\mathcal{F}\equiv\Lambda_{y}L
.

Consider also the endomorphism
\widetilde{J}
of
C^{\infty}(\widetilde{M}_{\pm};\Lambda\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm})
defined like
J
,

\widetilde{J}\tilde{\alpha}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{y})=\int_{1}^{\tilde{\rho}}\tilde{\alpha}(\tilde{\rho}_{1},y)\,d\tilde{\rho}_{1}\;,

using (
4.2.12
) and the identity
\Lambda_{(\rho,\tilde{y})}\mathcal{F}_{\pm}\equiv\Lambda_{\tilde{y}}\widetilde{L}
. Clearly,
\widetilde{J}
corresponds to
J
via

\pi_{M_{\pm}}^{*}:C^{\infty}(M_{\pm};\Lambda\mathcal{F}_{\pm})\to C^{\infty}(\widetilde{M}_{\pm};\Lambda\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm})\;.

Using (
4.2.11
), we can also write

(5.6.2)

\widetilde{J}\tilde{\alpha}(\tau,\tilde{y})=e^{F(\tilde{y})}\int_{e^{-F(\tilde{y})}}^{\tau}\tilde{\alpha}(\tau_{1},\tilde{y})\,d\tau_{1}\;,

with the change of variable
\tilde{\rho}_{1}=e^{F(\tilde{y})}\tau_{1}
, because
\tilde{\rho}=\pm e^{F(\tilde{y})}\tau
.

For fixed
0<\epsilon<1
, consider the collar neighborhoods
T_{\pm}:=T_{\pm,\epsilon}
and
