\widetilde{T}_{\pm}=\widetilde{T}_{\pm,\epsilon}=\pi_{M_{\pm}}^{-1}(T_{\pm})
of the boundaries in
M_{\pm}
and
\widetilde{M}_{\pm}
. Using (
3.2.3
), consider
\eta\in C^{\infty}(M_{\pm};\Lambda^{1}\mathcal{F}_{\pm})
and
\tilde{\eta}\in C^{\infty}(\widetilde{M}_{\pm};\Lambda^{1}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm})
.

Proposition 5.6.3
.

Jd_{\mathcal{F}_{\pm}}=(d_{\mathcal{F}_{\pm}}-\eta\wedge)J
on
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(T_{\pm};\Lambda\mathcal{F}_{\pm})
.

Proof.

Take open subsets
\widetilde{B}\subset\widetilde{L}
such that
\pi_{L}:\widetilde{B}\to B:=\pi_{L}(\widetilde{B})
is a diffeomorphism. Since the open sets of the form
\varpi_{\pm}^{-1}(B)\equiv[0,\infty)_{\rho}\times\widetilde{B}_{\widetilde{\varpi}}
cover
M_{\pm}
and
J
preserves the spaces
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(T_{\pm}\cap\varpi_{\pm}^{-1}(B);\Lambda\mathcal{F}_{\pm})
, it is enough to prove the stated equality on
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(T_{\pm}\cap\varpi_{\pm}^{-1}(B);\Lambda\mathcal{F}_{\pm})
. In turn, this follows by checking that
\widetilde{J}d_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm}}=(d_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm}}-\tilde{\eta}\wedge)\widetilde{J}
on
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(\widetilde{T}_{\pm};\Lambda\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm})
because

\pi_{M_{\pm}}\equiv\operatorname{id}\times\pi_{L}:\widetilde{\varpi}_{\pm}^{-1}(\widetilde{B})\equiv[0,\infty)_{\tilde{\rho}}\times\widetilde{B}_{\widetilde{\varpi}}\to\varpi_{\pm}^{-1}(B)\equiv[0,\infty)_{\rho}\times B_{\varpi}

is a diffeomorphism. Let
\tilde{\alpha}\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(\widetilde{T}_{\pm};\Lambda\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm})
and
(\tau,\tilde{y})\in[0,\infty)\times\widetilde{L}
with
\tau<e^{-F(\tilde{y})}\epsilon
, which means that
(\tau,\tilde{y})
corresponds to an element of
T_{\pm}
via (
4.2.11
); in particular,
\tilde{\alpha}(e^{-F(\tilde{y})},\tilde{y})=0
. So, by (
5.6.2
) and since
d_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm}}\tau=0
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{J}d_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm}}\tilde{\alpha}(\tau,\tilde{y})

\displaystyle=e^{F(\tilde{y})}\int_{e^{-F(\tilde{y})}}^{\tau}d_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm}}\tilde{\alpha}(\tau_{1},\tilde{y})\,d\tau_{1}=e^{F(\tilde{y})}d_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm}}\int_{e^{-F(\tilde{y})}}^{\tau}\tilde{\alpha}(\tau_{1},\tilde{y})\,d\tau_{1}

\displaystyle=d_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm}}\,e^{F(\tilde{y})}\int_{e^{-F(\tilde{y})}}^{\tau}\tilde{\alpha}(\tau_{1},\tilde{y})\,d\tau_{1}-e^{F(\tilde{y})}\tilde{\eta}(\tilde{y})\wedge\int_{e^{-F(\tilde{y})}}^{\tau}\tilde{\alpha}(\tau_{1},\tilde{y})\,d\tau_{1}

\displaystyle=(d_{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm}}-\tilde{\eta}\wedge)\widetilde{J}\tilde{\alpha}(x,\tilde{y})\;.\qed

Proposition 5.6.4
.

We have
\partial_{\rho}d_{\mathcal{F}_{\pm}}=(d_{\mathcal{F}_{\pm}}+\eta\wedge)\partial_{\rho}
on
C^{-\infty}(M_{\pm};\Lambda\mathcal{F}_{\pm})
.

Proof.

Apply (
3.2.3
), (
3.2.8
) and (
4.2.15
).
∎

Corollary 5.6.5
.

For all
m\in\mathbb{R}
,
E_{m,\pm}d_{\mathcal{F}_{\pm}}=d_{\mathcal{F}_{\pm}}E_{m,\pm}
on
\mathcal{A}^{m}_{\text{\rm c}}(T_{\pm};\Lambda\mathcal{F}_{\pm})
.

Proof.

It is enough to consider the case
m<0
. Then apply
5.6.3
and
5.6.4
, using the given definition of
E_{m,\pm}
and the density of
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(T_{\pm};\Lambda\mathcal{F}_{\pm})
in
\mathcal{A}^{m}_{\text{\rm c}}(T_{\pm};\Lambda\mathcal{F}_{\pm})
(see
Section

2.5.10
).
∎

5.6.3.
A partial extension map on
M

Let us apply the notation of
Section

2.6
to the suspension foliation (that notation is compatible with the notation of
Sections

3.1.12
,
4.1.2
and
4.2
). Recall that
\boldsymbol{M}=M_{-}\sqcup M_{+}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}
is the combination of
\mathcal{F}_{\pm}
, and
\boldsymbol{\pi}:\boldsymbol{M}\to M
is the combination of
\pi_{\pm}:M_{\pm}\to M
. The version of the commutative diagram (
2.6.41
) for
\Lambda\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}\equiv\boldsymbol{\pi}^{*}\Lambda\mathcal{F}
is

(5.6.3)

\begin{CD}\mathcal{K}(\boldsymbol{M};\Lambda\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}})@>{\iota}>{}>\dot{\mathcal{A}}(\boldsymbol{M};\Lambda\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}})@>{R}>{}>\mathcal{A}(\boldsymbol{M};\Lambda\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}})\phantom{\;.}\\
@V{\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}}V{}V@V{\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}}V{}V@V{\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}}V{\cong}V\\
K(\mathcal{F})@>{\iota}>{}>I(\mathcal{F})@>{R}>{}>J(\mathcal{F})\;.\end{CD}

Moreover
d_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}}\in\operatorname{Diff}_{\text{\rm b}}(\boldsymbol{M};\Lambda\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}})
is the lift of
d_{\mathcal{F}}
. Hence the operators defined by
d_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}}
on the spaces of the top row of (
5.6.3
) correspond to the operators defined by
d_{\mathcal{F}}
on the spaces of its bottom row via the homomorphisms
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}
(
Section

2.6
). According to
Section

2.5.14
,
d_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}}
preserves the subspaces
\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(s)}(\boldsymbol{M};\Lambda\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}})
and
\mathcal{A}^{m}(\boldsymbol{M};\Lambda\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}})
.

The partial extension maps of
Section

5.6.2
,

E_{m,\pm}:\mathcal{A}^{m}(M_{\pm};\Lambda\mathcal{F}_{\pm})\to\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(s)}(M_{\pm};\Lambda\mathcal{F}_{\pm})\;,

can be combined to define a continuous linear partial extension map

\boldsymbol{E}_{m}:\mathcal{A}^{m}(\boldsymbol{M};\Lambda\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}})\to\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(s)}(\boldsymbol{M};\Lambda\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}})\;.

Then, according to
2.6.4
and its proof, a continuous linear partial extension map
E_{m}:J^{m}(\mathcal{F})\to I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
is given by the composition

J^{m}(\mathcal{F})\xrightarrow{\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}^{-1}}\mathcal{A}^{m}(\boldsymbol{M};\Lambda\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}})\xrightarrow{\boldsymbol{E}_{m}}\dot{\mathcal{A}}^{(s)}(\boldsymbol{M};\Lambda\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}})\xrightarrow{\boldsymbol{\pi}_{*}}I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})\;,

which is a continuous inclusion map if
m\geq 0
. Recall that
T\equiv(-\epsilon,\epsilon)_{\rho}\times L_{\varpi}
and

\boldsymbol{T}=T_{-}\sqcup T_{+}=\boldsymbol{\pi}^{-1}(T)\equiv[0,\epsilon)_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\times\partial\boldsymbol{M}_{\boldsymbol{\varpi}}\;.

Like in
Section

5.5.4
, consider the restriction
E_{m,T}:J^{m}_{\text{\rm c}}(T;\Lambda\mathcal{F})\to I^{(s)}_{\text{\rm c}}(T;\Lambda\mathcal{F})
of
E_{m}
. Suppose
\epsilon<1
, like in
Section

5.6.2
.

Corollary 5.6.6
.

For all
m\in\mathbb{R}
,
E_{m,T}
satisfies
E_{m,T}d_{\mathcal{F}}=d_{\mathcal{F}}E_{m,T}
.

Proof.

Apply
5.6.5
.
∎

5.7.
Functoriality and leafwise homotopy invariance

5.7.1.
Pull-back of conormal leafwise currents

Let
M^{\prime}
be another closed manifold, and let
\phi:M^{\prime}\to M
be a smooth map transverse to
\mathcal{F}
. Then
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}:=\phi^{*}\mathcal{F}
is another transversely oriented foliation of codimension one satisfying the conditions
3
and
4
in
Section

4.1.2
with
M^{\prime\,0}:=\phi^{-1}(M^{0})
.

Remark 5.7.1
.

The results of
Section

5.7
have direct extensions to the case where
M
or
M^{\prime}
may not be compact, with the condition that
M^{0}
and
M^{\prime\,0}
are compact.

According to
Section

2.2.8
, the map (
3.2.23
) has a continuous extension

(5.7.1)

\phi^{*}:I(\mathcal{F})\to I(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\;.

defined as the composition

(5.7.2)

I(\mathcal{F})\xrightarrow{\phi^{*}}I(M^{\prime},M^{\prime\,0};\phi^{*}\Lambda\mathcal{F})\xrightarrow{\phi^{*}}I(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\;,

like (
2.8.19
), using (
2.2.20
) with
E=\Lambda\mathcal{F}
. We can also describe (
5.7.1
) as the restriction of (
2.8.18
) to conormal currents of bidegree
(0,\bullet)
, like in (
3.2.34
). The map (
5.7.1
) is also a restriction of (
3.2.34
).

Similarly, the analogs of (
2.2.20
) with
E=\Lambda\mathcal{F}
for (
2.6.42
) and (
2.6.43
) induce continuous homomorphisms

(5.7.3)

\displaystyle\phi^{*}:K(\mathcal{F})\to K(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\;,

(5.7.4)

\displaystyle\phi^{*}:J(\mathcal{F})\to J(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\;.

By passing to cohomology and reduced cohomology, we get continuous homomorphisms,

(5.7.5)

\left\{\begin{gathered}\phi^{*}:H^{\bullet}K(\mathcal{F})\to H^{\bullet}K(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\;,\\
\begin{aligned} \phi^{*}:&H^{\bullet}I(\mathcal{F})\to H^{\bullet}I(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\;,&\quad\phi^{*}:&\bar{H}^{\bullet}I(\mathcal{F})\to\bar{H}^{\bullet}I(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\;,\\
\phi^{*}:&H^{\bullet}J(\mathcal{F})\to H^{\bullet}J(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\;,&\quad\phi^{*}:&\bar{H}^{\bullet}J(\mathcal{F})\to\bar{H}^{\bullet}J(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\;.\end{aligned}\end{gathered}\right.

The assignment of the homomorphisms (
5.7.1
)–(
5.7.5
) is functorial.

5.7.2.
Description of
\phi^{*}:K(\mathcal{F})\to K(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})

For
\omega^{\prime}=\phi^{*}\omega
and
\eta^{\prime}=\phi^{*}\eta
, we have
T\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\ker\omega^{\prime}
and
d\omega^{\prime}=\eta^{\prime}\wedge\omega^{\prime}
(the Frobenius integrability condition for
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
). Thus

(5.7.6)

\phi^{*}:C^{\infty}(M^{0};\Lambda)\to C^{\infty}(M^{\prime\,0};\Lambda)

is a cochain map for
d_{s\eta}
and
d_{s\eta^{\prime}}
(
s\in\mathbb{R}
) (
Section

2.9.3
). In other words,
\phi
induces

(5.7.7)

\phi^{*}:C^{\infty}(M^{0};\Lambda\otimes\Omega^{s}NM^{0})\to C^{\infty}(M^{\prime\,0};\Lambda\otimes\Omega^{s}NM^{\prime\,0})\;,

given by

(5.7.8)

\phi^{*}(\alpha\otimes|\omega|^{s})=\phi^{*}\alpha\otimes|\omega^{\prime}|^{s}\;,

which is another cochain map for the de Rham differentials defined with the flat bundle structures of
\Omega^{s}NM^{0}
and
\Omega^{s}NM^{\prime\,0}
.

If
\rho
is a defining function of
M^{0}
in some open neighborhood
T
, then
\rho^{\prime}:=\phi^{*}\rho
is a defining function of
M^{\prime\,0}
in
T^{\prime}=\phi^{-1}(T)
, and (
5.7.7
) satisfies

(5.7.9)

\phi^{*}(\alpha\otimes|d\rho|^{s})=\phi^{*}\alpha\otimes|d\rho^{\prime}|^{s}\;.

Note the compatibility of (
5.7.8
) and (
5.7.9
) with (
4.2.5
). Furthermore, the inverse image of
T:=T_{\epsilon}\equiv(-\epsilon,\epsilon)_{\rho}\times M^{0}_{\varpi}
, for
\epsilon>0
small enough, is a tubular neighborhood
T^{\prime}\equiv(-\epsilon,\epsilon)_{\rho^{\prime}}\times M^{\prime\,0}_{\varpi^{\prime}}
of
M^{\prime\,0}
in
M^{\prime}
, where
\varpi^{\prime}:T^{\prime}\to M^{\prime\,0}
satisfies
\phi\varpi^{\prime}=\varpi\phi
as maps
T^{\prime}\to M^{0}
. Thus

\phi\equiv\operatorname{id}\times\phi:T^{\prime}\equiv(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\times M^{\prime\,0}\to T\equiv(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\times M^{0}\;,

which is proper because
M^{\prime\,0}
is compact. We can use these tubular neighborhoods to define the operators
\partial_{\rho}
and
\partial_{\rho^{\prime}}
on
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(T;\Lambda\mathcal{F})
and
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(T^{\prime};\Lambda\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
(
Section

2.6
), which are used in the identities of
5.3.1
for
K(\mathcal{F})
and
K(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
(
Section

5.6.1
). Clearly,

(5.7.10)

\partial_{\rho^{\prime}}\phi^{*}=\phi^{*}\partial_{\rho}\;,

as maps
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(T;\Lambda\mathcal{F})\to C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(T^{\prime};\Lambda\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
,

Proposition 5.7.2
.

According to
5.3.1
, the map (
5.7.3
) is given by

\phi^{*}\equiv\bigoplus_{k}\phi^{*}\equiv\bigoplus_{k}\phi^{*}\;,

where the terms of the first direct sum are given by (
5.7.6
), and the terms of the second direct sum are given by (
5.7.7
), taking
s=-k-1
.

Proof.

The second identity follows from the first one and (
5.7.8
). To prove the first identity, by (
5.7.10
), it is enough to consider the term with
k=0
.

For
\alpha\in C^{\infty}(M^{0};\Lambda)
, let
u=\alpha\otimes|d\rho|^{-1}\in C^{\infty}(M^{0};\Lambda\otimes\Omega^{-1}NM^{0})
. Using the first identity of
5.3.1
for
k=0
, we have
u\equiv\delta_{M^{0}}^{u}=\varpi^{*}\alpha\cdot\rho^{*}\delta_{0}
in
K(\mathcal{F})
, using Dirac sections (
Section

2.2.6
). Here,
\rho^{*}\delta_{0}\in K(T,M^{0})
is defined because
\rho:T\to(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
is transverse to
0
. Moreover
u^{\prime}:=\phi^{*}u=\phi^{*}\alpha\cdot|d\rho^{\prime}|^{-1}
by (
5.7.9
). As before,
u^{\prime}\equiv\delta_{M^{\prime\,0}}^{u^{\prime}}=\varpi^{\prime*}\phi^{*}\alpha\cdot\rho^{\prime*}\delta_{0}
in
K(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
. Take a sequence
f_{i}\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
converging to
\delta_{0}
in
C^{-\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
. Then

\displaystyle\phi^{*}\delta_{M^{0}}^{u}

\displaystyle=\phi^{*}(\varpi^{*}\alpha\cdot\rho^{*}\delta_{0})=\lim_{i}\phi^{*}(\varpi^{*}\alpha\cdot\rho^{*}f_{i})=\lim_{i}\phi^{*}\varpi^{*}\alpha\cdot\phi^{*}\rho^{*}f_{i}

\displaystyle=\lim_{i}\varpi^{\prime*}\phi^{*}\alpha\cdot\rho^{\prime*}f_{i}=\varpi^{\prime*}\phi^{*}\alpha\cdot\rho^{\prime*}\delta_{0}=\delta_{M^{\prime\,0}}^{u^{\prime}}\;.\qed

Remark 5.7.3
.

The equality
\partial_{\rho^{\prime}}\phi^{*}=\phi^{*}\partial_{\rho}
has a continuous extension as maps
C^{-\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(T;\Lambda\mathcal{F})\to C^{-\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(T^{\prime};\Lambda\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
, and the computations of the above proof also work also with
\alpha\in C^{-\infty}(M^{0};\Lambda)
. So we get similar expressions of
\phi^{*}:C^{-\infty}_{M^{0}}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})\to C^{-\infty}_{M^{\prime\,0}}(M^{\prime};\Lambda\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
according to
5.3.4
.

5.7.3.
Push-forward of conormal leafwise currents

With the notation and conditions of
Section

5.7.1
, suppose that moreover
\phi
is a submersion such that the vertical bundle
\mathcal{V}
is oriented (
Section

3.2.15
). Thus
\phi:M^{\prime\,0}\to M^{0}
is also a submersion whose vertical bundle is
\mathcal{V}|_{M^{\prime\,0}}\subset TM^{\prime\,0}
, also oriented. Then the case of (
3.2.33
) on smooth leafwise forms has a continuous extension

(5.7.11)

\phi_{*}:I_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\text{\rm cv}}}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\to I_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\cdot}}(\mathcal{F})\;.

This map can be described as the restriction of the map (
2.8.24
) to conormal currents of bidegree
(0,\bullet)
, like (
3.2.33
) in
Section

3.2.15
. We can also describe (
5.7.11
) as the composition

I_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\text{\rm cv}}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\Lambda\mathcal{F})\xrightarrow{\pi_{\text{\rm top}}}I_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\text{\rm cv}}}(M^{\prime},L^{\prime};\phi^{*}\Lambda\mathcal{F}\otimes\Omega_{\text{\rm fiber}})\xrightarrow{\phi_{*}}I_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\cdot}}(M,L;\Lambda\mathcal{F})\;,

like in (
3.2.35
), where
\phi_{*}
is given by (
2.2.23
) for
E=\Lambda\mathcal{F}
. The map (
5.7.11
) is also a restriction of the case of (
3.2.33
) for leafwise currents.

According to
Section

2.6.15
, the map (
5.7.11
) induces homomorphisms

(5.7.12)

\displaystyle\phi_{*}:K(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\to K(\mathcal{F})\;,

(5.7.13)

\displaystyle\phi_{*}:J_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\text{\rm cv}}}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\to J_{{\text{\rm c}}/{\cdot}}(\mathcal{F})\;.

Like in
Section

5.7.1
, we get induced continuous homomorphisms,

(5.7.14)

\left\{\begin{gathered}\phi_{*}:H^{\bullet}K(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\to H^{\bullet}K(\mathcal{F})\;,\\
\begin{aligned} \phi_{*}:&H^{\bullet}I_{\text{\rm c}}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\to H^{\bullet}I_{\text{\rm c}}(\mathcal{F})\;,&\quad\phi_{*}:&\bar{H}^{\bullet}I_{\text{\rm c}}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\to\bar{H}^{\bullet}I_{\text{\rm c}}(\mathcal{F})\;,\\
\phi_{*}:&H^{\bullet}J_{\text{\rm c}}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\to H^{\bullet}J_{\text{\rm c}}(\mathcal{F})\;,&\quad\phi_{*}:&\bar{H}^{\bullet}J_{\text{\rm c}}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\to\bar{H}^{\bullet}J_{\text{\rm c}}(\mathcal{F})\;.\end{aligned}\end{gathered}\right.

The assignments of homomorphisms (
5.7.11
)–(
5.7.14
) are clearly functorial.

5.7.4.
Description of
\phi_{*}:K(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\to K(\mathcal{F})

For
\phi
as above, consider the notation of
Section

5.7.2
. Then

(5.7.15)

\phi_{*}:C^{\infty}(M^{\prime\,0};\Lambda)\to C^{\infty}(M^{0};\Lambda)

is a cochain map for
d_{s\eta}
and
d_{s\eta^{\prime}}
(
s\in\mathbb{R}
) (
Section

2.9.3
). That is,
\phi
induces

(5.7.16)

\phi_{*}:C^{\infty}(M^{\prime\,0};\Lambda\otimes\Omega^{s}NM^{\prime\,0})\to C^{\infty}(M^{0};\Lambda\otimes\Omega^{s}NM^{0})\;,

given by

(5.7.17)

\phi_{*}(\alpha\otimes|\omega^{\prime}|^{s})=\phi_{*}\alpha\otimes|\omega|^{s}\;,

which is another cochain map for the de Rham differentials defined with the flat bundle structures of
\Omega^{s}NM^{0}
and
\Omega^{s}NM^{\prime\,0}
induced by the Bott flat
T\mathcal{F}
-partial connection (
Section

3.1.3
). Like in (
5.7.9
) and (
5.7.10
), we have

(5.7.18)

\displaystyle\phi_{*}(\alpha\otimes|d\rho^{\prime}|^{s})=\phi_{*}\alpha\otimes|d\rho|^{s}\;,

(5.7.19)

\displaystyle\partial_{\rho}\phi_{*}=\phi_{*}\partial_{\rho^{\prime}}\;,

where (
5.7.19
) holds as maps
C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(T^{\prime};\Lambda\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\to C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(T;\Lambda\mathcal{F})
.

Proposition 5.7.4
.

According to
5.3.1
, the map (
5.7.12
) is given by

\phi_{*}\equiv\bigoplus_{k}\phi_{*}\equiv\bigoplus_{k}\phi_{*}\;,

where the terms of the first direct sum are given by (
5.7.15
), and the terms of the second direct sum are given by (
5.7.16
), taking
s=-k-1
.

Proof.

The second identity follows from the first one and (
5.7.17
). To prove the first identity, by (
5.7.19
), it is enough to consider the term with
k=0
.

For
\beta\in C^{\infty}(M^{\prime\,0};\Lambda)
, let
v^{\prime}=\beta\otimes|d\rho^{\prime}|^{-1}\in C^{\infty}(M^{\prime\,0};\Lambda\otimes\Omega^{-1}NM^{\prime\,0})
. Like in the proof of
5.7.2
, we have
v^{\prime}\equiv\delta_{M^{\prime\,0}}^{v^{\prime}}=\varpi^{\prime*}\beta\cdot\rho^{\prime*}\delta_{0}
in
K(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
. Moreover
v:=\phi_{*}v^{\prime}=\phi_{*}\beta\cdot|d\rho|^{-1}
by (
5.7.18
), with
v\equiv\delta_{M^{0}}^{v}=\varpi^{\prime*}\phi_{*}\beta^{\prime}\cdot\rho^{*}\delta_{0}
in
K(\mathcal{F})
. Take a sequence
f_{i}\in C^{\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
converging to
\delta_{0}
in
C^{-\infty}_{\text{\rm c}}(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
. We get

\displaystyle\phi_{*}\delta_{M^{\prime\,0}}^{v^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\phi_{*}(\varpi^{\prime*}\beta\cdot\rho^{\prime*}\delta_{0})=\lim_{i}\phi_{*}(\varpi^{\prime*}\beta\cdot\rho^{\prime*}f_{i})=\lim_{i}\phi_{*}\varpi^{*}\beta\cdot\rho^{*}f_{i}

\displaystyle=\lim_{i}\varpi^{*}\phi_{*}\beta\cdot\rho^{*}f_{i}=\varpi^{*}\phi^{*}\beta\cdot\rho^{*}\delta_{0}=\delta_{M^{0}}^{v}\;.\qed

The analog of
5.7.3
for
\phi_{*}:K(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\to K(\mathcal{F})
is true.

5.7.5.
Leafwise homotopy invariance

With the notation of
Section

3.2.16
, let
H:(M^{\prime}\times I,\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\times I)\to(M,\mathcal{F})
(
I=[0,1]
) be a smooth leafwise homotopy such that
H_{0}
is transverse to
M^{0}
and
H_{0}^{-1}(M^{0})=M^{\prime\,0}
. Then, for every
p^{\prime}\in M^{\prime}
, the map
H_{t*}:N_{p^{\prime}}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\to N_{H_{t}(p^{\prime})}\mathcal{F}
is the composition of
H_{0*}:N_{p^{\prime}}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\to N_{H_{0}(p^{\prime})}\mathcal{F}
with the parallel transport along the leafwise path
s\in[0,t]\mapsto H_{s}(p^{\prime})
. It follows that every
H_{t}
is transverse to
M^{0}
and
H_{t}^{-1}(M^{0})=M^{\prime\,0}
. Hence
H
is transverse to
M^{0}
and
H^{-1}(M^{0})=M^{\prime\,0}\times I
. Then, by (
3.2.38
) and according to
Sections

5.7.1
and
5.7.3
, the corresponding leafwise homotopy operator
\mathsf{h}:C^{\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F})\to C^{\infty}(M^{\prime};\Lambda\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
has continuous linear extensions,

\mathsf{h}:K(\mathcal{F})\to K(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\;,\quad\mathsf{h}:I(\mathcal{F})\to I(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\;,\quad\mathsf{h}:J(\mathcal{F})\to J(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\;.

By continuity and according to
Section

3.2.16
, we have
H_{1}^{*}-H_{0}^{*}=\mathsf{h}d_{\mathcal{F}}+d_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}\mathsf{h}
with
H_{0}^{*}
and
H_{1}^{*}
given by (
5.7.1
), (
5.7.3
) and (
5.7.4
). Hence we get the following.

Proposition 5.7.5
.

Let
\phi,\psi:(M^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\to(M,\mathcal{F})
be smooth foliated maps transverse to
M^{0}
with
\phi^{-1}(M^{0})=\psi^{-1}(M^{0})=M^{\prime\,0}
. If
\phi
is leafwise homotopic to
\psi
, then
\phi
and
\psi
induce the same homomorphisms (
5.7.5
).

5.8.
Action of foliated flows on the conormal sequence

Let
\phi=\{\phi^{t}\}
be a foliated flow with transversely simple preserved leaves on a compact foliated manifold
(M,\mathcal{F})
. The homomorphisms (
5.7.5
) induced by the maps
\phi^{t}
define actions of
\mathbb{R}
on
H^{\bullet}K(\mathcal{F})
,
H^{\bullet}I(\mathcal{F})
and
H^{\bullet}J(\mathcal{F})
, denoted by
\phi^{*}=\{\phi^{t*}\}
, and actions on
\bar{H}^{\bullet}I(\mathcal{F})
and
\bar{H}^{\bullet}J(\mathcal{F})
, denoted by
\bar{\phi}^{*}=\{\bar{\phi}^{t*}\}
. By
5.7.5
, they only depend on the flow-leafwise-homotopy class of
\phi
(
Section

3.1.7
).

With the notation of
Section

4.3.3
, the foliated flow
\xi=\{\xi^{t}\}
has transversely simple preserved leaves and satisfies
\bar{\xi}=\bar{\phi}
and
\xi^{t}=\operatorname{id}
on
M^{0}
. By
3.1.4
, there is a flow-leafwise homotopy between
\phi
and
\xi
, and therefore
\phi^{*}=\xi^{*}
on
H^{\bullet}K(\mathcal{F})
. Consider the tubular neighborhood with defining function,
T_{\epsilon}\equiv(-\epsilon,\epsilon)_{\rho}\times M^{0}_{\varpi}
, like in
Section

5.7.2
.

Proposition 5.8.1
.

According to
5.3.2
and
5.3.3
,

\phi^{t*}\equiv\bigoplus_{k,L}e^{-(k+1)\varkappa_{L}t}\equiv\bigoplus_{k,L}e^{-(k+1)\varkappa_{L}t}

on
H^{\bullet}K(\mathcal{F})
, where
k
runs in
\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
L
in
\pi_{0}M^{0}
.

Proof.

Since
\xi^{t*}\rho=e^{\varkappa_{L}t}\rho
on every
T_{L,\epsilon}\cap\xi^{-t}(T_{L,\epsilon})
by (
4.2.7
), it follows from (
5.7.9
) and
5.7.2
that

\xi^{t*}\equiv\bigoplus_{k,L}e^{-(k+1)\varkappa_{L}t}\equiv\bigoplus_{k,L}e^{-(k+1)\varkappa_{L}t}

on
K(\mathcal{F})
, according to
5.3.1
and Remark
5.3.3
. Hence
\phi^{t*}=\xi^{t*}
has the stated expression on
H^{\bullet}K(\mathcal{F})
.
∎

5.3.1
,
5.3.2
,
5.3.3
and
5.8.1
show
1.3.1
.

Chapter 6
Dual-conormal leafwise reduced cohomology

6.1.
Dual-conormal sequence of leafwise differential forms

Assume the conditions of
Section

5.1
on
(M,\mathcal{F})
. According to
Section

2.3.4
, the LCHS

I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})=I^{\prime}\Lambda^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F}):=I^{\prime}(M,M^{0};\Lambda\mathcal{F})

is a topological complex with
d_{\mathcal{F}}
. It induces the
dual-conormal leafwise cohomology
and
dual-conormal leafwise reduced cohomology
of
\mathcal{F}
(or of
(\mathcal{F},M^{0})
). The notation
BI^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
,
ZI^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
,
\bar{B}I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
,
H^{\bullet}I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
and
\bar{H}^{\bullet}I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
is used like in
Section

5.1
.

For a leafwise flat vector bundle
E
, we can also consider the topological complex

I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F};E)=I^{\prime}\Lambda^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F};E)=I^{\prime}(M,M^{0};\Lambda\mathcal{F}\otimes E)

with
d_{\mathcal{F}}
. The LCHS
I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F};E)
is also defined for an arbitrary vector bundle
E
, missing the leafwise differential map
d_{\mathcal{F}}
.

Moreover, the LCHSs

I^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})=I^{\prime\,(s)}\Lambda^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F})=I^{\prime\,(s)}(M,M^{0};\Lambda\mathcal{F})\quad(s\in\mathbb{R})\;.

also become topological complexes with
d_{\mathcal{F}}
. The notation
BI^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
,
ZI^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
,
\bar{B}I^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
,
H^{\bullet}I^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
and
\bar{H}^{\bullet}I^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
is used like in
Section

5.1
.

Remark 6.1.1
.

Although
\Omega M
has no leafwise flat structure in general, we can assume
\mathcal{F}
is oriented by working locally or passing to the double cover of orientations of
\mathcal{F}
. Then we can apply (
3.2.19
)–(
3.2.21
) and the leafwise flat structure of
\Omega N\mathcal{F}
to define
d_{\mathcal{F}}
and
d_{\mathcal{F}}^{\text{\rm t}}
on every
I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F};\Omega M)\equiv I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F};\Omega N\mathcal{F})
. Since the condition of being in
I^{(s)}(\mathcal{F};\Omega M)
is local for elements of
C^{-\infty}(M;\Lambda\mathcal{F}\otimes\Omega)
, this procedure gives the definition of
d_{\mathcal{F}}=d_{\mathcal{F}}^{\text{\rm tt}}
.

For
s^{\prime}\leq s
in
\mathbb{R}
, we have the continuous linear restriction maps (
Section

2.3.1
)

(6.1.1)

j^{\prime}_{s}:I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\to I^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})\;,\quad j^{\prime}_{s,s^{\prime}}:I^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})\to I^{\prime\,(s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F})\;,

where
j^{\prime}_{s}=j_{-s}^{\text{\rm t}}
and
j^{\prime}_{s,s^{\prime}}=j_{-s^{\prime},-s}^{\text{\rm t}}
for the version of (
5.1.1
) with
\Omega M
. The induced homomorphisms in cohomology and reduced cohomology are denoted by
j^{\prime}_{s*}
,
j^{\prime}_{s,s^{\prime}*}
,
\bar{\jmath}^{\prime}_{s*}
and
\bar{\jmath}^{\prime}_{s,s^{\prime}*}
. The homomorphisms
j^{\prime}_{s,s^{\prime}*}
and
\bar{\jmath}^{\prime}_{s,s^{\prime}*}
form projective spectra, giving rise to projective limits as
s\uparrow+\infty
. Like in (
5.1.2
), the maps
j^{\prime}_{s*}
and
\bar{\jmath}^{\prime}_{s*}
induce canonical continuous linear maps,

(6.1.2)

\left\{\begin{gathered}\tilde{\jmath}^{\prime}_{*}:=\varprojlim j^{\prime}_{s*}:H^{\bullet}I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\to\widetilde{H}^{\bullet}I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F}):=\varprojlim H^{\bullet}I^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})\;,\\
\hat{\jmath}^{\prime}_{*}:=\varprojlim\bar{\jmath}^{\prime}_{s*}:\bar{H}^{\bullet}I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\xrightarrow{\cong}\widehat{H}^{\bullet}I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F}):=\varprojlim\bar{H}^{\bullet}I^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})\;,\end{gathered}\right.

where the second one is a linear isomorphism (
Section

6.2
). The canonical maps of the inductive limits to the steps are denoted by

\tilde{\jmath}_{s*}:\widetilde{H}^{\bullet}I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\to H^{\bullet}I^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})\;,\quad\hat{\jmath}_{s*}:\widehat{H}^{\bullet}I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\to\bar{H}^{\bullet}I^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})\;.

Using the above type of notation, the LCHSs
J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
and
K^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
are also topological complexes with
d_{\mathcal{F}}
(
Section

2.7.5
), with corresponding spaces
BJ^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
,
ZJ^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
,
\bar{B}J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
,
H^{\bullet}J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
and
\bar{H}^{\bullet}J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
, and the same for
K^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
.

Similarly, we have topological complexes
J^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
,
J^{\prime\,m}(\mathcal{F})
and
K^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
(
s,m\in\mathbb{R}
) (
Sections

2.7.4
and
2.6.13
), with corresponding spaces
BJ^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
,
ZJ^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
,
\bar{B}J^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
,
H^{\bullet}J^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
and
\bar{H}^{\bullet}J^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
, and the same for
J^{\prime\,m}(\mathcal{F})
and
K^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
. There are obvious versions for
J^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
and
K^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
of the maps (
6.1.1
) (
Section

2.7.1
), also denoted by
j^{\prime}_{s}
and
j^{\prime}_{s,s^{\prime}}
, giving rise to projective spectra in cohomology and reduced cohomology, and the corresponding projective limits. In the case of
J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
, the maps
j^{\prime}_{s,s^{\prime}}
and
j^{\prime}_{s}
are continuous inclusions (
Section

2.7.1
).

There are also continuous inclusion maps (
Section

2.7.1
)

(6.1.3)

\left\{\begin{gathered}j^{\prime}_{m}:J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\hookrightarrow J^{\prime\,m}(\mathcal{F})\;,\quad j^{\prime}_{m,m^{\prime}}:J^{\prime\,m}(\mathcal{F})\hookrightarrow J^{\prime\,m^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F})\quad(m^{\prime}\leq m)\;,\\
j^{\prime}_{m,s}:J^{m}(\mathcal{F})\hookrightarrow J^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})\quad(m>s+n/2+1)\;,\\
j^{\prime}_{s,m}:J^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})\hookrightarrow J^{m}(\mathcal{F})\quad(s\geq m,0)\;,\end{gathered}\right.

denoted like in (
5.1.1
) with some abuse of notation. The homomorphisms induced by the maps
j^{\prime}_{m,m^{\prime}}
in cohomology and reduced cohomology form projective spectra whose inductive limits as
m\uparrow+\infty
agree with the previous ones for
J(\mathcal{F})
, and the maps
j^{\prime}_{m}
induce a continuous linear isomorphism analogous to (
6.1.2
).

It will be shown (Corollary
6.3.2
) that the canonical projections are TVS-identities,

(6.1.4)

\left\{\begin{gathered}H^{\bullet}K^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\equiv\bar{H}^{\bullet}K^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\;,\quad H^{\bullet}K^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})\equiv\bar{H}^{\bullet}K^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})\;,\\
H^{\bullet}K^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\equiv\varprojlim H^{\bullet}K^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})\;,\quad\bar{H}^{\bullet}K^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\equiv\varprojlim\bar{H}^{\bullet}K^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})\;.\end{gathered}\right.

The version of the bottom row of (
2.7.8
) with
\Lambda\mathcal{F}
is a short exact sequence of continuous homomorphisms of topological complexes,

(6.1.5)

0\leftarrow K^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\xleftarrow{R^{\prime}}I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\xleftarrow{\iota^{\prime}}J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\leftarrow 0\;,

using the notation
R^{\prime}=\iota^{\text{\rm t}}
and
\iota^{\prime}=R^{\text{\rm t}}
. The exactness of the induced sequences,

(6.1.6)

\displaystyle 0\leftarrow H^{\bullet}K^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\xleftarrow{R^{\prime}_{*}}H^{\bullet}I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\xleftarrow{\iota^{\prime}_{*}}H^{\bullet}J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\leftarrow 0\;,

(6.1.7)

\displaystyle 0\leftarrow H^{\bullet}K^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\xleftarrow{\bar{R}^{\prime}_{*}}\bar{H}^{\bullet}I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\xleftarrow{\bar{\iota}^{\prime}_{*}}\bar{H}^{\bullet}J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\leftarrow 0\;,

will be proved in
Section

6.5
; in particular, this shows
1.3.6
.

Taking the transpose of the analog of (
5.1.8
) with
\Omega M
, we get continuous linear maps

(6.1.8)

R^{\prime}_{s}:K^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})\to I^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})\;,\quad\iota^{\prime}_{s}:I^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})\to J^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})\;.

Like in
Section

5.1
, the subscript “
s
” may be also added to the elements of the cohomologies or reduced cohomologies of
K^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
,
I^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
or
J^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
.

6.2.
Projective limits in reduced cohomology

The goal of this section is to prove the linear isomorphism (
6.1.2
), and its version for
J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
. The case of
K^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
is given by (
6.1.4
).

To simplify the notation, we write

\widetilde{H}^{\bullet}I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})=\varprojlim H^{\bullet}I^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})\;,\quad\widehat{H}^{\bullet}I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})=\varprojlim\bar{H}^{\bullet}I^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})\;,

and the canonical maps of the projective limits to the steps are denoted by

\tilde{\jmath}^{\prime}_{s}:\widetilde{H}^{\bullet}I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\to H^{\bullet}I^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})\;,\quad\hat{\jmath}^{\prime}_{s}:\widehat{H}^{\bullet}I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\to\bar{H}^{\bullet}I^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})\;.

The same type of notation is used in the cases of
J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
and
K^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
.

Lemma 6.2.1
.

BI^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
is dense in every
BI^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
is dense.

Proof.

Use that the image of
J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
is dense in
J^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
(
Section

2.3.1
) and
d_{\mathcal{F}}
is continuous on
J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
and
J^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
(
Section

2.7.5
).
∎

Recall that
\bar{B}J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
(resp.,
\bar{B}J^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
) denotes the closure of
BJ^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
(resp.,
BJ^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
) in
J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
(resp.,
J^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
).

Corollary 6.2.2
.

As vector spaces,

\bar{B}J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})=\bigcap_{s}\bar{B}J^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})\;.

Proof.

By the definition of the projective topology of
\bigcap_{s}J^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})

[
Sch71
, Section II.5]
and using
6.2.1
, we get that
BJ^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
is dense in
\bigcap_{s}\bar{B}J^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
. Moreover, this intersection is closed in
J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
. Then the stated equality is true.
∎

Lemma 6.2.3
.

As vector spaces,

ZJ^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})=\bigcap_{s}ZJ^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})\;.

Proof.

Consider the commutative diagram

\begin{CD}0@>{}>{}>\bigcap_{s}ZJ^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})@>{}>{}>\bigcap_{s}J^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})@>{d_{\mathcal{F}}}>{}>\bigcap_{s}BJ^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})\\
&&@A{}A{}A\Big\|@A{}A{}A&\\
0@>{}>{}>ZJ^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})@>{}>{}>J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})@>{d_{\mathcal{F}}}>{}>BJ^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})@>{}>{}>0\;.\end{CD}

Here, the central vertical equality is the analog (
2.5.54
), the arrows that are not given by
d_{\mathcal{F}}
and do not go to
0
denote inclusion maps, and the bottom row is exact. Since the surjective maps
d_{\mathcal{F}}:J^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})\to BJ^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
form a homomorphism between projective spectra whose kernel is the projective spectrum consisting of the spaces
ZJ^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
, the top row is also exact
[
Wen03
, Proposition 3.1.8]
. Thus the left-hand-side vertical arrow is an equality of vector spaces.
∎

Proposition 6.2.4
.

The canonical map
\bar{H}^{\bullet}J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\to\widehat{H}^{\bullet}J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
is a linear isomorphism.

Proof.

Consider the commutative diagram

\begin{CD}0@>{}>{}>\bigcap_{s}\bar{B}J^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})@>{}>{}>\bigcap_{s}ZJ^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})@>{}>{}>\widehat{H}^{\bullet}J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})@>{}>{}>0\phantom{\;.}\\
&&\Big\|\Big\|@A{}A{}A&\\
0@>{}>{}>\bar{B}J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})@>{}>{}>ZJ^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})@>{}>{}>\bar{H}^{\bullet}J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})@>{}>{}>0\;.\end{CD}

Here,
6.2.2
and
6.2.3
give the vertical equalities of vector spaces, the vertical arrow is canonical, and the other maps are canonical; in particular, the bottom row is exact.
6.2.1
also shows that every
BJ^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
is dense in
BJ^{(s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F})
for
s^{\prime}<s
. Hence the right derived functor
\varprojlim^{1}
satisfies
\varprojlim^{1}\bar{B}J^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})=0
as
s\uparrow+\infty

[
Wen03
, Theorem 3.2.1]
, obtaining that the top row is also exact by
[
Wen03
, Corollary 3.1.5]
. Then the result follows.
∎

On the other hand, the kind of arguments that will be given in
Section

6.5
can be adapted to show the exactness of the sequence

(6.2.1)

0\leftarrow H^{\bullet}K^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\xleftarrow{\widehat{R}^{\prime}_{*}}\widehat{H}^{\bullet}I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\xleftarrow{\hat{\iota}^{\prime}_{*}}\widehat{H}^{\bullet}J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\leftarrow 0\;,

where
\widehat{R}^{\prime}_{*}=\varprojlim\bar{R}^{\prime}_{s*}
and
\hat{\iota}^{\prime}_{*}=\varprojlim\bar{\iota}^{\prime}_{s*}
, using the homomorphisms induced by (
6.1.8
). This fits into a commutative diagram

\begin{CD}0@<{}<{}<H^{\bullet}K^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})@<{}<{}<\bar{H}^{\bullet}I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})@<{}<{}<\bar{H}^{\bullet}J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})@<{}<{}<0\phantom{\;,}\\
&&\Big\|@V{}V{}V@V{}V{\cong}V\\
0@<{}<{}<H^{\bullet}K^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})@<{}<{}<\widehat{H}^{\bullet}I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})@<{}<{}<\widehat{H}^{\bullet}J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})@<{}<{}<0\;,\end{CD}

where the top row is the exact sequence (
6.1.7
), and the vertical arrows are canonical. The last vertical arrow is a linear isomorphism by
6.2.4
. Then the central vertical arrow is also a linear isomorphism by the five lemma.

6.3.
Description of
H^{\bullet}K^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})

As explained in
Section

6.1
, there is no loss of generality in assuming
\mathcal{F}
is oriented, and then we can apply (
3.2.19
)–(
3.2.21
) to get
K^{(s)}(\mathcal{F};\Omega M)\equiv K^{(s)}(\mathcal{F};\Omega N\mathcal{F})
, where we can consider
d_{\mathcal{F}}
or
d_{\mathcal{F}}^{\text{\rm t}}
using the leafwise flat structure of
\Omega N\mathcal{F}
.

Consider the notation of
Section

5.3
. Since
d\omega=\eta\wedge\omega
and
d_{\mathcal{F}}
satisfies the derivation rule on products of smooth leafwise currents and smooth leafwise forms (
Section

3.2.8
), it follows that the version of
5.3.1
with coefficients in
\Omega N\mathcal{F}
states that

(6.3.1)

\left\{\begin{gathered}K(\mathcal{F};\Omega N\mathcal{F})\equiv\bigoplus_{k}C^{\infty}(M^{0};\Lambda)\equiv\bigoplus_{k}C^{\infty}(M^{0};\Lambda\otimes\Omega^{-k}NM^{0})\;,\\
d_{\mathcal{F}}\equiv\bigoplus_{k}d_{-k}\equiv\bigoplus_{k}d\;,\end{gathered}\right.

where
k
runs in
\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Moreover the subcomplex
K^{(s)}(\mathcal{F};\Omega N\mathcal{F})\subset K(\mathcal{F};\Omega N\mathcal{F})
corresponds to the finite direct sum with
k<-s-1/2
. Taking dual spaces and transposing maps, using (
2.8.4
) and (
2.9.8
), we get the following consequence.

Corollary 6.3.1
.

We have identities of topological complexes,

\displaystyle K^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\equiv\prod_{k}C^{-\infty}(M^{0};\Lambda)\equiv\prod_{k}C^{-\infty}(M^{0};\Lambda\otimes\Omega^{k}NM^{0})\;,

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{F}}\equiv\prod_{k}d_{k}\equiv\prod_{k}d\;,

where
k
runs in
\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Moreover the quotient complex
K^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
corresponds to the finite direct sum with
k<s-1/2
.

Corollary 6.3.2
.

We have TVS-identities,

H^{\bullet}K^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\equiv\prod_{k}H_{k}^{\bullet}(M^{0})\equiv\prod_{k}H^{\bullet}(M^{0},\Omega^{k}NM^{0})\;,

where
k
runs in
\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Moreover
H^{\bullet}K^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
is the quotient space of
H^{\bullet}K^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
given by the finite product with
k<s-1/2
. In particular, (
6.1.4
) is satisfied.

Remark 6.3.3
.

The differential complexes on
M^{0}
used in
6.3.1
split into direct sums of the same complexes given by leaves
L\subset M^{0}
. The same applies to their cohomologies, used in Corollary
6.3.2
.

Corollary 6.3.4
.

There is a canonical TVS-isomorphism,

H^{\bullet}K^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\equiv H^{\bullet}(M^{0})\oplus H^{n^{\prime\prime}-\bullet}K(\mathcal{F})^{\prime}\;,

Proof.

Apply
5.3.2
and
6.3.2
, and (
2.9.6
).
∎

6.4.
Description of
\bar{H}^{\bullet}J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})

Like in
Section

5.4
, by (
2.7.4
) and (
2.7.5
), for
m\in\mathbb{R}
,

(6.4.1)

\displaystyle J^{\prime\,m}(\mathcal{F})\cong\boldsymbol{\rho}^{m}H_{\text{\rm b}}^{-\infty}(\boldsymbol{M};\Lambda\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}})\equiv\boldsymbol{\rho}^{m-\frac{1}{2}}H^{-\infty}(\mathring{\boldsymbol{M}};\Lambda\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}})\;,

(6.4.2)

\displaystyle J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\cong\bigcap_{m}\boldsymbol{\rho}^{m}H_{\text{\rm b}}^{-\infty}(\boldsymbol{M};\Lambda\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}})=\bigcap_{m}\boldsymbol{\rho}^{m}H^{-\infty}(\mathring{\boldsymbol{M}};\Lambda\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}})\;,

as topological complexes with
d_{\mathcal{F}}
,
d_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}}
and
d_{\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}}}
, using the b-metric
\boldsymbol{g}
to define
H_{\text{\rm b}}^{-\infty}(\boldsymbol{M};\Lambda\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}})
, and using
\boldsymbol{g}|_{\mathring{\boldsymbol{M}}}
to define
H^{-\infty}(\mathring{\boldsymbol{M}};\Lambda\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}})
. The leafwise version of (
2.9.4
) (
Section

3.5
) also gives isomorphisms of topological complexes,

(6.4.3)

\boldsymbol{\rho}^{-m+\frac{1}{2}}:\big(\boldsymbol{\rho}^{m-\frac{1}{2}}H^{\infty}(\mathring{\boldsymbol{M}};\Lambda\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}}),d_{\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}}}\big)\xrightarrow{\cong}\big(H^{\infty}(\mathring{\boldsymbol{M}};\Lambda\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}}),d_{\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}},m-\frac{1}{2}}\big)\;.

By (
6.4.1
) and (
6.4.3
), and the analog of (
3.4.16
) for
\Delta_{\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}},m-\frac{1}{2}}
in
H^{-\infty}(\mathring{\boldsymbol{M}};\Lambda\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}})
(
Section

3.5
), we get induced TVS-isomorphisms

(6.4.4)

\displaystyle\bar{H}^{\bullet}J^{\prime\,m}(\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\cong\bar{H}^{\bullet}\big(\boldsymbol{\rho}^{m-\frac{1}{2}}H^{-\infty}(\mathring{\boldsymbol{M}};\Lambda\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}}),d_{\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}}}\big)

(6.4.5)

\displaystyle\cong\bar{H}^{\bullet}\big(H^{-\infty}(\mathring{\boldsymbol{M}};\Lambda\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}}),d_{\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}},m-\frac{1}{2}}\big)

(6.4.6)

\displaystyle\cong\ker\Delta_{\mathring{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{F}}},m-\frac{1}{2}}\;.

By the analog of (
6.1.2
) for
J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
and (
6.4.6
), the LCHS
\bar{H}^{\bullet}J(\mathcal{F})
is a projective limit of a sequence of Hilbertian spaces, and therefore a Fréchet space. The isomorphisms (
6.4.4
) and (
6.4.5
) are also true in cohomology.

1.3.5
follows from the analog of (
6.1.2
) for
J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
and (
6.4.1
)–(
6.4.3
).

6.5.
Short exact sequence of dual-conormal reduced cohomology

The goal of this section is to prove the exactness of (
6.1.7
). Some remarks will indicate how to adapt the proof to show also the exactness of (
6.1.6
) and (
6.2.1
).

6.5.1.
The maps
F^{\prime}_{m}

For every
m\in\mathbb{R}
, let

F^{\prime}_{m}=E_{-m}^{\text{\rm t}}:I^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})\to J^{\prime\,m}(\mathcal{F})\;,

where
s=0
if
m\leq 0
, and
m<s\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
if
m>0
, where

E_{-m}:J^{-m}(\mathcal{F};\Omega M)\to I^{(-s)}(\mathcal{F};\Omega M)

is given by the version of
5.5.2
with
\Omega M
(see
6.1.1
); thus

(6.5.1)

F^{\prime}_{m}d_{\mathcal{F}}=d_{\mathcal{F}}F^{\prime}_{m}\;.

Since
s\geq m,0
, the map
j^{\prime}_{s,m}
is defined, and we have

(6.5.2)

F^{\prime}_{m}\iota^{\prime}_{s}=E_{-m}^{\text{\rm t}}R_{-s}^{\text{\rm t}}=(R_{-s}E_{-m})^{\text{\rm t}}=j_{-m,-s}^{\text{\rm t}}=j^{\prime}_{s,m}\;.

6.5.2.
The maps
E^{\prime}_{m}

For
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
m>s+n/2+1
, let

E^{\prime}_{m}=F_{-m}^{\text{\rm t}}:K^{\prime\,(s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F})\to I^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})\;,

where
s^{\prime}=0
if
m\leq 0
, and
m<s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
if
m>0
. Here, we use the map

F_{-m}:I^{(-s)}(\mathcal{F};\Omega M)\to K^{(-s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F};\Omega M)

given be the version of
Section

5.5.2
with coefficients in
\Omega M
(
5.5.3
). Consider

(6.5.3)

F^{\prime}_{m}=j^{\prime}_{m,s}F^{\prime}_{m}:I^{\prime\,(s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F})\to J^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})\;,

which is the transpose of the version of (
5.5.2
) with coefficients in
\Omega M
,

E_{-m}:J^{(-s)}(\mathcal{F};\Omega M)\to I^{(-s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F};\Omega M)\;.

Then (
6.5.2
) becomes

(6.5.4)

F^{\prime}_{m}\iota^{\prime}_{s}=j^{\prime}_{s,s^{\prime}}\;.

Transposing the versions of (
5.5.3
)–(
5.5.5
) with coefficients in
\Omega M
, we get

(6.5.5)

\displaystyle\iota^{\prime}_{s}F^{\prime}_{m}+E^{\prime}_{m}R^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}=j^{\prime}_{s^{\prime},s}:I^{\prime\,(s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F})\to I^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})\;,

(6.5.6)

\displaystyle R^{\prime}_{s}E^{\prime}_{m}=j^{\prime}_{s^{\prime},s}:K^{\prime\,(s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F})\to K^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})\;,

(6.5.7)

\displaystyle E^{\prime}_{m}d_{\mathcal{F}}=d_{\mathcal{F}}E^{\prime}_{m}\;.

Take greater numbers,
s_{1}>s
,
m_{1}>m
and
s^{\prime}_{1}>s^{\prime}
, satisfying the same inequalities as
s
,
m
and
s^{\prime}
. Using (
6.5.3
), the transposition of the versions of (
5.5.6
) and (
5.5.7
) with coefficients in
\Omega M
give

(6.5.8)

\displaystyle F^{\prime}_{m}j^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}_{1},s^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=j^{\prime}_{s_{1},s}F^{\prime}_{m_{1}}\;,

(6.5.9)

\displaystyle E^{\prime}_{m}j^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}_{1},s^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=j^{\prime}_{s_{1},s}E^{\prime}_{m_{1}}\;.

6.5.3.
The equality
\ker\bar{R}^{\prime}_{*}=\operatorname{im}\bar{\iota}^{\prime}_{*}

We already know that
\ker\bar{R}^{\prime}_{*}\supset\operatorname{im}\bar{\iota}^{\prime}_{*}
. To prove
\ker\bar{R}^{\prime}_{*}\subset\operatorname{im}\bar{\iota}^{\prime}_{*}
, take any class
\overline{[u]}\in\ker\bar{R}^{\prime}_{*}
in
\bar{H}^{\bullet}I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
. Thus there is some net
v_{l}
in
K^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
such that
R^{\prime}u=\lim_{l}d_{\mathcal{F}}v_{l}
in
K^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
. Write
u_{s}=j^{\prime}_{s}u\in I^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
and
v_{l,s}=j^{\prime}_{s}v_{l}\in K^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
. Take
s
,
m
and
s^{\prime}
satisfying the the conditions of
Section

6.5.2
, obtaining
E^{\prime}_{m}:K^{\prime\,(s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F})\to I^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
and
F^{\prime}_{m}:I^{\prime\,(s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F})\to J^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
. Let
a_{s}=F^{\prime}_{m}u_{s^{\prime}}\in J^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
and
b_{l,s}=E^{\prime}_{m}v_{l,s^{\prime}}\in I^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
. By (
6.5.1
),

d_{\mathcal{F}}a_{s}=F^{\prime}_{m}d_{\mathcal{F}}u_{s^{\prime}}=0\;.

Moreover, by (
6.5.5
) and (
6.5.7
),

\displaystyle u_{s}

\displaystyle=j^{\prime}_{s^{\prime},s}u_{s^{\prime}}=\iota^{\prime}_{s}F^{\prime}_{m}u_{s^{\prime}}+E^{\prime}_{m}R^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}u_{s^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\iota^{\prime}_{s}a_{s}+\lim_{l}E^{\prime}_{m}d_{\mathcal{F}}v_{l,s^{\prime}}=\iota^{\prime}_{s}a_{s}+\lim_{l}d_{\mathcal{F}}b_{l,s}\;.

Now consider the above notation for greater real numbers
s_{1}
,
m_{1}
and
s^{\prime}_{1}
, satisfying the same properties as
s
,
m
and
s^{\prime}
. By (
6.5.8
) and (
6.5.9
),

\displaystyle j^{\prime}_{s_{1},s}a_{s_{1}}=j^{\prime}_{s_{1},s}F^{\prime}_{m_{1}}u_{s^{\prime}_{1}}=F^{\prime}_{m}j^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}_{1},s^{\prime}}u_{s^{\prime}_{1}}=F^{\prime}_{m}u_{s^{\prime}}=a_{s}\;.

\displaystyle j^{\prime}_{s_{1},s}b_{l,s_{1}}=j^{\prime}_{s_{1},s}E^{\prime}_{m_{1}}v_{l,s^{\prime}_{1}}=E^{\prime}_{m}j^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}_{1},s^{\prime}}v_{l,s^{\prime}_{1}}=E^{\prime}_{m}v_{l,s^{\prime}}=b_{l,s}\;.

Therefore, taking
s\uparrow+\infty
,
m\uparrow+\infty
and
s^{\prime}\uparrow+\infty
, satisfying the above relations, the elements
a_{s}\in J^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
and
b_{l,s}\in I^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
define elements
a:=(a_{s})_{s}\in ZJ^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
and
b_{l}:=(b_{l,s})_{s}\in I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
, and we have
u=\iota^{\prime}a+\lim_{l}d_{\mathcal{F}}b_{l}
. Hence
\overline{[u]}=\bar{\iota}_{*}(\overline{[a]})
.

Remark 6.5.1
.

A similar argument, taking an element
v\in K^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
instead of a net
v_{l}
, shows the inclusion
\ker R^{\prime}_{*}=\operatorname{im}\iota^{\prime}_{*}
in
H^{\bullet}I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
.

Remark 6.5.2
.

As before, to prove
\ker\widehat{R}^{\prime}_{*}=\operatorname{im}\hat{\iota}^{\prime}_{*}
in (
6.2.1
), we only have to prove “
\subset
”. For any
\hat{u}:=(\overline{[u_{s}]}_{s})_{s}\in\ker\widehat{R}^{\prime}_{*}
, there is some
v\in K^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
such that
R^{\prime}_{s}u_{s}=d_{\mathcal{F}}v_{s}
, where
v_{s}=j^{\prime}_{s}v
. Moreover,
j^{\prime}_{s^{\prime},s}u_{s^{\prime}}=u_{s}+\lim_{l}d_{\mathcal{F}}g_{l,s,s^{\prime}}
for some net
g_{l,s,s^{\prime}}
in
I^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
. Take
a_{s}
and
b_{s,l}
as above. The given argument shows that

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{F}}a_{s}=0\;,\quad u_{s}+\lim_{l}d_{\mathcal{F}}g_{l,s^{\prime},s}=\iota^{\prime}_{s}a_{s}+\lim_{l}d_{\mathcal{F}}b_{l,s}\;,

\displaystyle j^{\prime}_{s_{1},s}a_{s_{1}}=a_{s}+\lim_{l}d_{\mathcal{F}}F^{\prime}_{m}g_{l,s^{\prime}_{1},s^{\prime}}\;,\quad j^{\prime}_{s_{1},s}b_{l,s_{1}}=b_{l,s}\;.

Hence
\hat{a}:=(\overline{[a_{s}]}_{s})_{s}\in\widehat{H}^{\bullet}J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
is defined and
\hat{\iota}^{\prime}_{*}(\hat{a})=\hat{u}
.

6.5.4.
Injectivity of
\bar{\iota}^{\prime}_{*}

Let
\overline{[u]}\in\bar{H}^{r}J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
such that
\bar{\iota}^{\prime}_{*}(\overline{[u]})=0
. This means that there is a net
v_{l}
in
I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
such that
\iota^{\prime}u=\lim_{l}d_{\mathcal{F}}v_{l}
in
I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
. Write
u_{s}=j^{\prime}_{s}u\in ZK^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
and
v_{l,s}=j^{\prime}_{s}v_{l}\in I^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
. With the notation of
Section

6.5.3
, let
b_{l,s}=F^{\prime}_{m}v_{l,s^{\prime}}\in J^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
. By (
6.5.1
) and (
6.5.4
),

u_{s}=j^{\prime}_{s^{\prime},s}u_{s^{\prime}}=F^{\prime}_{m}\iota^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}u_{s^{\prime}}=\lim_{l}F^{\prime}_{m}d_{\mathcal{F}}v_{l,s^{\prime}}=\lim_{l}d_{\mathcal{F}}b_{l,s}\;.

Like in
Section

6.5.3
, it can be shown that, taking
s\uparrow+\infty
,
m\uparrow+\infty
and
s^{\prime}\uparrow+\infty
as above, the elements
b_{l,s}\in J^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
define elements
b_{l}:=(b_{l,s})_{s}\in J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
, and we have
u=\lim_{l}d_{\mathcal{F}}b_{l}
. Thus
\overline{[u]}=0
in
\bar{H}^{r}J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
.

Remark 6.5.3
.

Like in
6.5.1
, we also get the injectivity of
\iota^{\prime}_{*}
.

Remark 6.5.4
.

To prove the injectivity of
\hat{\iota}^{\prime}_{*}
, take any
\hat{u}:=(\overline{[u_{s}]}_{s})_{s}\in\ker\hat{\iota}^{\prime}_{*}
in (
6.2.1
). Then there is some net
v_{l,s}
in every
I^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
such that
\iota^{\prime}_{s}u_{s}=\lim_{l}d_{\mathcal{F}}v_{l,s}
in
I^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
. Moreover,
j^{\prime}_{s^{\prime},s}u_{s^{\prime}}=u_{s}+\lim_{l}d_{\mathcal{F}}g_{l,s,s^{\prime}}
for some net
g_{l,s,s^{\prime}}
in
J^{\prime\,(s)}(\mathcal{F})
. Take
b_{l,s}
as before. The above argument shows that

u_{s}+\lim_{l}d_{\mathcal{F}}g_{l,s^{\prime},s}=\lim_{l}d_{\mathcal{F}}b_{l,s}\;.

So
\hat{u}=0
in
\widehat{H}^{\bullet}J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
.

6.5.5.
Surjectivity of
\bar{R}^{\prime}_{*}

Take any
[u]\in H^{\bullet}K(\mathcal{F})
, and write
u_{s}=j^{\prime}_{s}u\in ZK^{(s)}(\mathcal{F})
. With the notation of
Section

6.5.2
, we have
v_{s}:=E^{\prime}_{m}v_{s^{\prime}}\in ZI^{(s^{\prime})}(\mathcal{F})
by (
6.5.7
), and
R^{\prime}_{s}v_{s}=j^{\prime}_{s^{\prime},s}u_{s^{\prime}}=u_{s}
by (
6.5.6
).

Now consider the above notation for greater real numbers
s_{1}
,
m_{1}
and
s^{\prime}_{1}
, satisfying the same properties as
s
,
m
and
s^{\prime}
. By (
6.5.9
),

j^{\prime}_{s_{1},s}v_{s_{1}}=j^{\prime}_{s_{1},s}E^{\prime}_{m_{1}}v_{s^{\prime}_{1}}=E^{\prime}_{m}j^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}_{1},s^{\prime}}v_{s^{\prime}_{1}}=E^{\prime}_{m}v_{s^{\prime}}=v_{s}\;.

So
v:=(v_{s})_{s}\in ZI^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
satisfies
R^{\prime}v=u
, and therefore
\bar{R}^{\prime}_{*}(\overline{[v]})=\overline{[u]}
.

Remark 6.5.5
.

Using cohomology instead of reduced cohomology, the analogous argument gives the surjectivity of
R^{\prime}_{*}
.

Remark 6.5.6
.

To prove the surjectivity of
\widehat{R}^{\prime}_{*}
in (
6.2.1
), for any
[u]\in H^{\bullet}K(\mathcal{F})
, define
u_{s}
and
v_{s}
as above. We also have
R^{\prime}_{s}v_{s}=u_{s}
and
j^{\prime}_{s_{1},s}v_{s_{1}}=v_{s}
. Thus
\hat{v}:=(\overline{[v_{s}]}_{s})_{s}\in\widehat{H}^{\bullet}I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})
and
\widehat{R}^{\prime}_{*}\hat{v}=[u]
.

6.6.
Functoriality and leafwise homotopy invariance

6.6.1.
Pull-back of dual-conormal leafwise currents

Consider the notation and conditions of
Section

5.7.3
(including the conditions of
Section

5.7.1
). According to
Section

2.3.5
, the map (
3.2.23
) has a continuous extension

(6.6.1)

\phi^{*}:I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\to I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\;,

defined as the composition

I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\xrightarrow{\phi^{*}}I^{\prime}(M^{\prime},M^{\prime\,0};\phi^{*}\Lambda\mathcal{F})\xrightarrow{\phi^{*}}I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\;,

like (
5.7.2
), using (
2.3.11
) with
E=\Lambda\mathcal{F}
. We can also describe (
6.6.1
) as the restriction of (
2.8.24
) to dual-conormal currents of bidegree
(0,\bullet)
, like in (
3.2.33
). The map (
6.6.1
) is also a restriction of (
3.2.33
).

(
2.3.11
) with
E=\Lambda\mathcal{F}

Similarly, the analogs of (
2.2.20
) with
E=\Lambda\mathcal{F}
for (
2.7.9
) and (
2.7.10
) induce continuous homomorphisms

(6.6.2)

\displaystyle\phi^{*}:K^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\to K^{\prime}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\;,

(6.6.3)

\displaystyle\phi^{*}:J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\to J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\;.

By passing to cohomology and reduced cohomology, we get continuous homomorphisms,

(6.6.4)

\left\{\begin{gathered}\phi^{*}:H^{\bullet}K^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\to H^{\bullet}K^{\prime}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\;,\\
\begin{aligned} \phi^{*}:&H^{\bullet}I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\to H^{\bullet}I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\;,&\quad\phi^{*}:&\bar{H}^{\bullet}I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\to\bar{H}^{\bullet}I^{\prime}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\;,\\
\phi^{*}:&H^{\bullet}J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\to H^{\bullet}J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\;,&\quad\phi^{*}:&\bar{H}^{\bullet}J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F})\to\bar{H}^{\bullet}J^{\prime}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})\;.\end{aligned}\end{gathered}\right.
